FOR THE V I S H N E E K E E S

Preface
Reminiscing about Nietzsche in his early thirties, Malwida von Meysenbug
remarked, "How cheerful he could be, how heartily he could laugh."1 Nietz-
sche's scholarly interpreters, even if they notice his frequent paeans to laugh-
ter, tend to hear more contempt than heartiness in his sense of humor.2
Perhaps they recall such accusatory passages as this:
Cramped intestines betray themselves—you can bet on that—no less than
closet air, closet ceilings, closet narrowness.—This was what I felt just now
as I closed a very decent scholarly book—gratefully, very gratefully, but
also with a sense of relief.3
The belittling tone of this passage from The Gay Science (Die Frohliche
Wissenschaft) seems designed to offend those of us who engage in scholarly
writing. And yet, Nietzsche aims to incite an amused reaction even among
members of his own former profession. That his aim is not simply to display
contempt is indicated by his further remarks:
[M]y scholarly friends, I bless you even for your hunched backs. And for
despising, as I do, the "men of letters" and culture parasites. And for not
knowing how to make a business of the spirit. And for having opinions
that cannot be translated into financial values. And for not representing
anything that you are not.4
The scholar's case is but an instance of the general truth that "every craft
makes crooked."5
Why, then, create the unflattering portrait in the first place? Nietzsche, in
a book addressed to those who care about scholarship (the Wissenschaft of
Frohliche Wissenschaft), reminds us, in the words of Monty Python's Flying
Circus, that however admirable our enterprises, "Um ... well,... the whole
thing's a bit silly." Nietzsche may expect the cries of outrage that greet this
remark in the Monty Python sketch, but perhaps he hopes that, on reflection,
his scholarly readers might also conclude, "Silly! I suppose it is, a bit."6
If we do draw this conclusion, what are we to do about it? Nietzsche
denies that education can correct the tendency of any developed discipline


Vlii
PREFACE
to cripple. All mastery, while enabling, is also disabling. By practicing certain
moves and skills, one unlearns alternative habits. One cannot undo such
ingrained practices, nor would one want to undercut the expertise that one
has taken so long to master. One can, however, minimize the crippling effects
Nietzsche mentions by maintaining one's flexibility. The Gay Science is Nietz-
sche's attempt to promote mental resilience, not just among scholars, but
among all who find themselves rigidly caught in their well-trained methods.
The book not only encourages but also actually engages its readers in prac-
tices of approaching topics flexibly, often doing so by means of humor.
Nietzsche's The Gay Science has not usually been read as funny. It has
rarely even been treated as a book. Despite their occasional praise for its
form, scholars have tended to treat The Gay Science as a mine for remarks
on various topics, giving little attention to its structure as a whole. Here I
will consider the book as a coherent enterprise, one that employs literary
strategies, particularly parody and other comic techniques, to achieve phil-
osophical and religious ends.
Concentrating on the book in its first edition, I explore Nietzsche's at-
tempt at lighthearted scholarship and the means he uses to illustrate it. In
chapter i I explain my particular approach and my focus on Nietzsche's
treatment of central themes, or leitmotifs, in The Gay Science, such as per-
spectivism, the theatrical interplay of tragedy and comedy, the death of God,
eternal recurrence, and Zarathustra. In the case of virtually all of these
themes, scholars have underemphasized Nietzsche's parodic play with literary
precedents. I attempt to rectify this situation, resisting the view that is some-
times raised by proponents of deconstruction and postmodernism that Nietz-
sche's goals are primarily those of a demystifier (a matter I consider in in-
terlude 2).
For example, in chapter 2 I read the odd "Prelude in German Rhymes"
which opens the first edition of the book against the farce by Goethe that
shares its title, "Scherz, List und Rache" (Joke, Trick, and Revenge). By
alluding to Goethe's plot, which involves the mistaken identification of med-
icine as poison, Nietzsche suggests that the medicine that he himself offers—
an antidote to the ill effects of traditional moralism—will be seen by some
as poison. Goethe's plot, a farce about power struggles which makes no effort
to moralize, also resembles the "plot" of The Gay Science, which describes
human experience naturalistically, without moralizing platitudes, in terms of
human beings' natural desire for power. (Nietzsche praises the theater gen-
erally for helping us to see human beings extra-morally, as I discuss in in-
terlude 3.)
Similarly, in chapter 6 I draw attention to the literary precedents for the
famous parable of section 341, often seen as the first published statement of
Nietzsche's doctrine of eternal recurrence. Eternal recurrence, described by
a demon in section 341, offers an account of the meaning of life that recalls
the worldviews suggested by demons in the writings of Plato and Descartes.
By use of parody, Nietzsche underscores his intent to pose eternal recurrence


PREFACE
IX
as an alternative to the "otherworldly" visions of life's significance provided
by the Western philosophical and religious traditions.
In interlude 4 I also consider the literary source of Nietzsche's adoption
of Pindar's slogan "Become who you are." This formula, which arises in the
context of an ode that employs the image of Ixion's torture on a perpetually
rolling wheel, involves a rereading of what might well be considered a mor-
alistic platitude. Although "Become who you are" can be seen as a caveat to
recognize one's limitations, the context in Pindar indicates that the admo-
nition is to recognize and live up to the full measure of one's power. Through
our doing so, our own eternally rolling wheel, time as it is depicted by the
doctrine of eternal recurrence, becomes a means of self-realization, not eter-
nal punishment.
I also explore Nietzsche's use of humorous strategies to dislodge readers
from their typically somber philosophical habits and to incite their own
imaginative thinking. In chapter 4, for example, I consider the passages on
women that occur early in Book II of The Gay Science, which are often read
as a perverse digression that mars more important discussions. I read them,
instead, as designed to prompt the reader to reconsider commonplace as-
sumptions and to seriously entertain perspectives that are different from their
own. This strategy demonstrates that Nietzsche's thinking about the sexes is
complex, and that (at least in The Gay Science) he should not be pigeonholed
as a misogynist. It also provides an illustration of the impact our perspectives
have on our analyses of our situation, a point that Nietzsche applies to schol-
arship, as I consider in chapter 3.
Nietzsche's name is associated with ideas about power for many individ-
uals in Western culture (along with several associations that I consider in
interlude i). Often, however, the important religious dimension of Nietz-
sche's counsels regarding power are unappreciated. I argue in chapter 5 that
Nietzsche is convinced by Ludwig Feuerbach's argument that human beings
have impoverished themselves by projecting their own power into their con-
ception of God, and that he accordingly sees the death of God (Western
culture's shift to a secularized self-understanding) as an opportunity for hu-
man beings to rediscover "who they are." Human beings are the creators of
value; and the meaning of life is an artistic outcome of human acts of in-
terpreting and cultivating certain possibilities within their experience. The
death of God might occasion a new stage of human development, that of
homo poeta, "man" the artistic interpreter.
Nietzsche sees the rise of the myth of science, however, as a potential
obstacle to this new stage of development. Although science was the precon-
dition for the secular vision of the natural world that has displaced the theo-
centric vision, too many look to science as the source of value. The preva-
lence of anthropomorphic terms applied to nature is, according to Nietzsche,
a symptom of Western humanity's continued habit of projecting its own
power outward. The scientific worldview, if taken to provide values on ob-
jective terms, diminishes our impression of human power at least as much


X
PREFACE
as did Christian monotheism. Modern science depicts us as an accidental
phenomenon on the surface of an obscure planet in an undistinguished part
of the universe. If we seek grounds for finding our lives significant in such
a worldview, we are bound to feel impotent and frustrated.
Nietzsche contends, however, that this is not the only option. If we rec-
ognize that we ourselves create significance through our own interpretive
acts, we will be in a position to recognize that meaning in life is our artistic
project, not a verdict established by some power outside ourselves, be it God
or nature. Science may give us information about the constraints of the
material with which we work, but it does not dictate the ways in which we
might shape that material.
Nietzsche's concern that we rediscover ourselves as creators of value helps
to explain his use of the figure of Zarathustra, which I consider in chapter
7. Nietzsche's Zarathustra is usually seen as only casually related to the orig-
inal Persian prophet of that name. The reason for this is that Zarathustra
announced a sharp distinction between good and evil, the moral binary that
Nietzsche so often attacks. My reading departs from others' in taking Nietz-
sche's Zarathustra to be a theatrical depiction of the original Persian prophet.
That is, Nietzsche reconsiders the significance of Zarathustra's philosophizing
much as a playwright might reconsider the story of a historical figure.
Zarathustra, in Nietzsche's vision, is a philosophical hero engaged in the
activity of legislating values. The distinction between good and evil is an
original act of creative interpretation, the West's first philosophical expres-
sion of the basic human activity of evaluating. The continuation of this ac-
tivity that would have been in keeping with the spirit of Zarathustra would
have been for Western humanity to continue to refine its discernment and
revise its value judgments. Instead, Western thought froze Zarathustra's in-
itial evaluation, taking it out of its context and applying it indiscriminately.
Western moralism accepted Zarathustra's distinction as a substitute for de-
veloping judgment. Nietzsche's "immoralist" depiction of Zarathustra rep-
resents his alternative reading of moral history. What our tradition has con-
sidered morality, the fetishizing of a single step in moral judgment, is in
direct opposition to the vital activity of interpretation that Zarathustra began.
Nietzsche's presentation of Zarathustra accordingly heralds a return to the
condition that preceded Western morality and its effects. Having led any
serious reader of The Gay Science to entertain unfamiliar perspectives over
the course of the book, Nietzsche suggests that we are now prepared to
reconsider the moral tradition. With Zarathustra's appearance, we are en-
couraged to engage again in the activity of the historical prophet—to value
and to revalue within our naturalistic context, recognizing this as our in-
herent human power.
Revaluation, an art that depends on flexibility, counters the pressuring
impact of our cultivated outlook, which inevitably has a "leaden ceiling ...
that presses and presses down upon the soul until that becomes queer and
crooked."7 We need relief, Nietzsche tells us, from the effects of our own
learning, but the solution is not a new-found ignorance. The best relief from


PREFACE
xi
the strain of our accumulated learning is further reflection. Nietzsche's pre-
scription for alleviating the cramped intestines and other ailments of modern,
disciplined thought can be summarized in the words of his Zarathustra:
"learn—to laugh!"8

about Nietzsche. I am especially grateful for his demonstrating how appre-
ciation of Nietzsche's insights could be combined with critical distance, and
how originality is enhanced by clear expression. Louis Dupre, Charles Fei-
delson, John L. Swanay, and Hans Uffelmann were other teachers whose
visions of Nietzsche have shaped my own, divergent as their views are from
one another. I am grateful as well to James P. Davis, who persuaded me to
read Nietzsche and who first showed me Nietzsche's funny side.
Many others have helped bring my thinking about Nietzsche into focus.
Among these are many with whom I have interacted at sessions of the North

classes on Nietzsche. This project was nurtured most directly by my ongoing

regarding parts of this manuscript, and also to Karen Mottola and Andrew

COMIC RELIEF


This page intentionally left blank


Frolicking Wissenschaft
Lonely as a Rhino
David Bowie, on National Public Radio sometime back, startled his inter-
viewer by saying that guitar style was a good guide to humor. A person with
a broad sense of humor, Bowie elaborated, will reveal it through musical
risks. We should keep Bowie's principle in mind, I think, in reading Nietz-
sche's Frohliche Wissenschaft, The Gay Science. My thesis, straightforwardly,
is that in this work Nietzsche often aimed to be funny.1
Of course, humor was not Nietzsche's sole purpose, but it is intrinsic to
the project announced by the title, to demonstrate a lighthearted approach
to Wissenschaft, or scholarly investigation. Granted, Nietzsche is not mainly
known as a humorist.2 This is so for a number of reasons. Nietzsche's levity
reflects his being a very serious man. Like the humor of certain stand-up
comedians, Nietzsche's jokes are often black-humored, pointing out incon-
gruities that might in other moods cause tears. Tragedy and comedy, as the
first section of The Gay Science insists, are kindred outlooks, perpetually
overwhelming each other, as one wave succeeds another, in turn to be over-
come.
Bowie's maxim offers a partial explanation for the underappreciation of
Nietzsche's humor. His jokes are sometimes too risky, to the point of being
lost on his commentators. For example, consider Nietzsche's letter to his
friend Carl von Gersdorff, in which Nietzsche reports that he read an English
translation of an early Buddhist poem. The poem involves the refrain "Let
one wander alone like a rhinoceros," which Nietzsche restates as "I wander
lonely as the rhinoceros."3 In making his case that Nietzsche did not actively
pursue an interest in Indian philosophy, Mervyn Sprung dismisses this as "a
typical Nietzschean re-reading of a text."4
Indeed, this paraphrase is quintessential Nietzsche, for it is a pun-like
conflation of the Buddhist text (with its reference to the weighty rhinoceros)
with a classic English poem, Wordsworth's "I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud."
Perhaps Nietzsche's reading the Buddhist text in English partially suggested
ONE
3


4
COMIC RELIEF
the splice, although Nietzsche formulated his restatement primarily in
German. But what Sprung takes as a symptom of sloppy scholarship is, I
submit, a product of two motivations, both of which Nietzsche sees as crucial
but often absent in scholarship. First, it is a function of Nietzsche's putting
his scholarly inquiries to work in his own life. The context of his paraphrase
to Gersdorff makes this clear: "I have already made domestic use of one of
the refrains of a Sutta: Thus I wander, lonely as the rhinoceros."5
Nietzsche's splice is also a rather outlandish joke about the gravity of
scholars. His repetition of the rhino line in Daybreak makes evident the intent
of self-caricature in this regard:
Since the progress of the sage, who as the Buddhist hymn says, "walks
alone like the rhinoceros," is heavy and crushes all in its path—there is
need from time to time of a sign of a conciliatory and gentler humanity:
and by that I mean, not only a display of wit and a certain self-mockery,
but a self-contradiction and an occasional regression into the nonsense
currently in vogue. If he is not to resemble a steamroller which advances
like fatality, the sage who wants to teach has to employ his faults as an
extenuation....6
Presumably Nietzsche includes himself as one of the scholars caricatured
(though if so, this self-mockery keeps him at least from being a steamroller).
Anyone who has seen a full-length photograph of Nietzsche knows that he
did not cut a svelte figure. Milan Kundera has reminded us of the importance
of gravity (literal gravity) in Nietzsche's thought7; and considering Nietzsche's
emphasis on the influence of bodily conditions on thought, I doubt he spared
himself from physical humor at his own expense. Elsewhere he even com-
ments that the belly prevents us from taking ourselves to be gods.8
Besides the common expectation that philosophy will not be funny, other
factors, too, have obscured the comic Nietzsche. Perhaps his appearance itself
has misled readers. His melancholy expression, darkened by bushy eyebrows
and mustache in his most famous picture—could a man with such a face be
capable of hilarity? Nietzsche, however, characterizes the famous mustache
as a disguise:
We are too prone to forget that in the eyes of people who are seeing us
for the first time we are something quite different from what we consider
ourselves to be: usually we are nothing more than a single individual trait
which leaps to the eye and determines the whole impression we make.
Thus the gentlest and most reasonable of men can, if he wears a large
moustache, sit as it were in its shade and feel safe there—he will usually
be seen as no more than the appurtenance of a large moustache, that is to
say a military type, easily angered and occasionally violent—and as such
he will be treated.9
This passage is comic, literalizing synecdoche, the figure of speech that takes
a part for the whole.10 The self-reference is too oblique, however, to have
shown Nietzsche's legion of readers the twinkling eyes behind the mustache.


FROLICKING WISSENSCHAFT
5
Our views are more thoroughly shaped by the famous picture that adorns
the covers of many books by and about Nietzsche, one with shaggy facial
hair and a distant gaze. This image stems from the decade of Nietzsche's
madness and was posed by his sister Elisabeth, who sought a ponderous
image to serve her own self-promoting agenda. One would be hard put to
find it a barometer of humor. But this contrived and painful depiction is
hardly indicative of Nietzsche's demeanor before disease undermined his ex-
pressive powers. We would do better to ponder the photograph in which he
and Paul Ree pose as the horses for the cart that pulls Lou Salome, whip in
hand. That photograph, after all, was posed by Nietzsche himself, and it
portrays him grinning, perversely pleased.
Still a further reason why Nietzsche's humor in The Gay Science has been
overlooked is that the book has too seldom been read as a book. Instead, it
has typically been read as a collection of freestanding sections, their inter-
connections scarcely noted. Even when considered in its own right, the book
is most often read as an alternative presentation of themes addressed else-
where in Nietzsche's works, the sections mere prototypes for later analyses.
Walter Kaufmann aptly complains about this approach.
What may at first seem to be a haphazard sequence of aphorisms turns
out to be a carefully crafted composition in which almost every section
means much more in context than will ever be noted by readers who
assume, in flat defiance of Nietzsche's own repeated pleas to the contrary,
that each section is a self-sufficient aphorism. The structure is extremely
important. ..."
Richard Schacht similarly observes that in The Gay Science, "we have much
more than the disjointed collection of reflections and aphorisms which it
may at first glance appear to be."12 We have, I contend, a book that guides
the reader on a tour of diverse perspectives, with section breaks signaling
new bursts of thinking, sometimes, but not always, adding to a growing train
of thought.13
My purpose is to offer an interpretation of Nietzsche's book as a unified
project. I am concerned mainly with The Gay Science in its first incarnation,
which began with a prelude in rhymes and ended with the appearance of
Zarathustra at the end of Book IV. This strategy may seem radically inap-
propriate to most anglophone philosophers who consider the book. Typically,
they focus on Book V, which is more argumentative and, therefore, more in
keeping with the current gladiatorial atmosphere in the field. This emphasis
also accords with the general preference for Nietzsche's late works, which are
primarily critiques of the Western tradition, especially Beyond Good and Evil
(1886) and On the Genealogy of Morals (1887). Part V, like Nietzsche's other
late works, engages in more sustained argumentation and oppositional po-
lemic than his earlier, more aphoristic works, including the original edition
of The Gay Science.
By contrast, I am interested in the vision that unifies the original work. I
therefore analyze the shape of the book in its original form, ending with


6
COMIC RELIEF
Book IV. I do not entirely ignore Book V, but I usually make use of passages
from it (some of which are literary gems) when they relate to topics that
arise in the course of analyzing the book in its earlier form.14 That form
draws inspiration from the techniques of theater, traditionally symbolized by
tragic and comic masks side by side. I argue that the book is conscientiously
theatrical, shaped deliberately for dramatic effect, sometimes for shock value.
As Bernd Magnus, Stanley Stewart, and Jean-Pierre Mileur observe, "few
philosophers have been more alert to the variety of dramatic possibilities
than Nietzsche, the theorist of tragedy and comedy alike."15
Theatrical Perspectives
By the time he wrote The Gay Science, Nietzsche had been thinking about
theater for some time. Theatrical tragedy in ancient Greece was the topic of
his first book, The Birth of Tragedy. Moreover, Nietzsche wrote the later book
subsequent to a close relationship with Richard Wagner, in which the two
had discussed aesthetic matters over a number of years. Nietzsche had been
immersed in Wagner's concerns and privy to Wagner's running commentary
on his operatic work in progress.16 Surely this immersion into discussion of
musical drama made Nietzsche himself alert to the dramatic potential of his
literary sequences. Nietzsche took pains over the ordering of passages in all
his "aphoristic" works.17 The Gay Science, the culmination of that series,
exploits such dramatic features as aggressive juxtapositions, jumps in se-
quencing, and framing devices.
Perhaps it would not be going too far to see The Gay Science as resembling
a theatrical opening night, deliberately rivaling Wagner's Bayreuth open-
ings.18 Indeed, as I consider in chapter 2, the book begins with a jarring
reference to a farcical drama, and some of Nietzsche's remarks in corre-
spondence suggest that he consciously contrasted it with Wagner's Parsifal.19
The friendship between Nietzsche and Wagner had long been ruptured by
the time he produced The Gay Science, but Wagner remained a potent psy-
chological presence, often serving as subject matter.20
Whatever the precise impact of Wagner, Nietzsche frames the prose sec-
tions of The Gay Science in terms of tragedy and comedy. From the first
section these alternative theatrical forms are presented as paradigms for in-
terpreting life.21 So viewed, tragedy and comedy are fundamentally philo-
sophical forms, representing divergent outlooks on life. The contrast between
tragic and comic worldviews illustrates strikingly the impact of perspective
on our experience, a central theme within the book. In general, the purpose
of Wissenschaft will appear very different, depending on whether the life in
which it is pursued is deemed tragic or comic.
Nietzsche's decision to frame so much of the book in terms of tragedy
and comedy may make my emphasis on humor seem dubious. If tragedy is
as much a part of the picture as comedy, why select one over the other? In
part, I do so because Nietzsche's tragic concerns are well recognized. Yet


FROLICKING WISSENSCHAFT
7
Nietzsche treats the two perspectives as complementary, the ebb and flow of
our confidence that our lives have value.22 Our understanding of his emphasis
on tragedy is distorted unless we acknowledge its relation to comedy. Tragedy
and comedy are both responses to whatever obstructions intrude on our
horizon, challenging our sense that our life has coherence and meaning. For
Nietzsche, the tragic and comic are two ways of resolving such crises, as
Lawrence Hatab notes:
In Nietzsche's eyes, the tragic and the comic are two sides of the same
coin, two ways in which negation can be acknowledged and affirmed....
In fact, comedy and laughter can emerge as a particular positive response
to the tragic, without, however, denying the negativity of the tragic. In
other words, comedy, for Nietzsche, becomes a special way in which a
tragic situation need not involve a negative state of mind.23
In the book's opening section, Nietzsche goes so far as to suggest that the
comic is a more profound approach than the tragic.24 Taking this as a cue,
I emphasize Nietzsche's treatment of comedy in The Gay Science, acknowl-
edging all the while that tragedy is an essential part of the story—and perhaps
the ultimate motive of Nietzsche's philosophizing.
Nietzsche's Postcards
From the start Nietzsche's idiosyncrasies are evident in The Gay Science. The
decision to open the book with sixty-four outbursts in doggerel is itself tes-
timony to the author's peculiarity. Within the body of the book, he takes
delight in reversing habitual valuations.25 He several times described the book
as one of his most personal. And his causal accounts are thoroughly non-
staridard. For example, he suggests that those who transform the general taste
are usually driven by "some oddity in their life style, nutrition, or digestion,
perhaps a deficit or excess of inorganic salts in their blood and brain; in
brief, in their physis."26
Nietzsche's acquaintances also deemed him an eccentric, bipolar person-
ality. He was emotionally sensitive but hampered by his "over-subtlety," as
his onetime friend Lou Salome described him.27 Malwida von Meysenbug
described him as having a "generous, amiable nature" which at optimum
times "was in equilibrium with his acerbic intellect.28 As for the acerbic side,
Nietzsche was certainly capable of trenchant and unnerving observation; at
the same time, he was intuitive, able to empathize with others' motives,
sometimes, one suspects, to the point of incapacity.29 His own remarks re-
inforce our sense that he was eccentric. From his letters we know that he
actually believed merchants who told him, "For you, I have my very best
grapes."30
Recent Western philosophy does not often deal with the personal features
of anyone's experience. Nietzsche's novel demand that philosophies be seen
as reflections of the individuals who construct them has been often noted


8
COMIC RELIEF
but little observed. A few well-known anecdotes from Nietzsche's personal
history (his break with Wagner, his unsuccessful flirtation with Lou Salome,
and his infamous mental breakdown) have often been mentioned as pretexts
to certain themes in his work; but emphasis has more often been placed on
his foresight regarding cultural developments that were later generally ac-
knowledged.
No one denies that Nietzsche was a strange man; but many scholars think
that one can avoid his peculiar biases and find value in those claims and
arguments that less peculiar characters might also defend.31 From such a
standpoint, one might try to construct a coherent vision from Nietzsche's
philosophy, discounting those areas in which his thought is vague or, from
our point of view, deficient. I concur with part of this strategy; I doubt that
one can read much of Nietzsche without making some judgments about
which of his views are worthwhile and which are defective.
One does more than this, however, when one interprets Nietzsche's
thought as systematic, ignoring those features of his thought that one con-
siders marginal. The apparent merit of this approach is that it renders Nietz-
sche more like those philosophers whose works are systematic and thereby
prone to the usual philosophical critiques. Its disadvantage, however, is that
it ignores the uniqueness of Nietzsche's eye for the startling anomaly.
I start on the assumption that Nietzsche's philosophical perspective is
eccentric. His presentation in The Gay Science is not systematic but very
carefully orchestrated, and its uniqueness is precisely what we should find
valuable. We do not read Nietzsche for analyses that many others could give
us; we read Nietzsche because he presents a perspective that we do not en-
counter elsewhere. Nietzsche's description of The Gay Science as one of his
most personal books suggests that one should not expect it to be offering
universalistic propositions.32 My concern is to ascertain what it does offer us
instead.
Gary Shapiro proposes a useful metaphor for the book as personal memoir
when he remarks that Nietzsche might be viewed as "the philosopher of the
postal age," whose voluminous correspondence "suggests a writer energeti-
cally disseminating his work on all fronts."33 Although I doubt that all of his
actual correspondence should be considered part of Nietzsche's "work" in
the grand sense (some of it being rather functional or cursory),34 this image
of the postal writer is appropriate to the format of The Gay Science. Each
new aphorism appears as a new missive from the writer we know from
previous sections, although perhaps displaying a mood that we have not seen
before. Concerns resurface, much as they do in a personal correspondence,
but new interests assume the foreground for a time, later to give way to
further reflections on earlier themes.
Yet why should we find Nietzsche's perspective valuable, especially if it is
eccentric and disjointed as a series of postcards from a traveler? Nietzsche
asks the same question himself in his preface to the second edition of The
Gay Science. "But let us leave Herr Nietzsche: what is it to us that Herr
Nietzsche has become well again?"35 In answering, he suggests that his book


FROLICKING WISSENSCHAFT
9
should be read as a kind of lab book, in which his own experiments have
been recorded, experiments that developed his understanding of "the relation
of health and philosophy": [A]ssuming that one is a person, one necessarily
also has the philosophy that belongs to that person... ."36 In effect, Nietzsche
suggests that he is presenting himself and his observations as material for
the budding "philosophical physician,"
one who has to pursue the problem of the total health of a people, time,
race or of humanity—to muster the courage to push my suspicion to its
limits and to risk the proposition: what was at stake in all philosophizing
hitherto was not at all "truth" but something else—let us say, health, fu-
ture, growth, power, life.37
Reading The Gay Science as Nietzsche's lab book, we can do what we will
with his data. It may encourage us to seek confirmation or falsification of
his results, or it may encourage us to look for a different experimental design.
It is relevant to us if we share Nietzsche's philosophical commitment to seek
the grounds of human flourishing. In light of this project, we can also answer
the question, "What is it to us that Herr Nietzsche is trying to be funny?"
Nietzsche is proposing that a comic outlook—a view that adjusts for and
delights in incongruities that undercut one's expectations—is optimal if a
person is to thrive.
"Alas, I Am a Nuance."38: A Defense of Intentionalism
My effort to read The Gay Science in light of Nietzsche's own intentions may
seem suspect in the current climate of literary criticism. It may also appear
questionable in that of anglophone philosophy, which has long preferred
objective description to subjective accounts and has sought to minimize spec-
ulation. I should therefore defend my reference to Nietzsche's purposes.
The New Critics of the 19505 contended that literary works should be
judged internally, without reference to their authors' personalities.39 Contem-
porary literary critics who reject this modernist doctrine nevertheless rein-
force the New Critics' proscription of "the intentional fallacy" in their in-
terpretive avoidance of biographical evidence in favor of revelatory details
within texts. Alexander Nehamas falls into this category. Nehamas drives a
wedge between interpretation of Nietzsche's texts and psychobiography by
emphasizing the difference between the writer, with his or her particular
psychology, and the persona the texts present. Through his writings, Nietz-
sche "created a character out of himself," and Nehamas describes Nietzsche's
texts as constituting "Nietzsche himself." For Nehamas this persona is the
"Nietzsche" of interest, not the quirky person who produced it.40
A somewhat similar distinction is made by Roland Barthes, and utilized
by certain interpreters of Nietzsche, particularly Magnus, Stewart, and Mi-
leur. Barthes distinguishes between the "writer," the person who is the "ef-
ficient cause" of a text, and the "author." The "author," a technical term,


10
COMIC RELIEF
refers to a construct that emerges from the complex of the written work and
the various interpretations that affect its perceived meaning.41 If one adopts
this strategy, one should not expect direct encounter with the writer through
a text, and one should not attribute the writer's personal psychology to the
author.
Although I grant that meaningful distinctions can be made between writer
and persona, or writer and "author" in Barthes's technical sense, I think that
these distinction are important only in certain interpretive efforts. They are
helpful in comparing the emphases of different texts, for example, particu-
larly with regard to how accommodating they are to readers. For most pur-
poses, however, I think we do well to mean by "the author" just what was
meant before these distinctions were articulated: the author is the person one
encounters in the text. (Indeed, the term "person" conveys the ambiguity
that so interests recent literary theorists. The "person" is the mask, with the
mask's ability to obscure.)42
The enthusiast of Barthes's distinction might counter that to understand
"the author" as I suggest is to project onto the text the idea of a person, the
writer, that the text cannot present. I am reminded of Nietzsche's taunts at
the realist, who believes that one can get beneath appearances to reality:
"Subtract the phantasm.... If you can!"43 I think a similar taunt could be
leveled at anyone who insists that there actually are separable characters, an
"author" and a "writer." "The author" and "the writer" are both as much
theoretical constructs as "the real thing," or as "the person one encounters
through the text." We cannot avoid postulation.
I agree with interpreters of a postmodern bent that direct encounter is
too much to expect from a text. Indeed, "direct" encounter would be un-
mediated by words on pages. Nevertheless, I assume that writing is an effort
at communication, and that writers aim to communicate, even when they
themselves despair of communicating "totally." We never get the author an
sich in a literary work; we never get the person an sich through communi-
cative gestures of any sort. Any gesture calls for interpretation. This does not
imply that communication never occurs.
Actual communication depends on receptivity; and the reader's receptivity
is a case in point. Commitment to the view that communication is impossible
is a posture that prevents receptive reading, although it is not the only reason
communication is sometimes blocked. Written communication inherently
risks failure because the writer is not present to insist that a particular in-
terpretation is not what he meant at all. I am convinced nonetheless that
communication, albeit with limitations, often occurs when a reader is willing
to entertain what he or she construes as the writer's meaning.
Certainly, Nietzsche himself considered writing a mode of communication
that is inevitably connected with the experiences and purposes of the person
doing the writing. Nietzsche describes philosophical texts as unconscious per-
sonal memoirs of their authors.44 He analyzes the work of other writers and
artists (Chamfort, Shakespeare, and Beethoven, for example) in motivational
terms. He similarly argues in Ecce Homo that style aims to communicate an


FROLICKING WISSENSCHAFT
II
inward state.45 In the preface to the second edition of The Gay Science, he
insists not only that the book depends on his experiences, but also that those
who lack similar experiences are unlikely to understand it.46 Even his ac-
knowledgment that writing sometimes does not convey meaning presupposes
a communicative aim: "It is not by any means necessarily an objection to a
book when anyone finds it impossible to understand: perhaps that was part
of the author's intention—he did not want to be understood by just 'any-
body.' "47
The fact that Nietzsche endorses an intentionalist approach does not, of
course, legitimate it. I consider it justified in this context, not because Nietz-
sche concurs with it, but because it makes sense of the design of the book.
The text induces reflection on how perspectives structure our apprehension
of reality, and it does so at times by meddling with the reader's perspective.
The sequence of presentation is manipulative, and the reader can hardly
avoid the impression of visiting perspectives not his or her own. Nietzsche's
willfulness as tour guide is evident, and the program of the tour clearly
"intentional."
I accept Nehamas's view, however, that Nietzsche idealizes his experiences
in The Gay Science, conveying impressions of his character that are not iden-
tical to those we would gain from psychobiography. The book is comparable
to a lab report only to a certain degree; it is crafted artistically in a way that
would not suit laboratory notes (although it might suit a talk based on lab
work). Nevertheless, the distinction between Nietzsche as a person and Nietz-
sche as he displays himself is not as clear or convincing as Nehamas contends
when he writes, "In engaging with his works, we are not engaging with the
miserable little man who wrote them but with the philosopher that emerges
through them, the magnificent character these texts constitute and mani-
fest. .. ."481 do not see Nietzsche's writings as a substitute life for an otherwise
pathetic man.49 Instead, they are a record and central component of Nietz-
sche's way of life as an unusually aware human being.
A Throng of Leitmotifs
So far I have suggested that The Gay Science is intricately assembled in de-
liberate segues of aphorisms. My analysis does not simply mirror these pat-
terns, although I hope to draw attention to them. Instead I organize several
chapters around central themes, as they sequentially become prominent in
the subdivisions of the text.
Eugen Fink suggestively compares these central themes to leitmotifs,
which he claims retain importance in Nietzsche's later work:
In The Gay Science the central key ideas of Nietzsche's philosophy are
already hinted at in a manner not unlike the leitmotiv. They form the
deeper background . . . : the death of God, the Will to Power, the eternal
recurrence of the same, and the overman.50


12
COMIC RELIEF
The leitmotif, a musical label attached to a particular character or concept,
was one of Wagner's musical innovations. The system of leitmotifs was de-
signed to bring various moments in the drama to bear on one another. Once
mnemonically associated with a character or theme, the leitmotif could suc-
cinctly imply something about the relationship of its referent to the present
situation in a music drama. Sometimes the mention of a leitmotif in a par-
ticular context would be startling, perhaps suggesting that whatever his ac-
tions, a character's thoughts were elsewhere, with the leitmotifs referent
(whether person or purpose). Almost always, the leitmotif would remind the
audience of the fuller psychological texture behind the surface.
My list of central themes differs somewhat from Fink's. I see little concern
with the overman (or Ubermensch) in The Gay Science, while perspectivism
is a prominent interest. Nevertheless, I think his comparison between Nietz-
sche's and Wagner's techniques of thematic weaving helps to clarify the
book's structure. Nietzsche's meditations on various themes are enriched by
his discussing them in different contexts, in the light of new reflections.
Moreover, by structuring his presentation as a series of fragments, Nietzsche
directs our thinking into specific sequences, manipulating our experience of
reflecting to provoke certain associations. Like the Wagnerian listener who
attends to the leitmotifs, we draw connections on our own, yet at Nietzsche's
direction.
Fink's comparison between Nietzsche's and Wagner's methods may be apt
in another regard, too: both risk misfiring. Wagner's use of leitmotifs did
not strike everyone as well suited to his purposes. Debussy, for example,
claimed that "the Leitmotiv system suggests a world of harmless lunatics who
present their visiting-cards and shout their names in song."51 Those who
question Nietzsche's literary judgment might consider Debussy's description
applicable to The Gay Science, particularly to the rhymes that open the book.
Especially if we imagine them sung, as Nietzsche encourages when he com-
pares them to troubadour songs, what better judgment than "harmless lu-
nacy" can we apply to such literary outbursts as the this?
Die krumme Nase
Die Nase schauet trutziglich
In Land, der Niister blahet sich—
Drum fallst du, Nashorn ohne Horn,
Mein stolzes Menschlein, stets nach vorn!
Und stets beisammen fmd't sich das:
Gerader Stolz, gekrummte Nas'.
A Crooked Nose
Its nostrils proud and pliant,
The nose looks out defiant.
That's why, a rhino without horn,
You fall forward, proud little man;
And straight pride generally grows
Together with a crooked nose.52


FROLICKING WISSBNSCHAFT
13
Particularly ludicrous is the suggestion that you can subtract the horn
from a Nashorn (rhinoceros)—which yields, in German, a Nas' (nose). "Sub-
tract the rhino—if you can!" one might imagine Nietzsche taunting the
reader. As we look directly at the book's opening verses, we will see Nietz-
sche's risky humor in action, scientific for all its gaiety.53


Nietzsche's Nursery Rhymes
Nietzsche's Conceit
What should we make of the poems that begin The Gay Science*. Titled "Joke,
Trick, and Revenge," they are cast as somewhat ominous. But when one
reads them, they seem light, at times a bit silly, and at their harshest pointedly
sarcastic. The series of poems is unified, furthermore, more by means of
their simple rhyme scheme than by subject matter. They often sound child-
ish, resembling nursery rhymes with their regular meters and rhyming ca-
dences. What on earth could be Nietzsche's point?
Nietzsche's own remarks scarcely enlighten us. At times he seems to have
been somewhat nervous about the opening rhymes. This is especially evident
in his letters to scholars, who would be unaccustomed to such frivolous
versifying in an apparently scholarly book. Writing to Jakob Burckhardt, that
the book as a whole "is so personal," he adds "and everything personal is
indeed comic" and goes on to ask, "And my verses?"1 Yet Nietzsche at times
mentions the poems with apparent pride, as when he announced to his pub-
lisher, "For the fall you will be able to have a manuscript from me: Title,
"The Gay Science" (with many epigrams in verse!!!)."2 This may, however,
be more revelatory of Nietzsche's business sense than of his personal attitude;
publishers paid more for pages of poetry than for pages of prose.3 Nietzsche
also sent a prepublication copy of the "prelude in German rhymes" to Lou
Salome, the woman he seems to have been most eager to impress.4 Still, he
referred to them in a later letter with the far from boastful "meine Verschen"
(my little verses), and describes them as "meinen Reimereieren" ("my dog-
gerel" or "my jingles") in a letter to Heinrich Koselitz (a.k.a. Peter Gast),
whose attitude toward the poems was consistently enthusiastic.5
Perhaps Nietzsche's most informative comment about the opening rhymes
appears in a letter to Erwin Rohde: "As for the title 'Gay Science,' I thought
only of the gaya scienza of the troubadours—hence also the little verses."6
This suggestion gives us a clue to a possible basis for interpreting the prelude
14
Two


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
15
in rhymes. They may amount to Nietzsche's call for a rebirth of the trou-
badour spirit.
Nevertheless, other bases for interpretation also have merit. The nursery
rhyme format recalls childhood rhythms and the lessons they conveyed. If
we follow this interpretive line, the poems lead us back to childhood and
our earliest educational experiences. The title "Scherz, List und Rache," how-
ever, offers still another entree, for it is also the title of a farcical Singspiel
by Goethe.7 Which of these is Nietzsche's model? I would hazard the guess,
"All of them!"
The most obvious objection to this conclusion is that it seems to ignore
Occam's razor. Why should we assume, if one basis for interpretation serves
sufficiently, that we need any other? My answer is simple: Nietzsche was
conceited. I do not mean by this that he was an especially arrogant man,
although his biography offers considerable evidence for this contention. In-
stead, I mean that like John Donne's religious and erotic poetry, Nietzsche's
work abounds in conceits, dense contractions of elaborate imagery. He took
particular delight in condensing multiple messages into minimally presented
images, much in the fashion of the Freudian dreamwork.
Some may find my interpretations of Nietzsche's conceits a bit far-fetched,
on the grounds that I suppose Nietzsche to be splicing together references
to diverse areas, some of them obscure, to make jokes that he could not
reasonably expect many others to get. Nietzsche may indeed have pitched
his humor too high for his average reader. But his elitist posture makes it
obvious that this would not have distressed him greatly—or at least that the
policy would have been compatible with his stated orientation.8 Moreover,
Nietzsche was erudite, as were his well-educated contemporaries.
We have difficulty, perhaps, with Nietzsche's resonant references because
the culture we draw upon in our humor is not as fundamentally literate and
historically informed. Our expression "out of touch" refers to one who is
ignorant of images from popular culture. For Nietzsche, by contrast, cultural
literacy required awareness of central figures in European history and knowl-
edge of the impact made by important works of literature or scientific dis-
coveries. In our era, unless we are in the disciplines directly devoted to the
study of these specialties, we can admit considerable ignorance without em-
barrassment. I once heard a professional philosopher, speaking at an Ivy
League school, admit to ignorance of the Four Humors with no hint of
humiliation or curiosity.
In such a context, Nietzsche's reveries on historical scenes and allusions
to famous works of literature strike us as obscure. These allusions are rele-
vant, however, to what Nietzsche advocates as "the gay science," a broadly
based but lighthearted scholarship. Nietzsche's allusive gestures crossing var-
ious disciplinary borders are a demonstration of what he advocates.
Indeed, if I am right to read The Gay Science as a complex tapestry of
such allusions, this strategy amounts to a propaganda campaign. Each of the
three lines of interpretation I consider treats the prelude as a challenge to


16
COMIC RELIEF
conventional morality, but they take disparate views of its tonal character.
As suits a book that ponders the importance of perspective, the preface ad-
dresses several sensibilities simultaneously. Were it to be classified in the
fashion of a video store, the prelude might be filed as Adventure, Comedy,
or Drama. But by describing it as "song," Nietzsche invokes the characteristic
appeal of music, which can appeal in different ways to different listeners,
depending on their circumstances and interests, but can nevertheless make
possible a good deal of sharing.
Reading i: Chivalry Regained
Although the troubadours' lives may have been something less than fun-
filled, the buoyant character of their lifestyle in the context of the medieval
period is certainly one reason why Nietzsche considers them kindred spirits.
Besides his remark to Rohde, he mentions them directly in accounting for
his reference to the gaya scienza (explicitly regarding the poems that end The
Gay Science):
The "Songs of Prince Free-as-a-Bird," written for the most part in Sicily,
are quite emphatically reminiscent of the Proven9al concept of gaya
scienza—that unity of singer, knight, and free spirit which distinguishes the
wonderful early culture of the Provencals from all equivocal cultures. The
very last poem above all, "To the Mistral," an exuberant dancing song in
which, if I may say so, one dances right over morality, is a perfect Prov-
encalism.9
The suggestion that gay Wissenschaft should be conducted in the spirit of
the troubadours is evocative. Wandering singers, the troubadours conducted
a nomadic existence. At the same time, they were connected to the Provencal
court, known for its experimentation in human relationships, its flirtation
with heresy, and its doctrines of refinement and aspiration.
The troubadours' practice was an aspect of the Provencal courtly culture
that had developed over the eleventh to the thirteenth centuries. La gaya
scienza, or gai saber, was an expression for the troubadours' art, which in-
cluded music, poetry, and gallant expression. Love, particularly of unavailable
ladies, was their prominent theme. Nietzsche would probably have appreci-
ated their defiance of the church in praising the expression of passion in
illicit sexual liaisons and defending the Abilgensian heretics. For Nietzsche
la gaya scienza probably represents the entire culture that grew up in me-
dieval Provence, celebrated by the troubadours. The novelty of this culture,
and especially its defiance of conventional mores, appealed to him. We shall
see that he draws more than his title from the Provencal, experience. Many
of the central emphases of Prove^al chivalry prove fundamental to Nietz-
sche's Gay Science as well. A brief description of some of the central motifs
of the Provencal movement will provide us with hints as to the way of life
that Nietzsche advocates as a model for modern scholarship and, more im-
portant, living.


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
IJ
Chivalry was, specifically, an elitist phenomenon. The troubadours, the
poets of the movement, were themselves often members of the nobility. The
Provencal movement called for a renovation in the values of the noble class—
not solely a change in external behaviors, but a fundamental change in taste.
According to Friedrich Heer, chivalry
gave the aristocratic ruling class a taste for formal elegance and the game
of wit; they introduced a number of definite rules for the writing of ver-
nacular poetry and rubrics for the liturgy of refinement; and lastly they
evolved the courtly concept of love by applying their code of chivalrous
conduct, based on a fusion of Oriental, gnostic and Islamic models, to the
relations between men and women.10
The taste that came to dominate, accordingly, was a highly aestheticized
sensibility. The lifestyle and etiquette that were glorified were those deemed
appropriate to the knight; and these were understood to involve certain aes-
thetic skills as well as military prowess. Like the martial arts of Asia, chivalry
was a means of inculcating these skills. It became an educational enterprise,
aimed at the development of a new kind of man. Besides receiving physical
training, knights were also trained in music, rhyme-making, and fine man-
ners.11 Etiquette was such a dominant emphasis that chivalric courtesy ex-
tended beyond the knight to those below him in the social scale. Will Durant
goes so far as to claim that "modern politeness is a dilution of medieval
chivalry."12
Besides etiquette, poetry was considered an essential ingredient of the
knight's education. The idea that poetry was a "manly" art might seem aston-
ishing in our own day (unless we consider the mystique of all-male rock
bands), but the Proven9al court construed poetry as a mode of adventure.
The term troubadour itself conveys an adventurous outlook. It comes from
a root that means "to find" or "to invent," and, appropriately, the trouba-
dours were proud of their originality. Heer summarizes (albeit in the gen-
dered terminology common until recent years) the grounds on which poetry
was deemed so crucial to knights—and, thus, to their education.
Man is a being whose moulding and proper education can only be per-
fected in a spirit of complete candour, in full participation in all the ele-
ments of the natural and supernatural world. This is where the age-old
wisdom of the poet, as a midwife of the imagination, has its part to play,
for it is only through images that man is fashioned into his own true image.
These images, with their many layers of meaning, work on him uncon-
sciously, to disturb, direct, arouse and satisfy the innermost core of his
being.13
The explicit goal of the chivalric education was transformation of the
individual. Ideally, this transformation would involve the metamorphosis of
the entirety of everyday life. The influential romances of Chretien de Troyes,
for example, conveyed the ideal that "everyone alike had the duty of striving
consciously to attain greater perfection in their daily lives."14 The trouba-
dours emphasized sensuous immediacy in everyday experience, an immedi-


l8
COMIC RELIEF
acy underscored by the fact that they began the first European tradition of
lyric singing in the vernacular, their everyday language, the langue d'oc.
The chivalric code also instilled a view of one's life as an unfolding, heroic
quest: "[t]he theme of the great romantic epics ... is initiation, dedication,
metamorphosis and absorption into a higher and fuller life, at once more
human and more divine."15 Self-control was central to the chivalric method.
Passion was valued, but as material to be molded, not as an uncontrolled
urge. Courage was also among the most important virtues for a knight, and
the willingness to undergo misfortune was considered essential to the knight's
courage.
Even radical individualism was included among the possible paths along
which a knight might travel. Heer observes that the central theme in Chre-
tien's romance Yvain is "that eccentricity, even apparent madness, may be a
stage in an individual's development, if such is demanded by inner neces-
sity."16 At the same time, the chivalric era encouraged a relatively lighthearted
sensibility. Inspired in part by the romance tales of the Knights of the Round
Table, the French court developed a taste for "marvellous adventures, un-
dertaken to satisfy mere caprice or a restless longing for change" and "ro-
mantic love, especially love par amours."17
The romances of the era attended to the psychological intricacies of the
human being. The psychological candor of the period was matched by con-
siderable openness regarding proper behavior. Perhaps most important to
Nietzsche, the Proven9al movement involved an explicit rejection of previous
social mores. The new codes of chivalric morals were explicitly in opposition
to the morals of the church, particularly those concerned with sexuality. John
Boswell observes that homosexual behavior was notorious in Provence, and
that homoerotic sentiment was explicit in some of the poems of the trou-
badours. Moreover, the Albigensian heretics of southern France were "in-
ternationally suspected of favoring homosexuality."18
Even more conspicuously, relationships between men and women were
reconsidered and reconstructed in the Provencal court. According to some
of the troubadours' poems, judicial "courts of love" in several locations tried
cases in which amour was involved. These cases were judged by a panel of
women, although it is not obvious that those courts successfully regulated
anyone's amorous behavior.19
Probably the best-known innovation of the Proven9al court is the devel-
opment of courtly love, or fin' amor (refined love). According to this doc-
trine, the lover who subjected himself to his lady would be thereby spiritually
ennobled.20 The spiritualization of life was seen as best accomplished by
means of idealized love of a particular woman. The knight was to exalt this
woman as perfect and obey her devotedly. Even then when the lady was out
of sight, she should be central in the knight's mind. The adventures of
knighthood were to be accompanied by thoughts of the lady, and pursued
as part of the quest of winning her admiration.21
The idealized beloved of a knight was almost never his wife. Most often
she belonged to someone else, and love was expressed secretively (sometimes


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
1C,
physically). Marriage was considered an obligatory arrangement, while the
love of the knight and his lady was free and spiritually ennobling. Love, as
the Provencal court understood it, was highly individualistic and actively
inspiring, "based exclusively on a state of tension, on the magnetic attraction
of two lovers."22
Nietzsche's admiration for these emphases of the troubadours' era is ev-
ident in the opening rhymes, of The Gay Science, which emphasize each of
the themes I have highlighted. Several of the poems are straightforwardly
elitist, making fun of those who are easily herded. Poem 49, for example,
"Der Weise spricht" ("The Wise One Speaks"),23 claims that wisdom is far
above the crowd. Similarly, the "hard" character of poem 26 describes his
"ascent" in terms of using others as steps.
Many of the rhymes urge a renovation of values. The opening poem,
"Einladung" (Invitation), which will be discussed in greater detail later in
this chapter, urges the reader to be bold enough to try the "fare" that is
offered and patient enough to acquire the taste for it. Revaluation, Nietzsche
insinuates, is the objective of the book. Poem 28, which offers encouragement
to beginners, similarly describes transformation as gradual, contending that
initially clumsy efforts will become graceful, in the manner of a young child's
learning to walk.
Lighthearted aestheticism also resounds in Nietzsche's poems.24 Teasing
and joking are described in Poem 9 as outgrowths of happiness. The poem
is also virtually slapstick in its description of the nose of a rose-sniffer being
attacked by thorns. Poem 57 humorously contemplates the question of the
ideal afterlife as if it were a matter of evaluating real estate. A little place in
paradise is considered good, but a place outside the gate—even better.
Poetry also appears as a mode of adventure in Nietzsche's verses, though
often comically and at Nietzsche's own expense. In poem 52 he compares
writing to the bold art of using one's own body in dancing or charging
forward. Poem 56 expresses something akin to professional pride regarding
the rhymes (although Nietzsche simultaneously describes them as "un-
sinn'ge"—"senseless" or "silly.") 25 In poem 59 Nietzsche compares writing
to fighting with his own pen; the poems, from this viewpoint, might be seen
as a kind of joust (albeit one that attacks the very instrument of writing).
The educational character of chivalry is reflected in the admonitory tone
of many of Nietzsche's poems. Poem 7 suggests that one follow the offered
advice by following oneself, a lesson that resembles the chivalric teaching
that the knight should respond to inner necessity. The same tone is evident
in some of the poems that describe psychological intricacies, usually in con-
nection with a particular type of foible. Self-inflation is considered in poem
21, where it is compared to the disposition of overfilled balloons, vulnerable
to pinpricks. Poem 42 mocks the "too-refined" individuals who systemati-
cally prefer sneaking to straightforward behavior. The desire for fame, ac-
cording to poem 43, is similarly dubious, for it requires the abdication of
honor. Significantly, these admonitions urge the individual to find his or her
own way, much like the education directed toward the medieval knight.


20
COMIC RELIEF
The chivalric call to individual feats and heroic quests is also reflected in
Nietzsche's poems. Many are paeans to daring. Among these are poem 16,
which urges climbers simply to climb without thinking about it, and poem
27, which reminds the wanderer of an earlier decision to leave the beaten
track, a choice that should not now be cause for panic. The latter, like many
of the other poems, stresses individual courage in the face of pressure from
traditionalists.
The rejection of traditional moral standards is evident in several poems
that assert a balance of conventionally "good" and "evil" characteristics in
the same individual. Poem 5 suggests that virtues should come and go nim-
bly, like dancers or like Homer's verses.26 Poem 11 recites a "proverb" in
which the speaker expresses satisfaction at being "dove at the same time as
snake and swine."27 Poem 15 advocates the desirability of acquiring some
rust. These verses announce a theme that resounds in the rest of the book,
that "virtue" and "vice" are interdependent, and each is only as strong as
the other.
Traditional Christian values are also directly countered in Nietzsche's
rhymes. Poem 30 rejects love of neighbor as an ideal. Poem 38 attacks the
view of those who accept Feuerbach's theory that humanity made God, but
nevertheless continue to believe in God, a view it calls "limping with the
devil's hoof."28
The nature of courtship between men and women is considered in some
of the poems, albeit in a more comic fashion than in most chivalric paeans
to courtly love. Poems 19 and 50 describe comparable situations in which an
individual is crazy with love without obvious reciprocation. The poems are
similar except for the sex of the distraught party. The man in poem 19 has
"involuntarily" seduced a woman through idle conversation, while the
woman in poem 50 has been rendered suddenly brilliant by a man's "losing
his wits" over her. Although both poems emphasize the position of the man,
their casting members of different sexes in the role of "victim of love" is a
hint that the relations between the sexes deserve further reflection (a prom-
issory note that is fulfilled, I will argue, in Book II). Similarly, poem 22
suggests that men and women have different characteristic styles, another
move toward contemplating these matters, if not yet a clear indication of
where Nietzsche will go with them.
The reference to the troubadours strikes Julian Young as the most plau-
sible basis for interpreting the preface of The Gay Science.
Why should anyone subtitle it la gaya scienza, a phrase of Provencal that
refers to the culture and poetry of the medieval troubadours; to their, as
Nietzsche describes it, gay, free, exuberant, childish, mocking, dancing,
light, floating spirit?. .. Why preface and conclude the work with a set of
poems—as if in fear that someone might fail to get the point that in it the
spirit of the troubadours is reborn?29
Young has mixed feelings about the poetic preface, expressing doubt that it
demonstrates good literary judgment. In fact, Young describes Nietzsche as


NIETZSCHE'S NURSERY RHYMES
21
rather imbalanced in The Gay Science. The tone, he claims, is "a kind of
manic frivolity which is really no more than a symptom of desperation and
despair."30
Young is convinced that The Gay Science reflects Nietzsche's belief at the
time he wrote it that only the superficiality of ecstasy could heal the nihilism
resulting from the death of God. Such ecstasy being unsustainable, Nietzsche
was driven to adopt a project of denial, evident in his tone of mania. Al-
though I agree that Nietzsche considered ecstasy to be an essential ingredient
of a post-Christian sense of meaning in life, I disagree that the mode of
ecstasy Nietzsche insists on is an unsustainable condition of manic near-
hysteria. Instead, The Gay Science demonstrates the extent to which Nietzsche
thinks that the more grounded ecstasy available in aesthetic experience is a
daily if not continuous possibility. The recurrent ecstasy available in everyday
life is, as we will see, central to the way of life that Nietzsche endorses in
The Gay Science.31
Indeed, the ballads of the troubadours serve as a precedent for this aes-
thetic basis for meaning. Defying moral tradition and specializing in love
songs, the troubadour glorified the meaning-giving potential of sensuality.
Thus, Nietzsche's opening poems, especially when they remark on mundane
matters, are not a sign that Nietzsche, the author, is giddy with hysteria, as
Young suggests.32 Instead, they aptly preface the book's effort to replace the
moral perspective on life's significance with an aesthetic sensibility toward
everyday matters.
Nevertheless, Young correctly identifies the character of Nietzsche's
rhymes as giddy. I think that the explanation for this, however, is not Nietz-
sche's relative despondency, but an effort to launch a book that will pursue
the interconnections between tragedy and comedy. As the first section of
Book I suggests more soberly, Nietzsche aims to propose these modes as
alternative orientations toward life, and to tip the balance in favor of comedy.
The comic character of the verses, prominent as it is, urges us to seek
further explanation for the preface in rhymes; for the troubadour motif is
not sufficient explanation of the verses' "giddy" sensibility. Moreover, we
might still consider the verses an exaggerated indication of the troubadour
theme, since the emphases connected with medieval Provence are prominent
throughout The Gay Science." The troubadour account, moreover, still leaves
us without explanation of the title of the preface. All three of these difficulties
will be alleviated if we turn to the original "Scherz, List und Rache," a wildly
comic singspiel by Goethe.
Reading 2: The Farce of Power
Nietzsche's immediate interest in Goethe's "Scherz, List und Rache" probably
had to do with the fact that his friend Heinrich Koselitz had composed music
for it. Koselitz was trying to find a publisher at the time that The Gay Science
was written, and Nietzsche himself was fond of the music.34 He wrote to Ida


22
COMIC RELIEF
Overbeck that he would rather hear "Scherz, List und Rache" than Wagner's
Parsifal, a comment that he repeated to Koselitz.35 Nietzsche seems aware of
the plot of the singspiel as well as of Koselitz's music; in a letter to Koselitz,
he asks whether a singer that Koselitz would have liked to cast was not too
old for the part.36
Nietzsche's remarks to the effect that he preferred "Scherz, List und Ra-
che" to Parsifal might suggest his personal motive. One might conclude from
this personal association that Nietzsche was simply making a private joke
with his title, in honor of his friendship with Koselitz. The insinuation would
be an endorsement of this friendship—in which Nietzsche felt himself to be
the senior partner—over the earlier friendship with Wagner, in which he
was treated as a scarcely glorified "gofer." Even the form of doggerel might
be read as a slam at Wagner, in that Wagner himself had once written a
dedication in humorous doggerel in a copy of his Gesammelte Schriften und
Dichtungen, which he gave to Nietzsche as a present.37
Yet if we examine the singspiel itself, we find that the choice of title reflects
literary craftsmanship, whatever personal agendas it might also have served.
The singspiel is both lightly comic and anti-moral. In these respects it con-
trasts with the Wagnerian work which Nietzsche particularly despised for
what he saw as its pompous and traditional moralism.38 The title might thus
be an endorsement of the orientation of the singspiel over that of Wagner's
opera. An examination of Goethe's libretto bears out this interpretation. I
will argue that Goethe's singspiel served Nietzsche as an explicit and revealing
model for his preface of rhymes.
Goethe's singspiel "Scherz, List und Rache" is a rhymed comic operetta
in which a married couple, named Scapin and Scapine, get the best of a
doctor who has cheated them out of a large sum of money—one hundred
dukates, to be exact. Their vindictive scheme involves their deceiving the
doctor as to their identities and convincing him that they are ill and in need
of medical treatment.
Scapin persuades the doctor that he is unable to eat because of his lack
of appetite. The doctor, moved by pity, urges Scapin to stay with him for
observation and treatment. Scapin's case intrigues the doctor, particularly
because he seems to function quite well (which is ensured by Scapin's gorging
nightly at a nearby inn). Since he cannot otherwise compensate the doctor
for his care, Scapin assumes the role of the doctor's house servant, a position
that proves instrumental for the revenge he desires.
While Scapin is waiting on the doctor, Scapine arrives, posing as a sick
patient. The doctor takes particular interest in her case, too, presumably
because he finds her attractive. Scapin suddenly announces that his appetite
has returned, and he grabs Scapine's cheek as if to take a bite. The doctor,
appalled, sends him out of the room and afterward asks Scapine about her
symptoms. She describes outlandishly neurotic symptoms (e.g., "When I see
a nose, I want to pick it; when I see a wig, I want to snatch it. . ,"39). The
doctor concocts a potion to relieve Scapine's alleged symptoms.


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
23
Meanwhile, Scapin smokes up the house by burning rags in the fireplace.
He incites pandemonium by yelling "Fire!" While the doctor is preoccupied
with the apparent emergency, Scapin switches a bottle of arsenic for a bottle
of medicine on the doctor's shelf of chemicals. He also pours much of Sea-
pine's medicinal concoction out the window.
Eventually, the commotion ends, the doctor having discovered the rags
in the fireplace. He tells Scapin to get out of his sight and tries to continue
the consultation with his pretty patient. Scapine, however, begins raving and
moaning, insisting that she is dying, that her sight and mind are becoming
dim. She pretends to die, after screaming that the doctor has poisoned her.
Scapin returns in time to witness Scapine's apparent death.
The doctor tells Scapin not to believe Scapine's allegation and insists that
he is innocent. Scapin, however, reveals the location of the bottle of arsenic.
The doctor becomes convinced that he did poison Scapine after all. Frantic,
he pleads with Scapin to stay quiet about the incident. Scapin refuses. The
doctor offers him money, but Scapin holds out for more. Eventually, the
doctor, despite his legendary miserliness, raises his bribe to the "terrible
sum" of fifty dukates, which Scapin accepts.
Taking the money, Scapin asks the doctor to let him stay in the under-
ground vault until nightfall, when he can slip away in the darkness. While
Scapin waits, Scapine revives, pretending to be a ghost, and she screams so
loudly that the doctor is afraid that his neighbors will hear. He tries to get
her to be quiet. She eventually agrees, on the condition that he pay her fifty
dukates. The doctor complies, grieving the loss of his money, which he calls
"the joy" of his life.
The play ends with Scapin and Scapine revealing their true identities and
taunting the doctor. Their inheritance of one hundred dukates is now re-
stored, thanks to the doctor's bribes. They jingle their bags of money and
continue to yell "Murderer!" while the doctor yells "Thieves!" The play ends
on this note, with everyone screaming, as the doctor insists to Scapin and
Scapine that they will be punished for this.
Goethe's singspiel (written in simple rhymes like Nietzsche's verses) is a
play with a plot, while Nietzsche's preface is not. Nevertheless, Nietzsche's
prelude resonates with certain features of Goethe's story. One of the most
obvious ways in which Nietzsche's text mimics Goethe's is its emphasis on
the practice of medicine. Many of the poems are straightforward prescrip-
tions, offered in a tone that might befit a doctor.
Poem 4, called "Zwiegesprach," (Dialogue Between Two), is literally about
illness and recovery.
A. War ich krank? Bin ich genesen?
Und wer ist mein Arzt gewesen?
Wie vergass ich alles das!
B. Jetzt erst glaub' ich dich genesen:
Denn gesund ist, wer vergass.


24
COMIC RELIEF
A. Was I sick? Am I recovered?
Who has been my doctor?
How could I have forgotten all of that!
B. Now I finally believe that you have recovered:
For whoever has forgotten is healthy.39
Poem 24, "Pessimisten-Arznei" (Pessimists' Medicine), takes the point of
view of a doctor who, not unlike the doctor in Goethe's play, prescribes
medicine to patients who rave at length about their pains.
Du klagst, dass nichts dir schmackhaft sei?
Noch immer, Freund, die alten Mucken?
Ich hor' dich lastern, larmen, spucken—
Geduld und Herz bricht mir dabei.
Folg' mir, mein Freund! Entschliess dich frei,
Bin fettes Krotchen zu verschlucken,
Geschwind und ohne hinzugucken!—
Das hilft dir von der Dyspepsei!
You complain that nothing would taste good to you?
Still, friend, the old whinger?
I hear you cursing, howling, spitting—
breaking my heart and my patience with that.
Follow me, my friend! Freely choose
To swallow a fat toad,
Quickly and without looking!—
This will relieve your dyspepsia.40
Still another poem, 39, "Im Sommer" ("In Summer"), asserts another
medical opinion. We should work not from desperate need, as a means of
meagerly surviving, but in the spirit of celebration, as doctors advise.
Im Schweisse unseres Angesichts
Soll'n unser Brot wir essen?
Im Schweisse isst man lieber nichts,
Nach weiser Arzte Ermessen.
Der Hundsstern winkt: woran gebricht's?
Was will sein feurig Winken?
Im Schweisse unsres Angesichts
Soll'n unsern Wein wir trinken!
In the sweat of our brow
we should eat our bread?
Good doctors don't allow
The Dog Star twinkles now.
Eating while in a sweat.
The Dog Star twinkles now.
Of what is this a sign?
In the sweat of our brow
We should drink our wine!41


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
25
Besides these poems that directly mention medicine, others from Nietz-
sche's preface are reminiscent of Goethe's doctor's role. The opening rhyme,
"Einladung" (Invitation), is particularly interesting in this regard, both for
its resonance with Goethe's singspiel, and for the hints it offers about Nietz-
sche's own purposes. The narrator of "Einladung" sounds quite a bit like
Goethe's doctor when he urges Scapin, allegedly not hungry, to try the food
in his house. Nietzsche's poem reads as follows:
Wagt's mit meiner Kost, ihr Esser!
Morgen schmeckt sie euch schon besser
Und schon iibermorgen gut!
Wollt ihr dann noch mehr,—so machen
Meine alten sieben Sachen
Mir zu sieben neuen Mut.
Walter Kaufmann translates this poem rather freely:
Take a chance and try my fare:
It will grow on you, I swear;
Soon it will taste good to you.
If by then you should want more,
All the things I've done before
Will inspire things quite new.42
As Clayton Koelb has pointed out, Kaufmann's translation misses the pun
in German on "seiben Sachen," which means both "seven things" and "odds
and ends" or "trifles" in this passage. Nietzsche compounds the wordplay,
according to Koelb, by recalling "the augmentative power of the number
seven.... a number of mystical power often associated with increase in the
Bible and other canonical texts." Thus, "One might even get 'culture' out of
this ridiculous stuff," Koelb concludes.43 An alternative translation might run:
Dare to take my fare, you eater!
Tomorrow already it will taste better
And even good by the morning after!
If by then you want still more—
I'll take cues from my old stuff
to make something new.44
Moreover, the term Kost, aptly translated as "fare," also bears an ety-
mological relationship to kostbar, or "costly," an economic image that might
or might not be insinuated by "fare," which could be the price of entry as
well as the general term for food and drink.
The prescriptive character and the suggestion of cost render Nietzsche's
poem more insidious in tone than Kaufmann suggests when he claims, "The
modesty of the first three lines is striking. Literally, the fare will taste better
tomorrow, and good only the day after tomorrow."45 Koelb, I think, makes


26
COMIC RELIEF
better sense of the passage when he draws attention to the deeper significance
of Nietzsche's wordplay—the traversing of perspectives and the transfor-
mation of trifles into insights:
Nietzsche implies, though he does not explicitly say, that the philosopher's
task must in part be to take what is familiar and to see it as a problem.
How does one do that? How does one "defamiliarize" the familiar? I bor-
row the language of Russian Formalism here, not to imply any kinship
between that movement and Nietzsche's work, but to suggest the funda-
mentally literary and rhetorical dimensions of the issue. The process of
making the familiar problematic is precisely the process we have seen again
and again in Nietzsche's practice of rhetorical rereading, What could be
more familiar to us than the language of daily life, expressions like ... "das
A und das O"?46
If we note the allusion to Goethe's "Scherz, List und Rache," the trans-
formation of banalities into something else takes on a sinister note, remind-
ing us that concoctions can be poison. Goethe's model suggests a reason for
seeing Nietzsche's invitation as rather dubious. We have no idea, as we read
his opening line, what his fare is. Nor do we know the spirit in which it is
offered. Nietzsche gives us occasion to wonder, Is his fare wholesome? Is it
poison?
Thus, "Einladung" is a "riddle" akin to a saying of the Delphic oracle,
for which the wrong interpretation can have fatal consequences. What per-
versity lies behind its insistence that one should "Dare!"47 to accept what is
offered? We might also hear the taunting overtones of Goethe's singspiel in
Nietzsche's title, "Invitation." Scapin and Scapine describe their scheme to
outwit the doctor as a mutual "invitation" at the end of Act i:
Ich lade dich auf heute
Zu neuen Ergb'tzungen ein.
Die Rache, die List, die Beute,
Wie soil sie, wie wird sie uns freun!
Today I invite you
to new amusements.
the revenge, the trick, the booty,
How they shall delight us!48
Yet the remark about daring may just be an appropriate bedside comment
by a doctor who knows that his medicine may be bitter. The doctor's de-
scriptions of the medications he offers are also akin to Nietzsche's first poem
in their suggestion that "daring" is what they demand from the patient.
These are the terms in which he urges Scapine to drink his potion:
Aus dem Becher, schon verguldet,
Sollst du, liebes Weibchen, trinken:
Aber la(3 den Mut nicht sinken,
Es ist bitter, doch gesund.


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
ZJ
Out of the cup, beautifully gilded,
