You should drink, little lady.
But do not let your courage sink.
It is bitter, yet healthy.49
Nietzsche's "invitation," like the invitations extended by the doctor, sug-
gests a context in which the value of his fare is at issue. Indeed, this is
precisely what remains to be seen—how kostbar, or costly, this Kost. Nietz-
sche presents himself as a healer, but we do not know how much we can
trust him. His invitation is verbally similar, after all, to the doctor's offering
of his fare to Scapin. Ironically, the doctor is offering his resources to some-
one from whom he has stolen—a fact that undercuts the status of his "in-
vitation," despite its tone of civility. Scapin reports to Scapine the outcome
of his conversation with the doctor:
Er sprach: Mein Tisch emport dir nicht das Blut,
Du kannst getrost in meinem Hause bleiben.
He spoke: My table will not enrage your blood,
You can remain comfortably in my house.50
Of course, the doctor's table—the extent of the resources at his disposal—
is precisely what does enrage Scapin's blood, though in a different sense than
the doctor intends. This suggests another question we might pose to Nietz-
sche: At whose expense is his healing offered?
The questionable significance of the doctor's medications reminds us,
when we recognize Nietzsche's allusions, that his own prescriptions might
be seen in various ways, depending on the person who sees them. Indeed,
the value of any medication can only be determined perspectivally. The par-
ticular biochemistry of the individual determines whether a concoction is
conducive to health or a poison. Nietzsche does not claim universal validity
for his recommendations. They have medicinal use only for those in partic-
ular circumstances—namely, the background of the Christian moral tradi-
tion, combined with the modern displacement of religion by science. This
background has encouraged spiritual malaise, according to Nietzsche, and
his prescriptions are aimed at counteracting it. Even so, their specific effects
depend on the patient.
Nietzsche's rhymes, besides being prescriptions, are, literally, Siebensachen,
or light amusements. In this respect, too, they resonate with Goethe's sing-
spiel, which is also light entertainment. Besides being amusements, both texts
also reflect on value, including the value ascribed to such fleeting satisfac-
tions. Goethe's doctor disparages those who waste their money on Sieben-
sachen, odds and ends whose value he finds puzzling. The miserly doctor
himself, by contrast, prefers cash to "seven trifles," as he makes clear in his
song about the joys of money, which opens Act 2:
Die meisten Menschen komrnen mir
Wie grofie Kinder vor,


28
COMIC RELIEF
Die auf den Markt mit wenig Pfennigen
Begierig eilen.
So lang' die Tasche noch
Das Bifichen Geld verwahrt,
Ach! da ist alles ihre.
Zuckerwerk und andre Naschereien,
Die bunten Bilder und das Steckenpferdchen,
Die Trommel und die Geige!
Herz, was begehrst du?—
Und das Herz ist unersattlich!
Es sperrt die Augen ganz gewaltig auf.
Doch ist fur eine dieser Siebensachen
Die Barschaft erst vertandelt,
Dann adieu ihr schonen Wunsche,
Ihr Hoffhungen, Begierden!
Most people seem to me
Like big children,
who hurry greedily to the market
with a few pennies.
As long as the smallest sum
remains in their pockets,
Ah! everything is theirs.
Candy and other sweets,
Colorful pictures and stickhorses,
Drums and violins!
Heart, what do you desire?—
And the heart is insatiable.
It forces one to have big eyes.
Yet it's for one of these trifles
That cold cash is first frittered away,
Then good-bye to your beautiful wishes,
Your hopes and dreams!51
This song's pun on Siebensachen draws attention to the displaced significance
of the objects on which people spend money. The things themselves are
placeholders, open-ended in meaning as the term Siebensachen itself, valuable
more because they become temporary objects of desire than because of their
inherent worth. These "seven things" are trivial, their importance to their
buyers obscure but fascinating. The doctor thinks he has most things figured
out, although the significance of the seven things on which most people have
frittered away their money remains perplexing to him.
Although the Siebensachen in Goethe's singspiel are mentioned only in
passing, the doctor's song draws attention to the diverse perspectives of the
characters and their different impressions of the significance of their inter-
changes. These differences, throughout the play, are registered in terms of
economic power. The economic reversal that constitutes the basic movement
of Goethe's plot depends on the ambiguous significance of "things" ex-


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
29
changed in the drama, and the plot consists primarily of extended power
plays for control of these things' significance.
The most important "thing" whose significance is disputed is the drink
the doctor offers Scapine. At first it appears to all involved as a harmless
medication for a peculiar complaint. It attains the significance of poison, an
ominous significance that could secure the doctor's execution, when he ac-
cepts Scapine's testimony. When the circumstantial evidence of the arsenic
bottle forces the doctor to accept that drink as poison, he fights to regain
control of the drink's meaning by attempting a cover-up, which might reduce
the drink's significance to the status of a buried clue. But, like Nixon's Wa-
tergate fiasco, this cover-up assumes a life of its own, ultimately costing the
doctor his position of power—the position that had previously assured him
that he knew exactly the significance of the Siebensachen around him.52
Along with medicine and insinuating invitation, power is a third theme
linking Nietzsche's rhymes to Goethe's singspiel. The doctor's position is
complicated by the power struggle in Goethe's play. Nietzsche's allusive med-
ical imagery also signals the power struggle pursued in The Gay Science, his
own challenge to the healing tradition of Christian morality. Nietzsche sug-
gests that for all its insistence that fare like his is poisonous, the Christian
moral tradition itself has substituted arsenic for innocent nutrition. By con-
trolling the significance of human actions and interpreting them in terms of
"sin," Christian morality has poisoned them. It has also offered questionable
cures for non-diseases, which were, according to Nietzsche, diagnosed as
illness by unscrupulous, vindictive parties seeking profit at others' expense.
Nietzsche sees the fare that he offers as wholesome, by contrast, healthful in
its avoidance of these common poisoning influences.53
The irrelevance of morality to Goethe's singspiel is suggested by Charles
E. Passage, who observes that it is in the form of commedia dell'arte, "in
the tradition of the old Italian clowning, horseplay and all, and without a
touch of sentiment." The moralistic attitude of Goethe's audience and the
taxingly manic pace of the play led to his eventual concession of its failure.
"Twenty years later," Passage reports, "Goethe ruefully admitted that lyric
elements were lost in the shuffle and that German audiences had no taste
for Mediterranean farce, where unrepentant villainy gloats in triumph over
equally unrepentant villainy."54
Far from being a morality play, Goethe's singspiel dismisses conventional
morality as irrelevant, and treats conventional moral practice (like the doc-
tor's cynical fear of his neighbor's moral accusation) as a source of downfall
and impoverishment. None of Goethe's characters represent the moral high
ground. Goethe's singspiel, in effect, is an anti-morality play. Nietzsche's
allusion to it announces his intention to oppose traditional morality in a
similarly lighthearted way.
The anti-moral character of Goethe's play helps to explain why Nietzsche,
in a series of poems that call for a return to the aspirations of the trouba-
dours, would cast himself in a role reminiscent of Goethe's doctor, who is


30
COMIC RELIEF
far from a hero. As the poems asserts, "A place where fool and sage convene:
All this I am and wish to mean ["Der Narren und Weisen Stelldichein: Dies
alles bin ich, will ich sein.... ]."55 Like Goethe's doctor, his character is
mixed. He has knowledge that can be useful in healing, but he is also a
greedy liar, prideful, and susceptible to being duped.
Nietzsche sees his position as comparable to the doctor's, at least in rec-
ognizing his own vulnerability to the demands and ill effects of traditional
morality. He also expects to be falsely accused of administering poison. He
prescribes his remedy to alleviate his society's spiritual ailment; but he ex-
pects traditional moralists to find the fare he offers harmful. These moralists,
in Nietzsche's view, are akin to Scapin and Scapine in their plot against the
doctor. Moralists' declarations of human sinfulness are, like the allegations
against the doctor, false charges of guilt, supported only by means of the
moralists' malicious assertions of their own interpretive power.56 Yet Nietz-
sche presents himself as also sharing traits with Scapin and Scapine, clever
tricksters who succeed in their efforts by means of sleight of hand.
Goethe's doctor is vulnerable in part because of his own errors. The prem-
ise of the play is that some past action on his part has led to a situation in
which Scapin and Scapine want revenge. He is inordinately concerned about
the opinion of his neighbors. Another mistake, from Nietzsche's point of
view, is his openness to pity, or Mitleid, the term also used to indicate Chris-
tian compassion, an emotion that Nietzsche denounces in many of his writ-
ings.57 Nietzsche's allusions to Goethe's singspiel reinforce his insistence that
pity is actually harmful, a claim that appears outright in the last poem of the
preface.58
Pity, in Nietzsche's account, presupposes a power differential, and the
pitying person assumes himself or herself to be securely in the premier po-
sition. Besides rarely resulting in actual benefit to any party involved in a
relationship of pity, pity is a temptation to distraction and to taking a detour
from one's own spiritual course.59 Goethe's doctor might be seen as illus-
trating Nietzsche's point. He is literally diverted from his business by the
pity he takes on Scapin and Scapine. Scapin gloats in describing his having
moved the doctor to pity:
In seinem Herzen war das Mitleid angekommen,
Ich war sein guter, lieber, armer Mann. . . .
Er sann und freute sich—und kurz und gut,
Mein Ubel war ihm mehr als ein Empfehlungsschreiben.
Pity entered into his heart,
I was his good, beloved, poor man. ...
He thought it over and was pleased—and in short,
My affliction counted more with him than a letter of recommendation.60
The insincerity of the doctor's pity toward the scampish duo is also typical
of the emotion of pity, in Nietzsche's account; for the main purpose of pity
is to feel actively superior, in a gesture akin to flexing one's muscles.


NIETZSCHE'S NURSERY RHYMES
31
The end of Goethe's play suggests a final reason Nietzsche may have for
drawing attention to it at the beginning of The Gay Science. The finale is
inconclusive. It involves a breathless interchange of taunts and curses among
the three characters, with no obvious end in sight. As the curtain falls, Scapin
and Scapine retain the money, but the doctor's desire for vengeance is ex-
pressed in the final line. The reversal of fortune in the singspiel does not
achieve a final state in which all is set right. The satisfaction of the duo's
agenda is achieved only by an act that instigates new motivations on the part
of the doctor, presumably to regain his power position once again.
The ongoing interplay of power dynamics, a central theme in The Gay
Science, is depicted comically in Goethe's singspiel. In various disguises, the
characters have been motivated by desire to attain the "feeling" of their own
power. In The Gay Science, Nietzsche emphasizes the feeling of power, more
than power itself, as a motive. The feeling of power is, in a sense, like the
Siebensachen, the ambient object of desire in ever-changing guises.
The oddity of the human comedy becomes especially clear when the lim-
itation of these achievements is recognized. Pursuits of power, even where
successful, are temporally finite, according to Nietzsche. The feeling of power
may be even more evanescent than power itself, since one can have power
without a thrilled feeling of having it. Even more absurd are the childish
means that may be required to "feel" one's power. The position of one who
wins this game is like that of Scapin and Scapine, jingling moneybags, chim-
ing their "nyah-nyahs," while the doctor vows, "I'll get you for this!"
The climax of singsong rhymes that sling taunts of this sort back and
forth is not only an indication of the virtuosity of Goethe's singers. It is also
a demonstration of the farcical dance of human power plays, reminding us
that the ultimate aim of so many endeavors is essentially these childish mo-
ments of victory. One might see this conclusion as a reason for dismay or a
sense of life's tragic dimension. But this would not be an artistically appro-
priate response to the silliness of a singspiel, any more than a tragic response
to Roadrunner cartoons would be. The spirit of Goethe's finale, like the spirit
of the singspiel's rhymes in general, is light and childlike—pre-moral, in a
sense. The same childlike mood is evident in Nietzsche's rhymes, and I con-
tend that this mood is a third aspect of relevance for interpreting the prelude
in rhymes.
Reading 3: Back to the Nursery
Although I disagree with Young's general assessment of The Gay Science as
despairing, I consider his description of Nietzsche's vision of the troubadours
as "gay, free, exuberant, childish, mocking, dancing, light, floating" as well
suited to the poems. It also draws attention to the poems' format, which is
reminiscent of nursery rhymes. This resemblance, I shall argue, is not acci-
dental. Nietzsche is manipulating the convention of rhymes for children to
make a point.


32
COMIC RELIEF
In the preface to the second edition of The Gay Science, Nietzsche de-
scribes the state of mind he meant to convey in the work as a renewed spirit
of childhood:
The trust in life is gone: life itself has become a problem. ... [F]rom such
abysses, from such severe sickness, also from the sickness of severe suspi-
cion, one returns newborn, having shed one's skin, more ticklish and ma-
licious, with a more delicate taste for joy, with a tenderer tongue for all
good things, with merrier senses, with a second dangerous innocence in
joy, more childlike and yet a hundred times subtler than one has ever been
before
61
Nietzsche goes on to link this return to the sensibility of childhood with
profundity—a profundity that he believes characterizes the most aware in-
dividuals of his era:
Oh, those Greeks! They knew how to live.... Those Greeks were superfi-
cial—out of profundity. And is not this precisely what we are again coming
back to, we daredevils of the spirit.,.62
The childish rhymes and rhythms of Nietzsche's preface of poems rep-
resent this return to basics by suggesting the lilt of childhood recitations.
The marked rhythms of Nietzsche's poems recall the bratty "nyah-nyah" of
a defiant child, absolutely confident of the obviousness of its intended mes-
sage.63 The child's devil-may-care attitude is in keeping with the defiant con-
tents of Nietzsche's verses. Poem 7, "Vademecum—Vadetecum" (Go with
Me—Go with Yourself), is doubly contrarian:
Es lockt dich meine Art und Sprach',
Du folgest mir, du gehst mir nach?
Geh nur dir selber treulich nach:—
So folgst du mir—gemach! gemach!
Lured by my style and tendency,
you follow and come after me?
Follow your own self faithfully—
take time—and thus you follow me.64
Poem 30, "Der Nachste" (The Neighbor), is similarly renegade, tackling the
Christian doctrine of love of neighbor and preferring the courtly doctrine of
distanced admiration:
Nah hab' den Nachsten ich nicht gerne:
Fort mit ihm in die Hoh' und Feme!
Wie wiird' er sonst zu meinem Sterne?
I don't like having a neighbor near:
Forth with him up high and far!
How else would he become my star?65


NIETZSCHE'S NURSERY RHYMES
33
Nietzsche's poems counter many of the basic teachings of his tradition,
absorbed by most of us through platitudes learned in childhood. Akin in
style to the nursery form through which we internalized these platitudes,
Nietzsche's poems suggest an alternative beginning to the one we actually
experienced. It also makes us aware of Nietzsche's attack on our mindless
repetition of these inculcated premises. "We are presented with grave words
and values almost from the cradle," says Zarathustra in Nietzsche's subse-
quent book.66 Nursery rhymes are usually limited to their intended context,
that of childhood. This format—which is simultaneously that of Goethe's
singspiel—is jarringly out of context in Nietzsche's book. It draws our aware-
ness to the way our customary mental habits were inculcated, precisely be-
cause Nietzsche's heretical message is so incongruous in this form.
Nietzsche's nursery rhymes also exploit and utilize the physical basis for
our early experiences of memorization and asserting power through words.
Strong rhymes and regular rhythms emphasize and render memorable the
messages with which they are conjoined. They are also tactical weapons in
verbal battle. Children early learn that "nyah-nyah" is a powerful refutation,
and that they can be rendered secure against their playmates' jeers by means
of rhythmic incantations such as "Sticks and stones may break my bones but
words will never hurt me."
Nietzsche's recognition of the propagandistic power of these "musical"
elements of verse is evident in later passages in The Gay Science:
Music as an advocate.—"I am thirsting for a composer," said an innovator
to his disciple, "who would learn my ideas from me and transpose them
into his language; that way, I should reach men's ears and hearts far better.
With music one can seduce men to every error and every truth: who could
refute a tone?... .67
Of eloquence.—Who has had the most convincing eloquence so far? The
drum roll; and as long as the kings command that, they remain the best
orators and rabble rousers.68
[Rjhythm is a compulsion; it engenders an unconquerable urge to yield
and join in; not only our feet follow the beat but the soul does, too ... 6 9
This last passage, taken from section 84, occurs in a discussion of prayer,
particularly prayers that are rhythmic: " [A] rhythmical prayer was supposed
to get closer to the ears of the gods."70 Nietzsche's association of rhythm and
prayer in his account in this section, titled "On the Origin of Poetry," sug-
gests a rather surprising comparison—that between the opening verses and
formulaic chanting, as found in traditional prayer. He reinforces this asso-
ciation in section 367, where he acknowledges that in the category of art
designed for witnesses, "we must include even the apparently monological
art that involves faith in God, the whole lyricism of prayer."71
Section 84 concludes with the suggestion that despite the strongest efforts
of intellectuals, none of us is immune from the power of rhythm:
In sum: What could have been more useful for the ancient superstitious
type of man than rhythm? It enabled one to do anything—to advance some


34
COMIC RELIEF
work magically; to force a god to appear, to be near, and to listen; to mold
the future in accordance with one's will; to cleanse one's own soul from
some excess (of anxiety, mania, pity, or vengefulness)—and not only one's
own soul but also that of the most evil demon: without verse one was
nothing; by means of verse one almost became a god. Such a fundamental
feeling can never be erased entirely; and even now, after men have fought
against such superstitions for thousands of years, the wisest among us are
still occasionally fooled by rhythm—if only insofar as we sometimes con-
sider an idea truer simply because it has a metrical form and presents itself
with a divine skip and jump.72
Presumably, Nietzsche's preface of verses reflects these insights about
rhythm in poems and prayers. Opening with rhymes, he concedes certain
facts about our psychology that traditional prayers have exploited. Like nurs-
ery rhymes, memorized prayers mold the orientations of those who learn
them in childhood. Prayers, however, may be even more powerfully condi-
tioned than nursery rhymes; for the prayers of childhood are mostly intended
for regular repetition over the course of a lifetime, not to be abandoned with
childhood playthings. The Christian worldview which Nietzsche inherited is
also conventionally reinforced by the recitation of scriptural passages which,
like prayers, are ritually repeated in sermons and pious formulas. These serve
to impose religious interpretations on the events of everyday life.
Nietzsche's writings are liberally garnished with allusions to well-known
prayers and scriptural passages. When he parodies both in the higher men's
litany to the ass in Thus Spoke Zarathustra, Part IV, he mimics lines that
much of his anticipated audience could recite in thorough mindlessness,
thanks to recurrent repetition:
Amen! And praise and honor and wisdom and thanks and glory and
strength be to our god, from everlasting to everlasting! ...
He carries our burden, he took upon himself the form of a servant....
You walk on straight and crooked paths; it matters little to you what
seems straight or crooked to us men....
.... Little children you let come to you ... ,73
In the episode that follows this litany, Zarathustra goes so far as to parody
perhaps the most oft-repeated formulaic prayer in Christianity, the conse-
cration of the Mass.74 Nietzsche's parody reinforces the format of its model
even while undercutting the model's original message:
"Do not forget this night and this ass festival, you higher men. This you
invented when you were with me and I take that for a good sign: such
things are invented only by convalescents.
"And when you celebrate it again, this ass festival, do it for your own
sakes, and also do it for my sake. And in remembrance of me."'5
The references to becoming gay again, brave nonsense, and convalescence
resemble Nietzsche's characterization of The Gay Science in his preface to the
second edition.76 This cluster of notions intimates that Nietzsche does ac-
knowledge an important place for behavior akin to traditional prayer. As


NIETZSCHE'S NURSERY RHYMES
35
convalescents from the crisis he describes as "the death of God," we need to
invent bold new contents for the expressive forms that have evolved to res-
onate with our psychology. New festivals, new songs, new recitations are
among the means we can use to bridge the chasm of nihilism that separates
traditional Christian faith from a newly grounded health, which locates
meaning within this life. In this sense, as I will argue in some detail in later
chapters, Nietzsche's vision is a religious orientation, offered as an alternative
to the Christian worldview.
In privileging festivals, songs, and recitations, Zarathustra emphasizes
form over content. I think this is precisely the move that Nietzsche is making
in the suggestions in section 84 about prayer. He admires and sees a healthy
future for both the form of prayer and its psychological role in human life.
What he questions, systematically, is the idea of a God who demands sub-
ordination from human beings and the related use of prayer (understood
here as a literary form) to lull human individuals into subservience.
The suggestion that Nietzsche finds value in prayer certainly does not
accord with his popular image. And it may seem incompatible with many of
his statements. His remarks on prayer are typically critical. In Part III of
Thus Spoke Zarathustra, for instance, Zarathustra chides the "apostates" who
return to religion after losing heart in their atheism: "But it is a disgrace to
pray! Not for everybody, but for you and me and whoever else has a con-
science in his head too. For you it is a disgrace to pray!"77 Zarathustra does
exempt ordinary people from this criticism, but only from the standpoint of
dismissing them as outsiders to his spiritual elite. This passage might be seen
as evidence that Nietzsche views prayer as, if not a disgrace, then a sign of
stupidity.
Zarathustra's response to his higher men's "ass festival," however, indi-
cates that this is not his final word on the topic. Zarathustra's denunciation
of the "apostates," moreover, suggests that Nietzsche's grounds for criticizing
"prayer" may not apply to every instance to which the term might be applied.
Zarathustra complains, first, that the new apostate's prayer reflects sick
and undesirable motivations—cowardice and desire for contentment. Zara-
thustra is addressing his comments to those who have been led to atheistic
conclusions by their own conscience but have not maintained the courage
of their conviction: "Your cowardly devil within you, who would like to fold
his hands and rest his hands in his lap and be more comfortable—this cow-
ardly devil urges you, 'There is a God.' " In light of Zarathustra's earlier
claim that apparently similar behavior can stem from either desirable or
undesirable motives,78 this criticism is not obviously directed at all prayerlike
chanting, but may apply only to that stemming from degenerate or dishonest
motives. Indeed, Zarathustra associates the prayer he disparages with com-
plete abandonment of dignity: "Just now I saw one groveling—crawling back
to the cross."79
A second basis for criticizing the apostate's prayer is that it is a symptom
of dishonesty and of a desire to ignore truths that the apostate recognizes.
Anyone who earlier espoused atheism from intellectual conviction, according


36
COMIC RELIEF
to Zarathustra, succumbs to the urge to pray only by burying his or her head
"deeper in night and haze." Zarathustra expresses Nietzsche's view that un-
conditional belief (typical of the religious) is an abdication of intellectual
honesty and of responsibility. Nietzsche states this point succinctly in Anti-
christ: " 'Faith' as an imperative is the veto against science—in practice, the
lie at any price."80
Third, the praying apostate opts to abandon his own lights for the sake
of community, however squalid:
For everywhere I smell little hidden communities; and wherever there are
closets there are new canters praying inside and the fog of canters.... Or
they learn to play the harp with pious pleasure—from a composer of songs
who would like to harp himself right into die hearts of young fe-
males . .. 8 I
Nietzsche's criticisms of prayer in Zarathustra focus on its function as a
symptom or reinforcement of disease. But insofar as "prayer" or prayerlike
behavior is a creative reflection of one's inner condition, as it is for the higher
men, Zarathustra comes to see it as psychologically valuable, ultimately no
more offensive than poetry. Indeed, Nietzsche describes prayer as a kind of
poetry; and he blurs the distinction by opening and closing Thus Spoke Zar-
athustra with Zarathustra's prayers to the sun.82 Zarathustra also praises mo-
ments in which one feels the need to speak in parables, again suggesting a
healthy impulse behind such scriptural events as the Song of Songs, the
Psalms, and Christ's parables, despite his conviction that these became im-
plicated in unsavory projects.83
Antichrist provides further evidence that Nietzsche's criticisms are directed
at the projects in which prayer is implicated instead of the psychological
principles that underlie it. To be sure, he expresses contempt for those whose
prayers are virtual "To Do" lists for God, conceived, in Gregory Moses's apt
phrase, as "a cosmic bellhop."84 "The principle of'Christian love': in the end
it wants to be paid well."85 Yet although Antichrist is his most shrilly anti-
Christian work, it rarely mentions prayer; and when it does, it criticizes
prayer's reinforcement of pernicious orientations, not the mechanism em-
ployed.86
Perhaps the most frequently cited of Nietzsche's remarks on prayer occurs
in The Gay Science, in the section titled "Excelsior." There an unidentified
speaker addresses a "man of renunciation" with a litany of things to be
renounced. The litany begins with "You will never pray again, never adore
again, never again rest in endless trust...." The speaker concludes the litany
on a skeptical note: "[m]an of renunciation, all this you wish to renounce?
Who will give you the strength for that? Nobody yet has had this strength!"87
On one level, this image is comic polemic. Nietzsche observes that the
supposed religious renunciate does not really renounce everything. Perhaps
human psychology is alleged to be ill-suited to complete abandonment of
such metaphysical solace, he admits, but no one has actually tried the ex-
periment that demonstrates whether this is so or not.88 The real renunciate


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
37
would be one who abjured the most soothing accouterments of traditional
religion: prayer, belief in ultimate teleologies, the quest for unconditional
support, and peace.
Nietzsche comments with a parable:
There is a lake that one day ceased to permit itself to flow off; it formed
a dam where it had hitherto flown off; and ever since this lake is rising
higher and higher. Perhaps this very renunciation will also lend us the
strength needed to bear this renunciation; perhaps man will rise ever higher
as soon as he ceases to flow out into a god.89
Nietzsche's parable reflects the unpredictable nature of the transitional
period between traditional Christian practice and a new spiritual future. The
consequences of widespread atheism have yet to be determined. Yet the im-
age of a lake growing higher and higher is an unstable one. If the water
continues to rise, it will eventually overflow the dam. Kaufmann notes that
a similar image is used in an earlier section of the book, again one that
stresses uncertainty and instability: "[w]e are, all of us, growing volcanoes
that approach the hour of their eruption; but how near or how distant that
is, nobody knows—not even God."90
Section 285 is ambiguous with respect to its religious implications. Nietz-
sche displays a similar ambivalence when he employs this image of damming
up energy in two sections of Twilight of the Idols. In the first, titled "Whis-
pered to the Conservatives," he emphasizes the potential for a damming
project to backfire:
But no one is free to be a crab. Nothing avails: one must go forward—step
by step further into decadence (that is my definition of modern "pro-
gress"). One can check this development and thus dam up degeneration,
gather it and make it more vehement and sudden: one can do no more.91
In the immediately following section, however, titled "My Conception of
Genius," Nietzsche announces:
Great men, like great ages, are explosives in which a tremendous force is
stored up; their precondition is always historically and physiologically, that
for a long time much has been gathered, stored up, saved up, and con-
served for them—that there has been no explosion for a long time.92
If Nietzsche is encouraging the construction of a dam to stop the dissi-
pation of human spiritual energy, this project would seem to have a limited
time frame. Perhaps, however, asserting control over this hydraulic system
is the recipe for greatness at this time. The image in "Excelsior" presupposes
Ludwig Feuerbach's theory that humanity has become impoverished by
having disowned its own spiritual resources, attributing them to God.93 Sug-
gesting that prayer is one mode of "flowing out into a god," Nietzsche im-
plies that prayer of certain sorts can actually impoverish our inner life. Prayer
can reinforce our doubts about our own ability to deal with our circum-
stances, and it can be instrumental for infantilizing ourselves in our imagined
dependence on God.


38
COMIC RELIEF
Nevertheless, Nietzsche considers another possibility for prayer elsewhere
in The Gay Science. Memorized prayer, he argues in section 128, was originally
designed "for those who do not know any elevation of the soul or at least
do not notice it when it occurs." Thus,
[t] o keep them at least from disturbing others, the wisdom of all founders
of religions, small as well as great, has prescribed to them the formulas of
prayers—as mechanical work for the lips that takes some time and requires
some exertion of the memory as well as the same fixed posture for hands,
feet, and eyes.... The main thing is that this work fixes them for a time
and makes them tolerable to look at.
Nietzsche's criticism seems most focused on the incapacity of these individ-
uals for "thoughts and elevations of their own." Relatively speaking, Nietz-
sche seems to admire the genuinely religious individual, who "is an exception
in every religion." And for such an individual, traditional formulaic prayers
have a certain value during "weary hours when a sequence of venerable
words and sounds and a pious routine are welcome."94 Although prayer was
not invented for the genuinely religious, they can benefit from prayer when
it is understood in naturalistic terms as having a role in psychological re-
newal.
The "welcome" aspect of memorized prayers appears to be aesthetic.
Prayers are seen as an aesthetically pleasing form of everyday habit, especially
valuable when one is in need of refreshment. These aesthetically rewarding
practices are among the most effective means that Christianity has used to
advance its worldview. By drawing attention to their naturalistic basis, Nietz-
sche extricates the power of enchanting incantations from the specific reli-
gious vision they have traditionally assisted. Liberated from the task of en-
forcing Christian ideology, prayers (or something like them) become a means
to revitalize one's spirit, presumably in order to be refreshed for later original
insight.95
As Nietzsche explains later in The Gay Science, he considers habit to be
psychologically indispensable.95 For those capable of "thoughts and elevations
of their own," those truly spiritual people whom Nietzsche appears to con-
sider higher individuals, even memorized prayer can contribute structure to
life, and in this way contribute to meaning. He is convinced that absolute
adherence to traditional formulas is more likely to reinforce entrenched and
undesirable beliefs than to facilitate genuine spirituality. But like most human
behaviors, the employment of religious formulas should be understood nat-
uralistically and judged according to the spiritual condition that motivates
it—and such conditions vary from person to person and from time to time.
Like that of medicine, prayer's value is relative to the particular person in
particular circumstances.
Nietzsche certainly does not shun the format of formulaic repetition. In-
deed, he sees this format as a tool for effecting the transvaluation of morality
which he considers the resolution to our crisis in values. Repetition of new


NIETZSCHE S NURSERY RHYMES
39
formulas is necessary to unlearn the damaging tradition that so many of our
nursery rhymes and traditional prayers have fostered: "We have to learn to
think differently
in order at last, perhaps very late on, to attain even more:
to feel
differently."97
In this sense, Nietzsche's preface in rhymes offers counterformulas to the
ones we actually learned as toddlers. The preface dramatizes a literal return
to innocence by spouting new nursery chants. The poems urge us to assume
the stance of openness that children have when learning nursery rhymes. Of
course, we cannot fully unlearn well-implanted lessons from our actual child-
hoods. But we can set about the task of replacing at least some of them, in
the hope of eventually transforming ourselves.
These considerations cast additional light on the opening rhyme's image
of offering us "new food." Nietzsche stresses here the importance of incor-
porating new values, of literally making them part of one's physical being.
Zarathustra's remarks on reading and writing emphasize the importance of
memorization in this effort:
Of all that is written I love only what a man has written with his blood.
Write with blood, and you will experience that blood is spirit....
Whoever writes in blood and aphorisms does not want to be read but
to be learned by heart. In the mountains the shortest way is from peak to
peak: but for that one must have long legs. Aphorisms should be peaks—
and those who are addressed tall and lofty.98
Analyzing Nietzsche's use of aphorisms, Arthur Danto remarks:
[l]anguage must implant itself in the reader, and wisdom comes from an
experience which is literary only in the sense that it is caused by a book.
... So we have to realize that in reading Nietzsche we are being attacked;
we need some kind of shield or the aphorism will land and we lose to the
words."
He adds:
Nietzsche was too much the classicist not to know that aphorism and
remembering are pragmatically co-implicated, or to be ignorant that the
earliest collection of aphorisms was attributed to Hippocrates, and consti-
tuted a kind of vade mecum of medical praxeology, a body of maxims
pointed and polished in order to stick in the intern's mind.100
Nietzsche had learned from the prayers and scriptural passages of his
religious background. He learned the worldview that he came to abhor; but
he also learned the psychological power of these formats themselves, evident
especially when he began to resist the contents they bore. Formulas in his
own memory had entrenched expectations so deeply in his mind that they
could scarcely be dislodged even when he became convinced of their false-
hood. In this respect, the power of religious formulas had pernicious effects.
Yet Nietzsche also recognized that words deeply rooted in memory could


40
COMIC RELIEF
feed the genuinely spiritual individual (the free spirit as opposed to the pious
person), especially at times when he or she needs refreshment.
Nietzsche's insight, reflected in his decision to begin The Gay Science with
"jingles," is akin to that expressed in the story of the rabbi who was asked
why Scripture says, "Place these words upon your heart." "Why," the ques-
tioner asked, "does it not say, 'Place these words in your heart'?" The rabbi
answered, "You should place the words upon your heart, so that when your
heart breaks, the words will fall into it."101
Nietzsche's Zarathustra says something similar when he announces, "I
love him who casts golden words before his deeds and always does even
more than he promises; for he wants to go under."102 "Golden words," words
distilled from our finest visions of value, are expressions of aspiration, which
is active spiritual energy. Well-worn incantations may serve some as substi-
tutes for vision and reinforcement of prejudice; but words refined into spir-
itual aphorisms can also recharge and stimulate the individual's own
"thoughts and elevations."
Do the words that we fling before us dictate our spiritual limitations, or
are they songs heralding our spiritual possibilities? This is Nietzsche's ques-
tion, and his rhyming format alone will not answer it. Yet the format is a
kind of response. Nietzsche considers many traditional formulas as encap-
sulating dubious values, and being so entangled with religious establishments
that they are unlikely to inspire original insights or genuine (i.e., individually
experienced) spirituality. Nietzsche offers new words in his verses, hoping
that these might nourish the modern free spirit at moments of frustration
and fatigue, and recognizing that a spiritual return to the nursery requires
new rhymes, both defiant and aspiring:
36. Jugendschriften
Meiner Weisheit A und O
Klang mir hier: was hort' ich doch!
Jetzo klingt mir's night mehr so,
Nur das ew'ge Ah! und Oh!
Meiner Jugend hor' ich noch.
Juvenilia
The A and O of my wisdom
Rang here for me: but what did I hear?
Now it sounds to me no more so,
Only the eternal Ah! and Oh!
of my youth do I yet hear.103
65. Sternenmoral
Verausbestimmt zur Sternenbahn,
Was geht dich, Stern, das Dunkel an?
Roll' selig hin durch diese Zeit!
Ihr Elend sei dir fremd und weit!


NIETZSCHE'S NURSERY RHYMES
41
Der fernsten Welt gehort dein Schein:
Mitleid soil Siinde fur dich sein!
Nur Bin Gebot gilt dir: sei rein!
Star Morals
Called a star's orbit to pursue,
What is the darkness, star, to you?
Roll on in bliss, traverse this age—
Its misery far from you and strange.
Let farthest world your light secure.
Pity is sin you must abjure.
But one command is your: be pure!104


When Nietzsche sent a newly published copy of Die Frohliche Wissenschaft
to Erwin Rohde, his cover letter added a word of warning: "It contains a
picture of me, and I certainly know that it is not the picture of me that you
carry in your heart."1 We might similarly heed Nietzsche's warning as we
approach The Gay Science. Not only do his opening rhymes assault our con-
ditioning, especially our moral indoctrination. They also jar with our ac-
quired ideas about Nietzsche. Nietzsche, for us, is a cultural icon. And noth-
ing makes this clearer than the motion picture industry.
Nietzsche has hit the cinematic big-time. This is hardly to say that the
role of Nietzsche is coveted by movie stars, although one could imagine
Erland Josephson, the actor who played Nietzsche opposite Dominique Sanda
in Beyond Good and Evil, 2 being pleased by the arrangement. More com-
monly, however, only Nietzsche's name makes a movie appearance. His name
is taken, in vain or otherwise, in a remarkable array of films, including Com-
pulsion* Rope,4 The Doors,5 Blazing Saddles,6 A Fish Called Wanda,7 The Fisher
King* A Kiss Before Dying,9 Sybil,10 and the 1991 version of Cape Fear.11 Even
Conan the Barbarian12 opens with a reference to Nietzsche, a voice-over of
the statement "What does not kill us, makes us stronger."13
Absurd though it is to think that Nietzsche meant strength a la Conan,
this insinuation is charitable by comparison with those made by many films
invoking Nietzsche. Since Compulsion and Rope treated the cold-blooded
murders committed by Leopold and Loeb, individuals whose obsession with
Nietzsche is a well-known historical fact, Nietzsche has been caricatured as
a monster in movies, and his name has often flagged a total break with
decency.14 Reference to Nietzsche in these films may be dictated by the sub-
ject matter, since Nietzsche was psychologically important to these killers,
much as the Beatles' song "Helter Skelter" inspired Charles Manson when
he launched a murder spree.15 One cannot say the same, however, of the
mention in Cape Fear of its villain's reading Nietzsche in a library. The point
is not to recall historical facts or to demonstrate the villain's intellectual
curiosity. Instead, mere interest in Nietzsche is presented as a sign of having
42
INTERLUDE I
Nietzsche in the Movies


NIETZSCHE IN THE MOVIES
43
no respect for human life.16 Roman Polanski makes a similar gesture in Death
and the Maiden." An apparently kind man who helps a well-known politician
with his flat tire quotes Nietzsche when the two chat afterward. The pro-
pensity for quoting Nietzsche later becomes one of the features that identify
this man as a doctor who had routinely raped and tortured political prisoners
in his younger life.
A Fish Called Wanda treats Nietzsche in a similar vein, although the film
is comic and its Nietzsche-reading criminal ridiculous. This character, named
Otto, is first shown waking to an alarm. Instantaneously, he grabs and fires
a large gun and then calmly returns to the book he was reading before fall-
ing asleep: Nietzsche's Beyond Good and Evil. Although he is a ne'er-do-
well whose exaggerated efforts to be a "Nietzschean" are laughable, his ap-
peals to Nietzsche are consistently conjoined with sadism. He reduces
an animal lover named Ken to a stuttering fit when he remarks that Nietzsche
called animals "God's second blunder." (Actually, Nietzsche calls the ani-
mals "God's first mistake," using "God's second mistake" in reference to
women.)18
Otto's cruelty reaches its climax when he later attempts to pry information
from Ken. After tying Ken to a chair, Otto munches on French fries and
gives his victim a quiz on Nietzsche, particularly on the idea of the superman
and Nietzsche's theory of cruelty. Briefly pausing after each question, Otto
announces the answer himself and penalizes his victim by putting a French
fry up one of his nostrils. This game culminates with Otto's eating some of
the bound man's prized fish (including the fish named Wanda), since fish
go with chips.
A third movie associating Nietzsche with violence and perversity is Oliver
Stone's The Doors. It begins with Jim Morrison, a film student at UCLA,
showing a short subject he has made to his class. The camera in Morrison's
film moves downward from the shoulders to the gyrating hips and legs of a
flimsily clad female dancer who, we discover, is performing on top of a
television set showing news clips of Hitler's Germany. Jim Morrison narrates,
at one point sensuously grabbing the dancer's legs, and he cites several lines
from Nietzsche:
Nietzsche said, "All great things must first wear terrifying and monstrous
masks in order to inscribe themselves on the hearts of humanity.". ...
"This world a monster of energy, without beginning, without end. Likewise
without increase or income, disclosing nothing, this world, this world is
will to power and nothing besides."19
For the final line, the image changes to one of Jim Morrison walking along
a high ledge, reading from The Portable Nietzsche.20
I do not mean to imply that Nietzsche serves only as emblem of amoral
cruelty in American popular culture. Another of his emblematic popular
culture roles is that of the morbidly serious thinker, as discussed in chapter
i. Clueless utilizes this association when it signals the relative seriousness of
Josh, the heroine's college-age stepbrother, by showing him reading a thick


44
COMIC RELIEF
edition of Nietzsche.21 The seriousness associated with Richard Strauss's Also
Sprach Zarathustra, a tone poem based on Nietzsche's work, is used to comic
effect later in the film when its famous opening accompanies a close-up of
a portable phone just before it rings. The impact here is to suggest the
heroine's frivolity by hinting that gossipy phone calls are precisely what she
takes seriously.
A Bass beer advertisement that made the rounds in the United States as
well as the United Kingdom utilized a famous picture of Nietzsche looking
quite earnest. "Why Does Man Exist?" the poster asks, as if this were a
caption for Nietzsche's thoughts. The bottom of the poster reveals the rela-
tionship between Nietzsche and the beer: "Bass helps you get to the bottom
of it all." An even more irreverent use of Nietzsche as morbidly deep (ab-
surdly so) occurs in Mel Brooks's Blazing Saddles. A pompous character
sitting at the head table of a town council meeting follows the chairman's
call to order with the remark, "Nietzsche says, 'Out of chaos comes order.' "
Another character tells him he should blow it out of a particular part of his
anatomy.22
These last two examples, like the others I have mentioned, suggest that
the name "Nietzsche" is thought by moviemakers to evoke definite associ-
ations in the viewer, associations that are various but generally negative.
Cruelty, violence, perversity, murkiness, and inappropriate ponderousness
are characteristics of the Nietzschean mystique. Combined with these images,
Nietzsche's insanity, portrayed in thoroughly deranged fashion at the end of
Lilliana Calivani's Beyond Good and Evil, appears too much like the finale of
a morality play, with madness being the consequence of disturbed and bar-
barous views.
Popular culture is not without its counters to this caricature. Although its
sketches of Nietzsche did not make it into the screenplay, Milan Kundera's
The Unbearable Lightness of Being depicts Nietzsche as sensitive but not mor-
bidly so.23 If anything, Kundera's Nietzsche is more balanced than most peo-
ple, for he recognizes the urgency of balance as a basic human project. Even
Kundera's picture of the mad Nietzsche, who signals his insanity by weeping
and hugging a horse being beaten, is presented as someone who was espe-
cially attentive and sensitive to his surroundings. Nietzsche's gesture, Kun-
dera speculates, was a request for forgiveness. Nietzsche sought forgiveness
for the whole Western philosophical tradition since Descartes, which has
treated animals as mere machines.
I imagine Nietzsche's madness as resembling the sound of a record after
the music ends, when the needle keeps going and produces an ululating white
noise. Like the end of a record, Nietzsche's madness, as I see it, does not
undercut the sense of what precedes it, any more than such white noise
annuls the earlier music. This is one of the reveries of Nietzsche that con-
ditions my reading, to which I presently return.


Aesthetic Perspectivism
The Overture: Laugh and the
World Keeps Laughing
Section i of The Gay Science functions as an overture. Like Wagner's overture
to Tristan und Isolde, it defies tradition by offering not a firm point of ref-
erence, but a vertiginous lunge into the body of the book. Again like the
overture, it avoids any stable cadence, precluding any sense of the direction
of the book as a whole.
In the first place, the narrator seems to be leaping from perspective to
perspective. Nietzsche opens not with a clear statement of position, but with
the suggestion that he typically shifts from view to view: "Whether I con-
template men with benevolence or with an evil eye... ."' He claims, like
God, to observe humanity at a distance, although he is not restricted by the
benevolence that characterizes God's view of humanity. Instead, he claims
great flexibility in his viewpoint. Perhaps he is more akin to an Athenian
god, eager to assume whatever form might lend him some advantage. If he
manages to avoid being stuck in a definite perspective, is he still engaged in
the action or just a distant observer?
In any case, Nietzsche takes a Schopenhauerian view in this opening,
observing that human beings all are instinctively absorbed in the perfor-
mance of a single task, "what is good for the preservation of the human
race."2 This, he claims, is essential to our species. If this sounds like a state-
ment of position, it is not one that Nietzsche holds for long.
Although this goal appears definite, the method for aspiring toward it is
not, and this is a second way in which section i conveys an impression of
instability. The economy of instincts comprises a marvelous variety of drives
in competition, each of which assumes some role in the performance of the
task. This economic metaphor draws attention to the dynamism that will
prove central both to this section and to the book as a whole.
A third layer of section i's insinuation of instability is its suggestion that
even humanity's basic goal, the preservation of the species, is historically
45
THREE


46
COMIC RELIEF
relative. Nietzsche doubts that we "are at all capable of living in a way that
would damage the species. ... What might have harmed the species may have
become extinct many thousands of years ago and may by now be one of
those things that are not possible even for God."3 The latter is an extremely
strong suggestion, and not just because Nietzsche later announces the death
of the deity. Only a logical impossibility is impossible for God. Can Nietzsche
really be suggesting that logical possibility extends only as far as is consistent
with the good of the species?4
Nietzsche proposes a new question: If the good of the species is assured
in any event, what should be our goal? One alternative is concern for the
individual. Species and individual are often opposed by Schopenhauer, one
of Nietzsche's youthful heroes, although Schopenhauer thinks we are often
duped to serve the species even when we believe that our motives are "self-
ish." Nietzsche seems to endorse this Schopenhauerian antagonism between
species and individual as he continues: "Pursue your best or your worst
desires, and above all perish! In both cases you are probably still in some
way a promoter and benefactor of humanity and therefore entitled to your
eulogists—but also to your detractors."5
The most important achievement of the individual, from the standpoint
of the species, is to die—hardly the most desirable accomplishment from the
standpoint of the individual, but important to the economy of the species,
which depends on the continual flow and reconfiguration of energies.6 The
reader, thus encouraged to "drop dead," is likely to respond defensively. We
can therefore see this remark as Nietzsche's confronting the reader's likely
perspective with a more threatening one. Here we encounter a fourth insin-
uation of instability—for the reader is jostled into shifting his or her own
response from openly entertaining Nietzsche's remarks to a more self-
interested posture.
Nietzsche, so far, seems to acquiesce in the aims of the species. After all,
he has just suggested that there is no possible escape from embracing them.
If, however, one pulls back from the impetus of Nietzsche's opening pres-
entation, one observes a further effort at forcing the reader off guard. Evil
drives, he claims, are as valuable to the species as good ones. Humanity needs
its share of hostile and aggressive motives. We should consider revising our
condemnation of these "instincts." They have their place along with instincts
we call "good."
This challenge to the traditional duality of good and evil is one of the
major themes in the book. In addition to defying linguistic conventions,
Nietzsche presents a picture that defies conventional morality. Since the Per-
sian prophet Zarathustra, Western morality has insisted that no human
choice is more important than that between good and evil, and that human
well-being—whether in this life or the next—depends on the choice. Con-
tentiously, Nietzsche asserts that this polarity is ultimately irrelevant to hu-
man thriving.
Yet Nietzsche is not content merely to proclaim a "new immoralism"; he
takes yet another unexpected orientation, a perverse stance of mirth. Not


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
47
only good and evil but we ourselves cannot be taken seriously from a natu-
ralistic standard of value. Indeed, we would have to see ourselves as laugh-
able.7
But you will never find anyone who could wholly mock you as an indi-
vidual, also in your best qualities, bringing home to you to the limits of
truth your boundless, flylike, froglike wretchedness! To laugh at oneself as
one would have to laugh in order to laugh out of the whole truth—to do
that even the best so far has lacked sufficient sense for the truth, and the
most gifted have too little genius for that.8
By this point Nietzsche has assaulted the reader's imagination by means
of an outrageous ad hominem attack, which predictably incites the reader to
defensiveness. Nietzsche worsens the blow by suggesting that honest self-
evaluation would capsize any sense of self-worth. Yet this harsh attack on
the reader's sense of dignity is lightened by the suggestion that the worst
possibility for the individual is merely laughter—to be laughed at by every-
one, including oneself. Laughter undercuts whatever one brings to the fore
as one's basis for pride, but self-mockery makes one a victor as well as a
victim. When one says, "Yes, I suppose I was being silly," one walks away
quite easily. Only one's mask has been damaged.
Nietzsche moves the discussion from the embarrassingly personal to the
collective experience of humanity:
Even laughter may yet have a future. I mean, when the proposition "the
species is everything, one is always none" has become part of humanity,
and this ultimate liberation and irresponsibility has become accessible to
all at all times. Perhaps laughter will then have formed an alliance with
wisdom, perhaps only "gay science" will then be left.9
At last, with the phrase "gay science," Nietzsche invokes Wissenschaft, the
apparent topic of his book. Wissenschaft, in plain English, is scholarship, and
should not be understood as "science" in the narrow sense. The only Wis-
senschaft with a place in the future, he tells us, may be that pursued "gaily,"
in a spirit of fun.
Yet that is not our current position. Currently "we still live in the age of
tragedy, the age of moralities and religions." Moreover, the "tragedians, too,
promote the interests of the species... by promoting the faith in life," con-
vincing us that life has a meaning, which is behind it. This tendency toward
tragic interpretations of life is an "instinct" that erupts "from time to time"
as another manifestation of the instinct for species preservation. Teachers of
religions and ethics "come on stage" and interpret life erroneously, "in order
that what happens necessarily and always, spontaneously and without any
purpose, may henceforth appear to be done for some purpose and strike
man as rational and an ultimate commandment."10
Crucial for such a teacher is the commandment "that we do not laugh at
existence, or at ourselves—or at him. . . ."n If we, as readers, have followed
Nietzsche to this point, we have probably felt a slight shudder at the thought


48
COMIC RELIEF
of humiliation. Now we consider the sages of history as having had the same
motive. Human beings have been susceptible to sages when people have felt
mockable. Sages have arisen as protectors at exactly such moments.
Sages have offered protection from humiliation by means of religions,
moral teaching, and serious art, particularly tragedy. Nietzsche suggests that
the tragic hero's appearance on stage coincides with the attainment of "the
gruesome counterpart of laughter, that profound emotional shock felt by
many individuals at the thought: 'Yes, I am worthy of living!' "12 While self-
mocking laughter often stems from the sudden sense that one is being a fool,
this alternative is the equally sudden sense that one deserves to be taken
seriously.
Yet eventually, Nietzsche insists that the comic perspective inevitably over-
comes all tragic perspectives:
There is no denying that in the long run every one of these great teachers
of a purpose was vanquished by laughter, reason, and nature: the short
tragedy always gave way again and returned into the eternal comedy of
existence; and "the waves of uncountable laughter"—to cite Aeschylus—
must in the end overwhelm even the greatest of these tragedians.13
Nietzsche reminds us that while we may laugh at those who considered Zeus
or witches or the little people responsible for phenomena in the world, our
own accounts will probably seem equally preposterous to our descendants.
Sooner or later, the most serious theory comes to seem absurd and naive.
The shape of human history resembles the ancient Athenian practice of fol-
lowing a series of tragedies with a comic satyr play. Oddly, a tragedian is
cited for recognizing that his work will always be drowned by comedy. But
then, who would know better that he will never get the last word?
Nietzsche's summation is as inconclusive as the rest of the section. Hu-
manity, unlike other animal species, has developed as a "condition of exis-
tence" the need "to believe, to know, from time to time why... [it] exists."14
This need arises because one has come to question one's own value. One
imagines that security depends on one convincing oneself and one's associ-
ates that at least some things are not laughable. Accordingly, one asserts one's
own dignity, bolstering that assertion with doctrines and taboos that one
views as nonnegotiable.
The price of self-worth is rigidity on these matters; but rigidity is its own
vulnerability. Whatever is rigid can snap, with sufficient eternal pressure. To
prevent this, one insists on external compliance with taboos. The more rigid
one's adherence to the doctrine of self-worth, the more rigidly one holds to
a set of moral or religious doctrines.
Still, laughter might undermine this whole doctrinal structure. Laughter
resolves self-doubt, not by insisting on the unquestionable importance of
that self, but by allowing for the disintegration of one's "self and allowing
the parts of one's psyche to rearrange themselves. Indeed, laughter literally
initiates physiological transformation, for it marks respiration, which contin-
ually reconfigures one's bodily relationship to the external environment.15


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
49
Laughter commonly affirms the continued interaction of self and the world,
and the transformation of self that is involved. To laugh at oneself out of
the whole truth would be to be flexible in one's identity. Metaphorically,
laughter is a gesture of sloughing off one skin and beginning to use another.
The most profound view of one's selfhood is that it is contingent and tem-
porary, with limitations that make it provisional, ideally eventually seen
through.
Nevertheless, Nietzsche comments,
the most cautious friend of man will add: "Not only laughter and gay
wisdom but the tragic, too, with all its sublime unreason, belongs among
the means and the necessities of the preservation of the species."
Consequently—. Consequently, Consequently. O, do you understand
me, my brothers? Do you understand this new law of ebb and flood? There
is a time for us, too!16
This conclusion resembles a slide projector, run by remote control, that
continues clicking forward despite its operator's desire to stop. In despite of
the encouraging suggestion that one could see things comically, one is not
at all confident, reading section i, that Nietzsche is taking a comic point of
view. Is he seriously defending the current dominance of tragedy in the larger
economy of human matters, despite his conviction that comedy is the truer
perspective? Perhaps he is only defending the "friend" of humankind for
saying this, not claiming to be such a friend himself. We are not really sure
who our narrator is.
What conclusion follows from Nietzsche's summation: "Consequently—"?
And what follows: "Consequently. Consequently"? Who is the "us" that finds
a place in the "new law of ebb and flood"? Nietzsche does not elaborate. Yet
his reference to "us" directs our attention to our own position in his reflec-
tions. Nietzsche is not describing the human situation in an objective man-
ner. Instead, he is implicating his reader in the situation described, and rais-
ing questions about the reader's precise location in the whole saga of human
experience.
Nietzsche is also manipulating the reader's attitude. The rhetorical moves
of the first section have been a series of pressures to doubt one's own status
as a human being. As readers, we have been pushed into the position de-
scribed, in which we are vulnerable to sages and their doctrines as means of
resurrecting a sense of self-worth. Section i has also encouraged us to see
through this strategy. By its end, we feel the need for some confirmation of
our value. Now Nietzsche contends that such confirmation has its place in
helping us to go forward, even if it is only one perspective, and not the truest
one at that.
The next few sections hint that Nietzsche is one of "us," his self-doubtful
readership, because he, more than most of his contemporaries, feels the need
to find purposes and to take life seriously. Yet we shall see that we cannot
draw this as a final identification, either. Our narrator keeps changing, as his
topics do, as if he is thinking his way from one outlook to another. The


50
COMIC RELIEF
seeming slide show of images continues, some transitions more abrupt than
others. Book I does constitute a unity, but one that is prismatic, emerging
from juxtapositions. Nietzsche disturbs any impression we might get of the
basic thrust of his project.
The significance of this vision is evident in the sections that make up
Book I and, indeed, the entire Gay Science. The book is a defense of per-
spectivism. Not only does it suggest considerations that might persuade us
of the merit of perspectivism, but as the narrator's viewpoint keeps shifting,
it also demonstrates perspectival thinking. Nietzsche seems bent on getting
us to keep changing our view as well.17 Each section is its own vantage point,
allowing one a new venue for assessing what has gone before, but each as
provisional as the last. We move along with the ground beneath us. But if
the ground keeps moving, what grounds our scholarly enterprise?
Dethroning Epistemology
Consequently. Consequently. Consequently. Scholarship generally, and phi-
losophy in particular, have come to be seen as trading primarily in argu-
ments, in drawing long chains of conclusions. And this type of scholarship,
Nietzsche submits, has been a tragic enterprise. The beelines scholars make
toward their conclusions stem from a desperate quest for stability, for a firm
sense of purpose despite the fear that there might be none.
According to Nietzsche, the dominance of epistemology within philosophy
has been an indication of Wissenschaft as a tragic pursuit, motivated by fear
of doubt. Weakness and timidity, in Nietzsche's view, have colluded to con-
vince philosophers that questions of knowledge can be settled, and that they
must be settled before values can be considered. Nietzsche remarks elsewhere:
Philosophy reduced to "theory of knowledge," in fact no more than a timid
epochism and doctrine of abstinence—a philosophy that never gets beyond
the threshold and takes pains to deny itself the right to enter—that is
philosophy in its last throes. ...18
Nietzsche's remarks on knowledge and other epistemological matters in
Book I should be seen in this context. The discussion of these issues so early
in the book should not be seen as Nietzsche's endorsement of the tendency
of philosophy, since Kant's Critique of Pure Reason, to seek secure episte-
mological grounds before pursuing anything else. Far from seeking to rein-
force this strategy, Nietzsche aims to discredit it. He insists that Wissenschaft
is always conditioned by the perspective of the scholar, and that we have
scarcely acknowledged the details that determine the specific hues of each
individual's vantage and palette of values. Given the fact that such consid-
erations are underexplored, Nietzsche argues, inquiry into the concrete de-
tails of human cognition would be a valuable wissenschaftliche enterprise. In


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
51
fact, it is essential if we are not to be bamboozled by our own pretense at
objectivity.
This is one of Nietzsche's own motives in insisting on the inadequacy of
our current conception of knowledge. As Kaufmann observes, Nietzsche is
not "for" or "against" science;19 but he does think that the nature of the
enterprise should be rethought. Richard Schacht's description of the project
of the book is essentially the general project that Nietzsche proposes for
Wissenschaft: "the elaboration of a philosophical anthropology, in the sense
of a comprehensive understanding of the nature and prospects of this re-
markable and peculiar creature that is at once animal and no longer merely
animal."20
Yet suppose, as section i suggests, that we come to see this whole scholarly
project as provisional, even as superficial. Countering what he sees as the
contemporary tragic cast of Wissenschaft, Nietzsche's "gay science" is schol-
arship understood as comic. How does scholarship appear from a comic
viewpoint? Scholars' conclusions would be more like punch lines than fix-
tures, each important only if it causes us double takes and giggles.
Such "gay science" does not treat knowledge like a building that one
wishes to reinforce or extend. Instead, it approaches knowing as an active
mode of thinking, with thinking understood as Nietzsche describes it in Twi-
light of the Idols: "Thinking wants to be learned like dancing, as a kind of
dancing."21 The gay science is an enterprise whose moves have contextual,
not absolute, significance. Wissenschaft is an art, in which a brilliant move is
one that resonates with what has come before, reverberates for some time
forward, or launches a bold new direction, even for a short period of time.
Nietzsche's own statement of his positive aspirations for Wissenschaft ap-
pears in the preface to the second edition of The Birth of Tragedy. He de-
scribes the motives already evident in his first book: "The task which this
audacious book dared to tackle for the first time" is "to look at science in
the perspective of the artist, but at art in that of life."22 Wissenschaft, properly,
must be contextualized, specifically within the broader enterprises of art and
life.
"To look at science in the perspective of the artist" is ambiguous. It may
mean that Wissenschaft can provide the subject matter for art. Alternatively,
it may mean that Wissenschaft is an artistic enterprise itself. Nietzsche's writ-
ings suggest that he holds both views. The Gay Science itself is an artistic
work, which takes Wissenschaft as its subject matter. At the same time, we
see in Book I a critique of contemporary Wissenschaft on the ground that a
rigid conception of knowledge has stifled awareness of its creative, interpre-
tive nature.
Nietzsche counters this epistemological project, claiming that knowledge
is never independent of the situation of the observer. Modern epistemology's
claim to objectivity depends on willful ignorance of factors that always filter
our knowledge of particulars. These include the features that individualize
our perceptions, our scales of valuation, and temporal considerations.


52
COMIC RELIEF
The Aesthetic Conditions of Knowledge
As Nietzsche's formulation reveals, he considers Wissenschaft an art form that
should be judged for its service to life. Our living is always situated, and
Nietzsche sees it as situated in the aesthetic domain.231 use the term aesthetic
here in its traditional sense of investigations into sense perception and what
we derive from it. This term is appropriate, I think, because it gets at the
root and range of the perspectival variables that are relevant to a true picture
of the situations in which we apprehend. An additional advantage of the
term is that Nietzsche's images drawn from the sphere of art and aesthetics
more narrowly conceived usually reverberate, illuminating features of life,
broadly conceived.
Nietzsche dethrones "traditional" epistemology from its queenly place in
philosophy in favor of aesthetics, the study of perception and value within
the perceptual sphere.24 Accordingly, over the course of Book I he takes up
explicitly aesthetic concerns: those of appearance, optics, and art. The preface
to the second edition of The Gay Science advocates being "superficial—out
of profundity." If we take these suggestions seriously, we are encouraged to
look at The Gay Science, generally, in terms of its treatment of appearance.
The Western philosophical tradition has typically opposed appearance and
reality. Descartes, for example, considers the appearances derived from the
senses as being difficult to distinguish from a dream:
[A]lthough the senses occasionally deceive us with respect to objects which
are very small or in the distance, there are many other beliefs about which
doubt is quite impossible, even though they are derived from the senses—
for example, that I am here, sitting by the fire, wearing a winter dressing-
gown, holding this piece of paper in my hands, and so on... .
A brilliant piece of reasoning! As if I were not a man who sleeps at
night, and regularly has all the same experiences while asleep as madmen
do when awake.... How often, asleep at night, am I convinced of just such
familiar events—that I am here in my dressing-gown, sitting by the fire—
when in fact I am lying undressed in bed. ... I see plainly that there are
never any sure signs by means of which being awake can be distinguished
from being asleep.25
Descartes considers the similar appearance of dreams and impressions de-
rived from the senses to be grounds for doubting the latter; he concludes
that because indubitable truth is not available through the senses, we should
recognize that reason is the source of our knowledge.
Like Descartes, Nietzsche compares appearances to a dream. Instead of
concluding that we should use reason to transcend the deceptions of the
senses, however, Nietzsche insists that living, for us, requires the dream:
I have discovered for myself that the human and animal past, indeed the
whole primal age and past of all sentient being continues in me to invent,
to love, to hate, and to infer. I suddenly woke up in the midst of this
dream, but only to the consciousness that 1 am dreaming and that I must


AESTHETIC PERSPECTJVISM
53
go on dreaming lest I perish—as a somnambulist must go on dreaming
lest: he fall. What is "appearance" for me now? Certainly not the opposite
of some essence: what could I say about any essence except to name the
attributes of its appearance!26
Nietzsche must keep dreaming because appearance itself is the reality of life:
Appearance is for me that which lives and is effective and goes so far in
its self-mockery that it makes me feel that this is appearance and will-o'-
the-wisp and a dance of spirits and nothing more—that among all these
dreamers, I, too, who "know," am dancing my dance; that the knower is
a means for prolonging the earthly dance and thus belongs to the masters
of ceremony of existence; and that the sublime consistency and interrelat-
edness of all knowledge perhaps is and will be the highest means to preserve
the universality of dreaming and the mutual comprehension of all dreamers
and thus also the continuation of the dream.27
Reason does not enable us to see through our sensory impressions to true
reality. Instead, it enables us to communicate about the world we encounter
through the senses, reinforcing the convincingness of appearances. The world
we "know" is the world as it appears to us, and the fact that our "knowledge"
constitutes an interrelated system of concepts means that we share the fac-
ulties that connect us to the world. It does not mean that we have pierced
the veil of appearance.
The insistence that the realm of appearance is both the field of knowledge
and organized by our knowing faculties is Kantian:
A concept is used transcendentally in any principle if it is referred to things
as such and in themselves; but it is used empirically if it is referred merely
to appearances, i.e., to objects of a possible experience.... [Ojnly the em-
pirical use can occur at all.... Just take as an example the concepts of
mathematics, and take them, first, in their pure intuitions. Space has three
dimensions; between two points there can be only one straight line; etc.
Although all these principles, and the presentation of the object that this
science deals with are produced in the mind completely a priori, yet they
would signify nothing if we could not always display their significance in
appearances (empirical objects). Hence, too, we require that an abstract
concept be made sensible ... because otherwise the concept would remain
(as we say) without sense, i.e., without signification. Mathematics fulfills
this demand by constructing the shape, which is an appearance present to
the senses ... 2 S
Following Kant in restricting knowledge to the phenomenal realm, Nietz-
sche shares a common supposition of most nineteenth-century German phi-
losophers. But he goes further than most in emphasizing the individualizing
features of any particular appearance. As he himself suggests elsewhere, he
outdoes Kant in respecting the limits of knowledge that Kant himself as-
serted.29 In Book III he uses the Kantian starting point to deny that human
"truths" are really "truth" in the traditional sense; and he concludes that
knowledge and truth cannot be correlated: "|T]he strength of knowledge


54
COMIC RELIEF
does not depend on its degree of truth but on its age, on the degree to which
it has been incorporated, on its character as a condition of life."30
Having restricted knowledge to that which is experienced, Kant neverthe-
less considered the significant structuring forms to be universal to human
beings. Nietzsche, while acknowledging certain common structural forms
(particularly time, as we shall see), explores the range of experiential features
that observers do not share. These depend mainly on the nuances of one's
particular experience and one's idiosyncratic background. Anticipating recent
humanistic anthropology as well as the "Twinkie defense" in court,31 Nietz-
sche cites a wide range of such features:
Where could you find a history of love, of avarice, of envy, of conscience,
of pious respect for tradition, or of cruelty? Even a comparative history of
law or at least of punishment is so far lacking completely. Has anyone
made a study of different ways of dividing up the day or of the conse-
quences of a regular schedule of work, festivals, and rest? What is known
of the moral effects of different foods?32
Many of Nietzsche's diverse meditations in Book I can be understood as
efforts to indicate the divergent perspectives of individual knowers. Images
of optics and distances are prevalent; and their disparity only underscores
Nietzsche's point that many factors shape how things appear to an individual.
We know only what we perceive; and what each individual can perceive is
limited by a variety of factors.
One of these is projection. What one sees in others depends on one's own
nature. Often we project our own characteristics onto others, even when we
recognize ourselves to be unusual. Nietzsche analyzes his own expectations
of others' intellectual conscience as being extrapolation from his own ten-
dencies.33 Similarly, he contends that people, being obsessively self-interested,
typically assume that everyone else is the same way.34
In order to see traits other than one's own, some distance is necessary.
Indeed, we need some imaginative distance from ourselves to see our own
traits. Like a charming mountain, "some greatness, like some goodness,
wants to be beheld only from a distance and by all means only from below,
not from above; otherwise it makes no impression."35 Nietzsche uses the
metaphor of physical distance to indicate a degree of psychological detach-
ment, much as this metaphor is commonly used in aesthetics to indicate
sufficient emotional remove to entertain given subject matter open-
mindedly.36
Distance is particularly crucial to comedy, for a reason already noted.
Human beings feel humiliated by being laughed at, or even by identifying
with a butt of humor.37 Nietzsche sees the roles of famous men as comic,
but he observes that they resist becoming aware of this. Indeed, this is just
what Nietzsche finds comical. They are like quick-change artists, but obliv-
ious to their own rapid stunts:
Their reputation keeps changing like their character, for their changing
instruments demand these changes and push now this and now that real


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
55
or fictitious quality out onto the stage. Their friends and allies belong ...
to these stage qualities.38
Here Nietzsche indicates a third factor influencing a person's perceptive
capabilities. Motives have considerable impact on how one apprehends
things. Desire for "the feeling of power," a kind of refrain in Book I, has
much to do with how one will register what one observes—and hence with
what one comes to know. Motivation is obscure, however. Most often, be-
havior is directed by more than one motivation.
A precursor of Freud, Nietzsche insists that human motives are often
