decipherable only by means of deep interpretation. In Book I he offers a
variety of deep interpretations, often the opposite of "commonsense" belief.
For example, in analyzing alleged virtues, Nietzsche indicates motives more
typically associated with vice. He analyzes love of neighbor as fueled by greed:
"[I]s it not a lust for new possessions?"39 Similarly, observing that romantic
love "has furnished the concept of love as the opposite of egoism," he sug-
gests that "it actually may be the most ingenuous expression of egoism."40
He sees it, too, as a hunger to possess.
Nietzsche thinks that inquiry into the aims of love suggest greed as the
typical motivation. Yet he does not think that apparently similar behavior is
always or even often produced by the same motive. Commonly, the "same"
behavior can result either from overflowing energy or from neediness. As he
summarizes this point in Book V, "I ask in every instance, 'is it hunger or
superabundance that has here become creative?' "41 Beyond this basic di-
chotomy, motivations for similar behavior can admit of wide individual var-
iation:
There is a garrulousness of wrath—frequently encountered in Luther as
well as Schopenhauer. A garrulousness due to a superabundant supply of
conceptual formulations, as in Kant. A garrulousness due to the delight in
ever new twists of the same thing: to be found in Montaigne. A garru-
lousness of spiteful characters.... A garrulousness due to the delight in
good words and language forms: not at all rare in Goethe's prose. A gar-
rulousness due to an inner pleasure in noise and confused emotions: for
example, in Carlyle.42
Some motivations are subtle enough to escape detection altogether. Nietz-
sche contends that we have qualities "which we know either badly or not at
all and which also conceal themselves by means of their subtlety even from
very subtle observers, knowing how to hide, as it were, behind nothing."
Among these are "invisible" moral qualities, which "follow their own
course":
Thus we have, for example, our industry, our ambition, our acuteness—
all the world knows about that—but in addition to all that we probably
also have our industry, our ambition, our acuteness; but for these reptile
scales no microscope has been invented as yet.43
The invisibility of motivational nuances stems from a fourth factor that
influences our perceptions. We have been trained, or conditioned, to fit our


56
COMIC RELIEF
perceptions into standard categories. We learn, for instance, to classify our
ambitiousness and their ambitiousness as the same phenomenon, in the pro-
cess habituating ourselves to overlook the differences between them. In be-
coming socialized we learn a network of categories and evaluations, which
allows us to navigate and communicate about the items in one's environment
with attention to little besides assigning labels. Some people are so accus-
tomed to navigating by means of the standard grid of categories that they
achieve what Nietzsche calls "a stupid humility":
As soon as a person of this type perceives something striking, he turns on
his heel, as it were, and says to himself: "You have made a mistake. What
is the matter with your senses? This cannot, may not, be the truth." And
then, instead of looking and listening again, more carefully, he runs away
from the striking thing, as if he had been intimidated, and tries to remove
it from his mind as fast as he can.44
A well-learned network of cultural categories, however necessary, can ob-
struct perception, according to Nietzsche. The more adept we become at
inference—at remarking, "Consequently,"—the more we ignore the extent
to which our classification scheme is arbitrary. We usually notice only what
defies our categories, as Nietzsche reminds us in Book V:
What is familiar is what we are used to; and what we are used to is most
difficult to "know"—that is, to see as a problem; that is, to see as strange,
as distant, as "outside us."45
The tendency to take the familiar for granted is evident, for example, in the
legislative policies of nations:
It is a serious mistake to study the penal code of a people as if it gave the
expression to the national character. The laws do not betray what a people
are but rather what seems to them foreign, strange, uncanny, outlandish.46
The grade of the categorial grids through which we identify things
amounts to a fifth factor affecting what we come to know. The fineness of
their scale determines the extent to which we assign labels and, accordingly,
what can and cannot appear to us. Nietzsche draws attention to the scales
of valuation by means of economic imagery in Book i. This imagery deserves
discussion at some length.
Nietzsche as Economist
One of the rhetorical peculiarities of The Gay Science is its use of economic
imagery. Already in section i we encounter straightforwardly economic meta-
phors:
It is easy enough to divide our neighbors quickly, with the usual myopia,
from a mere five paces away, into useful and harmful, good and evil men;
but in any large-scale accounting, when we reflect on the whole a little


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
57
longer, we become suspicious of this neat division and finally abandon it.
... Hatred, the mischievous delight in the misfortunes of others, the lust
to rob and dominate, and whatever else is called evil belongs to the most
amazing economy of the preservation of the species. To be sure, this econ-
omy is not afraid of high prices, of squandering, and it is on the whole
extremely foolish.47
The images of accounting and irrational economic decision making seem
strange in a discussion of the purpose of existence, of tragedy and comedy,
or of lighthearted Wissenschaft. Yet these images recur. In section 2, for ex-
ample, Nietzsche refers to good and evil as measures on "scales," and sus-
picious ones at that:
[T]he great majority of people lacks an intellectual conscience. ... Everybody
looks at you with strange eyes and goes right on handling his scales, calling
this good and that evil. Nobody even blushes when you intimate that their
weights are underweight; nor do people feel outraged; they merely laugh
at your doubts.48
Nietzsche alludes here to a prominent image in Kant's account of mo-
rality. Kant observes that it is often difficult to distinguish whether a person
is acting appropriately, behaving from the motive of doing the right thing,
or whether the motive is self-interest. He cites the following case:
For example, it certainly accords with duty that a grocer should not over-
charge his inexperienced customer; and where there is much competition
a sensible shopkeeper refrains from so doing and keeps to a fixed and
general price for everybody so that a child can buy from him just as well
as anyone else. Thus people are served honestly; but this is not nearly
enough to justify us in believing that the shopkeeper has acted in this way
from duty or from principles of fair dealing; his interests required him to
do so.49
Kant's moral praise is directed not at the practice of using accurate scales in
weighing and charging, but at engaging in this practice because of its
"weight" in accordance with the scales of good and evil. Nietzsche refor-
mulates this image, suggesting that Kant's example subjects the wrong scales
to question. Nietzsche is more worried about the legitimacy of the scales of
good and evil than of the scales used in trade. A grocer with a moral con-
science would have reason to blush if someone caught him or her using a
scale with weights that were underweight, for this would imply that the cus-
tomers were being overcharged. By contrast, when Nietzsche suggests that
people are using scales that are crude and misleading in their moral evalu-
ations, most people neither blush nor feel accused. The intellectual abomi-
nation does not strike them as comparable to the moral offense. Yet the
scales of good and evil are underweight; this crude binary system is insuffi-
cient for taking the full measure of an action in terms of its real effects. Still,
even when told this, most people continue to use these moral scales as before.
Even were people willing to replace their crude scales with more sensitive
instruments, however, they would still need to improve their eyesight. How-


58
COMIC RELIEF
ever precise the scales, the observer's measuring ability depends on refine-
ment of eyesight:
Where the poor power of the eye can no longer see the evil impulse as
such because it has become too subtle, man posits a realm of goodness.
.. . Hence, the duller the eye, the more extensive the good.50
The subtlety of one's eyesight, according to Nietzsche, limits one's ability to
see the shapes within one's environment, even the shapes of others' impulses.
Nietzsche's economic images often accompany references to "good" and
"evil," which he treats as crude markings on a scale. The dichotomy of good
and evil is prominent in Book I, yet Nietzsche's view of this polarity does
not seem consistent. In section i he seems to affirm both "good" and "evil"
instincts, but in section 2 and elsewhere he disputes their value as standards.
In the just cited passage he seems to make use of "evil" as an evaluation,
criticizing those who do not recognize an evil when it becomes subtle. In
section 4 he again uses the term "evil" as though it were a standard he
endorsed: "The strongest and most evil spirits have so far done the most to
advance humanity."51
The contradiction among Nietzsche's discussions of good and evil is only
apparent. When he uses the term "evil," he typically indicates what tradition
judges to be so. He goes on in section 4, for example, to contend:
In every teacher and preacher of what is new we encounter the same "wick-
edness" that makes conquerors notorious, even if its expression is subtler
and it does not immediately set the muscles in motion, and therefore also
does not make one that notorious. What is new, however, is always evil,
being that which wants to conquer and overthrow the old boundary mark-
ers and the old pieties; and only what is old is good.52
Nietzsche uses "evil," in other words, to refer to what is rejected as harmful
or wicked on moral grounds. It is a term of classification, and Nietzsche uses
these terms to pick out the set of things commonly indicated by it. The same
is the case for its counterpart "good," although in this case Nietzsche uses
the term more equivocally. When paired with "evil" in the moral tradition
he is attacking, "good" is taken to mean absolutely good in itself. Nietzsche
also uses "good" as an instrumental term, in the sense of "good for" some
person or persons. This is the sense used in section i when Nietzsche refers
to what is "good for the preservation of the human race." In this sense,
"good" picks out a set of things that serve the instrumental purpose indi-
cated, a set that may well differ from that picked out by "good" as the
counterpart of evil.
Yet Nietzsche's critique of "good" and "evil" operates on the meta-level;
he proposes that this classification scheme is outmoded and arbitrary. Ac-
cordingly, Nietzsche is urging that the contents of the sets called "good" and
"evil" should be reanalyzed. One justification for revaluing these sets is sug-
gested in this passage; the contents will be revalued in any event, for what
strikes a society as evil is a function of its historical novelty. Unfortunately,
however, "good" and "evil" have been interpreted as absolute assignments.


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
59
Nietzsche explicitly attacks the idea that moral good and evil are objective
features of the natural sphere. As part of his call for a Wissenschaft that is
restricted to the realm that we can know, the natural world, he suggests that
the basic dichotomy of good and evil should give way to nonmoralistic ap-
praisal of values relevant to concrete pragmatic purposes. Nietzsche proposes
an alternative dichotomy which reflects relative and contingent relationships
within the natural world, "strong" and "weak":
Is it: virtuous when a cell transforms itself into a function of a stronger
cell? It has no alternative. And is it evil when the stronger cell assimilates
the weaker? It also has no alternative.53
The image of weights used in commercial exchange underscores Nietz-
sche's call for a reassessment of familiar values, most strikingly "good" and
"evil." He recurrently uses the image of scales in referring to old and new
values. For example, he characterizes the human herd through much of his-
tory as being offended by the person who sought "to be a self and to esteem
oneself according to one's own weight and measure."54 The noble person, by
contrast, is exceptional in discovering "values for which no scales have been
invented yet."55 Such a person can even influence the era, metaphorically in
the manner of an auditor: "When a human being resists his whole age and
stops it at the gate to demand an accounting, this must have influence.
Whether that is what he desires is immaterial; that he can do it is what
matters."56 Nietzsche also affirms his own conviction that standards need
reassessment in terms of the commercial scale. "In what do you believe?" he
asks himself. "In this, that the weights of all things must be determined
anew."57
By comparing "good" and "evil" to measures on scales used in commerce,
Nietzsche indicates their role as instruments used in making a living and
obtaining nutrition. The scales of "good" and "evil" are not the unerring
judgment of the goddess Justice, who reveals the truth of all things. Instead,
they are the fetishes of Luddite grocers, who condemn themselves to failed
businesses unless they abandon the notion that their scales mark the absolute
truth.
Nietzsche's images from economics indicate our natural circumstances in
another way as well. Section i's image of "an economy" suggests a bounded
sphere, like the "household" to which the term "economy" etymologically
refers.58 I read Nietzsche as indicating the bounded ecosystem in which we
live, the domain of the natural world. On this reading, the economic imagery
reminds us that Nietzsche restricts his conception of reality to the natural
world. This is in keeping with a theme that he develops later in the book,
that there is no supernatural dimension that might miraculously supplement
our resources.59
Economic imagery also suggests a system of power differentials, but one
in which discrepancies can widen only to a certain point before the integrity
of the system collapses. The economic sphere is inherently concerned with
power, for it involves dynamic transactions that are motivated by efforts to


60
COMIC RELIEF
sustain and enhance one's capacities (whether or not the system is capitalistic
and geared to profit and dominance).
Recent French and French-influenced discussions of Nietzsche have
turned to Nietzsche's economic imagery to explicate certain dynamics of
small-scale interpersonal interaction as well, especially in connection with
power. For example, Georges Bataille associates Nietzsche's image of "over-
flow" with what he calls the economy of "expenditure."60 Gary Shapiro em-
phasizes the importance of Nietzsche's economic metaphors, especially those
concerned with establishing equivalences of values, for Nietzsche's under-
standing of reading and writing, which are variants of thinking.61 Helene
Cixous draws inspiration from Derrida's consideration of Nietzsche in con-
structing her dichotomy of "masculine" and "feminine" economies. The
masculine, "political" economy is geared to appropriation and the quid pro
quo. The feminine, "libidinal" economy is expressed through gifts, given
without concern for self-interest.62
Alan Schrift describes Nietzsche's own economic discourse as having
"feminine" aspects on Cixous's model. Nietzsche suggests an economy of
excess, claims Schrift, as opposed to the traditional model of "the reciprocal
exchange of private property that is itself grounded on the economic as-
sumption of scarcity."63 Does Schrift's analysis apply to Nietzsche's economic
imagery in The Gay Science7.
Schrift bases his reading primarily on Nietzsche's On the Genealogy of
Morals, where Nietzsche suggests that the economic concepts of debt and
repayment lie at the root of such moral concepts as guilt, punishment, and
justice. "Guilt" originally meant "debt"; indeed, the German word Schuld
covers both concepts. Those who failed to repay debts deserved punishment,
understood as a substitute for what was owed; the pleasure of inflicting cru-
elty on the debtor was considered an equivalent value. Primitive justice, sim-
ilarly, supposed that "debts" could be repaid by means of substituting equiv-
alents, so much so that the notion of "an eye for an eye" came to be the
measure of justice.
Eventually, however, societies become strong enough to become more
merciful. The policy of forgiving and forgetting is testimony to a society's
strength, and to that of individuals as well. Nietzsche associates mercy with
strength,64 while he associates ressentiment with weakness. Drawing from
Nietzsche's comments in Daybreak and Zarathustra about the desirability of
putting an end to revenge and punishment, Schrift concludes that Nietzsche
distinguishes two types of economy, each with an associated conception of
justice.
Schrift's analysis is sensitive to the texts he cites, and the distinction he
finds in Nietzsche is certainly evident. I question, however, his correlation
of the economic orientations toward accounting that Nietzsche distinguishes
in Genealogy with Cixous's masculine and feminine economies. I also doubt
that "Cixous's reflections on the logic of the gift" will actually help us to
recognize "an unacknowledged 'feminine' side of Nietzsche's economic dis-


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
6l
course,"65 although I grant that Nietzsche is interested in the "overflowing
strength" expressed in generosity.
In the first place, Cixous's distinction is problematic. She counters the
masculine economy, concerned that exchange be reciprocal, with the femi-
nine economy, modeled on the mother's act of giving birth, a gift in which
the mother is allegedly not self-interested and does not anticipate a reciprocal
gift from her gift's beneficiary.66 "If there is a self proper to woman, para-
doxically it is her capacity to depropriate herself without self-interest," write
Cixous and Catherine Clement.67
This model for the "feminine" economy is questionable. The biological
phenomenon of giving birth does not depend on any particular moral mo-
tivation, as Cixous's model insinuates. The pregnant woman can be self-
interested or not; if her pregnancy comes to term, labor will eventually arrive
regardless. Nietzsche agrees with Cixous's implicit suggestion that a mother's
orientation toward a child is different from the father's.68 But the idea that
mothers give birth without self-interest does not jibe with the psychological
facts. Even Cixous and Clement acknowledge, "Really, there is no 'free' gift.
You never give something for nothing." Nevertheless, they go on to char-
acterize the difference between the two economies by saying that, unlike the
traditional man, "she doesn't try to 'recover her expenses.' "69 This notion is
challenged by the plethora of Jewish mother jokes, in which the mother's
claim to forgo such repayment is implicitly guilt-inducing: "Don't mind me;
I'm only your mother."70
Cixous's idea that maternal love is not self-interested is suspect for reasons
that Nietzsche himself indicates. In a passage from The Gay Science that we
will consider further in the next chapter, Nietzsche suggests that contem-
porary mothers (at least those in the upper socioeconomic bracket) do indeed
understand giving birth in self-interested terms:
Women easily experience their husbands as a question mark concerning
their honor, and their children as an apology or atonement. They need
children and wish for them in a way that is altogether different from that
in which a man may wish for children.71
Nietzsche also moves directly from a description of animal mothers to con-
clusions about human mothers. Of animals he maintains:
The females find in their children satisfaction for their desire to dominate,
a possession, an occupation, something that is wholly intelligible to them
and can be chattered with: the sum of all this is what mother love is.... 7 2
Even if one doubts Nietzsche's expertise regarding maternal psychology,
he raises a number of plausible interests that women may have in having
children. By comparison, the suggestion that mothers are typically not self-
interested seems far less likely. The fact that mothers sometimes risk their
own lives for the sake of their children may seem to support Cixous's vision;
but, mercifully, these cases are rare. Maternal behavior in these circumstances


6z
COMIC RELIEF
(as well as self-risking behavior in others) is noteworthy precisely because it
contrasts with people's behavior most of the time. This includes the behavior
of mothers, who often make self-interested decisions, even when these affect
their children.
Cixous's account of masculine economy is also dubious. She characterizes
gift giving as difficult for those engaged in the masculine economy. Schrift
explicates Cixous's analysis by reference to the philosophical conversation of
Derrida, Marcel Mauss, and others about the implicit obligation that receiv-
ing a gift involves. Cixous remarks, "[F]or the moment you receive some-
thing you are effectively 'open' to the other, and if you are a man you have
only one wish."73 Schrift fills in the blank: "to return the gift as quickly as
possible."74 Schrift goes on to say that "Nietzsche himself recognizes this
point as he notes that deep within the noble soul resides an 'instinct for
repayment' that is uncomfortable with clemency (Gnade) among equals
(BGE, 26s)."75
Granted, it is of tremendous interest if men and women feel differently
about gifts; and evidence in support of this conclusion continues to accu-
mulate. I do not think, however, that this shows that the genders tend to
operate on inherently different economies. Deborah Tannen has suggested
that gift giving is inherently ambiguous in terms of social communication:
If you have a friend who repeatedly picks up the check when you dine
together, is she being generous and sharing her wealth, or is she trying to
flaunt her money and remind you that she has more of it than you do?
... Both interpretations exist at once: connection—she is paying to be
nice—and status—her generosity reminds you that she is richer and makes
you feel obligated. That is why offering to pick up the check is not just
ambiguous but polysemous: It means both at once.76
Presumably it means both to members of either sex. The differences between
the genders has to do with relative difference of focus. Tannen argues that
men in American society, at any rate, tend to focus more on the way a given
interaction establishes relative status while women tend to focus more on the
way it establishes rapport. This empirical disparity may be one of the bases
for Cixous's terminology of "masculine" and "feminine," despite her own
acknowledgments that the same individual may use both kinds of economy.77
Nevertheless, the fact that men and women typically focus on different as-
pects of the gift-giving exchange, and that men more than women tend to
feel burdened, does not indicate that the sexes have different ideas about the
need for reciprocity.
William Ian Miller also analyzes gender differences in American gift-giving
practices, observing, for example, that under the heterosexual norm, "men
... are less likely to shop to buy gifts for men than women are to buy gifts
for women."78 Yet, he does not incorporate gender difference into his con-
tention that "[Gjift exchange supposes a commitment to sociable forms, to
continuing relations, even if the gift is one of the ways we exact deference
and achieve dominance among those with whom we relate."79 Women and


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
63
men may differ in their focus, but Miller implies that both are aware of gifts
as simultaneously status indicators and relational gestures. Similarly, while
discussing the social discomfort involved in various gift-giving situations, he
draws from "the homeliness of the topics" the non-gender-specific conclu-
sion that "the problems inherent in these types of exchanges are not a de-
viation from some normal ideal of smooth-running exchange but are the
problems inherent in normality itself."80
Miller also contends that given the sexist status quo, in which men often
feel that a woman's receiving gifts establishes sexual obligation, women are
unlikely to think of gifts as being outside the cycle of reciprocity:
Women, I would bet, must feel most keenly the intrusiveness, the aggressive
aspect of gifts, every time they are the recipients of gifts from men with
whom they have not already regularized relations to their own satisfac-
tion.81
In American society, at least, Cixous's gendered labels for the two economies
seem to misfire. In The Gay Science, Nietzsche also suggests that women have
been known to take what Cixous calls the "masculine" perspective, assuming
that gift giving obligates the recipient. Indeed, in section 65 he offers a "li-
bidinal" example, that of the type of woman who loses her virginity with the
usually unfulfilled expectation that the man involved will feel great obliga-
tion.82 If Nietzsche's analysis is correct, libidinal economics in nineteenth-
century Europe did not take the form that Cixous calls "feminine."
The Gay Science, however, shows more decisively that Nietzsche sees giving
as thoroughly self-interested and appropriative:
Benefiting and hurting others are ways of exercising one's power upon
others; that is all one desires in such cases.... We benefit and show be-
nevolence to those who are already dependent on us in some way...; we
want to increase their power because in that way we increase ours, or we
want to show them how advantageous it is to be in our power; that way
they will be more satisfied with their condition and more hostile to and
willing to fight against the enemies of our power.83
He goes on to say that benefiting others is the more agreeable route; harming
others "is a sign that we are still lacking power, or it shows a sense of
frustration in the face of this poverty."84 From this point of view, the person
prone to forgiveness would be stronger than the one demanding vengeance,
as Nietzsche later claims in Genealogy; but the seemingly un-self-interested
gesture is only superficially so.
Similarly, Nietzsche contends that the good-natured "whose faces radiate
good will" are as acquisitive as those who are more obviously so:
They are cheered by the sight of another person and .. . their first judgment
is: "I like him." What distinguishes these people is a rapid succession of
the following states: the wish to appropriate, . . . quick appropriation,
delight in their new possession, and action for the benefit of their latest
conquest.85


64
COMIC RELIEF
We have already seen that Nietzsche also characterizes sexual love and the
quest for knowledge, each often taken as a paradigm of selflessness, as mo-
tivated by the desire for acquisition.
Schrift could say that Nietzsche himself has absorbed the masculine econ-
omy—predictably enough, given Nietzsche's historical and cultural back-
ground—but that the feminine economy is a possibility toward which Nietz-
sche gestures. This would, however, be hard to prove or disprove. "Gestures
toward" can encompass any move that can be incorporated into a particular
trajectory; and Schrift himself draws certain moves by Nietzsche into a tra-
jectory that concludes with the positing of the "masculine" and "feminine"
economic alternatives. That leaves us in doubt as to whether Nietzsche would
endorse anything like the image of "feminine" economy as Cixous charac-
terizes it.
Nietzsche's various characterizations of the noble person who "overflows"
provides evidence of the high value that he places on generosity.86 Schrift
cites some of these; and there is no question that he justifies his claim that
Nietzsche associates strength with generosity. The Gay Science itself makes
reference to "being rich enough" for various experiences that do not seem
desirable from the standpoint of crude self-interest. These include defeat and
having secret enemies, a "luxury for which the morality of even elevated
spirits is usually not rich enough."87 Yet this qualification is interesting.
Nietzsche feels that one's capacity for generosity is limited, even if one is a
noble person. The range of possible attitudes toward giving, as Nietzsche
describes it, seems to be more of a continuum than a set of alternatives, and,
significantly, a continuum that does not include complete abandonment of
a sense of self-interest.
Nietzsche clearly feels that obsession with self-interest is undesirable, even
a deficiency of character.88 He does not, however, think that the attitude of
giving without thought of direct repayment is un-self-interested. Indeed, it
would be in one's interests to be less geared to personal advantage than most
people are. Those who do what seems counter to their advantage are noble
because they have a more subtle, more spiritualized notion of what their
advantage is:
Justice.—I'd sooner have people steal from me than be surrounded by
scarecrows and hungry looks; that is my taste. And this is by all means a
matter of taste, nothing more.89
Poor.—He is poor today, but not because one has taken everything away
from him; he has thrown away everything. What is that to him? He is used
to finding things.90
Temporal Considerations
We have already observed how Nietzsche thinks that our standard valuations
evolve historically. More generally, he considers time a crucial factor that


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
65
structures our relationship to our world. Temporality is a condition of all of
our experience, and yet time's role in establishing the character of our per-
spective at any point is rarely appreciated with anything like the specificity
we accord to space, except with respect to making appointments. Time can
be considered either a limiting constraint on any particular perspective or
the sea to be navigated as we move from one to another. Its dual role in
establishing perspectives and facilitating liberation from them is vital to
Nietzsche's perspectivism. The gay science depends on a sensitivity to our
temporal situatedness and the ongoing music of change.91 What we know,
we know at a given time, perhaps only for a certain time; and our knowledge
is vastly more maneuverable when we take time into account.
Awareness of temporality is basic to Nietzsche's design for a rehabilitated
Wissenschaft. In place of the traditional map of knowledge, which has been
sought in the quest of definite, unchanging boundaries, Nietzsche proposes
a map that is more akin to a navigator's chart, enhanced by indications of
the present patterns of currents, even of individual waves.
That time is relevant to Wissenschaft is, in one sense, obvious. Knowledge
(in the sense of things known) has changed over the course of history. But
Nietzsche reminds us that the evolution of knowledge has been more than
a series of discoveries made with a growing pile of information as its goal.
The history of knowledge is a dynamic saga in which knowledge has been
subject to various sweeps of interpretations, waves that submerge other
waves, with resulting movements that accord with still different vectors.
These currents appear in a variety of forms. Often they are simply changes
of taste, exerted by strong individuals:
Every great human being exerts a retroactive force; for his sake all of history
is placed in balance again, and a thousand secrets of the past crawl out of
their hiding places—into his sunshine. There is no way of telling what may
yet become part of history.92
The historical context, however, constrains or facilitates an individual's ef-
fectiveness. In this way, too, the temporal character of human life conditions
perspective, even the perspective of whole peoples:
It is preeminently in the generations and castes that conserve a people that
we encounter such recrudescences of old instincts, while such atavisms are
improbable wherever races, habits, and valuations change too rapidly. For
tempo is as significant for the development of peoples as it is in music....93
Nietzsche insists, however, that the relationship between an age and its way
of thinking may be missed on first inspection. In a particularly fascinating
passage, Nietzsche suggests that an era's tendency to philosophize about suf-
fering may indicate the absence, not the presence, of serious pain.94
The emergence of pessimistic philosophies is by no means a sign of great
and terrible misery. No, these question marks about the value of all life
are put up in ages in which the refinement and alleviation of existence
make even the inevitable mosquito bites of the soul and the body seem


66
COMIC RELIEF
much too bloody and malignant and one is so poor in real experiences of
pain that one would like to consider painful general ideas as suffering of
the first order.95
Nietzsche considers many historical transformations of evaluation to be
gradual developments that few detect. He suggests that revaluation can also
be a conscious choice. He compares this situation to the case of "a wise
gardener" who "places the poor little stream ... in the arms of a nymph and
thus finds a motive for its poverty."96 Here Nietzsche suggests that interpre-
tation and relative emphasis within a configuration of details are means by
which the insights gained through Wissenschaft become artistic.
If we acknowledge the temporal transformation of human values, we re-
contextualize human values in a manner that is incompatible with moral
absolutism. I have already noted Nietzsche's claim that things valued as evil
at one point may later come to be seen as good. New evaluations may also
defy the received word on specific vices and virtues. A time-lapse analysis of
the progress of a virtue may even suggest a reductio ad absurdum for the
case that it is a virtue:
How often I see that blindly raging industriousness does create wealth and
reap honors while at the same time depriving the organs of their subtlety,
which alone would make possible the enjoyment of wealth and honors;
also that this chief antidote to boredom and the passions at the same time
blunts the senses and leads the spirit to resist new attractions.97
Contrarily, one is less likely to interpret a society's decline in terms of vice
if one recognizes it as a historical phase:
The times of corruption are those when the apples fall from the tree: I
mean the individuals, for they carry the seeds of the future and are the
authors of the spiritual colonization and origin of new states and com-
munities. Corruption is merely a nasty word for the autumn of a people.98
Nietzsche's efforts to historicize values are also efforts to relativize them.
I will not discuss Nietzsche's efforts to relativize values temporally, however,
for we will be considering his understanding of the connection between time
and evaluation at greater length in chapter 5. For the moment, I simply note
that both societal and personal values are subject to change, and that the
individual who resists the reigning values of the larger society is often the
instrument of change. The individual is the means of revaluation—and also
the means by which Wissenschaft might be reenvisioned.
Beyond the God's-eye View:
A Defense of Idiosyncrasy
Modern Wissenschaft, according to Nietzsche, has been a hubristic enterprise.
Its aims have been defined by traditional epistemology's ideal of knowledge
as a purely objective, comprehensive picture of reality. Coupled with this


AESTHETIC PERSPECTIVISM
67
epistemological goal, traditional Wissenschaft has sought the perspective that
the Judeo-Christian tradition has attributed to God. Its quest has been a
simultaneous glimpse of the whole, without perspectival limitation. Wissen-
schaft has aimed to formulate a map of reality whose increasing detail ap-
proaches the vision available to God. This aspiration to God's omniscience
is an impossible dream. We can be God-like, but as creators, not as knowers.
As Babette Babich puts the point:
The presumption of a transcendental epistemic locus contradicts what, for
Nietzsche, is the essentially perspectival nature of knowledge.. .. The failing
standard is a god-like perspective (i.e., for Nietzsche, in the same theolog-
ical parallel, an omniscient, transcendent knowledge of true and false). In
its stead, we have merely our ecophysiological interests: our experience as
perspectival beings. Judgment is possible—or, better, unavoidable—on this
basis, but such judgment is ineluctably interpretive."
None of us has a map of the world as seen by God—thus one might
summarize Nietzsche's complaint against the traditional "knowledge" game.
This is far from a reason to dispense with maps, however. God needs no
map because he is devoid of perspective; he is everywhere. Precisely because
we are perspectival individuals, we do need maps. Nietzsche urges us to begin
our mapmaking differently—not with traditional epistemology, or even
Kant's epistemological framework, with its acknowledgment that the range
of our knowledge is restricted, but with an appreciation of the situatedness
of all of our knowledge and our creative cartographical role.
Nietzsche insists that our perceptions, and therefore our knowledge, are
restricted by a variety of perspectival factors: projection, distance, motivation,
and the grids we use to analyze. Knowledge is always contextualized by one's
era, by personal sensibility, by what one has learned, by what one lacks. This
ultimately vindicates the oddity of the individual. Indeed, the idiosyncrasies
of individuals are what allow our cultural maps to develop:
What is originality? To see something that has no name as yet and hence
cannot be mentioned although it stares us all in the face. The way men
usually are, it takes a name to make something visible for them.—Those
with originally have for the most part also assigned names.100
No individual's perspective is the final word. Human experience, as Genesis
observes, involves assigning names. Yet Nietzsche adds an important adden-
dum: the names, and the maps they help to formulate, keep changing.
Nietzsche opposes his perspectivism, which acknowledges change, to mod-
ern epistemology, which does not. The latter's pretension to rigor, in Nietz-
sche's view, is only a sign of rigor mortis, inflexibility as a symptom of its
inability to sustain life. Nietzsche does not, however, deny the usefulness of
our cultural maps. Quite to the contrary, the maps that result from the
pooled perspectives of individuals are vastly more detailed, and more appro-
priate to the varying circumstances in which we use them.
The presumption of Nietzsche's approach, despite its elitism, is that there
is no one from whom we cannot learn something. And this is what one


68
COMIC RELIEF
would expect from a Wissenschaft performed gaily, in recognition that one's
own view is fragmentary, laughable when mistaken for "the truth." Yet
Nietzsche also thinks that the tragic Wissenschaft of our era, with its sense
of urgency in seeking knowledge, presupposes some awareness that it cannot
dispense with any individual's perspective.
Modern Wissenschaft is hubristic just because it cannot rest content until
it has absorbed every perspective. Nietzsche characterizes "the sigh of the
search for knowledge," which says:
Oh, my greed! There is no selflessness in my soul but only an all-coveting
self that would like to appropriate many individuals as so many additional
pairs of eyes and hands—a self that would like to bring back the whole
past, too, and that will not lose anything that it could possibly pos-
sess. ... "»
The search for knowledge in its current, tragic mode aims at the viewpoint
of God, an idea itself drawn from awareness of multiple perspectives.
How far apart are these projects? To "gaily" supplement one's vision with
more and more perspectives externally seems to require the same effort as
seeking to seize all of them. The difference is mainly a matter of how one
considers oneself as knower. The tragic knower ensures a sense of individual
insufficiency by attempting to contain the whole within the part. The self
cannot literally consume the whole. This obvious fact can be interpreted as
an obstacle to one's ends and engender frustration and despair. Or to see
oneself in this light, with the proper distance, might be seen instead as funny.
One might dwell on the ludicrousness of the endeavor of trying to consume
the whole, for example, by considering what one might take for the resulting
indigestion, or what beverage might help one wash it down.
Again, the alternative perspectives of tragedy and comedy involve radically
different assessment of one's own goals, even though one's specific tasks are
not, in kind, so different. Stressing the individual's position in the project of
knowing, Nietzsche reminds us that it can be seen as limiting to the point
of hopelessness, or as a unique vantage for gaining insight and aesthetic
delight. The former is the probable consequence of refusing the insight that
one is, as an individual, incongruously ill-suited to the task of the true cosmic
vision, and therefore laughable. The latter is a possibility for the knower who
accepts this incongruity and continues the enterprise, not as striving against
the gods, but as part of the dream.
Nietzsche begins Book II by considering some of the motives behind the
specific dreams we dream. After remarking on his agenda as debunker of the
aspirations of contemporary Wissenschaft, we will consider his observations
on fantasy as it conditions our comprehension of our world.


Why Not Call It "Deconstruction"?
Nietzsche's reversals within The Gay Science suggest resonance with strategies
described by some as "deconstructive." Rudolf Kuenzli, for example, applies
the term to Nietzsche's maneuvers when he argues that they are central to
the humor of the book.
What is the difference between a note in Nietzsche's notebook and the
same note included in one of his works? Nietzsche composed The Gay
Science from his numerous notes. But the notes included in The Gay Science
are caught up in the laughter, in the movement of deconstruction, whereas
any note in the notebook is isolated and unambiguous, since its meaning
has not been put into question. We may recognize now the danger and
the problem involved in quoting not only from Nietzsche's notes but from
his published works as well, since by quoting we "freeze" the meaning of
a passage, which is kept in play through the overall process of his move-
ment of "ebb and flow," forgetting and remembering.1
It has become fashionable to term literary challenges to basic cultural
assumptions "deconstruction," especially when their rhetorical force depends
on a strategy of undermining presuppositions by displaying inherent tensions
within them. I abstain from using this term, however, in my discussion of
The Gay Science. Nietzsche does employ ongoing processes of self-irony and
double takes; and perhaps some who call him "deconstructive" mean only
to indicate these techniques. One problem with the term is that "deconstruc-
tion" has been used in so many ways that it no longer has a clear meaning,
to the extent that it ever had. (Indeed, haute couture applies the term to
clothing flaunting jagged seams.) But insofar as it has a fairly common usage
in literary criticism, I believe the term is misleading when applied to Nietz-
sche.
One reason for resisting "deconstruction" as a description of Nietzsche's
method is that the term suggests a more radical departure from traditional
efforts in philosophy than Nietzsche seems to intend. Nietzsche describes his
own discussions of transformative processes as according with the method
69
INTERLUDE 2


70
COMIC RELIEF
of traditional philosophy, although he doubts that this has been much rec-
ognized.
A philosopher who has traversed many kinds of health, and keeps travers-
ing them, has passed through an equal number of philosophies; he simply
cannot keep from transposing his states every time into the most spiritual
form and distance: this art of transfiguration is philosophy.2
The same sentiment is expressed in Beyond Good and Evil, where he describes
philosophy "so far" as "a kind of involuntary and unconscious memoir" of
its author.3 All philosophers have been instructive eccentrics, Nietzsche tells
us; he is different only in being conscious of this role.
To the extent that "deconstruction" specifies a methodology (and is not
merely a fancy term for any analysis), it has come to connote a primarily
negative project of reversing traditional binaries, and displacing the force of
the distinctions they were initially designed to indicate.4 Deconstruction de-
stabilizes to undercut. Nietzsche, by contrast, does not attempt to dismiss
the importance of making basic distinctions in philosophizing; instead he
aims to complicate our thinking about them.5 Indeed, Nietzsche insists, with
respect to his philosophical effort, that destruction is essential to the process
of creation. His emphasis, in other words, is on construction, not the dis-
memberment suggested by "deconstruction":
How foolish it would be to suppose that one only needs to point out this
origin and misty shroud of delusion in order to destroy the world that
counts for real, so-called "reality." We can destroy only as creators.—But
let us not forget this either: it is enough to create new names and esti-
mations and probabilities in order to create in the long run new "things."6
(Is deconstruction a new "thing," in this sense?)
Schrift concurs in this view, even while defending the use of the term
"deconstruction" in application to Nietzsche.
Nietzsche's critique of philosophical language ... takes the form of a de-
construction, an Ab-bau of these systems, a dismantling or undoing of their
epistemological constructions by tracing them back to their metaphorical
or rhetorical roots. The success of the Nietzschean de-construction will not
allow the philosopher to escape from within the nets of language, however;
but facilitating such an escape was never his intention. Rather, by revealing
the hope of a literal designation and an adequate, natural relation between
words and things to be unrealizable, Nietzsche directs himself toward the
critical task of demonstrating that philosophers do not know what they
think they "know."7
Certainly, this is a traditional project; and the employment of rhetoric in this
effort is evident already in Plato's Socrates. Moreover, while Nietzsche may
attempt to undermine ill-founded philosophical distinctions, this is not his
ultimate aim. He makes commonplace distinctions more versatile, more
prone to reverberation. His goal, even when he destabilizes, is not collapse
as such, but the possibility of new creations occasioned by this collapse.


WHY NOT CALL IT "DECONSTRUCTION"?
Jl
My most fundamental reason for thinking that "deconstruction" is a mis-
leading term for Nietzsche's techniques in The Gay Science is that it obscures
the humor of the book. "Deconstruction" is simply too serious and pedantic
a term for describing such a funny book. Deconstruction, for all its alleged
playfulness, is a controlled, strategic enterprise, with the (perhaps naive) in-
tent of systematically undercutting structures that have supported Western
thought. The result may be that one is left in a field that is largely unmapped,
and is therefore threatening. But the procedure itself is systematic, akin to
that used to dismantle an explosive device.
Nietzsche, by contrast, is eager for explosions.8 (He claims in Ecce Homo,
for example, "I am no man; I am dynamite."9) While his efforts are delib-
erate, his employment of the strategies indicated by Kuenzli is risky. Those
operating within the deconstructive method may see Nietzsche's risk taking
as a demonstration of the inherent "undecidability" of his texts, and perhaps
of any texts whatsoever. But Nietzsche's stunts can be analyzed less anach-
ronistically in terms of a more traditional exploitation of undecidability—
the generation of humor.
Sander Gilman observes that Nietzsche was much influenced by Schopen-
hauer's theory of humor. Schopenhauer contended that the effect of humor
is achieved when the following occurs: A feature that contingently charac-
terizes a certain object is taken to be a feature of some universal category to
which the object belongs. Next, this feature is attributed to an inappropriate
object that also falls under that category. Then, finally, one concludes that
the subsumption of the feature under the general category is inappropriate,
by recognizing that the particular characterization is ludicrous. "Thus the
more heterogeneous the incongruence between the universal and the real,
the more marked (and cruder) is the comic effect."10
For example, someone in the habit of saying "excuse me" when acciden-
tally brushing an arm against someone is ludicrous in applying the principle
involved when brushing an arm against a chair, and even more so when
apologizing to a mannequin. One is more likely to mistake a mannequin for
a person because of the intended resemblance. Therefore, it is even more
conspicuous that the apologizer has subsumed the mannequin under the
category of "person (to be treated politely)" because of sharing some ap-
parent features. Similarly, if children hear loud grumbling sounds coming
from a house, a child from that home may feel embarrassed if the grumbler
is its mother. The situation becomes ludicruous, however, if the grumbler
turns out actually to be a parrot. The principle that one's mother's fussing
reflects poorly on a person is misapplied to a bird. Humor, on Schopen-
hauer's theory, is always a recognition of the limitations of one's conceptu-
alizing, and in particular that one has forgotten that concepts extend only
so far.
Far from insisting that we should dismantle our conceptual scaffolding in
rigorous deconstructionist fashion, Nietzsche encourages us to continue us-
ing our concepts, but with awareness of their arbitrariness and evolving
meaning. Nietzsche goes out of his way, as we shall see, to indicate the ways


72
COMIC RELIEF
in which abandoning our arbitrary assumptions may be hazardous. I suspect
that he would urge us to laugh about deconstruction, too, as one more layer
of our conceptualizing. Deconstruction, after all, poses Nietzsche as a gazer
into the abysses of meaning. Surely the deconstructionist movement, as much
as any other scholarly posture, deserves Lawrence Hatab's reminder that
[a]s philosophers and teachers ... we have had to decode, modify, expli-
cate, excavate, reconstruct, deconstruct, analyze, synthesize, push, pull and
drag out the "argument," the sense of it all. We have done this. But why
didn't Nietzsche do what we do?... Nietzsche's manner of writing was not
an accident; it was very much a part of his message. Therein lies our own
peculiar "tragic" dilemma: When we "translate" Nietzsche into our pro-
fessional philosophical agenda, we do what must be done, but in so doing,
we bring to ruin something special and vital, something equally necessary,
equally "true." It seems we must "murder to dissect." ... Therein, too, lies
a comedy.11
My own efforts, too, are bound to appear ludicrous in this light. Am I
not, after all, attempting to explain jokes? Yes, inevitably. At best, however,
I am hoping to make Nietzsche's humor a bit more visible. I think that it
has often been obscured by scholarly apparatus; hence, I consider this book
a short course in remedial clowning.12


Woman, All-Too-Woman?
Some Dubious Feminist Complaints
against Nietzsche
In his novel When Nietzsche Wept, Irwin Yalom reiterates the common por-
trait of Nietzsche as sexist tiber alles. Much as the quip "Isn't business ethics
a contradiction in terms?" ubiquitously accosts philosophers involved in that
subdiscipline, "What's a nice girl like you doing studying a misogynist like
that?" has haunted my career in Nietzsche scholarship. I have often wondered
whether the point is about me or about Nietzsche. A sympathetic but jovial
colleague perplexed me further by remarking, when he heard I was writing
on this topic, "Yes, that must be interesting subject matter when you aren't
even shallow." Yes, Nietzsche did say that women, far from deep, were not
even shallow.1 But does this remark prove him a sexist? Are we even certain
what it means?
Certainly, Book II of The Gay Science complicates any appraisal of Nietz-
sche's alleged sexism. I will proceed to consider his passages on women in
that book, suggesting along the way reasons why at least some readings of
these as sexist should be rejected. More positively, I contend that these pas-
sages urge a reassessment of the relationship between men and women that
can be seen as a contribution toward feminist theory (if an unwitting one),
and that one of Nietzsche's intended impacts on his male readers is to initiate
a radical revolution in their thinking about women, sometimes by using
humor as a tactic.2
I use the term gender advisedly in this context, for I argue that Nietzsche
made the distinction that is common in feminist discussion between sex (the
biological potential to play one role rather than another in reproduction)
and gender (the contingently assigned roles that a society attaches to those
who are biologically male or female). Nietzsche, I argue, urged his readers
to recognize the contingency of gender roles and to consider the desirability
of changing them.
73
^FOUR


74
COMIC RELIEF
I can hardly begin to consider these suggestions, however, without con-
fronting the allegation that Nietzsche was a paradigmatic sexist. Although
many of the most important names in Western philosophy have been singled
out for feminist censure, Nietzsche is often held up as an exemplar of all
that is misogynistic, both in the philosophical tradition and within patriarchy
generally. Nietzsche is identified as a misogynist for a variety of reasons. He
is seen as an essentialist, an opponent of women's rights, an enthusiast of
masculinist ideals, and an advocate of male domination. While I think that
none of these attributions is entirely apt, I agree with Nietzsche's feminist
critics that he was, in certain respects, a sexist. At least sometimes he was
too willing to rest content with truisms about women and to vent personal
rage in the guise of philosophy.
That being said, I find some of the attacks on Nietzsche both unfair and
unfortunate. I am not the only feminist to think so. Deborah Bergoffen,
Maryanne Bertram, Maudemarie Clark, and Sarah Kofman have all suggested
that certain of Nietzsche's views offer starting points for feminist theorizing.3
I will describe some attacks made on Nietzsche by certain other feminists,
however, to indicate what I take to be pitfalls that feminists would do best
to avoid. I focus on articles by Carol Diethe and Ellen Kennedy as represen-
tative of a rather common feminist outlook on Nietzsche, the view that his
philosophy is based on sexism of an unmitigated sort.
In her article "Nietzsche and the Woman Question," for example, Diethe
criticizes Nietzsche in a number of ways that I think are unwarranted. Con-
sider, for instance, her complaint that Nietzsche's conception of the will to
power entails an affirmation of the domination of women:
If we take his key concept, the will to power, we find that it is so gender
based that it practically comes to represent the archetypal phallic symbol.
[J. A.] Bernstein describes it as a phenomenon which includes the "will to
violate" so that the "feeling of power" (Machtgefuhl) is generated, yet when
he goes on to mention "Nietzsche's desire to avoid reducing the will to
power to sexuality" he fails to realise that his own critique has relied heavily
on the language of rape and the whole area of male dominance of women
which stretches behind words such as "violation."4
Diethe simply accepts Bernstein's characterization of Nietzsche's view as
accurate, even if she disputes his choice of terminology. She may be right in
her claim that the will to power can be read as a phallic symbol, although I
would want to know what assumptions are built in to the latter notion. Even
if so, it is not obvious why Nietzsche's employment of the concept is nec-
essarily sexist. More generally, Diethe's passage involves a move that is far
too prevalent in feminist writings on Nietzsche: the tendency to reduce Nietz-
sche's philosophy to a few obsessions and to take these as his basic point in
all his remarks about women.
One common counterargument to feminist critiques of Nietzsche is that
he was simply blinded by the sexism of his era.5 Feminist critics rightly
complain that a thinker should not be excused for common prejudice just


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
75
because it is common prejudice. Nevertheless, the recognition that Nietzsche
accepted some features of his society's sexist perspective on women does not
warrant the judgment that he endorsed every one of his era's sexist senti-
ments. Diethe, for example, engages in this type of conflation when she
equates Nietzsche's position with Schopenhauer's: "As Brann has pointed
out, Nietzsche echoes Schopenhauer's ideas on women 'wortgetreu' [word for
word] as the following passage demonstrates. Written by Schopenhauer, it
could just as easily have been written by Nietzsche...." Diethe goes on to
cite Schopenhauer, beginning with "Because basically women are only there
to propagate the race, and they fulfil themselves thereby, they live life more
as a sexual partner than as an individual... ."6 The counterfactual about the
authorship of this passage is unfalsifiable, but blaming Nietzsche for a passage
he did not write is certainly inappropriate.
Ellen Kennedy, not quite so unfairly, insists in her article "Nietzsche:
Women as Untermensch," that "Nietzsche really does have nothing more to
offer ... than the common prejudices of his age and sex."7 Her conclusion,
however, combines oversimplification of Nietzsche's philosophy with a wor-
risome insinuation that his work can be appropriately read only as a defense
of the patriarchy:
Both the ground for women's subordination in women's biology and
Nietzsche's coherently masculine state of adventurers and warriors are
founded on a Darwinistically derived master-sex. In light of this the pre-
occupation with Nietzsche as anti- or philo-Semitic seems rather anti-
quated; his ideal state is not "Teutonic," nor is Nietzsche a racialist in the
earlier sense. But he is, in our sense of it, the founder of peculiarly modern
patriarchy and the inventor of one of the crassest and most subtle misog-
ynies: "The enormous expectation in sexual love and the sense of shame
in this expectation spoils all perspective for women from the start."8
The passage from Nietzsche is not obviously sexist or crass. If one reads
this as a psychological interpretation of the social pressures that constrain
women's outlook on sexuality, one can see it as sympathetic—and a recog-
nition of the social cultivation of certain attitudes in women. Nietzsche's
remark is not so distant from more recent feminist descriptions of the in-
ternal obstacles to feminist liberation that women confront. Isn't Nietzsche
close to hinting that love is a sexist plot?
Aside from her questionable reading of Nietzsche's passage, Kennedy's
comment is disturbing for its implication that, currently, sexism is a problem
of vastly more importance than anti-Semitism. The insinuation that women
and Jews are in competition for the status of most oppressed is philosoph-
ically as well as morally suspect. I find it hard to believe that the mechanisms
that support prejudice are essentially different in one case than in another.
Indeed, from a political standpoint, I think that competition among victims
of prejudice only diverts energy that might more profitably be spent fight-
ing it.
Kennedy's comment strikes me as symptomatic of a dangerous tendency
sometimes evident within the women's movement—and probably also within


76
COMIC RELIEF
any other movement aimed at overcoming oppression. I will term this ten-
dency the Parrot News Syndrome,9 in reference to one of the skits presented
on Monty Python's Flying Circus.10 This skit was devoted to "Parrot News,"
a news broadcast that made mention of a number of events around the world
and ended each report with the remark, "No parrots were involved." The
target of satire here was the wide-ranging phenomenon of parochial news
coverage, which in the United States typically involves concluding interna-
tional reports with some mention of how a few Americans were involved.
The Parrot News Syndrome in feminist discussion is the tendency to be-
lieve that women and responses to women should be foregrounded whenever
possible. My counterproposal accords with Freud's comment that sometimes
a cigar is just a cigar. Sometimes, I contend, it is as inappropriate to fore-
ground women and their concerns as it has traditionally been inappropriate
to push women consistently into the background.
The Parrot News Syndrome is evident in Diethe's account of Nietzsche's
humor:
Although Schopenhauer and Nietzsche are clearly wrong in their assump-
tions about women's natural instincts they can, to a certain extent, be
respected for their views on the importance of childbearing, since so many
women shared similar views (although, one must hasten to add, they often
acted with a false consciousness of their own objective position in society).
What is unforgivable in Nietzsche is his use of the sexist joke.11
The idea that Schopenhauer's and Nietzsche's opinions should be respected
just to the extent that they coincide with women's opinions is a case of
inappropriately foregrounding women's opinions. Especially given the asser-
tion that many of the women of Nietzsche's era were blinded by "false con-
sciousness," their opinions are unlikely to be the best basis for assessing the
merits of his or Schopenhauer's ideas.
As for sexist jokes, Nietzsche has to use them, according to Diethe, be-
cause
sexual discourse is vital to Nietzsche's canon; he constructs a virile male
and must keep women servile. Sexist jokes are an efficient tool to use to
that end. Even when he is talking about men, there is a hidden discourse
on women beneath the text.12
I agree with the claim that Nietzsche's remarks about men are implicitly
about women, although I do not think that Nietzsche has a consistent hidden
discourse, or even that he consistently opposes women and men. I mention
this passage here because it characterizes Nietzsche's humor as a sexist tool.
I want to challenge this position.
As an example of sexist humor, Diethe refers to a remark Nietzsche makes
when he complains that "abortive women" use the feminist movement as
an attack on "women who have turned out well":
By raising themselves higher, as "woman in herself," as the "higher
woman," as a female "idealist," they want to lower the level of the general


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
77
rank of woman; and there is no surer means for that than higher education,
slacks, and political voting—cattle rights.13
Certainly, Nietzsche's remark is not one that most feminists would ad-
mire. But why does Diethe read this as a sexist joke? In the first place, why
see it as a joke at all? Diethe obviously considers it "not funny"—but that
surely does not imply that it is a joke. Second, why is this seen as a sexist
joke? Diethe's gloss is as follows:
Women's education is downgraded by being given equal importance with
women's clothing, and women's votes are discounted by the reference to
cattle. To be fair to Nietzsche, he had some uncomplimentary things to
say about herd men and indeed about male scholars, but the sexual slur is
reserved for women.14
But this simply is not true. Nietzsche is no more referring to women when
he speaks of cattle than a University of Pennsylvania student (allegedly) re-
ferred to blacks when he called his noisy neighbors "water buffaloes."15 Nietz-
sche fairly consistently refers to cattle, paradigmatic herd members, when he
discusses voting, whether or not he is commenting on female suffrage. In
section 174 of The Gay Science, for example, he describes the right to vote as
a herd phenomenon:
Apart.—Parliamentarianism—that is, public permission to choose between
five basic political opinions—flatters and wins the favor of all those who
would like to seem independent and individual, as if they fought for their
opinions. Ultimately, however, it is indifferent whether the herd is com-
manded to have one opinion or permitted to have five. Whoever deviates
from the five public opinions and stands apart will always have the whole
herd against him.16
Nietzsche's association of voting with cattle—presumably cattle of both
sexes—is even more unmistakable in section 368:
No one brings along the finest sense of his art to the theater, nor does the
artist who works for the theater. There one is common people, audience,
herd, female, pharisee, voting cattle, democrat, neighbor, fellow man...
the neighbor reigns, one becomes a mere neighbor.17
Although I think that Diethe is wrong to claim that Nietzsche's humor is
typically sexist, I think that she is right to see humor as an essential element
of his rhetoric. Nietzsche's humor, I contend, indicates a much more radical
approach to gender consciousness than is evident in the thought of most of
his philosophical contemporaries.18
Diethe's assumption that Nietzsche's "cattle" comment is sexist may reveal
that she takes it for granted that the right to vote is desirable (a view that I
share). On the assumption that Nietzsche is not disparaging the vote, she
may reason, he must be disparaging women. Nietzsche, however, does dis-
parage the vote. Men may vote by virtue of sexist privilege, but Nietzsche
does not consider the vote's being desired to be evidence for its desirability.


78
COMIC RELIEF
Unfortunately, feminists sometimes move too quickly from the observa-
tion that men do something by virtue of male privilege to the view that the
practice is necessarily desirable. I continue the herd imagery by terming this
strategy the Green Grass Syndrome, referring to the adage that the grass
belonging to others is always greener than one's own. This syndrome is
sometimes bolstered by the corollary that whatever women have traditionally
done while excluded from male privilege is to be devalued.
Diethe's reading of another of Nietzsche's "sexist jokes" at least borders
on this perspective. She cites his comment about feminist attempts to "en-
lighten men" about the feminine essence in Beyond Good and Evil: "Unless
a woman seeks a new adornment for herself in that way—I do think adorn-
ing herself is part of the Eternal Feminine?—she surely wants to inspire fear
of herself—perhaps she seeks mastery."19 Diethe sees this passage as a joke
referring to Goethe's "eternal feminine," but she does not find the parody
elevating:
This type of sexist joke, by which the mystique of Goethe's "ewig Weibliche"
(sufficiently problematic in itself) is downgraded to include woman's love
of finery, indicates Nietzsche's own lack of confidence. He is unable to
construct a proper theory to justify "das Aufklaren" as a male province
and retreats behind a jibe which pulls the reader's attention in another
direction (Gretchen). This provides a veneer of respectability whilst at the
same time seriously distorting Goethe's intention. The final comment on
Herrschaft is poles apart from Goethe's portrayal of woman's power. Nietz-
sche's attitude towards woman is in turns aggressive and defensive, and his
only solution to this ambivalence is the philosophical construct of the mas-
terly man.20
Why is adornment necessarily degrading? Nietzsche suggests elsewhere
that adorning oneself (whether one is male or female) is crucial to human
relationships' achieving their potential. His character Zarathustra announces
(to a seemingly male audience), "You cannot groom yourself too beautifully
for your friend: for you shall be to him an arrow and a longing for the
overman."21 Moreover, in the passage from Beyond Good and Evil, Nietzsche
inserts a question mark at the end of his parenthetic remark—"I do think
adorning herself is part of the Eternal Feminine?"—a gesture that suggests
that he is far from certain what to make of feminine adornment.22 And isn't
Nietzsche's inquiry a legitimate question about the gesture some members
of one sex are making toward the other?23 Nietzsche may be accused of being
heterosexist in this passage, since he considers the possibility that the gesture
might be used to gain some advantage in the game of attraction between the
sexes; but he does not seem to assume, as does Diethe herself, that a woman's
interest in adornment is necessarily trivial or, when used as an instrument
of power, pathetic.24
One assumption that Diethe and Kennedy share is that Nietzsche held a
stable perspective on women; and perhaps this assumption is the root of
their oversimplification of his views. Kennedy remarks, "In Nietzsche's case,
it is not an unfair conclusion to say that on the question of women, his


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
79
refusal of us was as radical and thorough-going as it was consistent with the
inherited prejudice of centuries of philosophers before him."25 Diethe simi-
larly comments, "Nietzsche's ideas on women ... might well be unfortunate,
but they are not an aberration:.. . [H]is views on female sexuality and gender
division are crucial to his thesis and remain constant throughout his work."26
Significantly, neither pays much attention to The Gay Science in formulating
this assessment. Kennedy does not refer to it at all; Diethe's article mentions
only a fragment from the fifth book, added in 1887, five years after the central
discussion of women was published.
Considering The Gay Science, I concur with Sister Prudence Allen, who,
surveying the range of Nietzsche's comments on women, concludes that they
exhibit a number of specifiable tensions: "The internal incoherency in Nietz-
sche's theory resulting from this judgment suggests that Nietzsche's tension
about women of the status quo did not flow from a sustained thinking about
the subject."27 Allen notes in particular the openness evident in The Gay
Science, and Nietzsche's far less flexible attitude after the "perceived betrayal"
of Lou Salome, one of the few women toward whom he displayed romantic
interest.28
Who knows what different directions Nietzsche's thought might have taken
if he and Lou Salome had been able to form a long-lasting and deep re-
lationship? His writing about women before and during their relationship
opens the door to new possibilities in woman's identity; immediately after
the break the door is slammed shut.29
Allen concludes, "The potential dialectic of contraries found in Nietzsche's
various forms of tension about women never achieved its full potential."30
Nietzsche's strategy in The Gay Science, I submit, suggests how promising, if
short-lived, that potential actually was.
How Does Art Pertain to Women?
Nietzsche's remarks on women in The Gay Science have rarely been seen as
anything out of the ordinary. Indeed, when they have been considered in
themselves, they have most often been dismissed as low points in the book,
reflecting a basically hostile stance.31 Nietzsche's remarks on women in the
Book II of The Gay Science occur in a context that is largely devoted to art
and the realm of appearance. This might be seen as an indication that women
are a part of the "decorative" side of life, lacking substance but pleasant,
given their own interest in prettifying themselves.
Yet The Gay Science presents an exciting entree into gender theory. Let
us consider the first of its forays into this territory, section 59:
When we love a woman, we easily conceive a hatred for nature on account
of all the repulsive natural functions to which every woman is subject. We
prefer not to think of all this; but when our soul touches on these matters
for once, it shrugs as it were and looks contemptuously at nature: we feel


8o
COMIC RELIEF
insulted; nature seems to encroach on our possessions, and with the pro-
fanest hands at that. Then we refuse to pay heed to physiology and decree
secretly: "I want to hear nothing about the fact that a human being is
something more than soul and form." "The human being under the skin"
is for all lovers a horror and unthinkable, a blasphemy against God and
love.32
This passage is certainly dated, and its rather disgusted tone seems in
keeping with the disparaging remarks by some of Nietzsche's contemporar-
ies.33 Were the reaction that Nietzsche describes really as fundamental as his
tone suggests, modern-day fathers would resist being present at the births of
their children much more strenuously than many do. The passage would also
be hard to square with the romanticism of American youth in the late 19605,
who simultaneously celebrated "love" and revamped the reigning dress and
grooming code to acknowledge much more visibly the physical actualities of
women's bodily experience. Nietzsche's squeamishness here recalls that of
Ruskin, who reportedly abstained from intercourse throughout his life be-
cause he was so shocked to discover that his wife had pubic hair.
Does this passage represent Nietzsche's conception of women? Even read-
ing no farther, we can see that Nietzsche is discussing the attitudes of men,
not women. "We" means "we men" in this passage. Nietzsche is describing
the kind of idealized eyes of love that might lead a man to insist that his
beloved is exempt from the physical conditions that a more scientific eye
would unquestionably acknowledge. Nietzsche's point is that when motivated
by desire to see the world a certain way, an individual will deny the obvious.
Nietzsche goes on to describe the mental gyrations that the loving man
will perform to keep his beloved unsullied in his imagination. He presents
these gyrations, however, as an analogy. This situation is comparable, Nietz-
sche argues, to that of the theist who denies natural laws because they seem
incompatible with the omnipotence of God. Following on Book I of The Gay
Science, which dealt largely with the status of knowledge and belief, in section
59 he is still discussing epistemological topics.
Now, however, Nietzsche is confronting his reader more personally than
in Book I. By mentioning women he reminds each (presumably male) reader
of his own unwillingness to consider his (presumably female) beloved from
a purely scientific point of view. The woman who appears at the beginning
of this section functions to arouse self-reflection in the male reader. The
claims of Book I about the dubiousness of human "knowledge" are now
brought close to home. Indeed, by the end of section 59, Nietzsche describes
even the current "scientific" individual as "moonstruck and God-struck."
"We somnambulists of the day! We artists!" he remarks.34 Including himself
among those ensconced in dreams more than realities, he continues describ-
ing the status of knowledge in his era.
The (supposedly male) reader is unlikely to muster much resistance to
this assault, for the opening two sections of Book II, which directly precede
section 59, have set him up. The first, which we briefly considered in chapter
i, challenges philosophical realists, who "hint that the world really is the way


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
8l
it appears" to them. These realists are "far too similar to an artist in love"
who endeavors to enhance what is loved by means of fantasy. This is the
context in which Nietzsche taunts, "Subtract the phantasm. ... If you can!"35
Human beings, he insists, are incapable of eliminating their own fantastic
contributions to the way that things appear.
Worse yet, Nietzsche argues in the section that follows, we all take ap-
pearances to be the essence.
This has given me the greatest trouble and still does: to realize that what
things are called is incomparably more important than what they are. The
reputation, name, and appearance, the usual measure and weight of a
thing,—what it counts for— ... all this grows from generation unto gen-
eration, merely because people believe in it, until it gradually grows to be
part of the thing and turns into its very body. What at first was appearance
becomes in the end, almost invariably, the essence and is effective as such.36
We are all practicing realists, Nietzsche argues, imagining that we see the
essence of things when we merely see how they look to ourselves. Moreover,
mere awareness will not stop our fantasies.
To follow these two passages with several sections on women—indeed,
several sections on the ways that women appear—is rhetorically provocative.
Nietzsche has already included the reader among the benighted realists,
thereby challenging the reader to self-examination. He also preempts the
reader's first line of defense: merely admitting that one takes appearance for
reality does not prevent one from being bamboozled. The reader is thus in
the uncomfortable position of being attacked without ready recourse when
the discussions of women begin. Already steered into self-analysis by the
previous sections, the (male) reader presumed by Nietzsche is now given a
specific example of the self-deception that has so far been described ab-
stractly. Nietzsche can expect that the reader will find the topic of male
fantasies about women uncomfortably personal.37 Whatever else, Nietzsche
expects a response.
Nietzsche continues this strategy in section 60, where he again discusses
male fantasies about women. He slightly varies the image of the previous
section, now describing the man who
stands in the midst of his own noise, in the midst of his own surf of plans
and projects.... [T]hen he is apt also to see quiet, magical beings gliding
past him and to long for their happiness and seclusion: women. He almost
thinks that his better self dwells there among the women....
Again, Nietzsche describes the appearance of woman to the man in question
as a dream constructed by virtue of a particular motive, this time a desire
to escape his own noise:
Yet! Yet! Noble enthusiast, even on the most beautiful sailboat there is a
lot of noise, and unfortunately much small and petty noise. The magic and
the most powerful effect of women is, in philosophical language, action at
a distance, actio in distans; but this requires first of all and above all—
distance.3"


82
COMIC RELIEF
One might interpret the characterization of "beautiful sailboats" with
"much small and petty noise" as a sexist jibe. The next section (section 61),
however, urges a double take. Here Nietzsche describes the ancient view that
friendship is higher than self-sufficiency. Following on the previous section,
this passage has the effect of qualifying the suggestion that men should main-
tain distance from women. Although section 61 does not explicitly discuss
women, the reader has reason to think that Nietzsche means to include them;
for Book I contains a passage that calls for friendship as a sublimation of
sexual love:
Here and there on earth we may encounter a kind of continuation of love
in which this possessive craving of two people for each other gives way to
a new desire and lust for possession—a shared higher thirst for an ideal
above them. But who knows such love? Who has experienced it? Its right
name is friendship.39
