Nietzsche does not describe the friendship emerging from love as an exclu-
sively male affair, even if he did take the homosexual relationships of Athens
as a primary model for friendship elsewhere.40 Indeed, his description seems
entirely open-ended, deliberately including women as potential candidates
for friendship.
In the sections that follow, Nietzsche offers a number of vignettes or
generalizations from vignettes, mostly involving relationships between the
sexes, and making rather acute if surprising observations about various
women's motivations.41 Section 62 proclaims that love forgives the lover even
lust. Kaufmann translates this as "his lust," suggesting that a woman is for-
giving a man's lust. The German is noncommittal, however, with respect to
the imaged sexes of lover and beloved. Moreover, the direction of the lust is
unspecified; it might be directed toward or away from the person doing the
forgiving.
Section 63 compares "a musical mood" to a woman's reveries concerning
love and the religious congregation's gathering in churches. How are these
comparable? Sultry winds inspire each. The section offers examples to bear
out Nietzsche's repeated insistence that much human behavior follows phys-
ical stimulation. Although the woman's reveries are described more as phys-
ical response than as intellectual activity, she is treated no differently in this
regard than the church member or the emotionally inspired artist, male or
female.
Section 64 describes "old women" as taking a superficial stance toward
experience as a matter of etiquette. Although Nietzsche's preface did not
appear until the second edition of The Gay Science, this passage is reminiscent
of Nietzsche's characterization of himself there as one who has become su-
perficial out of profundity, thanks to the wear of his experiences.42 Section
65 analyzes the plight of "a noble type of woman" who expresses deep de-
votion by sacrificing her virtue to a man who feels no devotion in response.
These two sections are especially striking in that they reinterpret in sympa-
thetic terms female behavior that is commonly denigrated by men. Nietzsche


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
83
suggests motivations that his primarily male readers are likely to understand,
even respect. The old woman is presented as no fool; the fallen woman is
presented as a heroine.
Section 66 describes a feminine maneuver that it attributes to "alien
Frauen." Allen Frauen can be rendered "all women," as Kaufmann translates
it. Nietzsche's term for women in this, as in most of these psychological
vignettes, is the relatively respectful Frau, which typically means a married
woman.43 Section 66 is excessively general, but Nietzsche's comments seem
most apt for women who are matrons in their homes. Nietzsche's claim is
that all Frauen exaggerate their weakness as a ploy to make men feel "clumsy,
and guilty on that score." This feeling is particularly pronounced in the
domestic sphere, where women can be "hurt even by a speck of dust." Nietz-
sche presents such exaggeration as a manipulative move devised by genuine
weakness; but he also sees it as a clever tactic. "Thus they defend themselves
against the strong and 'the law of the jungle [Faustrecht].' "44
Section 67 reports another vignette, this time about a woman who, al-
though apparently high-strung during her courtship with her husband, feels
a relative inner calm now that the relationship is secure. Nietzsche suggests
that to keep the man she loves captivated, she would best feign her earlier
instability. The section is titled "Simulating—Oneself," and Nietzsche seems
to applaud such a possibility: "Wouldn't she do well to simulate her old
character? To simulate lack of love? Is this not the counsel of—love? Vivat
comoedia [Long live comedy!]."45
Granted, Nietzsche is not entirely complimentary to the women he de-
scribes. Cows intrude again: Nietzsche describes the currently confident
woman in section 67 as being "like a cow."46 The women attempting to make
men feel clumsy are described as genuinely weak. The fallen woman who
gives herself to the man she loves is described as "afflicted with a certain
poverty of the spirit," although she is "noble." Nevertheless, Nietzsche's re-
marks reveal a genuine concern for the psychological motivations both of
women generally and of specific types of women. Indeed, by suggesting that
this situation can be viewed as part of the comedy of life, Nietzsche is ac-
cording women's situation a status comparable to that of men, their fellow
players.
That Nietzsche is concerned with the psychological motivations of partic-
ular types of women is true as well in certain later sections of The Gay Science.
In section 119 he describes the type of woman who becomes a function of
her husband; and in section 227 he describes the folly of the woman who
thinks that a man with no self-control will be easy to control. This whole
set of passages counters the more typical treatment of women in the literature
of Nietzsche's era, which may extol the Eternal Feminine, but pays little
attention to particular actual women. Nietzsche contends that the feminine
is neither eternal nor a unified principle; and he offers an array of female
motivations to indicate the complexity of the psychological terrain.
Nietzsche makes this even more obvious in the subsequent passages that
describe the cultural conditioning of women. In section 68 he reports a con-


84
COMIC RELIEF
frontation between a sage and someone who brings a youth to him, claiming,
"Look, he is being corrupted by women." The section goes on to report the
response of the sage:
"It is men," said he, "that corrupt women; and all the failings of women
should be atoned by and improved in men. For it is man who creates for
himself the image of woman, and woman forms herself according to this
image.... Will is the manner of men; willingness that of women. That is
the law of the sexes—truly a hard law for women. All of humanity is
innocent of its existence; but women are doubly innocent. Who could have
oil and kindness enough for them?"
Someone from the crowd that is apparently present yells that "women need
to be educated better!" But the sage responds that "men need to be educated
better."47 Although the sage beckons the youth to follow him, we are told
that the youth does not. Presumably, the youth does not follow the advice
of the person who brought him to the sage either. In all probability he returns
to his business—that of being "corrupted."
This passage might be read as an "essentialist" characterization of gender,
or an effort to put women "in their place."48 The sage, however, speaks in
a descriptive tone. And insofar as he describes the relative roles of men and
women in heterosexual relations, he is probably not overstepping the facts
to describe the style of nineteenth-century males as expressing will and that
of their female counterparts as displaying "willingness."49
In keeping with his emphasis on change throughout The Gay Science,
Nietzsche seems to believe change possible in the relations between the sexes.
The sage seems to advocate such change when he urges that men be educated
better. While Nietzsche may find the differences between male and female
erotic styles to be biologically based, he does seem to think that the way men
and women behave toward each other is malleable. Thus, while he may be
a biological foundationalist, he is not a biological determinist.
Moreover, Nietzsche suggests that women's education is important, al-
though, admittedly, he does not appear to think it should be identical to that
of men. He stresses the importance of education, without the intermediary
of the fictional sage, in section 71, titled "On Female Chastity." There he
marvels at the "amazing and monstrous... education of upper-class
women." These women, he contends, are taught to be "as ignorant as pos-
sible of erotic matters" and to be so filled with shame at any hint of such
topics as to respond with flight. Then they are "hurled, as by a gruesome
lightning bolt, into reality and knowledge, by marriage—precisely by the man
they love and esteem the most! ... Thus a psychic knot has been tied that
may have no equal."50 Individual women deal with this problem in different
ways, but Nietzsche doubts that anyone has offered a full account of an
individual woman's accommodation.
This barbaric education, Nietzsche submits, results in a woman's delib-
erate assumption of a kind of blindness. "Afterward, the same deep silence


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
85
as before. Often a silence directed at herself, too. She closes her eyes to
herself." Probably, too, the woman will desire children "as an apology
or atonement" for the "question mark" that her husband raises regarding
her honor. Nietzsche concludes, "In sum, one cannot be too kind about
women."51 Besides revealing what appears to be genuine sympathy for
women, Nietzsche prefigures contemporary feminist arguments that certain
characteristics of women are "cultural constructions," and dubious construc-
tions at that.52
We are now prepared to explain how Book H's passages on women relate
to the subsequent passages on art. Traditional art depends on an illusion
that is accepted as a likeness to reality. Far from trivializing women by sug-
gesting the analogy with art, Nietzsche takes men's fantasies about women
to provide the paradigm cases of projections that are taken as objectively
true. Nietzsche reminds his readers that these fantasies are neither "objec-
tively" true nor even an approximation of women's own perspectives.
Women's reality is quite different from traditional male fabrications, and
Nietzsche jars his readers into recognizing the discrepancy between their
habitual thinking and actual women's points of view.
For a male audience, the passages on women are bound to be startling.
By introducing the theme of "women," a topic on which Nietzsche can
expect that his readers have both thoughts and feelings, he forces the reader
into double takes.53 By continuing with a whole series of sections referring
to women, he enhances the likelihood that further double takes will deepen
the processes of self-examination and reassessment of one's own responses
to women. Perspective is not fixed, Nietzsche's strategy reminds us. Perspec-
tivism is an activity, and one in which Nietzsche's readers are forced to
participate.
The consequence is striking: merely to entertain Nietzsche's vignettes is
to reenvision women along culturally nonstandard lines. Nietzsche challenges
his readers to consider how the women he describes might understand them-
selves from the inside. Admittedly, these readers have no "objective" court
of appeals to judge the accuracy of their experimental projections; but to
attempt such an experiment at all is pioneering in an era when the typical
philosophical pronouncement about women took no interest in women's
perspectives. That Oliver Stone's movie The Doors shows Jim Morrison quot-
ing Nietzsche is more than biographically apt. Nietzsche tries to stimulate
his readers to "break through" the barrier of gendered consciousness, to
imagine life on "the other side."54
Nietzsche in Relation to Contemporary Feminism
I have suggested that Nietzsche is not consistent in his attitude toward the
opposite sex. Certainly, he would contend that no one is fully consistent on
these matters; and in this he is not unlike most major theorists in the psy-


86
COMIC RELIEF
choanalytic movement, which learned much from Nietzsche about inter-
preting human motivations. He considers apparent consistency in such per-
sonal matters a mask, if not a symptom.
Nietzsche's inconsistency may itself attract feminist ire; and the aims of
the feminist political activist oppose Nietzsche's in fundamental respects.
Nevertheless, Nietzsche is a forebear of feminist philosophy. In the first place,
like Nietzsche, feminist philosophers begin by embracing perspectivism, the
view that philosophy can do justice to human experience only by taking
perspectival differences into account. Insofar as Nietzsche both articulates
and defends the importance of perspectivism, he lays the groundwork for
feminist analyses of the gender-based foundation of certain perspectival dif-
ferences. Moreover, his tactics for inducing perspectival thought experiments
in his readers provide a valuable precedent for feminist practice, assuming
that feminist aims include insight into both male and female motivations,
and not only strategic advantage for women.55
Second, at least some feminist philosophers are interested in the methods
of revolutionary transformation of consciousness. Nietzsche describes phi-
losophy as a chronicle and transfiguration of "the states of health" one has
undergone.56 His own chronicles include his confessions, much in the fashion
of a participant in a male consciousness-raising group, to his own sexist
habits of thinking.57 In The Gay Science, Nietzsche also seems interested in
transforming perspectives on gender. While his ultimate goal is presumably
neither limited to nor focused on gender, he takes gender to be a noteworthy
case in point.
Third, Nietzsche takes stances on issues of special concern to feminists.
For example, he rejects unisex androgyny as a goal. Yet, he also embraces
the notion that, optimally, an individual will develop traits without any effort
to conform to culturally prescribed gender stereotypes. Nietzsche seems
closer to urging a proliferation of genders than to insisting on fixed roles for
anyone.58 He consistently disputes the value of any ethical ideal that proposes
the same traits for everyone. Even if he is still locked into the notion of a
heterosexual binarism (a contention that is debatable), his suggestions that
there are many types of women with different psychological takes on reality
initiate an exploration of the possibilities.
Fourth, Nietzsche encourages a reconsideration of relationships between
men and women. More than most of his contemporaries, he is committed
to the notion that the roles and relations possible for members of the dif-
ferent sexes are subject to change and that change of this sort is desirable.
Fifth, in suggesting that changes should be considered in gender roles and
relationships, and that individuals should not be constricted by social ster-
eotypes, Nietzsche problematizes gender. Gender may not have been his own
central concern, or even a topic that sustained his interest over his career.
Nevertheless, in The Gay Science, at least, Nietzsche was a pioneer in gender
theory.


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
87
Sexist Humor—a Reconsideration
Nietzsche concludes his remarks on women with what is arguably a sexist
joke. Kaufmann describes section 75, the final section on women in Book II,
as an "absurd" aphorism:
The third sex.—"A small man is a paradox but still a man; but small females
seem to me to belong to another sex than tall women," said an old dancing
master. A small woman is never beautiful—said old Aristotle."59
Kaufmann reads this as a poor paraphrase of Aristotle:
Aristotle actually says: "Greatness of soul implies greatness, as beauty im-
plies a good-sized body, and small people may be neat and well-
proportioned but cannot be beautiful" (Nicomachean Ethics, iiisb).. . .
Kaufmann's note concludes.
With this absurd aphorism the pages on women . .. reach their nadir and
end. The rest of Book II... deals with art.60
What should we make of section 75? Is it akin to Schopenhauer's remark
that women are an "undersized, narrow-shouldered, broad-hipped, and
short-legged race"?61 Could the joke, perhaps, be on Schopenhauer, if at
women's expense, with Nietzsche claiming that the female sex is not one
undersized race but two? When Aristotle discusses human beings, he takes
the male to be the paradigm; does Nietzsche include women here only to
make fun of them? Is the joke sexist?
I do not think we should assume so. If anyone appears absurd in section
75, it is Aristotle himself. To cast Aristotle as a dancing master seems prima
facie ridiculous, in light of the ploddingly methodical gait of the Aristotelian
texts that survive. Nietzsche is also making light of Aristotle's Peripatetic
("Walking Up and Down") school.62 More important, Aristotle is cast here
as someone who is screening potential dance partners, and demanding a
partner who matches his own stature. As a "dancing master," Aristotle seems
to find ludicrous the idea of dancing with someone of a vastly different size.
Nietzsche draws out what he sees as absurd in Aristotle's view on human
scale. If he were to teach dancing, Aristotle would likely differentiate women
on this basis as well—postulating a new essence, a third sex, a caste of women
whose height is vastly dissimilar to that of a man, at least of a "great-souled"
man,63
But what is more perspectival than height? By rather unsympathetically
paraphrasing Aristotle, Nietzsche suggests that Aristotle formulated his "ob-
jective" observations from the stance of his own perspective. Aristotle, the
original proponent of "essences" in Western philosophy, is as dependent on
the contingencies of his own physiological perspective as is anyone else.
Nietzsche makes his mirth regarding Aristotle's pronouncement more ob-
vious in a later passage in The Gay Science:


88
COMIC RELIEF
Great man.—From the fact that somebody is "a big man" we cannot infer
that he is a man; perhaps he is merely a boy, or a chameleon of all the
ages of life, or a bewitched little female.64
In other words, the scale of body demanded by Aristotle is far from "essen-
tial" to the matter of having a great soul—or to one's "gender," if this is
understood as conformity to socially imposed ideals. The "big man's" body
that so impressed Aristotle might house the psyche of precisely the tiny
woman that the fictional Aristotle rejects as a dancing partner. The big man
might be a child, or a woman, or perhaps a member of the third sex.
In matters of height, we all have perspectives; and Nietzsche's joke on the
great man Aristotle raises questions about his own position in this matter.
Some of Nietzsche's acquaintances do offer testimony on this topic, but their
comments only fortify the view that height is a matter of perspective. Ida
von Miaskowski, for example, wrote in 1907:
Nietzsche's external appearance at the beginning of the seventies of the past
century still stands vividly in my memory. He was only of medium height,
but of slender build, brisk and lively. His features seemed ordinary to me,
but the wonderful eyes and impressive forehead made one forget this, and
on the whole one had the impression of a personality that towered above
the average, even externally.65
Ludwig von Scheffler disputes Miaskowski's impression:
[H]e was of short rather than medium height. His head deep in the shoul-
ders of his stocky yet delicate body. And the gleaming horn-rimmed glasses
and the long hanging mustache deprived the face of that intellectual ex-
pression which often gives even short men an impressive air.66
Adolf Ruthardt took a still different view:
Above middle height, slender, well-formed, with erect but not stiff stance,
his gestures harmonious, calm and sparing; the almost black hair, the thick
Vercingetorix mustache, his light-colored, but distinguished-looking suit of
the best cut and fit, allowed him so little to resemble the type of a German
scholar that he called to mind rather a Southern French nobleman or an
Italian or Spanish higher officer in civilian clothes.67
We may be entitled to draw the conclusions that Nietzsche was probably
not tall, and that height is in the eye of the beholder. I think that we should
also conclude that Nietzsche's humor is not straightforward. Often, at least,
Nietzsche's jokes are startling—and this is a prima facie reason to doubt that
they are sexist. A joke bent on reinforcing prejudice is unlikely to prove
startling. If sexist jokes are not funny, at least part of the reason is that their
point is tedious.
At the same time, jokes are almost always unfair. They are pointillistically
perspectival, without any concern to round out the picture. The medium is
ill-suited to do justice to the texture of female experience—or to any other


WOMAN, ALL-TOO-WOMAN?
89
texture. Nevertheless, jokes can, I think, be liberating. The vision they convey
can undercut complacent commonplaces that entrench inflexible attitudes.
Whatever one might make of any particular joke, I think that feminists
should eschew "political correctness" on humor as a rigidly held ideal.68 This
is a third pitfall that feminists should try to avoid. In keeping with the tenor
of my labels for the other two syndromes that I have mentioned, I will call
this the Water Buffalo Syndrome.69 In urging feminists to avoid pedantic
stances of political correctness, I do not mean to endorse that tiresome and
usually unjustified complaint that feminists lack humor. "Being serious" is
a respectable strategy in anyone aiming to make important points, feminists
included. To say to someone engaged in serious criticism, "You're just being
serious!" strikes me as a foolish (defensive) response.
Humor itself, however, should be recognized as a respectable strategy of
provocation, as well as a spontaneous product of imagination. Any renova-
tion of gender roles will require imagination and the willingness to be star-
tled. If jokes accomplish double takes on gender, as Nietzsche's do in The
Gay Science, they may be of service to feminists. I think that feminists do
well to recognize humor as a legitimate strategy.
Ultimately, Nietzsche does not require any particular conclusion from the
readers of The Gay Science. But he almost ensures, through his sections on
women, that those who are honest with themselves will recognize their own
perspectival stances based on gender. Far from proselytizing for the entrench-
ment of his culture's sexual stereotypes, Nietzsche forces his readers to stop
taking gender for granted. In this he is far ahead of his contemporaries. As
he commends himself in another context, he saw questions where his con-
temporaries did not.70


A Fool's Cap, Above All
The experiential engagement that Nietzsche enlists in Book II with his com-
ments about women intensifies an enterprise begun in Book I. By juxtaposing
meditations on diverse topics, Nietzsche forces the reader to assume new
viewpoints and then move on to others. Nietzsche characterizes "the perfect
reader" in Ecce Homo as one who hates to deduce where he can guess.1
Fighting our scholarly quests to say "Consequently," Nietzsche keeps us
guessing in The Gay Science. His sequential, aphoristic method of presenta-
tion demands continual readjustment and reassessment.
Book II's passages on women virtually ensure that the reader will engage
in introspection about his or her mental machinations, including some that
are personally sensitive. The latter part of Book II draws the reader back
from private reveries, directing attention to one of our most collective in-
terpretive enterprises, that of the arts. In particular, Nietzsche focuses on the
art of theater, reminding us of his earlier account of our undertakings as
part of the grand ebb and flow of tragic and comic outlooks.
Book II's switch from romantic experience to more widely intersubjective
topics is welcome, but Nietzsche is hardly eager to remind us of the "com-
mon sense" the arts engage, in the manner of Kant.2 His particular reflections
reveal that he is most concerned with the arts' power to make us vary our
perspectives. The first section after the passages on women, section 76, ac-
knowledges the societal usefulness of "a law of agreement," based on a faith
that some judgments are not arbitrary. This is because
the greatest danger that always hovered over humanity and still hovers over
it is the eruption of madness—which means the eruption of arbitrariness
of feeling, seeing, and hearing, the enjoyment of the mind's lack of disci-
pline, the joy in human unreason.
Yet "precisely the most select spirits bristle at this universal binding force—
the explorers of truth above all. ... It is in these impatient spirits that a
veritable delight in madness erupts because madness has such a cheerful
90
INTERLUDE 3


A FOOL S CAP, ABOVE ALL
91
tempo." Siding with this band, Nietzsche concludes that "we," the danger
and exception, "need eternally to be defended"—and that "there actually are
things to be said in favor of the exception, provided that it never wants to
become the rule."3
In the following section, Nietzsche praises the arts for having provided
such defenses, not only for Nietzsche and his select band of truth seekers,
but for each individual:
Only artists, and especially those of the theater, have given men eyes and
ears to see and hear with some pleasure what each man is himself, expe-
riences himself, desires himself; only they have taught us to esteem the
hero that is concealed in everyday characters; only they have taught us the
art of viewing ourselves as heroes—from a distance and, as it were, sim-
plified and transfigured.. . . Only in this way can we deal with some base
details in ourselves.4
Nevertheless, what art reveals is a genuine revelation, not a mere concealment
of our flaws. A hero, in fact, is concealed in our everyday characters. Our
ordinary outlook takes whatever it sees as part of the foreground. In our
own case, it renders our faults as prominent as any other traits. The failure
to stylize our view of ourselves and all else renders us, in the proverbial
expression, incapable of seeing the forest for the trees:
Without this art we would be nothing but foreground and live entirely in
the spell of that perspective which makes what is closest at hand and most
vulgar appear as if it were vast, and reality itself.
In particular, art's beautifying transformation of our perspectives on our-
selves (as individuals and as human beings generally) "should win our grat-
itude."5
Nietzsche admires Shakespeare most of all for transfiguring a murky soul
into a hero:
I could not say anything more beautiful in praise of Shakespeare as a
human being than this: he believed in Brutus and did not cast one speck
of suspicion upon this type of virtue.... Independence of the soul!—that
is at stake here. No sacrifice can be too great for that: one must be capable
of sacrificing one's dearest friend for it, even if he should also be the most
glorious human being, an ornament of the world, a genius without
peer.... 6
It is not that Shakespeare, or Nietzsche, takes the betrayal of a friend lightly:
"The height at which he places Caesar is the finest honor that he could
bestow on Brutus: that is how he raises beyond measure Brutus's inner prob-
lem as well as the spiritual strength that was able to cut this knot." Shake-
speare even "prostrated himself," bringing a poet on stage and presenting
him as contemptible by comparison with Brutus.7
Nietzsche is convinced that it is the individual soul, filled by its "gloomy
hour and . . . evil angel," that Shakespeare illuminates:


92
COMIC RELIEF
Could it really have been political freedom that led this poet to sympathize
with Brutus—and turned him into Brutus's accomplice? Or was political
freedom only a symbol for something inexpressible?8
Politics pale for Nietzsche as almost incidental by comparison with the vision
of the individual human soul, weighted by contradictory tensions, compelled
to act. The vision of Brutus discovering his own "scales," and acting upon
their readings, overpowers the significance of any collective outcome, from
Nietzsche's point of view.
I suspect that this remark gives the key to interpreting many of Nietzsche's
comments about political figures, whether contemporary or historical. Nietz-
sche is notorious for presenting monsters in political history in a favorable
light. He remarks on this himself in Ecce Homo, while describing various
misreadings of Thus Spoke Zarathustra: "Those to whom I said in confidence
that they should sooner look even for a Cesare Borgia than for a Parsifal,
did not believe their own ears."9 Kaufmann quite reasonably points out that
this is a very qualified endorsement of Borgia: "This eher noch leaves no
doubt that Nietzsche considered Cesare Borgia far from admirable but pre-
ferred even him to the Parsifal ideal." Nietzsche's references to Borgia can
be explained, he argues, because "Nietzsche found it ridiculous to consider
Cesare Borgia unhealthy in contrast to an emasculated man who is alleged
to be healthy."10
Even if his admiration is mitigated, Nietzsche's references to Borgia are
noteworthy. Nietzsche presents him as an object of theatrical interest, not of
moral censure. He takes a similar view of Tiberius in section 36. In these
cases, Nietzsche's main focus is the angle of the individual's character and
the ways in which it articulates itself. In Twilight of the Idols, Nietzsche ob-
serves that Dostoyevsky discovered that "the convicts in Siberia—hardened
criminals for whom there was no way back to society... were carved out of
just about the best, hardest, and most valuable wood that grows anywhere
on Russian soil."11 Nietzsche takes a similar view of historical characters in
whom most see little to admire. He does not fault them morally, although
they are faultable. Instead, he considers their basic mettle, which he sees as
something of value.
In sum, Nietzsche treats the political figures he considers as theatrical
heroes. This is not the place to defend this claim across Nietzsche's works,
particularly since most of Nietzsche's references to political figures appear
outside The Gay Science. Nevertheless, section 78 provides an explanation of
why he eschews making moral judgments. These individuals are particularly
difficult cases to see "from a distance, and, as it were, simplified and trans-
figured."The idea that Nietzsche trivializes politics is unlikely to win the
admiration of those who have criticized him for holding appalling political
views. Those who take politics as more fundamental than personal character
have a basic philosophical disagreement with Nietzsche. He sees personal
character as decisive in determining the institutions and political organiza-
tions that come to be, not the other way around. As Nietzsche sees it, the
political is personal, both in its motivations and in its consequences.


A FOOL S CAP, ABOVE ALL
93
Taking a theatrical view of infamous characters not only enables us to
revise our view of them. It also enhances our appreciation of what human
beings are. Theater simultaneously transfigures our views of ourselves and
of others. Book II abounds in illustrations of the arts, allowing us to take
pleasure in what human beings actually are. In section 77 Nietzsche considers
the vulgarity in Italian opera and Spanish adventure novels as worthwhile in
its own right:
"The animal has as much right as any human being; let it run about freely.
And you, my dear fellow man, are also still animal in spite of everything!"
That seems to me the moral of this story.12
In section 80 he recalls the Greek delight in passion that can speak well, and
Italian opera's delight in "passion that sings":
With just a little more impertinence, Rossini would have had everyone sing
nothing but la-la-la-la—and that would have made good, rational sense.
Confronted with the characters in an opera, we are not supposed to take
their word for it, but the sound!13
These forms, again, present even our basest passions as articulate and he-
roic.14
The remainder of Book II consists largely of paeans to artistic idealization
of ourselves as human beings. He praises "the small dose of unreason in all
French esprit" for its superior social pleasantry, by comparison with those
who, like the Greeks, want to be right in every case.15 The Romans who stole
all from the Greeks felt no compunction in claiming authorship for what
they had merely translated: "They seem to ask us: 'Should we not make anew
for ourselves what is old and find ourselves in it? Should we not have the
right to breathe our own soul into this dead body? For it is dead after
all. . ,"16 Nietzsche counts festivals as a higher art than other art forms, for
festivals adorn and make visible "the great festival road of humanity" and
"commemorate high and happy moments."17
Of course, Nietzsche grants that artists and audiences sometimes have
motives aside from those he celebrates.18 Wagner was fundamentally driven
by vanity.19 Contemporary theater often seeks the exhaustion of its audi-
ence.20 Nietzsche hints that he may have a hygienic motive in his own writing:
"[S]o far, I have not discovered any other way of getting rid of my
thoughts."21 German music displays "a profound bourgeois envy of nobil-
ity."22 Even everyday German speech has become a rather farcical manifes-
tation of
the German reverence for everything that comes from the court.... [T]o
this day, German travelers in the midst of an Italian mob still sound very
rude, woodsy, and hoarse, as if they came from smoky rooms and impolite
regions.23
Finally, Nietzsche urges us to recall that artists are sometimes moved by the
Don Juan compulsion to seduce and seduce again.24


94
COMIC RELIEF
Book II concludes, however, with further praise for art, particularly tragic
and comic theater, as especially important in modern times. Section 107,
titled "Our Ultimate Gratitude to Art," recalls the reasoning of The Birth of
Tragedy. "As an aesthetic phenomenon existence is still bearable for us,"
Nietzsche tells us again.25 And as in The Birth of Tragedy, he describes the
recent finding of science "that delusion and error are conditions of human
knowledge and sensation." This discovery would be unbearable had the arts
not taught us to delight in something other than truth:
[A] rt furnishes us with eyes and hands and above all the good conscience
to be able to turn ourselves into such a phenomenon. At times we need a
rest from ourselves by looking upon, by looking down upon, ourselves and,
from an artistic distance, laughing over ourselves or weeping over ourselves.
We . . . must occasionally find pleasure in our folly, or we cannot continue
to find pleasure in our wisdom. Precisely because we are at bottom grave
and serious human beings—really, more weights than human beings—
nothing does us as much good as a fool's cap. .. .26
We return to themes from section i—that we can view ourselves in tragic
or comic terms, and that comedy must eventually displace tragedy. In section
107, however, Nietzsche makes a direct recommendation for us in the mod-
ern age. We have become far too serious, too tragic. We need to see ourselves
as comic characters, and to do this we need to resist our moral categories.
The section and Book II conclude:
We should be able also to stand above morality—and not only to stand
with the anxious stiffness of a man who is afraid of slipping and falling
any moment, but also to float above it and play. How then could we
possibly dispense with art—and with the fool?—And as long as you are in
any way ashamed before yourselves, you do not yet belong with us.27
This final line alludes loosely to the New Testament remark, "He who is
not with us is against us," which encourages rigorous consistency in matters
of faith and morals. The upshot is that one should examine one's conscience
and presumably experience shame. Nietzsche reverses this platitude. The "us"
in Nietzsche's formulation is not a moralistic community of saints but a
band of comic players. Assuming the role of coach for comics, Nietzsche
insists that shame is inimical to the project. Moralistic self-condemnation is
disaster for a clown. Nietzsche advises, "Abandon shame, all you who would
be fools."
Nietzsche suggests, however, that already a small band of frohliche scien-
tists has emerged, select spirits who explore truth as a matter of play. The
cheerful tempo of madness and a Sabbath from the law of agreement seem
to be winning. At the close of Book II, comedy appears as the successor of
our tragic age.
Or does it? Book III reminds us, soberly, that a tragic age does not end
with a single punch line.


God Is Dead
The Coroner's Report
"God is dead!" is the slogan of Book III, but it is hardly the punch line of
a joke. On the topic of God's death, Nietzsche tends to be a tragedian. The
death of God might initiate the end of our tradition's tragic age, but Nietz-
sche complains that contemporary scholars have failed to draw appropriate
conclusions from this event. A period of grieving is important in our tran-
sitional time, and in Book III Nietzsche urges his contemporaries to recognize
this situation. Nevertheless, his ultimate aim remains to herald a turn to
comedy. He continues to engage in humorous Wissenschaft, although Book
Ill's humor is sometimes black.
Even Nietzsche's "tragic" account of the spiritual situation assists his
comic mission. His agenda in Book III is to analyze the obstacles impeding
sheer delight. Our mythic background and its current reconfiguration both
ensure the continuation of the tragic era of our discontent—with ourselves
and the world around us. Our current orientation, however, is unstable, with
potential for either a tragic or a comic future. The moment is dramatic,
fertile for possibilities, yet the Western world pays no attention.
Book III opens with a dramatic counterpart to what Nietzsche sees as the
current cultural tradition. The statement "God is dead" is almost an aside,
as if Nietzsche's readers have heard this all before. The form of the first
section belies its real content. Explicitly and formally section 108 is about the
aftermath, not the death of God. But the aftermath of reading it is the
reader's looking backward and asking, "What?!"
New struggles.—After Buddha was dead, his shadow was still shown for
centuries in a cave—a tremendous, gruesome shadow. God is dead; but
given the way of men, there may still be caves for thousands of years in
which his shadow will be shown.—And we—we still have to vanquish his
shadow, too.1
95
CJlYE


96
COMIC RELIEF
Nietzsche alludes in section 108 to Plato's Myth of the Cave, in which
virtually everyone mistakes shadows on a cave's wall for reality. Only the
philosopher discovers the clear light of day, and when he does, he makes it
his mission to show this light to others, even though he risks their derision
and fury by doing so. Nietzsche casts himself in the role of this philosopher,
one of Western thought's most persistent tragic parts.
Section 108 is a declaration of war. But this war is not with God. The war
announced in Book III is to be fought against God's shadows, the persistence
of monotheistic habits. This helps to explain Book Ill's contents, which range
from direct attacks on the Christian moral worldview to meditations on the
falsifications involved in scholarly formulations, particularly in the context
of natural science. Nietzsche considers both targets to be shadows of God,
for both obscure the new spiritual possibilities that might evolve in the wake
of "God's death," whatever that means.
Martin Heidegger, I think convincingly, argues that Nietzsche calls atten-
tion to the situation of modern culture with his formula "God is dead."
What has died is our inherited conception of God and the worldview based
upon it. Heidegger's interpretation is to this extent accepted by the consensus
of Nietzsche scholars, and I do not challenge it here. Instead, I emphasize
the extent to which Nietzsche's discussion represents his own religious
thought—religious in the sense of seeking a new basis for spiritual health.
Nietzsche is not primarily the debunker that he is often thought to be, but
a seeker who hopes that spiritual nurturance might come from an earthly
life of playful delight and reverence.
Although most notably associated with Nietzsche, the statement "God is
dead" is not original with him. As Eric von der Luft demonstrates in his
excellent article "Sources of Nietzsche's 'God Is Dead!,' "2 the line resonates
with several precedents in Nietzsche's religious and philosophical back-
ground, with which he would have been familiar. The line occurs in Hegel's
Phenomenology of Spirit, in the section titled "Revealed Religion." Describing
the condition of Unhappy Consciousness, Hegel contends:
[T]his "unhappy consciousness" constituted the counterpart and the com-
plement of the perfectly happy consciousness, that of comedy. All divine
reality goes back into this latter type of consciousness; it means, in other
words, the complete relinquishment and emptying of substance. The for-
mer, on the contrary, is conversely the tragic fate that befalls certainty of
self which aims at being absolute, at being self-sufficient. It is consciousness
of the loss of everything of significance in this certainty of itself, and of
the loss even of this knowledge or certainty of self—the loss of substance
as well as of self; it is the bitter pain which finds expression in the cruel
words, "God is dead."3
This characterization is not far from Nietzsche's own description, in his
notes, of modern nihilism: "The highest values devaluate themselves. The
aim is lacking; 'why?' finds no answer."4 In these two descriptions all sig-
nificance has vanished.


GOD IS DEAD
97
Hegel refers to a similar situation in his account of unhappy conscious-
ness. Unhappy consciousness is, in Hegel's analysis, the stage of conscious-
ness that comes after stoicism and skepticism, two efforts by consciousness
to establish its own freedom and escape the misery of the world. Both of
these attempts involve a denial of the reality of the world, even if the skeptic
does acknowledge the world's reality in practice. The stage of unhappiness
comes about when consciousness recognizes its own internal contradiction,
its simultaneous denial of the world's reality and deliberate attempts to merge
with it. The earlier hostile duality of master and slave, described by Hegel as
the conflict that initiates self-awareness, is recapitulated inside the single con-
sciousness.
Robert C. Solomon observes that Saint Augustine best exemplifies this
condition historically, despite the fact that Hegel does not explicitly mention
him. Augustine
saw himself torn between two selves, the bodily self of desire and needs in
bewildering confusion, and the rational self of the will with its quest for
unity with God and the eternal.5
Solomon contends that this type of religious consciousness extended
from the ancient Hebrews through Luther and the Reformation, which
Nietzsche—following Hegel—called "slave religions." What these all have
in common is a "soul of despair," an attempt to escape from the hardships
of life through a metaphysical scheme, in which they themselves become
at one with reality, if at the same time pathetic because of it.6
Solomon reads unhappy consciousness as it is characterized in the section
titled "Revealed Religion" as "orthodox Christianity, which takes both God
and Christ to be something 'other' than oneself."7 The young Hegelian Feu-
erbach drew on this notion when he analyzed this alienation of the human
being from God as the liability of Christian theism. The dynamic involved
is the human being's disowning all traits viewed as valuable and projecting
them into God, who becomes all-good and all-powerful.
Book III reveals how important Feuerbach's argument is to Nietzsche.8
For example, Nietzsche characterizes the initial impulse of god-making as
being of human origin in a fairly obvious way:
For an individual to posit his own ideal and derive from it his own laws,
joys, and rights—that may well have been considered hitherto as the most
outrageous human aberration and as idolatry itself. The few who dared as
much always felt the need to apologize to themselves, usually by saying:
"It wasn't I! Not I! But a god through me." The wonderful art of creating
gods—polytheism—was the medium through which this impulse could
discharge, purify, perfect, and ennoble itself... ?
The human basis for monotheism in time became disguised, however. God
came to be seen as "honor-craving" and vindictive:


98
COMIC RELIEF
The Christian presupposes a powerful, overpowering being who enjoys re-
venge. His power is so great that nobody could possibly harm him, except
for his honor. Every sin is a slight to his honor ... —and no more. Con-
trition, degradation, rolling in the dust—all this is the first and last con-
dition of his grace: in sum, the restoration of his divine honor. ... Sin is
an offense against him, not against humanity.10
Indeed, God conceived in this way is motivated by human motives, but not
motives that are particularly honorable. A God who is concerned more fun-
damentally with his own vanity than with his creatures scarcely deserves
worship Nietzsche contends. We should come to see God as Frankenstein's
monster, our creation from bits of our own substance, now a fearsome power
gone awry.
In tracing the convolutions of unhappy consciousness, Hegel builds to the
climax at which the recognition of man's identity with God occurs. Solomon
explicates:
It is the death of God, not His historical life, that is of greater significance.
But this is not because . .. the death of Christ is an all-important event
which signifies the salvation of all true believers; the death of God signifies
the unimportance of Christ, and the fact that our lives too are Holy and
Immortal, through the universal Spirit of the community.. .. Indeed, the
most tragic mistake of picture thinking (i.e. Christianity) is the idea that
our salvation and unification with the Holy Spirit will come some time in
the distant future, when the truth is that the unification of ourselves with
Spirit is now if only we will realize it...."
This Hegelian stages corresponds to the development that Feuerbach and
Nietzsche endorse. Having emptied and alienated ourselves from ourselves
through our theism, we can restore our full humanity only by emptying our
concept of God. For Hegel, the concept of God is emptied when conscious-
ness recognizes its union with God. What "dies" is only the God that is
understood as separate from humanity.
The Hegelian/Feuerbachian reading of "God is dead" pushes the edge of
the era's theology. The line is not, however, original with Hegel, but has
sources in more traditional Lutheran imagery. J. B. Bailie, translator of The
Phenomenology, observes that Hegel is referring to "a hymn of Luther."12
Von der Luft concurs, observing that "[T]he idea of God's death is ... not
at all alien to Luther, and in one hymn, Christ lag in Todesbanden, [Christ
Lay in the Bonds of Death] the sentiment is quite close to that of Rist and
Hegel."13 Von der Luft indicates an even more obvious Lutheran source of
the expression, the hymn "Bin trauriger Grabgesang," (A Sorrowful Dirge)
by Johann Rist, a Lutheran pastor. The text of the song includes the stanza:
Oh great Need!
God himself lies dead,
he died on the cross,
and through it from love


GOD IS DEAD
99
gained the kingdom of heaven
for us.14
Von der Luft describes these hymns as expressing the existential sentiment
that for Christians is appropriate to Holy Saturday, the feeling of being
"alone, dejected, guilt-ridden, etc." For such a person, "God has died, in
effect, for him, i.e., he perceives no God."15
Von der Luft reads Nietzsche as appealing to his religious heritage: "When
he says, 'God is dead!' he means no more than that 'transcendence is lost'—
and not only lost, but purposefully done away with."16 The purpose, pre-
sumably, is the Feuerbachian project. Nietzsche's atheism, so characterized,
is not opposed to spirituality but conceived as the condition for humanity's
spiritual renewal. Nietzsche is an atheist, then, from what might be called
"religious" conviction (and not religious in the cheap sense of being rigidly
upheld).
Von der Luft also sees another source behind Nietzsche's slogan, one that
offered Nietzsche a reason for thinking that the death of God has already
occurred. Von der Luft refers to Heine's comment on Kant's Critique of Pure
Reason at the end of Book II of On the History of Religion and Philosophy in
Germany: "Do you hear the little bell tinkle? Kneel down—one brings the
sacraments for a dying God."17 Von der Luft comments:
Heine,... greatly admired by Nietzsche, at the end of the second book of
Zur Geschichte der Religion und Philosophie in Deutschland, cited Kant's
first Kritik as the sacrament brought to a dying God....
For Heine, as Kant swept God out of the epistemological realms of both
sense awareness and pure thought, he thus relegated any possible con-
sciousness of God's existence to the realm of faith, not the conditioned,
clarified faith of Augustine or Aquinas but the absurd faith of Tertul-
lian.. . .1S
In Heine's analysis, which Nietzsche shares, Kant succeeded in defining a
limit of theological speculation which rendered belief in a simultaneously
personal and transcendent God nearly impossible. We have seen earlier that
Nietzsche saw Kant's philosophy as the groundwork for abandoning faith in
the "truth" of our perceptions. With Heine, Nietzsche also seems to see Kant
as responsible, if unintentionally, for the death of the traditional God.19
Nietzsche remarks in section 335 of The Gay Science:
[T]he old Kant... had obtained the "thing in itself by stealth ... and was
punished for this when the "categorical imperative" crept stealthily into
his heart and led him astray—back to "God," "soul," "freedom," and "im-
mortality," like a fox who loses his way and goes astray back into his cage.
Yet it had been his strength and cleverness that had broken open the cage!20
Nietzsche's witty caricature of Western philosophical history in Twilight of
the Idols describes the impact of Kant's revolution similarly, in the following
series of stages:


100
COMIC RELIEF
3. The true world—unattainable, indemonstrable, unpromisable; but the
very thought of it—a consolation, an obligation, an imperative.
(At bottom, the old sun, but seen through mist and skepticism. The
idea has become elusive, pale, Nordic, Konigsbergian.)
4. The true world—unattainable? At any rate, unattained. And being un-
attained, also unknown. Consequently, not consoling, redeeming, or ob-
ligating: how could something unknown obligate us?.. .
5. The "true" world—an idea which is no longer good for anything, not
even obligating—an idea which has become useless and superfluous—
consequently, a refuted idea: let us abolish it!21
The comfort of a personal God who observes and meddles with the details
of our personal experience and a God we can experience in our lives becomes
a fairy tale with Kant. Whatever the Christian religion may offer, post-
Kantian Christianity cannot provide the basis for meaning in our personal
lives, as Nietzsche sees it. God has no real contact with a person's life at all,
on the Kantian account, except as a postulated fiction (along with that of
eternal life) introduced to support our moral insistence that goodness and
happiness converge.22
Nietzsche's atheism pays more attention to spirituality in everyday expe-
rience than does Kant's theism. This is why, I think, Nietzsche feels affinity
with Emerson's theistic denunciation of his contemporaries' behaving as if
God were dead, despite his own pronouncement. Having cherished Emerson
since childhood,23 Nietzsche had surely read Emerson's lament:
Men have come to speak of the revelation as somewhat long ago given and
done, as if God were dead....
It is my duty to say to you, that the need was never greater of new
revelation than now. From the views I have already expressed, you will
infer the sad conviction, which I share, I believe, with numbers, of the
universal decay and now almost death of faith in society. The soul is not
preached. The Church seems to totter to its fall, almost all life extinct.24
Far from distancing himself from Emerson's religious views in The Gay Sci-
ence, Nietzsche uses as the epigraph for the first edition a paraphrase of a
quotation from Emerson that speaks directly of holiness. Kaufmann's literal
translation is "To the poet and sage, all things are friendly and hallowed, all
experiences profitable, all days holy, all men divine."25
In his "Divinity School Address," from which the lament about the seem-
ing death of God is taken, Emerson describes what should be done in ways
that resonate, in some respects, with the thought of Feuerbach and Nietzsche:
And now, my brothers, you will ask, What in these desponding days can
be done by us? The remedy is already declared in the ground of our com-
plaint of the Church. We have contrasted the Church with the Soul. In the
soul, then, let the redemption be sought.... The stationariness of religion;
the assumption that the age of inspiration is past, that the Bible is closed;
the fear of degrading the character of Jesus by representing him as a man;


GOD IS DEAD
IOI
indicate with sufficient clearness the falsehood of our theology. ... The true
Christianity—a faith like Christ's in the infinitude of man—is lost.
Oh my friends, there are resources in us on which we have not drawn.26
Emerson contrasts the church with the human soul and insists that the
resources of the soul are the real home of divinity. Emerson sees the divinity
within human beings to be the genuine insight of Christ, a view that Nietz-
sche's occasional comments about Jesus seem to support, for example:
What are the "glad tidings"? True life, eternal life, has been found—it is
not promised, it is here, it is in you: as a living in love, in love without
subtraction and exclusion, without regard for station. Everyone is the child
of God—Jesus definitely presumes nothing for himself alone—and as a
child of God everyone is equal to everyone.27
Emerson's religious focus, on the possibility of making human beings aware
of the spiritual value available in everyday experience, is the same as Nietz-
sche's focus in The Gay Science, their different connotations of "God" not-
withstanding.
The expression "God is dead" already had a long history by the time
Nietzsche used it. While it had been variously employed by atheists and
theists, it had recurrently directed attention to the perceived impoverishment
of the soul. I have already observed that Nietzsche accepts Feuerbach's view
that the death of God is our best hope for spiritual renewal. Nevertheless, I
think Nietzsche draws on the slogan's theistic history to remind his readers
that contemporary atheism has brought about a spiritual forlornness that it
has not begun to address. As von der Luft observes:
For Nietzsche, Christianity is a religion of death; the death of faith in its
God is the death of death, a sort of resurrection, a beginning and a yea-
saying to life.. .. Anti-Christian though he is, Nietzsche somehow cannot
seem to escape falling back on that standard Christian imagery.28
Whodunit?
Section 125 of The Gay Science challenges contemporary atheistic society to
recognize this condition, a trauma that the terms of science cannot even
recognize. The parable depicts the man who mourns God as a madman,
which he must be from the viewpoint of the modern thinker who has sub-
stituted science for traditional religion. This madman resembles Diogenes,
who carried a lantern in the daylight and said, "I seek an honest man."
Nietzsche's madman "lit a lantern in the bright morning hours, ran to the
market place, and cried incessantly: 'I seek God! I seek God!' "29 This parallel
with Diogenes is itself a bit of Feuerbachian humor. If God is composed of
humanity's own qualities, the honest man would be exactly the one who
would admit that God is really—himself.30


102
COMIC RELIEF
Unfortunately, the madman's audience is made not of honest men but of
smug "modern" men who enjoy deriding him. When the madman says he
seeks God, his interlocutors jeer: "Has he got lost?... Did he lose his way
like a child?... Or is he hiding? Is he afraid of us? Has he gone on a voyage?
emigrated?"31 The tone recalls that of those who mock Christ's claims to be
the son of God at the Crucifixion.32
The madman, unlike Jesus, talks back, however:
The madman jumped into their midst and pierced them with his eyes.
"Whither is God?" he cried; "I will tell you. We have killed him—you and
I. All of us are his murderers. But how did we do this? How could we
drink up the sea? Who gave us the sponge to wipe away the entire horizon?
What were we doing when we unchained this earth from its sun? Whither
is it moving now? Whither are we moving? Away from all sun? Are we not
plunging continually? Backward, sideward, forward, in all directions? Is
there still any up or down? Are we not straying as through an infinite
nothing? Do we not feel the breath of empty space? Has it not become
colder? Is not night continually closing in on us?.. ,"33
The death of God is a monumental religious event—not in the saga of a
dead religion but within us. The madman continues:
"How shall we comfort ourselves, the murderers of all murderers? What
was holiest and mightiest of all that the world has yet owned has bled to
death under our knives: who will wipe this blood off us? What water is
there for us to clean ourselves? What festivals of atonement, what sacred
games shall we have to invent? Is not the greatness of this deed too great
for us? Must we ourselves not become gods simply to appear worthy of
it?34
But the madman, conjoining the insight of Christ with awareness of as-
trophysics, concludes that he has not reached his audience:
Here the madman fell silent and looked again at his listeners; and they,
too, were silent and stared at him in astonishment. At last he threw his
lantern on the ground, and it broke into pieces and went out. "I have come
too early," he said then; "my time is not yet. This tremendous event is still
on its way, still wandering; it has not yet reached the ears of men. Lightning
and thunder require time; the light of the stars require time; deeds, though
done, still require time to be seen and heard. This deed is still more distant
from them than the most distant stars—and yet they have done it them-
selves."35
The madman himself is reminiscent of Jesus, who is similarly misunderstood
at his time (and, Nietzsche would add, long beyond it). In Luke 23:34 Jesus
suffering on the cross says, "Father, forgive them, for they know not what
they do." The madman concludes by going his own spiritual way, which in
his case means that he performs the mourning rites that he considers the
first step in dealing with modernity's religious calamity:
It has been related further that on the same day the madman forced his
way into several churches and there struck up his requiem aeternam deo.


GOD IS DEAD
103
Led out and called to account, he is said always to have replied nothing
but: "What after all are these churches now if they are not the tombs and
sepulchers of God?"36
What does Nietzsche mean by the madman's claim, "We have killed
him—you and I"? A possible reading would be that mere disuse killed God—
that people stopped sustaining God and that he died in their lives. But this
moralistic line, that it is our own fault, is alien from Nietzsche's suggestion
that we dance above morality. Moreover, it is out of keeping with his an-
nouncement that we need to vanquish the shadows of God, for the latter
implies that our religious habits have not already disappeared from disuse.
Nietzsche gives a less figurative translation of how we have killed God in
Book V:
You see what it was that really triumphed over the Christian god: Christian
morality itself, the concept of truthfulness that was understood ever more
rigorously, the father confessor's refinement of the Christian conscience,
translated and sublimated into a scientific conscience, into intellectual
cleanliness at any price. Looking at nature as if it were proof of the good-
ness and governance of a god; interpreting history in honor of some divine
reason, as a continual testimony of a moral world order, and ultimate
moral purposes; interpreting one's own experiences as pious people have
long enough interpreted theirs, as if everything were providential, a hint,
designed and ordained for the sake of the salvation of the soul—that is all
over now, that has man's conscience against it.. . ,37
Christian values undermined themselves, in other words. By adhering to
Christian morality, we have grown beyond it.38 The idea that Christian
thought itself contained the seeds of its own destruction is a prominent
theme in The Gay Science. Christianity itself taught its adherents how to
doubt, Nietzsche contends:
Christianity, too, has made a great contribution to the enlightenment, and
taught moral skepticism very trenchantly and effectively.. .; it destroyed
the faith in his "virtues" in every single individual....
In the end, however, we have applied this same skepticism also to all
religious states and processes, such as sin, repentance, grace, sanctifica-
tion
39
Christian thought also surrounds God with so many if-clauses that God's
love became dubious:
What? A god who loves men, provided only that they believe in him, and
who casts an evil eye and threats upon anyone who does not believe in
this love? What? A love encapsuled in if-clauses attributed to an almighty
god? A love that has not even mastered the feelings of honor and vindic-
tiveness... ."If I love you, is that your concern?" is a sufficient critique of
the whole of Christianity.40
The human psychological motives that were projected into God have grown
to such an extent that God is conceived as having human motivations—even


104
COMIC RELIEF
the petty ones. Once God has absorbed our quirks as well as our virtues, the
time has come for humanity to realize the reason why we cannot distinguish
the divine mind from our own.
Why, then, have people believed in such a God for such a long time?
Nietzsche offers a psychological analysis of the authoritarian personality to
explain the appeal of the Judaic God of wrath:
[T]he Jews' enjoyment of their divine monarch and saint is similar to that
which the French nobility derived from Louis XIV. This nobility had sur-
rendered all of its power and sovereignty and had become contemptible.
In order not to feel this, in order to be able to forget this, one required
royal splendor, royal authority and plenitude of power without equal to
which only the nobility had access. By virtue of this privilege, one rose to
the height of the court, and from that vantage point one saw everything
beneath oneself and found it contemptible.. . . Thus the tower of the royal
power was built ever higher into the clouds, and one did not hold back
even the last remaining stones of one's own power.41
The structure of Feuerbach's analysis is again evident. In the case of both
God and the monarch, human beings allow themselves to become alienated
from their Own power. Feeling emasculated as a consequence, they identify
with the entity that has absorbed their power. The consequence is that even
more power is transferred to the latter.
Nietzsche thinks that natural occurrences themselves can encourage Chris-
tian monotheism, given the cultural habit of imposing supernatural inter-
pretations on experiences in the world. Satisfaction in one's natural experi-
ences can motivate a human being to believe that everything has turned out
for the best; and this belief can facilitate belief in "some petty deity who is
full of care and personally knows every little hair on our head and finds
nothing nauseous in the most miserable small service."42 This is, once again,
the vision of God as the "cosmic bellhop" or God as a switchboard opera-
tor.43
A particularly touching statement of the deficiency of this vision of God
was presented by a pubescent Liberian boy interviewed by a correspondent
for National Public Radio during the Liberian civil war. You feel like praying,
"Oh, God, please save me," according to the boy, but then you would look
around and see everyone else in bad shape, too. "That would not be a fair
prayer," the boy concluded.
Science as a Christian Myth
Nietzsche is apparently optimistic about the death of God, convinced that
humanity might emerge much better for it. Why then does he not simply
welcome a scientific worldview? Unfortunately, according to Nietzsche, the
modern attitude toward science, especially natural science, continues the


GOD IS DEAD
105
trappings of the Christian myth without the spiritual sustenance of myth.
Nietzsche is not averse to science, but he thinks that it needs to be conjoined
with a new myth if it is not to continue to impoverish the human spirit.
Hegel described unhappy consciousness as moving away from its dis-
tressed religious condition toward science and knowledge.44 Nietzsche seems
to concur that the inner tension that developed over the course of Christian
history has itself led to the contemporary world's fascination with science.
Contrary to the view that science has displaced religion by means of an
entirely different framework for interpreting human beings' position within
reality, Nietzsche contends that contemporary science is a new restoration of
Feuerbach's God.
The opening of Book III leads us to suspect that the "shadows of God"
will take an overtly religious form. We might expect that Nietzsche has in
mind our need to eliminate the practices that are no longer relevant if God
is dead—baptizing our children or saying "Thank God," for example.
The polemic that follows, however, is not directed against explicitly reli-
gious practices. Instead, it attacks the customs of the contemporary atheist
who is enraptured with science. Section 109 sermonizes against anthropo-
morphic images in science, using the parallel construction of preacherly or-
atory.45
Let us beware of thinking that the world is a living being. Where should
it expand? On what should it feed? How could it grow and multiply? We
have some notion of the nature of the organic; and we should not rein-
terpret the exceedingly derivative, late, rare, accidental, that we perceive
only on the crust of the earth and make of it something essential, universal,
and eternal, which is what those people do who call the universe an or-
ganism. This nauseates me. Let us even beware of believing that the uni-
verse is a machine: it is certainly not constructed for one purpose, and
calling it a "machine" does it far too much honor.46
Nietzsche goes on to urge wariness against "positing generally and every-
where anything as elegant as the cyclical movements of our neighboring
stars." The organic process of our order is "an exception of exceptions":
The total character of the world, however, is in all eternity chaos—in the
sense not of a lack of necessity but of a lack of order, arrangement, form,
beauty, wisdom, and whatever other names there are for our aesthetic
anthropomorphisms. ludged from the point of view of our reason, unsuc-
cessful attempts are by all odds the rule, the exceptions are not the secret
aim, and the whole musical box repeats eternally its tune which may never
be called a melody—and ultimately even the phrase "unsuccessful attempt"
is too anthropomorphic and reproachful. But how could we reproach or
praise the universe?47
Up to this point, we have had little clue as to why we should "beware"
these projections of order onto the natural world, but this last question about
praise and blame indicates one of Nietzsche's concerns. Our imagery en-


106
COMIC RELIEF
courages us to imagine that praise and blame are appropriately applied to
the natural scheme of things. It urges us, in other words, to project valuations
from ourselves onto the world. "Let us beware," Nietzsche continues,
of attributing to it heartlessness and unreason or their opposites: it is nei-
ther perfect nor beautiful, nor noble, nor does it wish to become any of
these things; it does not by any means strive to imitate man. None of our
aesthetic and moral judgments apply to it. Nor does it have any instinct
for self-preservation or any other instinct; and it does not observe any laws
either. Let us beware of saying that there are laws in nature.. .. Let us
beware of saying that death is opposed to life. The living is merely a type
of what is dead, and a very rare type.48
Order may emerge from chaos, but chaos is the world's basic character. The
world is no emblem for our ideals—nor does it conform to them. The uni-
verse does not have motivations akin to ours, whatever Schopenhauer's
"will" may suggest.49 The universe is not ordered in conformity to a plan,
as one long-standing answer to the problem of evil would have it. Death and
life are not warring alternatives, as we are accustomed to think from Chris-
tian imagery like that of "Christ Lag in Todesbanden."50 Life grows from
and returns to what is dead, not to what is alive.
Even materialism strikes Nietzsche as a theistic anthropomorphism:
Let us beware of thinking that the world eternally creates new things. There
are no eternally enduring substances; matter is as much of an error as the
God of the Eleatics. But when shall we ever be done with our caution and
care? When will all of these shadows of God cease to darken our minds?
When will we complete our de-deification of nature? When may we begin
to "naturalize" humanity in terms of a pure, newly discovered, newly re-
deemed nature?51
Nietzsche sees the same basic map that supported the Christian worldview
being employed in the modern "scientific" view of nature. The scientific
atheist speaking of conservation of matter (or matter/energy) describes eter-
nity differently from the traditional theist, but both share the view that fun-
damental reality is eternal. Similarly, the law of God has become the law of
nature; the will of God has become natural drives and tendencies; God's
everlastingness has become enduring matter; God's purposes have become
the emergent teleology of evolution. Mental habits conditioned by Christian
thought have thoroughly infiltrated our scientific discussions.
Feuerbach's analysis of God, Nietzsche suggests, can be applied to the
scientific worldview. In our scientific thought we project our own will, values,
projects, and ordering systems onto nature, and we then submit ourselves to
this picture. This "deification" of the natural order results in a diminution
of ourselves that is even more extreme than that effected by theism. At least
the theistic vision included an abstract nod in the direction of the human
being's value, the theory of the immortal soul. The scientific view eliminates
the immortal soul and so far has further emptied our conception of ourselves,


GOD IS DEAD
107
rendering us the fully subordinate subjects of a pattern over which we have
no control.
The trivialization of the human being by the scientific myth concerned
Nietzsche even in some of his earliest writings. He opened "On Truth and
Lies in a Nonmoral Sense," an unpublished essay of 1873, with the following
image:
Once upon a time, in some out of the way corner of that universe which
is dispersed into numberless twinkling solar systems, there was a star upon
which clever beasts invented knowing. That was the most arrogant and
mendacious minute of "world history," but nevertheless, it was only a
minute. After nature had drawn a few breaths, the star cooled and con-
gealed, and the clever beasts had to die.—One might invent such a fable,
and yet he still would not have adequately illustrated how miserable, how
shadowy and transient, how aimless and arbitrary the human intellect looks
within nature.52
Nietzsche's parable is a black-humored retelling of the Genesis story of the
fall of Adam and Eve, who choose the sin of eating the fruit that gives
knowledge of good and evil and in consequence lose Paradise, becoming
mortal. Nietzsche's story similarly shows the clever beasts inventing knowl-
edge, and subsequently having to die. It is more naturalistic than the Genesis
account, but hints at the deleterious consequence of our own "scientific"
knowing. By adopting the modern scientistic worldview, which treats myths
such as Genesis as dispensable, we have culturally lost "the immortal soul,"
and have come to see ourselves as but an undistinguished "minute" in the
scheme of things.
The current stage of our scientific worldview is this bleak, but Nietzsche
suggests that the Feuerbachian reversal could end this darkening of our
minds. "Our de-deification of nature" begins with our acknowledgment that
we have projected our own powers onto nature and then convinced ourselves
of our own relative impotence. When Nietzsche rhetorically urges the elim-
ination of theistic and anthropomorphic metaphors in science, he insists that
we recognize the indicated traits as our own.
Rather startlingly, Nietzsche sees Kant's Copernican revolution as a step
in this direction, though not one that Kant or anyone else has yet appreci-
ated.53 Kant reversed the West's impression of the human mind's relationship
to the natural world. The West had long believed that the mind is a passive
mirror for the forms impressed by the outside world. Kant contends instead
that our picture of the world, our "knowledge," is shaped by the structures
of our own minds. Section no is a Nietzschean extrapolation from Kant:
Over immense periods of time the intellect produced nothing but errors.
A few of these proved to be useful and helped to preserve the species: those
who hit upon or inherited these had better luck in their struggle for them-
selves and their progeny. Such erroneous articles of faith, which were con-
tinually inherited, until they became almost part of the basic endowment
of the species, include the following: that there are enduring things; that


Io8
COMIC RELIEF
there are equal things; that there are things, substances, bodies; that a thing
is what it appears to be; that our will is free; that what is good for me is
also good in itself.54
Nietzsche goes further than Kant in his inclusion of moral values as pro-
jection of our own nature. Indeed, since Kant is a defender of free will,
Nietzsche suggests that Kant himself miscategorized to the extent that he
attributed freedom to our noumenal selves in resolving the antinomy of
freedom and determinism, and was not content to view freedom simply as
a postulate constructed by our minds, the position that Kant defends else-
where. Nevertheless, Nietzsche seems to praise Kant's vision as a leap in the
evolution of self-awareness when he continues:
It was only very late that such propositions were denied and doubted; it
was only very late that truth emerged—as the weakest form of knowledge.
It seemed that one was unable to live with it: our organism was prepared
for the opposite; all its higher functions, sense perception and every kind
of sensation worked with those basic errors which had been incorporated
since time immemorial. Indeed, even in the realm of knowledge these prop-
ositions became the norms according to which "true" and "untrue" were
determined—down to the most remote regions of logic.55
Kant had precursors who challenged the instilled "errors" on which knowl-
edge depends, and they helped to launch the West's series of debates about
truth and error. Renewing his suggestion that Wissenschaft need not be pon-
derous, Nietzsche claims that this debate itself was spurred by multiple mo-
tives, including the impulse to play.56
[T]here was room for the expression of an intellectual play impulse, and
honesty and skepticism were innocent and happy like all play. Gradually,
the human brain became full of such judgments and convictions, and a
ferment, struggle, and lust for power developed in this tangle. Not only
utility and delight but every kind of impulse took sides in this fight about
"truths." The intellectual fight became an occupation, an attraction, a pro-
fession, a duty, something dignified—-and eventually knowledge and the
striving for the true found their place as a need among other needs.57
Once knowledge was a need, it "became a piece of life itself, and hence a
continually growing power—until eventually knowledge collided with those
primeval basic errors."58 Nietzsche's list of primeval errors includes many
"fictions" or exaggerations that make life appear more orderly than it is.
These errors idealize and simplify reality, just as art does, allowing human
beings greater confidence in their own power over the environment. The
errors present things as stable, and allow us to "treat as equal what is merely
similar."59 These notions are distortions, but they provided the preconditions
for the possibility of logic, and for developing our intellectual abilities as we
have. In this way, they have served the species, helping it to survive:
The beings that did not see so precisely had an advantage over those that
saw everything "in flux." ... No living beings would have survived if the


GOD IS DEAD
109
opposite tendency—to affirm rather than suspend judgment, to err and
make up things rather than wait, to assent rather than negate, to pass
judgment rather than be just—had not been bred to the point where it
became extraordinarily strong.60
The speed of these mechanisms is so great that they are concealed from
us.61 Yet Kant has shown the modern world that these obvious appearances
are functions of our own mental operations, not accurate reflections of reality
in itself. But by traditional reckoning, our basic cognitive functions, failing
to mirror reality, are errors, as Nietzsche observes:
We have arranged for ourselves a world in which we can live—by positing
bodies, lines, planes, causes and effects, motion and rest, form and content;
without these articles of faith nobody now could endure life. But that does
not prove them. Life is no argument. The conditions of life might include
The Kantian revolution presents our cognitive moves as rendering the world
in our own image. Nietzsche's restatement emphasizes the projection that
cognition involves, in a manner recalling Feuerbach's analysis of God:
[H]ow could we possibly explain anything? We operate only with things
that do not exist: lines, planes, bodies, atoms, divisible time spans, divisible
spaces. How should explanations be at all possible when we first turn every-
thing into an image, our image!
It will do to consider science as an attempt to humanize things as faith-
fully as possible; as we describe things and their one-after-another, we learn
how to describe ourselves more and more precisely. Cause and effect: such
a duality probably never exists. ... An intellect that could see cause and
effect as a continuum and a flux and not, as we do, in terms of an arbitrary
division and dismemberment, would repudiate the concept of cause and
effect and deny all conditionally.63
We are still sustaining ourselves through faith, Nietzsche contends, even
if we abandon the Christian God and adhere to nature instead. Nietzsche
urges us to acknowledge our own authorship of our current myths, those of
science as well as those of religion. We are self-deceived when we fantasize
that with scientific thought we escape faith—or the religious trap that Feu-
erbach described. Indeed, religious thought has at times been more conducive
than modern science to reowning our own freedom and creativity. In poly-
theistic thought, for example:
It was here that the luxury of individuals was first permitted; it was here
that one first honored the rights of individuals.... In polytheism the free-
spiriting and many-spiriting of man attained its first preliminary form—
the strength to create for ourselves our own new eyes.. .."
In science, by contrast, we are not as easily able to see ourselves, since we
rely on "incorporated errors" that are so efficient that they are virtually
unobservable.
error62


110
COMIC RELIEF
Nietzsche hints in Book III that the aim of "naturalizing humanity in
