terms of a pure, newly discovered, newly redeemed nature" is a long-term
project. Reflecting on science historically, he urges us to see the development
of science not as the growth of knowledge about the world, but as a gradual
discovery of our own nature.65
Nietzsche's aim, which is further articulated in Book IV, is to reclaim the
energies involved in both religion and science and to recognize them all as
jointly operating within our own "naturalized" humanity. The reappropria-
tion of our own traits precisely is the naturalization of our humanity.
The fully naturalized humanity is not the reduced, "lifeless" object of
science but the organic conjunction of artistic, practical, and scientific en-
ergies:
And even now the time seems remote when artistic energies and the prac-
tical wisdom of life will join with scientific thinking to form a higher
organic system in relation to which scholars, physicians, artists, and legis-
lators—as we know them at present—would have to look like paltry relics
of ancient times.66
The gay science, ultimately, would be the exploration of the world by this
larger organism, the naturalized human being. This being would be the free
spirit, pursuing spirituality freely.
The energies that religion has engaged are among those to be newly ex-
plored on this agenda. Nietzsche's critique of Christianity in Book III is not
part of a reductionistic quest to eliminate religious terms or to treat the
energies that function in religion as illusory.67 Nietzsche's critique challenges
the grip that our self-denigrating theological habits still have within Western
thought, in the hope that "man will rise ever higher as soon as he ceases to
flow out into a god."68
Was Zarathustra a Lutheran?
We are now in a position to make sense of Nietzsche's divergent remarks
about Martin Luther, both in The Gay Science and in other works as well.69
Nietzsche applauds Luther as authentically spiritual and courageous, but de-
nounces his views that disparage humanity. Luther's status in Nietzsche's
thought reflects Nietzsche's basic ambivalence about religion, the Christian
religion in particular.
Nietzsche's earliest works present Luther as a hero. Referring to the
German spirit's need to reawaken its dormant primordial energies, he in-
cludes Luther as a precursor, describing his chorales as Dionysian: "So deep,
courageous, and spiritual, so exuberantly good and tender did this chorale
of Luther sound as the first Dionysian luring call breaking forth from dense
thickets at the approach of spring."70 Nietzsche praises Luther as a musician
here, suggesting that his music sprang from felt experience much as did
German mythology. The assertion of independence from the Catholic


GOD IS DEAD
III
Church's demands for conformity in religious expression also meets with
Nietzsche's enthusiasm. Max L. Baeumer notes that Nietzsche's various early
passages of praise for Luther treat Luther as a political and cultural hero, not
a religious one.71 In this respect, Nietzsche treats Luther the way he treats
many historical figures, as a quasi-theatrical hero whose character is of
greater significance than his political or historical achievement. Nietzsche also
at times suggests his admiration for Luther's courage.
In The Gay Science and elsewhere, however, Nietzsche expresses disagree-
ment with Luther's religious views and criticism of his historical achieve-
ments.72 His comments about Luther localize his critique of Christianity,
demonstrating its relevance for those who share his religious background. By
making his objections to key tenets in Luther's theology apparent, he pre-
empts his German readers from taking his critique of Christianity to be in
keeping with Luther's worldview (Luther, after all, criticized Christendom
himself). For this reason, I think it is worthwhile to illustrate Nietzsche's
stated differences with Luther, even though I agree with Kaufmann that
Nietzsche treats Christianity as of a piece, not favoring Protestantism over
Catholicism.73 (Indeed, in this respect, Nietzsche's stance does resemble that
of Luther, who sought to restore Christianity as a whole to its original con-
dition, not to establish a new and improved religious sect.)74
Luther's noteworthy emphases are among the tendencies of Christianity
that Nietzsche explicitly targets in The Gay Science, Book III. Luther's doc-
trines include the claims that:
1. Human beings are inherently depraved.
2. God is a wrathful judge, who demanded the Crucifixion as atonement
for humanity's sins.
3. Faith alone justifies the human being.
4. Everyone can be a part of "the priesthood of all believers" and can
interpret Scripture without priestly mediation.
5. The continuation of Judaism is a blasphemy against God.
I will summarize Luther's rationales for each of these convictions and then
indicate Nietzsche's rebuttals.
Human Beings are Inherently Depraved.
Although Luther denies that the human will is "by nature evil," be contends
that it is "nevertheless innately and inevitably evil and corrupt" and therefore
"not free to strive toward whatever is declared good."75 The human being is
therefore unable to secure his or her own salvation.
Luther insists that human beings are inherently conflicted, a battleground
of the flesh and the spirit:
Man has a twofold nature, a spiritual and a bodily one. According to the
spiritual nature, which men refer to as the soul, he is called a spiritual,
inner, or new man. According to the bodily nature, which men refer to as
flesh, he is called a carnal, outward, or old man.. . . Because of this diversity


112
COMIC RELIEF
of nature the Scriptures assert contradictory things concerning the same
man, since these two men in the same man contradict each other, "for the
desires of the flesh are against the Spirit, and the desires of the Spirit are
against the flesh," according to Gal. s[:i7].7s
This conflict between the outer and inner human being has implications
for Luther's political views. The true Christian, whose will is completely sub-
ordinated to God's, does the right thing without coercion. The Christian,
therefore, does not really need the temporal law. Very few, however, are true
Christians, and most people follow the dictates of the flesh, not the spirit.
Thus, temporal authority is necessary.77
Although Luther denounces princes who exploit their subjects, he under-
writes their power nonetheless and posits the citizen's absolute duty to obey
the prince. Although war is often justified, according to Luther,78 this applies
only to defensive wars between equals. Subjects are never justified in waging
war against a sovereign, and the latter has every right to quash insurrections
such as the current Peasants' War: "We dare not encourage the mob very
much. It goes mad quickly.... And it is better for the tyrants to wrong them
a hundred times than for the mob to treat the tyrant unjustly but once."79
Luther's denigration of human beings and the fleshly part of their nature
is diametrically opposed to the Feuerbachian vision that Nietzsche endorses.
Luther is among Nietzsche's targets when he remarks, "The Christian resolve
to find the world ugly and bad has made the world ugly and bad."80 Nietzsche
particularly criticizes those who, like Saint Paul, "have an evil eye for the
passions: all they know of the passions is what is dirty, disfiguring, and
heartbreaking; hence their idealistic tendency aims at the annihilation of the
passions... ."81 Nietzsche directs this line of criticism at Luther when he
criticizes the latter's denunciation of mass uprising. Luther himself, Nietzsche
claims, was more "common man" than he realized:
He smashed an ideal that he could not attain, while he seemed to abhor
and to be fighting only against the degeneration of this ideal. Actually, he,
the man who had found it impossible to be a monk, pushed away the
dominion of the homines religiosi, and thus he himself made within the
ecclesiastical social order what in relation to the civic social order he at-
tacked so intolerantly—namely a "peasant rebellion."82
God Is a Wrathful Judge, Who Demanded the Crucifixion as
Atonement for Humanity's Sins.
Human beings crave evil as a consequence of original sin, according to Lu-
ther, and God's just response to this and to more recent sins is wrath. Jaroslav
Pelikan observes that Luther considered it a corollary of God's omnipotence
and eternal nature that "his fury or wrath toward smug sinners is also im-
measurable and infinite."83 Luther describes divine law itself in terms of
wrath; it is "a light that illumines and shows, not the grace of God or right-


GOD IS DEAD
113
eousness and life but the wrath of God, sin, death, our damnation in the
sight of God, and hell."84
God's wrath is a reflection of his absolute justice, according to Luther,
and God's justice requires Christ's suffering and crucifixion to "pay and
make satisfaction for" the sins of humanity.85 Christ the crucified was the
victor over death; and this Luther considered the "chief doctrine of the Chris-
tian faith."86 Luther even defines the term gospel in reference to crucifixion
and atonement. The authentic theologian, according to Luther, "compre-
hends the visible and manifest things of God seen through suffering and the
cross."87
In Nietzsche's view, the Christian conception of sin and the wrathful God
serves to divert attention from the reality of the natural world toward God:
"[EJvery deed is to be considered solely with respect to its supernatural con-
sequences, without regard for its natural consequences .. ,"88 Nietzsche makes
the notion of atonement the object of barbed humor in Book III of The Gay
Science:
If God wished to become an object of love, he should have given up judging
and justice first of all; a judge, even a merciful judge, is no object of love.89
Buddha says: "Do not flatter your benefactor!" Repeat this saying in a
Christian church: right away it clears the air of everything Christian.90
Faith Alone Justifies the Human Being.
Luther's concern with justification, the restoration of the appropriate rela-
tionship between the human soul and God, grew out of his objection to
indulgences. Luther's initial complaint was against the excesses of the indul-
gence system, which often amounted to extortion of funds from the laity to
support the clergy, usually extracted by presenting the "indulgence" granted
to almsgivers as the only route of escape from a horror-filled afterlife. Lu-
ther's objection eventually extended to the theological justification of the
entire practice of indulgences. He saw this practice as designed to bribe God
and to diffuse divine justice by means of human efforts.
The papal bull that defended indulgences in the face of Luther's original
attack used the notion of a treasury of merit over which the church had
authority. Because Christ and the saints had vastly more merit than they
needed for their own salvation, their extra merits were, in effect, banked in
this treasury. The church could transfer these merits to remit a sinner's tem-
poral punishment (punishment that divine justice required for sins com-
mitted, measured in terms of time in Purgatory). Indulgences were transfers
of this nature, and Christians could gain these through specific prayers and
practices (including almsgiving). Such transfers could work to the benefit of
the living or of the dead. Hence, the practice of earning indulgences for a
deceased loved one affected the amount of time he or she would spend in
Purgatory.


114
COMIC RELIEF
Luther was appalled by this application of accounting techniques to mat-
ters of salvation. It struck him as a hubristic assertion that the church could
barter with God. Forgiveness was a matter of God's power alone, according
to Luther. Human beings could do nothing to ensure it.
"Justification," in Luther's theology, is a matter of God's assertion of
justice within the soul of the human being. Although God's active justice
involves his imposition of punishment and reward in accordance with what
was deserved, God also displays "passive justice" toward human beings. "Pas-
sive justice" involves God's imparting justice into the soul of the person who
has faith in the historical actuality and personal significance of Christ's life,
death, and resurrection.91 Although faith is indispensable, justification is not
the consequence of human effort. Luther asserts the complete passivity of
the human being, who simply accepts, on faith, what God provides.92
This emphasis on human passivity is also evident in Luther's account of
grace. Luther rejected the scholastic definition, which held that grace was a
quality that God infused into the person. According to Luther, grace was
God's act of forgiveness, which restored a good relationship between the
sinner and God, and enabled the ongoing operation of the Holy Spirit on
the soul to proceeds.93
A corollary to the claim that faith alone justifies is Luther's insistence
that human efforts have no effect on salvation. Luther holds that it is sinful
to perform even the best of works with the aim of securing salvation.94
Christians will inevitably perform good works, according to Luther, but
these must flow spontaneously from the justified soul, not the other way
around.
Luther emphasizes the impotence of human beings in comparison with
God, and he stresses the importance of humility. Human confidence in rea-
son is dangerous and hubristic, according to Luther: "The blindness of hu-
man reason is so incomprehensible and infinite that it cannot form sound
judgments even about life and works, much less about the doctrine of
faith."95 Luther did allow that the soul could cooperate with God, but he
uses the Aristotelian image of the pregnant woman as mere substratum for
reproduction to stress that the believer is but passive material for God:
"Therefore even though we pray and beg for grace, yet when grace does
come and the soul is to be impregnated with the Spirit, it ought neither pray
nor act, but only be still."96
We have seen that Nietzsche considers the monotheistic God as a self-
impoverishing projection of humanity, and the Lutheran doctrine of "justi-
fication by faith alone" strikes Nietzsche as a further drain on humanity's
recognition of its own powers. Nietzsche hints at the conflict between the
Feuerbachian view and Luther's in his comic remark on "the conditions for
God":
"God himself cannot exist without wise people," said Luther with good
reason. But "God can exist even less without unwise people"—that our
good Luther did not say.97


GOD IS DEAD
115
Luther was onto something with his notion that faith justifies, Nietzsche
admits, but only insofar as this is a point of naturalized psychology. Belief,
as William James also observed, makes people feel good, but this conse-
quence does not depend on the truth of what is believed. Moreover, Nietz-
sche contends, faith often indicates a lack of intellectual virtue:
Virtue bestows happiness and a kind of bliss only on those who have not
lost their faith in their virtue—not on those subtler souls whose virtue
consists in a profound mistrust of themselves and of all virtue. Ultimately,
then, "faith makes blessed" here, too, and not—mark it well—virtue.98
In a note, Nietzsche also asks whether Luther's emphasis on "faith alone,"
as opposed to good works, is not an indication of his lack of virtue: "Faith
is a profound conviction on the part of Luther and his kind of their inca-
pacity for Christian works....""
As for human pride in reason, Nietzsche submits that this is not the only,
or even the primary, motive for abandoning Christianity. "What is now de-
cisive against Christianity is our taste, no longer our reasons."100
Everyone Can Be a Part of "the Priesthood of all Believers"
and Can Interpret Scripture without Priestly Mediation.
Luther rejects the Catholic view that the clergy has a special mediating role
with regard to salvation. Strictly speaking, Christ was the only true priest,
Luther claims, for he alone mediated between God and man. But in a broader
sense, one can speak of a "priesthood of all believers," since every believer
can help others to remain strong in their faith.
Luther eliminated the mediating role of the clergy not only with respect
to salvation but also in the interpretation of the Scriptures. Luther was
pleased by the simplicity of his doctrine. Jaroslav Pelikan notes that "Luther
felt able to boast that as a consequence of the Reformation 'a seven-year-old
child knows what the church is.' "101
Nietzsche considers Luther's democratization of religion, his elimination
of the clergy's mediating role, as psychologically disastrous. The average per-
son is not infused with the spiritual insight that Luther imagines:
[T]he religious person is an exception in every religion ...; the poor in
spirit do not know what to do, and if one forbade them their prayer-rattling
one would deprive them of their religion—as Protestantism shows us
more and more by day. What religion wants from the masses is no more
than that they should keep still with their eyes, hands, legs, and other or-
gans. . . ,102
In Book V of The Gay Science, Nietzsche dismisses Luther's democratic
innovations in summary fashion:
Today it is easy enough to see how in all cardinal questions of power
Luther's disposition was calamitously myopic, superficial, and incautious.
He was a man of the common people who lacked everything that one


Il6
COMIC RELIEF
might inherit from a ruling caste; he had no instinct for power. Thus his
work, his will to restore that Roman work became, without his knowing
or willing it, nothing but the beginning of a work of destruction. He un-
raveled, he tore up with honest wrath what the old spider had woven so
carefully for such a long time.103
Despite Nietzsche's own praise for individualism, he sees Luther's individu-
alistic emphasis as counterproductive, even with respect to Luther's own
goals. "He surrendered the holy books to everybody—until they finally got
into the hands of the philologists, who are the destroyers of every faith that
rests on books."104 Luther's move propelled another saga of Christianity un-
dermining itself, for by promoting the direct interpretation of Scripture by
non-clerics, Luther facilitated the eventual deflation of Scripture by the his-
torical theologians of Nietzsche's own century. Moreover, the idea that each
soul stands in direct relationship to God, making individual conscience the
ultimate religious authority, undercut "the church," another institution dear
to Luther:
He destroyed the concept of the "church" by throwing away the faith in
the inspiration of the church councils; for the concept of the "church"
retains its power only on condition that the inspiring spirit that founded
the church still lives in it, builds in it, and continues to build its
house.104
Psychologically, Luther's notion of a "universal priesthood of believers"
was incoherent.
He gave back to the priest sexual intercourse with woman; but three quar-
ters of the reverence of which the common people, especially the women
among the common people, are capable, rests on the faith that a person
who is an exception at this point will be an exception in other respects as
well.... Luther, having given the priest woman, had to take away from
him auricular confession; that was right psychologically. With that devel-
opment the Christian priest was, at bottom, abolished, for his most pro-
found utility had always been that he was a holy ear, a silent well, a grave
for secrets. "Everyone his own priest"—behind such formulas and their
peasant cunning there was hidden in Luther the abysmal hatred against
"the higher human being" and the dominion of "the higher human beings"
as conceived by the church.105
In sum, Luther's democratization of Christianity amounts to a formula for
damaging the inner life of the common person, who is not equal to the
challenge:
There is a human being who has turned out badly.... Such a person
who is fundamentally ashamed of his existence—perhaps he also harbors
a few little vices—and on the other hand cannot keep himself from be-
coming more and more spoiled and irritable by reading books to which
he is not entitled or by associating with more spiritual company than he
can digest; such a human being who has become poisoned through and


GOD IS DEAD
through ... eventually ends up in a state of habitual revenge, will to re-
venge.106
This will to revenge, according to Nietzsche, is always seeking a target.
Tie Continuation of Judaism Is a Blasphemy against God.
Luther was profoundly anti-Semitic. Outraged that the Jews did not endorse
Christianity's doctrines, he wrote:
What shall we Christians do now with this depraved and damned people
of the Jews?... I will give my faithful advice. First, that one should set fire
to their synagogues. ... Then that one should also break down and destroy
their houses.. .. That one should drive them out of the country.107
Bernhard Lohse associates Luther's hostility toward Judaism with his
apocalyptic notions, which became prominent in the latter part of his life:
[H]e thought that the end of the world was very near. Precisely for that
reason, Luther felt that the government should do everything it could to
delay the divine judgment as long as possible. When this strong apocalyptic
expectation is taken into account, we can understand why Luther, in his
last years, asserted that Jewish worship services should be forbidden. Lu-
ther, like many of his contemporaries, felt that what happened in the syn-
agogues was public blasphemy. They felt that such blasphemy had to be
prevented, otherwise they would call down God's immediate judgment
upon themselves. .. .10S
Lohse adds that "it is particularly noteworthy that Luther originally encoun-
tered the Jews in a much more open way." Nevertheless, he acknowledges
that "later in life... he spoke out against them with such vehemence that
leading National Socialists at the time of Hitler felt they were entitled to
claim Luther as a patron of their persecution of the Jews."109
In response to such attacks on Judaism in the name of Christianity, Nietz-
sche analyzes Christianity as a Jewish sect. Some of Luther's favorite "Chris-
tian" doctrines are actually his inheritance from Judaism. For example, sin,
the primary tool of Christianity's unification of the world, is a Jewish con-
ceptual innovation:
Sin, as it is now experienced wherever Christianity holds sway or has held
sway, is a Jewish feeling and a Jewish invention. Regarding this background
of all Christian morality, Christianity did aim to "Judaize" the world.110
The notion of a supremely powerful God is also of Jewish origin.111 Nietzsche
goes so far as to say that "[a] Jesus Christ was possible only in a Jewish
landscape—I mean one over which the gloomy and sublime thundercloud
of the wrathful Jehovah was brooding continually."112 Luther's emphasis on
the wrathful God, on this analysis, is a Judaic aspect of his doctrines. Saint
Paul, on whom Luther particularly relied (along with Augustine) in his ac-
count of sin and atonement, continued Jewish tradition in seeking the an-
117


Il8
COMIC RELIEF
nihilation of the passions as an antidote to sin: "—and the Christians?"
Nietzsche concludes. "Did they want to become Jews in this respect? Did
they perhaps succeed?"113
Nietzsche's counter to such anti-Semitical analyses as Luther's is to mimic
anti-Semitic slurs in his characterization of Christianity. For example, he
remarks that the Judaic image of the wrathful God is conceptually at odds
with the Christian notion of the law of love, but that Jesus did not notice
the discrepancy. His explanation: "The founder of Christianity was not re-
fined enough in his feelings at this point—being a Jew."114 Such remarks
may strike us as tasteless, but they challenge the anti-Semite's smug oppo-
sition of Christianity and Judaism. Although he is critical of both religious
traditions (seeing them as variations on a theme), Nietzsche is not anti-
Semitic in the usual sense (at least in Europe) of the term (Christians dis-
paraging Jews). In his remarks about the Jewish origins of Christianity, Nietz-
sche confronts Christian anti-Semites by implying, "Consider your
arguments against Judaism: they apply to your own belief as well."
The features of Christianity that Nietzsche targets are among those that Lu-
ther saw as most central. Arguably, then, Nietzsche's vision of Christianity
is distinctively Protestant (although he certainly does not favor Catholic or
Orthodox Christianity). Nevertheless, Nietzsche differs from Luther on al-
most all his signature doctrines. The one theoretical convergence they seem
to share is the view that politics should not be turned over to the masses.
This similarity might provide some insight into the origins of Nietzsche's
preference for authoritarianism over democracy and socialism; but this is
hardly a point of religious agreement.
Nietzsche is not hostile toward Luther as a historical figure, but neither
does he see him as tremendously foresighted. He argues, for instance, that
Luther should not be blamed for the developments he precipitated.
What afterward grew out of his Reformation, good as well as bad, might
be calculated approximately today; but who would be naive enough to
praise or blame Luther on account of these consequences? He is innocent
of everything; he did not know what he was doing.115
Even this seeming defense of Luther is not without barbs. As one who did
not know what he was doing, Luther is rhetorically relegated to the position
of those who crucified Christ. (Might this also be a jab at an anti-Semitic
view that the Jews were responsible for the Crucifixion?)
Nietzsche rejects Luther's religious views so thoroughly that he may have
had Luther in mind when he comments: "I do not want to be a holyman;
sooner even a buffoon. Perhaps I am a buffoon."116 The term translated
"buffoon" here is Hanswurst, and this leads me to wonder whether Nietzsche
was recalling Luther's polemical "Against Hanswurst" (1541). The Hanswurst
involved was Duke Henry of Braunschweig-Wolfenbuttel, who had written
against the Protestants and had commissioned a similar work which falsely
charged that Luther had called his own prince a buffoon. This claim was an


GOD IS DEAD
Iig
affront to both Luther's politics and his religious movement, and his re-
sponse, "Against Hanswurst," was Luther at his most vituperative. Of Duke
Henry he wrote,
He curses, blasphemes, shrieks, struggles, bellows, and spits, so that, if
people really heard him utter these words, they would gather with chains
and bars, just as if (like the man in the gospels [Mark 5:1-10]) he were
possessed by a legion of devils and had to be seized and bound.
He also remarks: "And that vulgar boor, blockhead, and lout from Wolfen-
biittel, that ass to cap all asses, screams his heehaws, judges, and calls men
heretical."117
Lohse observes that Luther's style was rather extreme even for the period.
Crude polemics were taken for granted on all sides of the issue in the
sixteenth century and were never unique to Luther. Even the controversies
in the late Middle Ages were crude in ways that are hard to match. Having
said this, it must also be said that Luther surpassed most of his contem-
poraries in the coarseness and roughness of his language. The same coarse-
ness and roughness also characterized Luther's language as he sat around
his dining-room table, as his published table talk indicates. This charac-
teristic is, however, particularly prominent in his writings. The longer Lu-
ther was engaged in controversy, the stronger his polemical attitude and
his ridiculing of his current opponent seemed to be.118
On this last point, Nietzsche seems to have followed Luther's example—
and not only on this point. For all of Nietzsche's disagreement with Luther's
doctrines, he admires Luther as an artist. Luther is one of his literary ex-
emplars, if also a literary rival. Nietzsche wrote to his friend Erwin Rohde.
From you, however, as a homo literatus, I will not keep back a confession—
it is my theory that with this Z I have brought the German language to a
state of perfection. After Luther and Goethe, a third step had to be taken—
look and see, old chum of mine, if vigor, flexibility, and euphony have
ever consorted so well in our language.... My line is superior to his [Goe-
the's] in strength and manliness, without becoming, as Luther's did, lout-
ish.119
Nietzsche pays homage to both Luther's authorial prowess and his au-
thentic spirituality by employing Lutheran imagery in his own suggestions
for an alternative approach to spirituality. Nietzsche's allusions to Luther's
terminology is a case of his attempting to address the spiritual needs of his
audience while rejecting the traditional answers given by Christianity, in its
Lutheran as well as other forms. His Lutheran vocabulary signals his inten-
tion to deal with the same needs that Christianity addressed, but in a new,
nontraditional way.
Some of Nietzsche's signature formulations borrow from Luther's theo-
logical imagery (albeit some derive ultimately from Scripture). For example,
the hammer that Nietzsche enlists later when he "philosophizes with a ham-
mer" is an image borrowed from Luther.120 Luther makes much of Jeremiah
23:29, "My word is a hammer which breaks the rock in pieces." According


120
COMIC RELIEF
to Luther, the inner war between flesh and spirit leads to despair. God uses
a "hammer" and anvil at this point to smash the sinner's pride, bringing
despair to a high pitch, at which point God imparts grace to the sinner.
[T]his presumption of righteousness is a huge and horrible monster. To
break and crush it, God needs a huge and powerful hammer, that is the
Law which is the hammer of death, the thunder of hell, and the lightning
of divine wrath.... Then the heart is crushed to the point of despair.121
For God wounds in order to heal. . . ,122
Nietzsche's latter association of the hammer with the hardness of a diamond
is also akin to another image from Luther. Complaining about the ecclesi-
astical authorities of Catholicism, Luther remarks.
We say the same thing about the canons. If they left them optional, we
would tolerate them; but because they make them binding, we utterly reject
them and are harder than an anvil and a diamond.123
Although retaining Luther's metaphors, Nietzsche recasts them in a man-
ner suited to his Feuerbachian analysis. He, the human being, now holds the
hammer and uses it to "sound out idols," gods that are not gods, checking
for hollowness. He stresses that the primary idol he has in mind is the Chris-
tian God.
This little essay is a great declaration of war; and regarding the sounding
out of idols, this time they are not just idols of the age, but eternal idols,
which are here touched with a hammer as with a tuning fork: there are
altogether no older, no more convinced, no more puffed-up idols—and
none more hollow. That does not prevent them from being those in which
people have the most faith; nor does one ever say "idol," especially not in
the most distinguished instance.124
Later, in Ecce Homo, Nietzsche makes it clear that he intends the hammer
to be a tool of destruction as well as a tuning tool. "Among the conditions
for a Dionysian task are, in a decisive way, the hardness of the hammer, the
joy even in destroying."125 The application of the hammer to the idol of
Christianity is precisely the step that Nietzsche urges his contemporaries to
take. He begins the effort to restore human power by casting a human being
as the judge of God, not the other way around.
Nietzsche's recurrent theme of human masks may be a related reversal of
a Lutheran image, that of God wearing "masks."126 Pelikan elaborates:
The true God wanted to "remain hidden and do everything behind his
masks";127 all of creation was such a "mask" of God as were the offices
and structures of human society. It was necessary to distinguish between
God and his masks. God was "a supernatural, inscrutable being who exists
at the same time in every seed, whole and entire, and yet also in all and
above all and outside all created things. . . . Nothing is so small but God is
still smaller, nothing so large but God is still larger."128 To grasp this God,


GOD IS DEAD
121
who was everywhere and yet nowhere, faith was summoned to look not at
his "masks," but at his revelation.129
On Nietzsche's Feuerbachian scheme, human beings are the mask wearers
and God the obscurantist mask.
Nietzsche frequently describes relative spiritual health in terms of affir-
mation and negation, the very terms in which Luther describes the soul in
relation to God's grace. At the height of despair, according to Luther, the
sinner's soul discovers "deeper than No, and above it, the deep, mysterious
Yes."130 Nietzsche uses the same image in describing the faith that grounds
his own opposition to Christianity.
[W]e have also outgrown Christianity and are averse to it—precisely be-
cause we have grown out of it, because our ancestors were Christians who
in their Christianity were uncompromisingly upright: for their faith they
willingly sacrificed possessions and position, blood and fatherland. We—
do the same. For what? For our unbelief? For every kind of unbelief? No,
you know better than that, friend! The hidden Yes in you is stronger than
all Nos and Maybes that afflict you and your age like a disease; and when
you have to embark on the sea, you emigrants, you, too, are compelled to
this by—a faith."1
The terms of "Yes" or "No" are also basic to the image of eternal recurrence
that appears near the end of The Gay Science, Book IV, which we will con-
sider in Chapter 6.
Luther's image that good works flow from the soul that God fills with the
spirit of affirmation is similarly recast by Nietzsche. Nietzsche's naturalistic
ideal is the spirit who is so rich in inner resources that it "overflows." Nietz-
sche characterizes those who are noble as having "a self-sufficiency that over-
flows and gives to men and things."132 In one of his notes, Nietzsche suggests
that this conception of overflowing is inherently at odds with the Christian
advocacy of sacrifice: "Philosophic des Uberfliissigen. Gegen die Aufopferung
als schadlich auf die Dauer. [Philosophy of the overflowing. Against sacrifice
as detrimental in the long run.]"133
Late in Book V of The Gay Science, Nietzsche explicitly describes his ideal
for the human spirit as "overflowing":
Another ideal runs ahead of us ...: the ideal of a spirit who plays naively—
that is, not deliberately but from overflowing power and abundance—with
all that was hitherto called holy, good, untouchable, divine; for whom those
supreme things that the people naturally accept as their standards, signify
danger, decay, debasement, or at least recreation, blindness, and temporary
self-oblivion; the ideal of a human, superhuman well-being and benevo-
lence that will often appear inhuman ... it is perhaps only with him that
great seriousness really begins, that the real question mark is posed for the
first time, that the destiny of the soul changes, the hand moves forward,
the tragedy begins.134


122
COMIC RELIEF
"The tragedy begins" is the title of the last section of the first edition of
The Gay Science. The hero of the section is Zarathustra, apparently the image
of this alternative ideal. Before considering why Nietzsche sees Zarathustra
as an emblem of a spiritual alternative to Christianity, as well as the initiator
of tragedy, however, Nietzsche sketches his own account of the great Yes—
eternal recurrence.


Become Who You Are
The latter part of Book III consists of a variety of psychologically acute
aphorisms, as if to demonstrate the process of exploring the human world
(particularly human behavior) in a naturalistic vein, without reference to a
superterrestrial realm. For example:
A: One is praised only by one's peers." B: "Yes, and whoever praises you
says: I am your peer."1
Must not anyone who wants to move the crowd be an actor who imper-
sonates himself? Must he not first translate himself into grotesque obvi-
ousness and then present his whole person and cause in this coarsened and
simplified version?2
Some do not know how rich they are until they experience how rich people
steal from them.3
The last several aphorisms take the form of questions and answers, in the
manner of a catechism, including:
In what do you believe?—In this, that the weights of all things must be
determined anew.
What does your conscience say?—"You shall become the person you
are." ...
Whom do you call bad?—Those who always want to put to shame.
What do you consider most humane?—To spare someone shame.
What is the seal of liberation?—No longer being ashamed in front of one-
self.4
Aphorism 270 employs the metaphor of becoming who you are, an image
of which Nietzsche is fond. For example, the subtitle of his autobiography
Ecce Homo is "How One Becomes What One Is." The line is taken from
Pindar's second Pythian Ode, line 72.5
123
INTERLUDE 4


124
COMIC RELIEF
The passage in Pindar is intriguing. The ode from which it is taken is
unique among the Pythian Odes, which are primarily encomiums celebrating
some victory in the Olympian games. This ode does not mention a particular
game, which has led some scholars to doubt that it was written on the oc-
casion of any specific game.6 Some have also speculated that the ode may
have been written on the occasion of a chariot victory when Hieron com-
missioned Baccylides, not Pindar, to write the official ode of celebration. On
this view, the admonition to Hieron, the tyrant of Syracuse, is motivated by
literary rivalry. Others, however, consider such a public display of envy to
be improbable. William H. Race remarks:
[A]lluding to such matters in a public ode on a ceremonial occasion would
be in remarkably bad taste and would offend the very patron one was
praising.
Instead, scholars have come to realize that Pindar never portrays just
the bright side of life, but is always conscious of the dark side with its
treachery, pain, and failure. From this awareness stems his frequent use of
negative examples that act as "foil" to highlight by contrast the precious-
ness of success in a world so prone to vice.7
In any case, Pythian Ode II makes a personal, impassioned appeal to
Hieron, calling on him to resist the pressures of sharp-tongued sycophants
in his court and to rise to the greatness of his own nature. Christopher Carey
describes the ode as containing "possibly the most extravagant praise to be
found" in the triumphant odes.8 This praise, however, is counterbalanced
with images of the consequences of false friendship and ingratitude. In par-
ticular, it draws a gripping sketch of Ixion, an apt image for treachery, since
Ixion murdered his father-in-law,9 and, after being pardoned by Zeus and
allowed to reside on Mount Olympus, attempted to rape Hera, Zeus' wife.
Ixion was punished by being tied to a wheel that would revolve eternally.
Ixion is the embodiment of hubris in Pindar's ode; he behaves as one
who does not know his place. "He had presumed as if he were a god," says
Carey. Carey notes that "the relationship between man and god" is "a prom-
inent theme in this ode."10 For example:
God reaches, as soon as thought, his ends:
God, who can catch the winged eagle
And overtakes the dolphin in the sea.
He can bring down any whose heart is high
And to others he will give unaging splendour.11
Ixion fails to recognize his own place in the natural scheme of things. Pindar
concludes that he brought about his own fate: "His two sins live and bring
him misery.... And so, bound to the four spokes, / He got his own ruin."12
The idea that Ixion acted contrary to nature by usurping the role of a
god seems foreign to Nietzsche's purpose in The Gay Science; and certainly
Nietzsche is giving a new spin to the line he cites. Allusion to the original


BECOME WHO YOU ARE
125
context serves Nietzsche's purpose, however: he presents the theological at-
titude of Christianity as the moral equivalent of hubris. The Christian world-
view, which urges shame before an all-powerful God, reveals a failure to
recognize one's real position in the scheme of things. Human beings feel
shame because they disown the traits they admire and acknowledge traits
they despise. In this respect we are like Ixion, causing our own pitiful cir-
cumstances by denying our real position in the world.
The ultimate point of Pindar's ode is not to itemize human limitations
but to draw attention to Hieron's own inner resources. This is the context
of the line that Nietzsche cites as the dictate of conscience. Carey describes
the line as "a critic-bedevilled sentence, where agreement is impossible." He
sees the meaning as "be yourself, having learnt my words."13 He notes, how-
ever, that the admonition to be yourself "means not 'what you are, a man'
but 'what manner of man you are.' "14 Other translations include:
"Be what you are, the man whose worth you have heard."15
"Know what you are and become it."16
"Show that you have learned what manner of man you are."17
"O find, and be, yourself!"18
"Be what you are, now that you have learnt your true character from me."19
Geoffrey S. Conway notes that this appeal to Hieron's sense of his own
worth is more in keeping with Greek values than with more recent moral
ideas: "It must be remembered that to a Greek of Pindar's day to take vig-
orous action against his enemies was a duty which his self-respect demanded
no less strongly, perhaps even more strongly, than that of giving help to a
friend."20 Despite the extensive description of Ixion's fall, Pindar describes
Hieron as being able to resist that fate. Nietzsche makes a similar point with
the line: we need only acknowledge our own powers to avoid eternal torment
by a Giod—or more accurately, by our idea of God.
Nietzsche's final aphorisms in Book III converge in urging a similar out-
look. Shame is a symptom of seeing oneself from the standpoint of others
(the moral herd or the Christian God). Nietzsche calls on his reader to aban-
don these external perspectives and to regain a more immediate appreciation
of one's real powers. As he argues in a passage in Book IV, the important
thing is to recognize and "give style" to one's own character. Whatever one's
traits, and however one configures them into a coherent style, the important
thing is self-satisfaction:
For one thing is needful: that a human being should attain satisfaction
with himself. ... Whoever is dissatisfied with himself is continually ready
for revenge, and we others will be his victims, if only by having to endure
his ugly sight.21
In the second Pythian Ode, the line that follows "Become who you are"
makes reference to an "ape": " 'O that / Lovely ape!' cry the children, 'O


126
COMIC RELIEF
how / Lovely!" Pindar goes on to praise the wise judgment of Rhadamanthys,
the judge of departed souls. Gordon Kirkwood reads this as a "contrast
between the superficiality and lack of discernment of the ape and the chil-
dren, and the real judgment of Rhadamanthys."22 While Nietzsche would
hardly endorse the idea of an unerring judge of the dead, his characteriza-
tions of "the herd" in morality and religion are akin to Pindar's negative
assessment of those who are taken in by the voices of the ill-motivated
many.23 Carey sees the discernment at issue as being that between friend and
foe: "The trouble with Ixion and the slanderers is that they make no dis-
tinction between friend and foe."24 From Nietzsche's point of view, this same
characterization would apply to the monotheist, who does not recognize that
the alleged benefactor is an image that depletes one's sense of one's own
value.
"Become who you are," like the Delphic oracle's "Know thyself," needs
to be weighed anew. The moralistic tradition reads such maxims as caveats
to know our limitations. Nietzsche urges instead that what is most needful
is that we come to recognize our power. He acknowledges, however, that we
are not unlike Ixion. We, too, are tied to an eternally revolving wheel.25 Our
challenge is to resist our tradition's interpretation of this wheel as a punish-
ment.26 Nietzsche weighs this image anew in Book IV, where he first presents
his account of eternal recurrence.


Eternal Recurrence: Not Again!
Recurrence as Puzzle
Sometimes repetition alone suffices for comedy. When Larry of the Three
Stooges puts a plank against a wall, it isn't funny that it falls to the floor;
but its falling every time he repositions it occasions laughter. British comedy
excels at ludicrous repetition. Returning to a slide of "the larch" becomes
hilarious from sheer redundancy on Monty Python's Flying Circus.1 So is the
cheese seller's recurrent negative reply when John Cleese asks if he has a
particular type of cheese. In daily life, too, I have found that sometimes a
repeated annoyance shifts from irritating to downright funny if it happens
one time too many.
And yet Nietzsche's notion of eternal recurrence does not seem particu-
larly funny. The idea of our lives returning in every detail is no joke. And
this seems particularly so as the idea is presented in section 341 of The Gay
Science, titled "The Greatest Weight":
What if some day or night a demon were to steal after you in to your
loneliest loneliness and say to you: "This life as you now live it and have
lived it, you will have to live once more and innumerable times more; and
there will be nothing new in it, but every pain and every joy and every
thought and sigh and everything unutterably small or great in your life will
have to return to you, all in the same succession and sequence—even this
spider and this moonlight between the trees, and even this moment and I
myself. The eternal hourglass of existence is turned upside down again and
again, and you with it, speck of dust!"
Would you not throw yourself down and gnash your teeth and curse
the demon who spoke thus? Or have you once experienced a tremendous
moment when you would have answered him, "You are a god and never
have I heard anything more divine." If this thought gained possession of
you, it would change you as you are or perhaps crush you. The question
in each and every thing, "Do you desire this once more and innumerable
times more?" would lie upon your actions as the greatest weight. Or how
127
Jix


128
COMIC RELIEF
well disposed would you have to become to yourself and to life to crave
nothing more fervently than this ultimate confirmation and seal?2
Nietzsche thinks we would be moved to take this vision extremely seri-
ously, and I suspect that most readers do. But it isn't obvious why. Ivan Soil,
among others, has argued that infinite repetition of our lives should not
matter to us at all. We would not remember from one cycle to another; why,
then, would it make any difference whether our lives were repeated or not?3
Indeed, this may be why eternal recurrence does not strike us as funny. We
have no experience of repetition on the theory. If it were "not... a new life
or a better life or a similar life" but just "this same, selfsame life," as Zar-
athustra puts it,4 a "recurrence" would not differ from the original iteration
of the life in any respect. Bernd Magnus has observed that the recurrence
would not be a repetition of the original with the addition of a recognition
that it is a recurrence. This recognition, or any memory of a previous iter-
ation, would be ruled out as deviations from the original life. Thus, this
present life, on the model of eternal recurrence, could not be distinguished
from either the original or any reiteration: " 'Recurrence' is another name for
'occurrence' in the doctrine of eternal recurrence. It does not name yet another
numerically distinct event apart from 'occurrence.' It is a seductive grammatical
construct."^ Certainly, one would not be able to tell whether this present life
is the original or a reiteration.
But, then, what is the point of eternal recurrence? What schema could
possibly be more useless? One's life would look the same whether eternal
recurrence were true or not. The indiscernibility of one's "original" life and
its reiterations have led many commentators to reject or deemphasize the
cosmological cast of the theory, despite Nietzsche's notebook sketches of
scientific "proofs" of recurrence. Instead, say such commentators, the main
point of the theory on such accounts is to test or express one's attitude
toward one's life. Magnus, Nehamas, Solomon, and others have offered var-
iations on the attitudinal reading, and I concur with this emphasis.6
Nevertheless, I see a comic (if not a cosmic) element lurking in section
341. I think that it is, in part, a parody. Read as a parody, the passage satirizes
some of the heavies of the Western philosophical tradition, in keeping with
Nietzsche's suggestion that comedy will inevitably follow long stretches of
tragic seriousness. The particular comedy presented here serves Nietzsche's
project of de-deifying nature, for the targets of satire are the Platonic and
Christian traditions, with their variant modes of demeaning our temporal
lives. Dramatically, eternal recurrence appears as a comic alternative to these
tragic visions, growing out of the realization that "God is dead," as its place-
ment in The Gay Science reflects.
The Demon's Precursors
Nietzsche's reference to a demon is a key to his enterprise. While much has
been made of section 34i's statement of the theory of eternal recurrence,


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
129
Western's philosophy's literary precedents for the demon are typically ig-
nored. The consequence, I am convinced, is that the connection of eternal
recurrence with other themes in The Gay Science have not been fully rec-
ognized.
Demons are prominent in two other locations within the Western phil-
osophical literature.7 One is the daimonion of Socrates, mentioned in a num-
ber of Plato's dialogues. The other is the evil demon who figures in Des-
cartes's Meditations. Section 341 alludes to both. It accomplishes a double
parody of the Western philosophical tradition, with its origin in Socrates,
and the modern "scientific" worldview's prolongation of Christian thought,
instantiated in Descartes.
The reference to a demon in section 341 should remind us of Socrates'
daimonion, if we read the book sequentially, for the preceding section is an
analysis of Socrates' last words. Although Socrates was usually as "great in
silence" as in his speech, this was not so at his final moment, Nietzsche
argues:
Whether it was death or the poison or piety or malice—something loos-
ened his tongue at that moment and he said: "O Crito, I owe Asclepius a
rooster." This ridiculous and terrible "last word" means for those who
have ears: "O Crito, life is a disease." Is it possible that a man like him,
who had lived cheerfully and like a soldier in the sight of everyone, should
have been a pessimist? He had merely kept a cheerful mien while concealing
all his life long his ultimate judgment, his inmost feeling. Socrates, Socrates
suffered
life! And then he still revenged himself—with his veiled, gruesome,
pious, and blasphemous saying. Did a Socrates need such revenge? Did his
overrich virtue lack an ounce of magnanimity?—Alas, my friends, we must
overcome even the Greeks!8
Although ultimately negative in its judgment of Socrates, Nietzsche's analysis
here is complex. The presupposition is Socrates' greatness in both speech
and silence. Socrates' last words do not rise to the rank of his character;
instead he breaks down at the end. Nietzsche raises questions about the
custom of reading the story of a person's whole life through the lens of his
or her last remarks. If we judge Socrates on his last words, our impression
of his greatness is diminished. If these words amount to a deathbed conver-
sion, it is conversion for the worse, and particularly ironic for one who
allegedly said that "those who really apply themselves in the right way to
philosophy are directly and of their own accord preparing themselves for
dying and death."9 If they reveal his lifelong judgment, alternatively, they
show Socrates as a long-term deceiver. Either way, the dying Socrates is a
poor spin doctor for his life. And yet the dying Socrates is the hero of
Western philosophy!10
Nietzsche makes similar remarks about last words in section 36, where
final words betray both Augustus and Nero. In Augustus' case, his last words
are out of keeping with his life. Strikingly, Nietzsche compares Augustus
Caesar to Socrates, calling him "that terrible man who had as much self-
control and could be as silent as any wise Socrates." And yet in his last


130
COMIC RELIEF
words—"Plaudite amici, comoedia finita est!" "Applaud, friends: the comedy
is done"—Augustus "became indiscreet at his own expense.... For the first
time he dropped his mask by implying that he had worn a mask and played
a comedy." Nietzsche concludes that Augustus' last words reveal "an actor's
vanity, an actor's garrulity! Truly the opposite of the dying Socrates!"11
Section 340 seems to reassess this conclusion. Socrates is held by our
philosophical tradition as the paradigm of a person who fully lived his phi-
losophy. If Socrates failed on the same count as Augustus in this passage,
are we to consider him an actor, too? Yet Nietzsche charges the dying Soc-
rates with a lack of magnanimity, suggesting that his philosophy did not flow
directly from who he was. These final words display Socrates' deepest feelings
about his life's work, and the seeming continuity between the man and his
work was finally just an act.
This is not the first time that Nietzsche has urged a double take on Soc-
rates. His first work, The Birth of Tragedy, presents a number of grounds for
reassessment, one of which is the presence of Socrates' inner voice, his dai-
monion. Nietzsche was sufficiently fascinated to discuss this marvel at length:
We are offered a key to the character of Socrates by the wonderful phe-
nomenon known as "the daimonion of Socrates." In exceptional circum-
stances, when his tremendous intellect wavered, he found secure support
in the utterances of a divine voice that spoke up at such moments. This
voice, whenever it comes, always dissuades. In this utterly abnormal nature,
instinctive wisdom appears only in order to hinder conscious knowledge
occasionally. While in all productive men it is instinct that is the creative-
affirmative force, and consciousness acts critically and dissuasively, in Soc-
rates it is instinct that becomes the critic, and consciousness that becomes
the creator—truly a monstrosity per defectum!12
Nietzsche's analysis of the daimonion is thoroughly critical of Socrates.
Socrates, usually so poised in his responses, has no idea what to do with
intuition and insights from what we would call "the unconscious." Only at
the very end of his life did he admit to himself that he feared the power
represented as the daimonion. Yet in his reliance on reason, Socrates con-
sorted with another power that was beyond his own control, when he placed
his confidence in reason:
Anyone who, through the Platonic writings, has experienced even a breath
of the divine naivete and sureness of the Socratic way of life will also feel
how the enormous driving wheel of logical Socratism is in motion, as it
were, behind Socrates, and that it must be viewed through Socrates as
through a shadow.13
Socrates' "absurd" rationality,14 on Nietzsche's account, was itself a kind
of daimonic presence, too, one that drove him far more steadily than the
daimonion restrained.
Nietzsche also describes the voice that Socrates encountered in dreams as
akin to the daimonic voice:


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
IJI
As he tells his friends in prison, there often came to him one and the same
dream apparition, which always said the same thing to him: "Socrates,
practice music." Up to his very last days he comforts himself with the view
that his philosophizing is the highest of the muses, and he finds it hard to
believe that a deity should remind him of the "common, popular music."
Finally, in prison, in order that he may thoroughly unburden his con-
science, he does consent to practice this music for which he has but little
respect. And in this mood he writes a prelude to Apollo and turns a few
Aesopian fables into verse. It was something akin to the demonic warning
voice that urged him to these practices; it was his Apollinian insight that,
like a barbaric king, he did not understand the noble image of a god and
was in danger of sinning against a deity—through his lack of understand-
ing. The voice of the Socratic dream vision is the only sign of any misgiv-
ings about the limits of logic ...15
As Nietzsche presents Socrates' last words in section 340 of The Gay Science,
they are in keeping with these latterday misgivings. Have the daimonic pow-
ers reversed roles? Has the restraining voice gained so much power that it
retroactively dissuades Socrates from the reason-advocating project of his
entire life? And if the daimonion has the final word on the meaning of Soc-
rates' life, should his whole life be seen as a mistake? Nietzsche leaves this
question hanging.
By raising this question in section 341, he suggests another—the question
to the reader, "Do you see your life as a mistake?" If so, the reader's outlook
is like that of Socrates when he uttered his final words. Yet Nietzsche suggests
in section 340 that Socrates' last words failed to rise to the stature of his
character. Would the reader who answered, "Yes, my life is a mistake," fail
in similar fashion? Certainly, this response does not recognize the "hero
concealed" in one's everyday character.
But the failure of the dying Socrates is exactly this everyday foible. Socrates
missed the concealed hero that we all now see in him, thanks to Plato's artistic
idealizing. Socrates, the absurd rationalist who inspired Plato as a young play-
wright to burn his plays, had no gratitude to art—and therefore failed to know
himself as hero. Perhaps eternal recurrence, along these lines, offers us oppor-
tunities for gratitude and self-idealization—to know ourselves as heroes. Even
if we fail to rejoice at the prospect of eternal recurrence, the thought experi-
ment of section 341 provokes us to look at our lives artistically and gain some
inkling of ourselves as heroes, our lives as aesthetic phenomena.
We will further consider this possibility. But let us now consider section
34i's allusion to the other great demon in Western thought, who appears in
Descartes's Meditations on First Philosophy. Descartes's demon appears in a
thought experiment that challenges our usual interpretation of our experi-
ence of the natural world. Section 341 parodies this thought experiment, its
own demon challenging the way we consider our natural experience.
Descartes introduces the demon as follows:
I will suppose therefore that not God, who is supremely good and the
source of truth, but rather some malicious demon of the utmost power


132
COMIC RELIEF
and cunning has employed all his energies in order to deceive me. I shall
think that the sky, the air, the earth, colours, shapes, sounds, and all ex-
ternal things are merely delusions of dreams which he has devised to en-
snare my judgement. I shall consider myself as not having hands or eyes
or flesh, or blood or senses, but as falsely believing that I have all these
things. I shall stubbornly and firmly persist in this meditation; and, even
if it is not in my power to know any truth, I shall at least do what is in
my power, that is, resolutely guard against assenting to any falsehoods, so
that the deceiver, however powerful and cunning he may be, will be unable
to impose on me in the slightest degree.16
Descartes gives his imaginary demon full power to invert the significance of
everything that is apparent to consciousness.
Descartes concludes that he Can rationally determine that our usual ep-
istemic view is superior to this demonic alternative. What we clearly and
distinctly perceive is true; we are not deceived about absolutely everything.
Nietzsche's demonic tale, by contrast, offers an alternative to our usual
view that is not rationally dismissible. He suggests that Descartes has dis-
missed his demon prematurely—that he has not escaped the demon after
all.
Against Nature
Nietzsche's references to Descartes are scattered, and he does not particularly
emphasize Descartes in the first three books of The Gay Science. Book IV,
however, opens with a clear allusion to Descartes's most famous line: "I still
live, I still think: I still have to live, for I still have to think. Sum, ergo cogito:
cogito, ergo sum."17 This beginning and the penultimate section's image of
the demon frame Book IV with Cartesian images.
Descartes presents his cogito in more than one work, but its most famous
appearance occurs in the Meditations. That work's project is to find a secure
foundation for all knowledge, a quintessentially tragic goal for Wissenschaft
by Nietzsche's lights. To this end Descartes adopts his methodology of radical
skepticism, systematically doubting to determine whether anything is indu-
bitable. Quickly dismissing the evidence of the senses, which is unreliable
and difficult to distinguish from imaginings while dreaming, he considers
other kinds of knowledge, such as knowledge of mathematical truths. But
even these, he considers, might be faulty; the evil demon could have instilled
all of these "truths" in his mind.
The specter of the demon gives way only when Descartes becomes self-
reflective. As one who engages in doubting, he is thinking; but if he is think-
ing, he concludes, then he must exist. "[I]f I convince myself of something
then I certainly existed.... J am, I exist, is necessarily true whenever it is put
forward by me or conceived in my mind."18 Armed with the indubitable
truth that he exists, Descartes proceeds to "prove" the correctness of many
of his other beliefs, such as the existence of God and of the external world.


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
133
Once God's existence is proven, Descartes asserts, we need no longer fear
that we are being deceived by a demon. God, being thoroughly good, would
not design our connection with the world in such a way that we were con-
stantly deceived:
[I]t is impossible that God should ever deceive me. For in every case of
trickery or deception some imperfection is to be found; and although the
ability to deceive appears to be an indication of cleverness or power, the
will to deceive is undoubtedly evidence of malice or weakness, and so
cannot apply to God.
... And since God does not wish to deceive me, he surely did not give
me the kind of faculty which would ever enable me to go wrong while
using it correctly.19
Nietzsche opens Book IV with a prankish variation on Descartes's rea-
soning. Descartes, a logical innovator and proponent of mathematical rea-
soning, should be tolerant of the principle of symmetry: the principle that
equivalence is preserved if an operation performed on one side of an equa-
tion is also performed on the other. Although it does apply in mathematical
logic, the principle of symmetry does not always apply to sentences in natural
language; but Nietzsche pretends that it does. Starting with Descartes's "I
think, therefore I am," Nietzsche adds the operator "I still have to" to both
sides of the implication, yielding "I still have to think, therefore I still live
(am)."
But the colon that precedes Nietzsche's formulation insinuates that this
conclusion follows from an earlier insight: "I still live, I still think." This
statement appears to report an observation, but it actually states Nietzsche's
rejection of Descartes's strategy. Either Nietzsche observes both processes
more or less simultaneously, or his observation "I still think" follows his
observation "I still live." Implicitly, he questions the cogito. Does one merely
infer that one is alive, and is this a conclusion from the prior observation that
one is thinking? Does it not seem as though one notices the two phenomena
together? And isn't living more basic than thinking, in that one must be alive
in order to think? At least, Nietzsche's naturalistic premises would suggest
so. Nietzsche restates Descartes's cogito in his own "proof that introspection
reveals the co-presence of thinking and existing: "Sum, ergo cogito: cogito,
ergo sum."
Elsewhere, too, Nietzsche reveals his skepticism toward the methodolog-
ical skeptic. He insinuates an anti-Cartesian argument in section i of The
Gay Science while making the point that even seemingly evil drives have their
place in the economy of the species. Recall his comment, "To be sure, this
economy is not afraid of high prices... . Still it is proven that it has preserved
our race so far."20 The emphasis on "proven" raises the red flag for the
reader. "How is it proven?" we might ask. But as soon as we do, we recall,
laughing at ourselves: "The species must exist; after all, I'm obviously here!"
In Thus Spoke Zarathustra, Nietzsche spoofs Descartes's skeptical meth-
odology when Zarathustra remarks, "That is the first instance of my human


134
COMIC RELIEF
prudence, that I let myself be deceived in order not to be on guard against
deceivers."21 To be, like Descartes, on guard against deception is not a pru-
dent course, at least from a human point of view. Zarathustra challenges
Descartes's strategy of doubting whatever is learned through experience, an
especially strange avoidance when conjoined with speculations on what God
would or would not do.
Nietzsche takes a direct potshot at the cogito in Beyond Good and Evil: "A
thought comes when 'it' wishes and not when T wish."22 His argument is
that one cannot tell, from the observation that thinking occurs, that an agent
necessarily exists. That Nietzsche has Descartes in mind in this argument is
indicated by a note of 1887:
"There is thinking; therefore there is something that thinks": this is the
upshot of all Descartes' argumentation. But that means positing as "true a
priori" our belief in the concept of substance—that when there is thought
there has to be something "that thinks" is simply a formulation of our
grammatical custom that adds a doer to every deed... . Along the lines
followed by Descartes one does not come upon something absolutely cer-
tain but only upon the fact of a very strong belief."23
The assumption that thinking presupposes an agent is just an instance of
Western philosophy's general view that all change presupposes an underlying
substance. Nietzsche contends that this is just a long-standing prejudice.
If thinking did supervene on an agent, presumably the agent would have
some control over the activity. But if we are attentive in our introspection,
we will notice that any "thinking self we imagine seems to have very limited
control over thought. At times we cannot remember a thought when we
would like to; thoughts we would like to banish appear nonetheless. Nietz-
sche contends in explanation that the will is something different from either
thinker and the thought, directly challenging the reasoning behind the cogito.
The Cartesian emphasis on consciousness also strikes Nietzsche as unfor-
tunate. Consciousness, according to Descartes, is our basis for certainty. Not
only does consciousness provide us with the assurance that we are not de-
ceived about anything (and, consequently, not deceived in our basic as-
sumptions about reality); it also offers clear moral guidance, "For it is clear
by the natural light that the perception of the intellect should always precede
the determination of the will."24
Section 11 of The Gay Science disputes the status that Descartes assigns to
consciousness. There Nietzsche characterizes consciousness as "the last and
latest development of the organic and hence also what is most unfinished
and unstrong."25 Nietzsche denies that consciousness is the "self that Des-
cartes believes that he has proven:
Before a function is fully developed and mature it constitutes a danger for
the organism, and it is good if during the interval it is subjected to some
tyranny. Thus consciousness is tyrannized—not least by our pride in it.
One thinks that it constitutes the kernel of man. . .. One denies its growth
and its intermittences. One takes it for the "unity of the organism."26


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
135
Along the same lines, section 22 puts Descartes as well as Kant in the line
of comic fire. Nietzsche describes a dream set in a royal court before which
at least one Frenchman, "M. Montaigne," is scheduled to appear. Nietzsche
puns on the etymology of "person" as a term meaning "mask": "We shall
receive a few persons (persons! what would that puffed-up old frog say, who
will be among them, if he heard this word! 'I am not a person,' he would
say, 'but always the substance itself) .. ,"27 Montaigne may be this "puffed-
up old frog," or perhaps it is Kant, who claims that the self with moral
dignity is noumenal, the "thing-in-itself'; but Descartes's assertion that the
conscious mind is a substance, indeed the substance to which "self" refers,
amounts to a similar denial that consciousness is merely a mask. That this
joke is part of a dream report also suggests that Nietzsche is targeting Des-
cartes, since Descartes takes our gullibility in dreams as a reason to be sus-
picious of what we learn through sensory experience.
In Book V of The Gay Science, Nietzsche applauds
Leibniz's incomparable insight that has been vindicated not only against
Descartes but against everybody who had philosophized before him—that
consciousness is merely an accidens of experience and not its necessary and
essential attribute; that, in other words, what we call consciousness con-
stitutes only one state of our spiritual and psychic world (perhaps a path-
ological state) and not by any means the whole of it.2*
Nietzsche also objects to Descartes's claims to having certain knowledge.
Nietzsche does not disparage the longing for epistemological certainty. In
section 2 of The Gay Science, for example, he (or perhaps his narrator) char-
acterizes "the desire for certainty" as "that which separates the higher human
beings from the lower."29 He continues:
But to stand in the midst of this rerum concordia discors and of this whole
marvelous uncertainty and rich ambiguity of existence without questioning,
without trembling with the craving and the rapture of such questioning,
without at least hating the person who questions, perhaps even finding him
faintly amusing—that is what I feel to be contemptible... .30
From these remarks, it would seem that Nietzsche sides with Descartes's
ambitions. Perhaps the mention of "the person who questions" is even for-
mulated with Descartes in mind. Yet Descartes's own methodology offers
grounds for concluding that we cannot be certain, a conclusion Descartes
resists. By stopping too soon, Descartes betrays tendencies akin to what
Nietzsche finds contemptible. Nietzsche aptly summarizes his assessment of
Descartes in section 25, in his lampoon of the person who "perceives some-
thing striking" but conclude: that he or she must have made a mistake.31
Nietzsche's critique of Descartes helps explain the references to Descartes
that frame Book IV. Descartes's philosophy is a paradigm of tendencies that
Nietzsche thinks we should put behind us. Nietzsche's naturalism runs di-
rectly counter to Descartes's views on: (i) the status of the natural world; (2)
the rational intellect's capacity for certain knowledge; (3) the relationship of


136
COMIC RELIEF
the mind to the body; and (4) the priority of being over becoming. These
areas of disagreement are among the most significant concerns of The Gay
Science; therefore, it is thoroughly appropriate to give Descartes such psy-
chological salience in the framing of Book IV and the first edition as a whole.
Let us consider Descartes's perspectives on these topics.
The Natural World
Historical accounts of Western philosophy usually describe Descartes as pro-
moting the development of the natural sciences. Descartes's position, how-
ever, is historically ambiguous. Laurence J. Lafleur characterizes him as
at once the progressive, or rather the radical intellectual rebel, ready to
break away from Mediaevalism and the Church to lay the foundation of a
new philosophy and to build his hopes for the future of mankind on the
development of science in general and of medicine in particular; and at
the same time he was the conservative, educated in the Mediaeval tradition
by the monks of the Jesuit order.32
From Nietzsche's point of view, Descartes keeps science subservient to
theology and fails to adopt a sufficiently naturalized perspective. He utilizes
God as a fundamental term in his arguments about the extent to which we
can trust our perceptions of the natural world. Foreshadowing William Pa-
ley's teleological argument for God's existence,33 Descartes considers nature
on the model of a clock, whose design reflects positively on God's ordering,
regardless of its actual performance:
Yet a clock constructed with wheels and weights observes all the laws of
its nature just as closely when it is badly made and tells the wrong time as
when it completely fulfils the wishes of the clockmaker.34
This passage shows a way in which Descartes uses "nature" equivocally.
At times, he uses "nature" to refer to empirical reality, which he does not
hold in particularly high esteem except insofar as it exemplifies a supernatural
scheme. Even the empirical sense of "nature" has decidedly theological over-
tones: "[B]y my own nature in particular I understand nothing other than
the totality of things bestowed on me by God."35
A second sense of "nature" is God's ordering of the world. This "nature"
encompasses the network of natural laws, which function perfectly regardless
of particular empirical occurrences. A related sense of "nature" consists of
the structural features of a particular kind of created thing. Descartes fre-
quently uses this sense in reference to the human being, as in his expression
"by my own nature."
Human insight depends on a third sense of nature—God's direct illu-
mination of the mind about particular matters. This illumination does not
depend on empirical experience, as do the conclusions yielded by scientific
observation and experimentation. Instead, this "light of nature" amounts to


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
137
reason, which gives us direct awareness of the truths of logic as well as the
nature of God. With respect to the latter, Descartes reasons,
it is quite clear to anyone who attentively considers the nature of time that
the same power and action are needed to preserve anything at each indi-
vidual moment of its duration as would be required to create that thing
anew if it were not yet in existence. Hence the distinction between pres-
ervation and creation is only a conceptual one, and this is one of the things
that are evident by the natural light.36
God the Creator must, as the natural light informs us, also be preserving us.
"Nature" has yet another meaning for Descartes, one more in keeping
with modern science. Descartes contends that we have self-evident intuition
of "simple natures," intuitable properties of bodies (such as size, shape, po-
sition, and extension) or of intellects (such as will and perception). Des-
cartes's aspiration was to place all knowledge on the foundation of such self-
evident intuitions, and to interconnect knowledge systematically by a priori
means. John Cottingham observes that this strategy amounted to a rejection
of an earlier, more Aristotelian appeal to nature: "Descartes in effect banishes
from metaphysics the Aristotelian realm of objective essence and nature,
which antecedently determines the objects of knowledge, and substitutes the
notion of intuitable objects directly and immediately accessed by the human
intellect."37 Although these "natures" appear less straightforwardly to depend
on a supernatural interpretation than the others, they themselves are divorced
from the empirical world, in that they are to be known by direct intuition
and systematized a priori. These simple natures, too, are discernible through
the human intellect, which according to Descartes is illumined by God.
Nietzsche rejects all of Descartes's senses of "nature," for they are imbed-
ded in a supernatural scheme of interpretation, which in Nietzsche's view
denigrates our experienced world. Nietzsche also considers the natural world
a closed system, whereas Descartes insists that it depends on God as an extra-
systematic support. Descartes's discussion of causation demonstrates his un-
willingness to restrict causality to the natural order. He explains that we
cannot account for our being by referring to our parents or "some other
causes less perfect than God"—naturalistically, in other words:
[I]t is quite clear that there must be at least as much in the cause as in the
effect. And therefore whatever kind of cause is eventually proposed, since
I am a thinking thing and have within me some idea of God, it must be
admitted that what caused me is itself a thinking thing and possesses the
idea of all the perfections which I attribute to God. In respect of this cause
one may again inquire whether it derives its existence from itself or from
another cause. If from itself, then it is clear from what has been said that
it is itself God, since if it has the power of existing through its own might,
then undoubtedly it also has the power of actually possessing all the per-
fections of which it has an idea—that is, all the perfections which I conceive
to be in God. If, on the other hand, it derives its existence from another
cause, then the same question may be repeated concerning this further


138
COMIC RELIEF
cause, namely whether it derives its existence from itself or from another
cause, until eventually the ultimate cause is reached, and this will be God.
It is clear enough that an infinite regress is impossible here, especially
since I am dealing not just with the cause that produced me in the past,
but also and most importantly with the cause that preserves me at the
present moment.38
Here, Descartes follows Thomas Aquinas, reasserting one of the cosmological
arguments for the existence of God. Descartes argues that by inquiring back-
ward from each natural condition to its cause, we are led to postulate a first
cause. He contends that this cause cannot be part of the natural order. This
assertion directly contradicts Nietzsche's view (derived from Kant and Scho-
penhauer) that causality applies only to the realm of natural entities.
Descartes also assumes in this argument that continuation in time does
not depend on natural causes. "Becoming" does not flow from previous
"becoming" but must depend, he assumes, on Being, on God. Again, he fails
to restrict causality to the experienced natural world, insisting that the most
important cause lies outside it.
Similarly, Descartes's account of God also disrupts the closure of the sys-
tem that interconnects the objects of knowledge. Descartes contends that we
could not trust our own reasoning were it not for God's support. Remarking
on a proof in geometry, he asserts:
But as soon as I turn my mind's eye away from the proof, then in spite of
still remembering that I perceived it very clearly, I can easily fall into doubt
about its truth, if I am unaware of God. For I can convince myself that I
have a natural disposition to go wrong from time to time in matters which
I think I perceive as evidently as can be. This will seem even more likely
when I remember that there have been frequent cases where I have re-
garded things as true and certain, but have later been led by other argu-
ments to judge them to be false.39
