Except at the moment one is entertaining them, even rational proofs are
dubitable unless one believes that God is maintaining the order of the world:
Now, however, I have perceived that God exists, and at the same time I
have understood that everything else depends on him, and that he is no
deceiver; and I have drawn the conclusion that everything which I clearly
and distinctly perceive is of necessity true. Accordingly, even if I am no
longer attending to the arguments which led me to judge that this is true,
as long as I remember that I clearly and distinctly perceived it, there are
no counter-arguments which can be adduced to make me doubt it, but on
the contrary I have true and certain knowledge of it.40
The Rational Intellect and Certainty
Nietzsche also considers the Cartesian system of knowledge to be at odds
with the "nature" we empirically experience. Descartes is a pioneer in de-
veloping the modern approach to knowledge, Nietzsche's target throughout


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
139
The Gay Science. Cottingham characterizes Descartes's "invention of highly
abstract structures of thought, neutral with respect to subject matter ... as
the hallmark of his new approach to knowledge."41 Reason divorced from
particulars is Descartes's royal road to secure knowledge. The indifference of
this methodology to subject matter strikes Nietzsche as dubious, and as likely
to be a projection of the knower as to be "objectively" informative.
Nietzsche's perspectival orientation could hardly be more dissonant with
Descartes's epistemological outlook. Nietzsche insists that the perspective and
biases of the observer are crucial to any formulation of knowledge, that there
is no observer-neutral knowledge divorced from subject matter. The only
"objectivity" we can have is to take many perspectives into our account.42
We certainly do not have the kind of certainty that Descartes seeks.
Insofar as he wants to construct a rational, quasi-scientific edifice, building
only upon stones that have passed his test for certainty, Descartes strikes
Nietzsche as self-deceived. "We simply lack any organ for knowledge," Nietz-
sche remarks in Book V.43 The types of organs we have and our purposes as
a "herd" determine the shape and nature of our "knowledge." Ultimately,
what we know depends on the kinds of bodies that we have, our minds
themselves being functions of these bodies.
The Mind and the Body
The naturalism that Nietzsche enjoins is rooted, fundamentally, in perception
and bodily experience. Cartesian dualism, Descartes's contention that mind
and body are separate substances, strikes Nietzsche as problematic, both as
an obstruction to naturalistic science and as a denigration of the person as
a natural, embodied being. In direct opposition to Cartesian dualism, Nietz-
sche denies that the body and mind are separate substances or separable. In
his character Zarathustra's words, "[T]he awakened and knowing say: body
am I entirely, and the soul is only a word for something about the body."44
As we have seen, Descartes discounts empirical evidence in favor of ra-
tionalistic formulations. His criterion of truth, clear and distinct perception,
is understood in contradistinction to sensory perception: "Admittedly my
nature is such that so long as I perceive something very clearly and distinctly
I cannot but believe it to be true."45 And "everything which I clearly and
distinctly perceive to belong to that thing really does belong to it.. . ,"46 The
paradigm cases of "clear and distinct perception," mathematical truths, are
not sensuously mediated. The latter, moreover, are themselves subordinated
to theology. God guarantees even the truth of what we perceive clearly and
distinctly.47 By contrast, Descartes considers the body an impediment to the
mind's attaining knowledge.
Nietzsche disparages Cartesian dualism in his account of the "idiosyncra-
sies of philosophers":
Now they all believe, desperately even, in what has being. But since they
never grasp it, they seek for reasons why it is kept from them. "There must


140
COMIC RELIEF
be mere appearance, there must be some deception which prevents us from
perceiving that which has being: where is the deceiver?"
"We have found him," they cry ecstatically; "it is the senses! These
senses, which are so immoral in other ways too, deceive us concerning the
true world. Moral: let us free ourselves from the deception of the senses,
from becoming, from history, from lies. Moral: let us say No to all who
have faith in the senses. . .. —And above all, away with the body, this
wretched idee fixe of the senses, disfigured by all the fallacies of logic,
refuted, even impossible, although it is impudent enough to behave as if
it were real!"48
Here we encounter another reference to Descartes's demon, the "de-
ceiver," who continues to haunt him and his tradition by casting suspicion
on what we learn through the senses. So far is Descartes from accepting the
testimony of the senses, Nietzsche remarks in The Birth of Tragedy, that
"Descartes could prove the reality of the empirical world only by appealing
to the truthfulness of God and his inability to utter falsehood."49 Descartes's
sole reason for giving any credence to his sense impressions is that he believes
in a God who would not deceive him.
Nietzsche considers this reasoning backward. What we learn through the
senses is far less dubitable than the God Descartes has merely assumed. Nietz-
sche calls attention to the "magnificent instruments of observation we possess
in our senses! The nose, for example,... is actually the most delicate instru-
ment so far at our disposal."50
Nietzsche also disputes the possibility of placing science on any other basis
than sense perception: "We possess scientific knowledge today to precisely
the extent that we have decided to accept the evidence of the senses." In this
Nietzsche directly attacks Descartes's rationalistic basis for science and all
philosophical subfields that are skeptical of sense perception:
The rest is miscarriage and not-yet-science: in other words, metaphysics,
theology, psychology, epistemology—or formal science, a doctrine of signs,
such as logic and that applied logic which is called mathematics. In them
reality is not encountered at all, not even as a problem .. .5I
Descartes, who employs every one of these "miscarriages" that are "not-yet
science," is a focal target for these criticisms. Indeed, he has a not-yet-
scientific motive for his dualism. His distinction between body and mind is
developed, in part, to defend the idea of an immortal soul which survives
the death of the body. Again, in Nietzsche's view, Descartes's supernatural
schema impedes his development of a scientific naturalism.
The Priority of "Being"
Descartes holds that the ultimate cause of everything within the natural world
must lie outside it, as we have already observed. This cause, he contends,
must have originated the natural causal continuum; but he also contends


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
141
that it must be continually operative to sustain our existence in time. This
follows from his assertion that time is infinitely divisible:
I do not escape the force of these arguments by supposing that I have
always existed as I do now, as if it followed from this that there was no
need to look for any author of my existence. For a lifespan can be divided
into countless parts, each completely independent of the others, so that it
does not follow from the fact that I existed a little while ago that I must
exist now, unless there is some cause which as it were creates me afresh at
this moment—that is, which preserves me.52
Causation does not connect one moment to another on this account.
God's sustaining activity must be renewed at every moment; the intercon-
nections of things within the natural sphere cannot ensure an entity's con-
tinuation. Descartes suggests that a supernatural source is necessary to finesse
the transition from a thing's being in one instant to its being in the successive
instant.
One of Descartes's arguments in support of his mind/body dichotomy
raises questions about his consistency in suggesting that God's creative action
must be renewed at each moment. According to this argument the body, like
time, is infinitely divisible, whereas the mind, the enduring entity, cannot be
divided: "... [T]here is a great difference between the mind and the body,
inasmuch as the body is by its very nature always divisible, while the mind
is utterly indivisible."53 The continued identity of the person depends on the
mind, the enduring aspect. But this raises a problem: if the mind is indivisible
and also our enduring aspect, why does God's creation of our minds have
to be reactivated at every moment?
In Descartes's argument about the divisibility of time, we see a reason
why eternal recurrence might strike Nietzsche as a rebuttal to Descartes.
Eternal recurrence, as Nietzsche develops this idea, directly counters this
notion that my temporal existence "can be divided into an infinite number
of parts, no one of which is any way dependent upon the others." Eternal
recurrence presents an image of time in which each moment is in herently
related to all other moments. Indeed, Nietzsche's temporal discussions
throughout The Gay Science suggest that moments in time are not nearly so
separated as Descartes implies, and that the flow of temporality involves the
flow of causality along with it.
Interestingly, Descartes considers time's binding together of events into
sequences to be evidence for the integrity—and the relative veracity—of
conscious experience. Late in the Meditations, he dismisses as implausible his
earlier fear that his conscious experience is as unreliable as dreams:
I should not have any further fears about the falsity of what my senses tell
me every day; on the contrary, the exaggerated doubts of the last few days
should be dismissed as laughable. This applies especially to the principal
reason for doubt, namely my inability to distinguish between being asleep
and being awake. For I now notice that there is a vast difference between


142
COMIC RELIEF
the two, in that dreams are never linked by memory with all the other
actions of life as waking experiences are.54
Temporal experience, then, is a basis for dismissing the grounds for doubt
that led Descartes to introduce the demon. Somewhat oddly, he now accepts
the evidence of memory that "links" moments together. This does not seem
easily compatible with his earlier assertion that moments in no way "depend"
on one another. He fails to demonstrate the possibility of "links" that pre-
serve the independence of moments. Nevertheless, the Meditations end with
his assertion of confidence that the demon has been vanquished.
A Reply to Descartes
Let us now return to Nietzsche's demon to see how he responds to Descartes
in section 341. Nietzsche's parable suggests alternatives to the four features
of Descartes's theory just considered. In the first place, Nietzsche's demon
offers an alternative to Descartes's vision of the natural world. According to
the latter, the natural world undergoes change in a linear fashion. We do
not experience any other realm on which it depends; the idea of another
realm has been postulated only to resolve certain questions that we ask about
the origins of this linear unfolding. Given the idea that time is linear and
unidirectional, we tend to look backward for a beginning and a power that
originated the temporal flow. Along these lines, Descartes contends that God
is the original cause.
Nietzsche's demon presents a vision that contrasts with the linear model
of time. Given recurrence, time has no starting point, and the temporal
sequence folds back on itself. Accordingly, we will not reach a beginning if
we inquire backward through time about previous causes. Time is simply the
structure of our experienced world. The demon's alternative also gives no
special status to a "first" moment. No particular moment is uniquely decisive
for the entire sequence that follows, as the moment of creation paradoxically
seems to be on the linear account. Every moment is as important to the
existence of the whole as every other one.
Second, Nietzsche's demon challenges Descartes's claims to certainty and
his conclusion that a supportive God, not an evil demon, conditions our per-
ceptions. Twentieth-century critics of Descartes have frequently complained
of the "Cartesian circle," Descartes's reliance on question-begging premises.55
On one formulation of this charge, Descartes is unable to dispel the demon
without accepting assumptions that are among those that the demon was
invoked to challenge. Nietzsche, in effect, makes the same argument. Des-
cartes claims to adopt a methodology of systematic skepticism but then em-
ploys God, whose existence and nature are not doubted, to dispel his skep-
tical considerations. His resolution of skepticism depends on unquestioned
faith to which he is not entitled according to his own methodology.
Descartes chooses to interpret the world as the coherent creation of God;
but appearances would support the evil demon's causal role just as well. The


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
143
empirical world would appear the same whether God or an evil demon were
responsible. Indeed, this is so even according to Descartes himself; this is
why he resorts to the cogito in the first place, as a means of establishing that
we cannot be deceived about everything.
Nietzsche calls attention to this Cartesian problem by offering another
vision of a comparable status. If one seriously entertains the thought exper-
iment of section 341, the account of eternal recurrence is just as compatible
with our experience as is the linear model that Descartes assumes. The visions
of Descartes and that proposed by the demon are undecidable on the basis
of our actual experience. Restricting oneself to purely naturalistic premises,
as Nietzsche's thought experiment of eternal recurrence does, one is not able
to ascertain that time is or is not cyclical. Descartes accepts linear time as he
accepts God's existence. In both cases, he simply begs the question.
We might, in this connection, reconsider Nietzsche's cosmological
"proofs" of eternal recurrence. Perhaps he considered them valuable because
they suggest the superstitious status of our belief in linear time. If eternal
recurrence is more consistent with contemporary science than the linear
model of time, that would amount to evidence that the linear model of time
was imported from other sources. Descartes's certainty comes from the same
source as his determination that an evil demon could not be deceiving him—
the unjustified assumption of God (here construed as the first cause). Des-
cartes has provided himself with emotional certainty, Nietzsche suggests, but
he has not achieved the epistemological certainty that he claims.
Third, the demon's image restricts personal identity to the natural world,
describing it as repetition within "the eternal hourglass of existence." By
locating the human being's identity entirely in the natural world, Nietzsche
undercuts Descartes's main motive for separating mind and body. For Des-
cartes, the soul's greater claim to reality stems from its location in another
realm; the body's lesser claim results from its natural status as mortal. Nietz-
sche locates the entire person in the natural sphere, with the mind and body
each equally real and important. In this respect, Nietzsche also counters the
cogito. Recognition of oneself, in the full sense of "self," involves awareness
of the mind/body continuum as a whole, not simply the disembodied intel-
lect.56
Finally, the focus on the flow of time in Nietzsche's parable counters
Western thought's traditional "prejudice in favor of Being." Taken together
with the linear model, this prejudice has usually been taken to imply a prime
mover, something immune from change that accounts for the temporal se-
quence. Nietzsche's model provides an alternative account that does not sup-
pose the need for an unchanging cause to account for the temporal sequence.
Our experience of the natural world does not give us warrant for the idea
of an unchanging Being, let alone that of a Creator God who "causes" the
world from a point outside nature.
By offering his own demon's alternative to the dominant interpretation
of time, Nietzsche indicates the mythological status of the linear theory of
time. The reader who wants to resist the demon's suggestion cannot rely on


144
COMIC RELIEF
experiential evidence, any more than can Descartes in his effort to disprove
the possibility of a systematically deceiving demon. Descartes counters his
demon only by postulating a supernatural, extratemporal world, but that,
according to Nietzsche, is an ungrounded fabrication.
Satisfaction Guaranteed?
Nietzsche associates the idea of linear time with the Christian worldview,
which treats the natural lifespan as a test to determine one's status in the
"real" world after death.57 Sectarian interpretations vary, but salvation or
damnation depends either on one's behavior in the natural world or on God's
election, reflected in one's behavior in the natural world. A cyclical view of
time is incompatible with the notion of reward or punishment in the afterlife.
It leaves little room for a life outside time, or for behavior (such as mortal
sin) that could be decisive for the overall significance of one's life.58 By con-
trast, the linear time model serves as a support for the Christian myth of sin,
salvation, and the afterlife, a myth that denigrates the natural world, in Nietz-
sche's opinion.
Nietzsche's parable of the demon's offer suggests an alternative to this
Christian myth.59 The cyclical model supports the emphasis that Nietzsche
places on the individual as creative interpreter of his ongoing experience.
Any moment might be the vantage for contemplating one's life as a whole
and pronouncing it good, in the manner of the Hebrew God's assessment of
his creation. No particular moment in time is the beginning of the cycle as
a whole, although particular moments might be the beginning of specific
configurations of energy within the cycle (including our individual selves).60
Thus, any moment is a vantage for contemplating our experiences taken as
a whole. On this view, Socrates' dying remark represents his perspective at
that moment, but it is not his definitive assessment of his life.
Again, Nietzsche positions the human being in a role traditionally assigned
to God, with the aim of transfiguring our outlook on our lives. Nietzsche
rejects the traditional Christian views of sin and the afterlife claiming that
they promote unhappiness. He suggests that the alternative model of eternal
recurrence might enable us to love life.
Why he thinks this, and how persuasively, have been quandaries for Nietz-
sche scholars. Bernd Magnus is among the least convinced. Magnus has ar-
gued that a rapturous response to the demon's offer is humanly impossible,
at least if one honestly considers one's life in all its details. Alluding to
Zarathustra's expression of affirming recurrence, "Thus I willed it," Magnus
argues, "On my reading, only an Ubermensch sincerely wills her own life. The
rest of us will our life and the world's in an edited version, if we are honest
with ourselves."61
Magnus is surely right, from a psychological point of view, that if we
rationally evaluate "each and every moment" of our own lives, there are
some that we want to revise, and that we would be monsters not to desire


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
145
revisions in the case of many "moments" recorded in world history.621 think
that Nietzsche implicitly acknowledges this when he formulates section 34i's
leading question: "The question in each and every thing, 'Do you desire this
once more and innumerable times more?' would lie upon your actions as
the greatest weight."
Magnus introduces what I see as an unnecessary complication in his re-
flections when he characterizes eternal recurrence and the Ubermensch in
terms of each other: I certainly agree with him that eternal recurrence rep-
resents an energetically affirmative attitude toward life, but I am not con-
vinced that the image of the Ubermensch helps to clarify the nature of this
attitude.63 In particular, by defining these terms in relation to each other,
Magnus comes close to conflating the singular moment described by section
341 with the ongoing lifestyle of the Ubermensch, as characterized in Thus
Spoke Zarathustra.
Because he defines eternal recurrence and the Ubermensch in terms of
each other, Magnus is led to explore the affirmation of eternal recurrence
in a conceptual, decontextualized fashion as applicable to the Ubermensch
at any particular moment.64 Eternal recurrence becomes a vision
affirmable
at "each and every moment," not simply a vantage for assessing one's life
as a whole, complete with all its moments. The ubermenschliche life of
nonstop ecstasy, however, is untranslatable into terms of human experi-
ence. Magnus concludes that Nietzsche knew this and intended to drama-
tize the unattainability of all such superhuman ideals.65 Thus Spoke Zara-
thustra, for example, is a
self-consuming gospel... which seeks to expose the need for gospels itself
as not only a living need, but a fraud and offense against the living....
[S]alvation, liberation, health, consists in seeing that "no one" can be
an Ubermensch in that sense and that that just is Nietzsche's mocking
point. .. .6S
Magnus contends that each of Nietzsche's central doctrines is "self-
consuming," requiring "as a condition of its intelligibility (or even iteration)
the very contrast it purports to set aside or would have the reader set aside."67
Eternal recurrence is a salient case:
The upshot of these remarks is that if one identifies the ideal life with the
Ubermensch—and identifies an Ubermensch as the person who could sin-
cerely say yes to eternal recurrence without self-deception or pathology—
then this conception of the ideal life, too, may be self-consuming—or self-
deconstructing—in the sense that it requires one to regard each and every
moment of one's life as Leibniz's God viewed it, namely, as the best of all
possible moments.68
In a sense, I agree with Magnus's "self-consuming" characterization of
the vision expressed in section 341. The thought experiment undercuts the
atomism of the demon's formulation, which itemizes "every pain and every
joy," as though these were separately identifiable. But I disagree about the


146
COMIC RELIEF
upshot. If the self-consuming operation applies to the temporal atomism
suggested by the demon's statement, affirmation of eternal recurrence would
not indicate criminal insanity.
I am also not certain that an affirmative response to the demon's proposal
requires one to go as far as Leibniz's God, who deems this the best of all
possible worlds. Leibniz's God is evaluating in terms of logical possibilities;
but in section 341 Nietzsche is encouraging us to consider our lives in nat-
uralistic terms. If one does so, each moment is so integrally bound to the
entirety of one's own life that the itemization of separately assessable mo-
ments is incoherent. Nietzsche is not suggesting that one could sanely survey
every moment of one's experience and judge it as "the best of all possible
moments" in a logical sense. He urges only that one consider each moment
internally necessary to the texture making up one's life.
Alexander Nehamas, in his account of eternal recurrence, analyzes the
type of necessity involved. The moments of one's life are necessary in the
sense that, taken collectively, they are identical to one's life.69 In other words,
our lives are exactly what they are; we have neither an alternative existence
nor the potential to make deletions. Our tradition has habituated many of
us to seek satisfaction in the idea that divine justice will eventually triumph,
setting right all terrestrial deficiencies. But Nietzsche asserts that there is no
outside standard, like divine justice, to rectify what we see as imperfect.70
We cannot rewrite all the details of the lives we have lived, but we can
reassess them. Again, Nietzsche urges us to reenvision our lives by reworking
them artistically. In the first section of Book IV, after observing that life
precedes thinking, Nietzsche has vowed to make peace with his own life. He
concludes the first section of Book IV: "I want to learn more and more to
see as beautiful what is necessary in things; then I shall be one of those who
make things beautiful. Amor fati: let that be my love henceforth!"71
This goal is presented as a new year's resolution. As such, it is certainly
not equivalent to surveying the moments of one's life without any thought
of improvement. One makes new year's resolutions precisely in order to im-
prove the way that one is living one's life, perhaps particularly when one
thinks that one has been taking the "wrong" approach to something. Al-
though it is geared to his attitude, not external behaviors, Nietzsche expects
his resolution to have beautifying effects on his world. To see ourselves as
noble is not the end of the matter, but it is essential if we are to really inhabit
the heroes within ourselves.
The entirety of Book IV is temporally situated. It follows on the sugges-
tions raised at the end of Book III that we probably still feel some "shame
in front of ourselves," and are therefore not yet comfortable becoming who
we are. The resolution of amor fati is urged at this juncture. This proposal
urges us to reconsider the moments of our lives, especially those of which
we are ashamed, as part of a continuing development.
Amor fati renders developments we have undergone as necessary, but not
judged by any standard outside themselves, such as those of abstract logic.
The particular course my life has taken is not the only logically possible route


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
147
that could have led to my writing this sentence. The necessity here is not
abstract but immanent. Considering my life from inside itself, I see the par-
ticular route taken as as the only route that could have led to my writing
that sentence, if I mean to include the full sense of that act, which includes
its role in my life. The complete reality of my particular act of writing is not
separable from the entire life that led up to that point. The act is what it is,
I am what I am, only through the saga behind us.
This view does not deny that any life includes objectionable features. In
his resolution Nietzsche asserts, "I do not want to wage war against what is
ugly. I do not want to accuse; I do not want to accuse those who accuse.
Looking away shall be my only negation."72 The project of Book IV is artistic
transfiguration, to conjoin the elements of one's life into a vision that is
beautiful, even if this requires "looking away" from some details:
How can we make things beautiful, attractive, and desirable for us when
they are not? And I rather think that in themselves they never are. Here
we could learn something from physicians, when for example they dilute
what is bitter or add wine and sugar to a mixture—but even more from
artists.... Moving away from things until there is a good deal that one no
longer sees and there is much that our eye has to add if we are still to see
them at all; or seeing things around a corner and as cut out and framed;
or to place them so that they partially conceal each other and grant us only
glimpses of architectural perspectives; looking at them through tinted glass
or in the light of the sunset; or giving them a surface and skin that is not
fully transparent.. ,73
In other words, the lens of amor fati is that of being superficial out of pro-
fundity. Surely, such superficial views are not the only possible perspectives;
but neither, claims Nietzsche, are the self-denigrating perspectives that West-
ern religion has made habitual. Amor fati is the converse of the strategy of
Bernard of Clairvaux and other Christian thinkers, who urged Christians to
magnify their unworthiness by comparison with God.74 Amor fati involves
magnifying the tremendous moments (the "high and happy moments" of
"the great festival road of humanity," as section 89 puts it) and looking at
the rest of one's experience in light of them.
Nietzsche demonstrates by directing a beautifying light on his religious
background, despite his strenuous objections:
[T]he whole of religion might yet appear as a prelude and exercise of some
distant age. Perhaps religion could have been the strange means to make
it possible for a few single individuals to enjoy the whole self-sufficiency
of a god and his whole power of self-redemption. Indeed—one might
ask—would man ever have learned without the benefit of such a religious
training and prehistory to experience a hunger and thirst for himself, and
to find satisfaction and fullness in himself*.
Religion, on this view, might even be necessary for overcoming God:
Did Prometheus have to fancy first that he had stolen the light and then
pay for that—before he finally discovered that he had created the light by


148
COMIC RELIEF
coveting the light and that not only man but also the god was the work of
his own hands and had been mere clay in his hands?75
Looking at his own religious background, Nietzsche might say to the tradi-
tion: "We were friends and have become estranged. But this was right, and
we do not want to conceal and obscure it from ourselves as if we had reason
to feel ashamed."76
Loving our lives does not depend on our seeing them as "the best" of
conceivable options. (We may be God, on Nietzsche's account, but we are
not the greatest that can be conceived.) To love is to value something just
because it is what it is, whatever its oddities. This way of valuing is not a
response of decontextualized judgment. Our love is not a symptom that we
can conceive nothing better. Instead, love develops; it has to be learned:
This is what happens to us in music: First one has to learn to hear a figure
and melody at all, to detect and distinguish it, to isolate it and delimit it
as a separate life. Then it requires some exertion and good will to tolerate
it in spite of its strangeness, to be patient with its appearance and expres-
sion, and kindhearted about its oddity. Finally there comes a moment when
we are used to it, when we wait for it, when we sense that we should miss
it if it were missing; and now it continues to compel and enchant us
relentlessly until we have become its humble and enraptured lovers who
desire nothing better from the world than it and only it.
But that is what happens to us not only in music. That is how we have
learned to love all things that we now love.77
At his historical juncture, Nietzsche thinks, most people are far from lov-
ing their lives as lived. Personally and societally, most would have to pass
through a number of stages before they could really claim that they would
"miss" their lives if they were any different. But this is how we learn to love
even the quirky music of our lives.78
Philosophically, there is a precedent for this orientation in Spinoza's "in-
tellectual love of God." Spinoza's pantheistic model describes all reality as
being one substance, the substance of nature, or "God." Being one substance,
all features of reality are conjoined in making that substance just what it is.
Each is necessary to the whole. Accordingly, Spinoza concludes, it is rational
and desirable to accept reality as it is. In its complete development, this
acceptance amounts to what he calls "the intellectual love of God":
Blessedness consists in love towards God....
... [T]he wise man, in so far as he is considered as such, suffers scarcely
any disturbance of spirit, but being conscious, by virtue of a certain eternal
necessity, of himself, of God and of things, never ceases to be, but always
possesses true spiritual contentment.79
Significantly, for Spinoza, God is not separate from the world. As Alan Don-
agan puts it, "Spinoza's theology, in short, naturalizes God."80 Nietzsche's
naturalism bears some kinship with Spinoza's, despite the latter's theological
language. Nietzsche identifies all phenomena as aspects of a single whole,


ETERNAL RECURRENCE
149
although he does not call this "God"; and he urges full appreciation of the
necessity of all that is. The result, from his point of view, is also love, but
love of the whole, not love of "God."
Nietzsche seems to play Spinoza off Descartes deliberately, proposing his
amor fati in the same section as he pokes fun at the^ogito.81 Nietzsche is not
entirely supportive of Spinoza's views; he criticizes Spinoza in a number of
passages, sometimes explicitly in connection with "the intellectual love of
God."82 Yet the gestalt of Spinoza's vision strikes Nietzsche as akin to his
own. He describes Spinoza as returning to the innocence of nature:
[T] eased by who knows what recollection, he mused on the question of
what really remained to him of the famous morsus conscientiae [sting of
conscience]—he who had banished good and evil to the realm of human
imagination and had wrathfully defended the honor of his "free" God
against those blasphemers who asserted that God effected all things sub
ratione boni [for a good reason] ("but that would mean making God sub-
ject to fate and would surely be the greatest of all absurdities"). The world,
for Spinoza, had returned to the state of innocence in which it had lain
before the invention of the bad conscience: what then had become of the
morsus conscientiae^
Nietzsche also calls Spinoza his precursor in an elated note to Franz Over-
beck:
I am really amazed, really delighted! I have a precursor, and what a pre-
cursor! I hardly knew Spinoza: what brought me to him now was the
guidance of instinct. Not only is his whole tendency like my own—to make
knowledge the most powerful passion—but also in five main points of his
doctrine I find myself; this most abnormal and lonely thinker is closest to
me in these points precisely: he denies free will, purposes, the moral world
order, the nonegoistical, evil; of course the differences are enormous, but
they are differences more of period, culture, field of knowledge. In summa:
my solitariness which, as on very high mountains, has often, often made
me gasp for breath and lose blood, is now at least a solitude for two.
Strange!84
Nietzsche is no ideologue here. Although he rejects theistic formulations,
he recognizes in Spinoza a kindred thinker who finds beauty in the world's
internal structure and denies any external purposes. Significantly, this note
was written during the summer of r88r, the year when Nietzsche was for-
mulating the first edition of The Gay Science.*5
The demon's offer may function to remind us how far we are from pro-
found satisfaction in reality; and in this respect it may spur us to consider
how we may become more satisfied with our lives. Section 310 offers a vision
that might assist us in seeing our natural, finite lives as beautiful:
How greedily this wave approaches, as if it were after something! How it
crawls with terrifying haste into the inmost nooks of this labyrinthine cliff!
It seems that it is trying to anticipate someone; it seems that something of
value, high value, must be hidden there.—And now it comes back, a little


150
COMIC RELIEF
more slowly but still quite white with excitement; is it disappointed? Has
it found what it looked for? Does it pretend to be disappointed?—But
already another wave is approaching, still more greedily and savagely than
the first, and its soul, too, seems to be full of secrets and the lust to dig
up treasures. Thus live waves—thus live we who will—more I shall not
say.
Instead of continuing this reflection, Nietzsche addresses the waves them-
selves, picturing them as angry at him for revealing their secret. He con-
cludes:
Carry on as you like, roaring with overweening pleasure and malice—or
dive again.... [H]ow could I think of betraying you? For—mark my
word!—I know you and your secret, I know your kind! You and I—are
we not of one kind? You and I—do we not have one secret?66
Like waves, Nietzsche suggests, our lives have no purpose beyond them-
selves. We have no experience of another realm that might establish purposes;
any meaning they have is given by us. And yet we can see them as beautiful.
We do not need an overarching teleology or a standard outside of life. Ocean
waves are beautiful not because they fulfill some further purpose, but because
of the superficial purposiveness of their form.87 The purposiveness we see,
however, is our own projection, like Nietzsche's anthropomorphic address
to the waves. It is we who pronounce them beautiful and good.
Our pronouncing things good brings us back, full cycle, to section i, where
Nietzsche contemplates the human need to pronounce things good—as op-
posed to evil. He reconsiders this question more directly in the section that
follows the demon's parable, where Zarathustra first appears.


Zarathustra Lives
Like a Dancer
We require that a man should be so large and columnar in the landscape,
that it should deserve to be recorded, that he arose and girded up his loins,
and departed to such a place. The pictures most credible to us are those
of majestic men who prevailed at their entrance, and convinced the senses;
as happened to the eastern magian who was sent to test the merits of
Zertusht or Zoroaster [Zarathustra]. When a Yuani sage arrived at Balkh,
the Persians tell us, Gushtasp appointed a day on which the Mobeds of
every country should assemble, and a golden chair was placed for the Yuani
sage. Then the beloved Yezdam, the prophet of Zertusht, advanced into
the midst of the assembly. The Yuani sage, on seeing the chief, said, "This
form and this gait cannot lie, and nothing but truth can proceed from
them.' "'
According to George Stack, Nietzsche wrote " 'Das 1st es!" (That is it!) in
the margin alongside this passage from Emerson's "Character." Once again
Nietzsche reflects on the literal stature of a man, though in a way that con-
trasts with section io8's assessment of "the big man." Here a man's stature
is seen as a good guide to the person. Yet Emerson's point is not so much
about the massive male but about the impressiveness of the man's bearing.
Such a person presents himself as a compelling, living presence.
According to Nietzsche, Zarathustra "overtook" him.2 Zarathustra capti-
vates by embodying grace—not in the theological sense but in the human,
physical sense of the Emersonian character who steps suddenly, over-
whelmingly, before us. Nietzsche characterizes him in this way in the Pro-
logue of Thus Spoke Zarathustra. The saint who encounters him there re-
marks: "Yes, I recognize Zarathustra. His eyes are pure, and around his
mouth there hides no disgust. Does he not walk like a dancer?"3
From the standpoint of Europe in the nineteenth century, the historical
Zarathustra would seem a ghostly apparition, if present at all. But Nietzsche
recognizes a vital impulse, still active in his own era, that he traces back to
JEVEN
151


152
COMIC RELIEF
Zarathustra. Nietzsche announces that "The tragedy begins" with Zarathu-
stra, and he considers it a tragedy in which we remain implicated.
Nietzsche and the Historical Zarathustra
"It seems to have gone unnoticed...," Kaufmann observes, "how close
Nietzsche had come to the real Zarathustra's view."4 How historically accu-
rate is Nietzsche's portrait of Zarathustra? Jacques Duchesne-Guillemin, one
of our most prominent contemporary scholars of Zoroastrianism, considers
Nietzsche to have been knowledgeable but ironic in his depiction:
The Iranian prophet has never been a truly popular figure in Europe, and
it is significant that the only literary work that calls him by his real name,
Nietzsche's Also sprach Zarathustra, 1885-7, should present a picture of him
which is the almost exact opposite of truth. This was deliberate on the part
of Nietzsche, who was not at all ignorant of the real Zoroaster, but wanted
to use him as a mouthpiece for his own message. The public, however, did
not see the difference and Nietzsche's enormous irony was lost.5
This irony, presumably, is that the original Zarathustra preached the dis-
tinction of good and evil as his basic philosophical dichotomy, while Nietz-
sche's Zarathustra dismisses it as an all-too-human fabrication. We will fur-
ther consider the nature of Nietzsche's irony.
Aside from Kaufmann, Nietzsche scholars have made little effort to com-
pare Nietzsche's character with his historical prototype. Most scholarly treat-
ments give the impression that Nietzsche called his character Zarathustra just
because he liked the name. This strikes me as extremely unlikely. In the first
place, Nietzsche explicitly described his work to Ida Overbeck as an effort
"to revalue Zoroaster."6 His intention, apparently, was to reconsider the his-
torical Zarathustra's achievement, not to dismiss it or to construct a character
only marginally related to the original.
Second, Nietzsche's training as a classicist provided him with ample
knowledge of the ancient reports and legends about the original Zarathu-
stra—he was not speculating in ignorance.7 Nietzsche would surely have been
acquainted with Pliny's report that Zarathustra entered the world laughing
instead of crying,8 and with Aristotle's opinion that Zarathustra lived six
thousand years before the death of Plato.9 Nietzsche was also aware of He-
rodotus' claim that the Magi, hereditary priests, adopted Zoroastrianism but
retained some of their previous beliefs. Although he does not explicitly men-
tion Zarathustra, this account concurs with the picture of the Magi presented
by the Avesta (or Zend-Avesta), the Zoroastrian scripture.10 Herodotus also
reports that the Persians regarded lying as the most vile of acts.11 Nietzsche
presumably draws from this account in both Thus Spoke Zarathustra and
Ecce Homo, where the chief Persian virtue is described as telling the truth.12
Zarathustra is explicitly discussed by Diogenes Laertius, whom Nietzsche
knew well, having presented a lecture on Diogenes' sources to the Leipzig


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
153
student philological society and later written a prizewinning essay on the
thinker.13 Diogenes may be responsible for Nietzsche's decision to use Zar-
athustra's actual name rather than "Zoroaster," for he draws attention to the
latter's status as a descriptive characterization, literally meaning "adorer of
the stars."14 Diogenes also describes the Magi as opposing two powers, one
that of the good god and the other that of the devil.15 Similarly, Plutarch
describes Zarathustra as believing that "there are two divine powers working
in opposition to each other, the one is the creator of the good, the other is
the creator of the bad."16 Nietzsche would be aware of all these views just
by virtue of his philological education.
A third reason for thinking that Nietzsche aims to address the philosophy
of the original Zarathustra is that his style in Thus Spoke Zarathustra appears
to be modeled, at least in part, on the Avesta. Jung thinks that Nietzsche
must have studied the Avesta in detail:
Nietzsche was a well-read man, in many ways very learned, so it is quite
probable or even certain, that he must have made some special studies
along the line of the Zend-Avesta, a great part of which was already trans-
lated in his days. ... It consists of books of very different periods, the ear-
liest of which, the Yasna, includes the so-called Gathas, sermons in verse.
These are called the verse sermons of Zarathustra and are written in a
special dialect of old Iranian; as they are very archaic, the oldest of all, it
is assumed that they really go back to the time of Zarathustra. And these
would form the model for the verse sermons of Nietzsche's Zarathustra."
Roger Hollingrake also believes that Nietzsche was acquainted with the
Avesta. Commenting on Nietzche's critical reconsideration in Part IV of the
affirmation that concludes Part III, Hollingrake concludes, "This original
twist in the plot might perhaps be traced back to the Avesta, which offers
many parallels with Buddhist and Christian scripture in treating of the rivalry
and warfare between the competing supremacies of good and evil."18
Nietzsche's depiction of Zarathustra in section 342 converges in several
respects with what is known about the life of the historical Zarathustra.
Zarathustra's age of thirty, specified in this section, is traditionally cited as
the age at which the historical Zarathustra's mission began. The ten years
that follow, mentioned in section 342, coincide with the span of time cited
in the Zoroastrian religion's texts. Admittedly, the historical Zoroaster was
actively involved in proselytizing between the ages of thirty and forty, while
Nietzsche's Zarathustra "does not tire" of his mountain cave. Nevertheless,
Zarathustra was not successful in attracting any followers during these first
ten years; and the first years after forty, the age when Nietzsche's character
begins his mission, are also times of significant challenge in the life and
mission of the historical prophet.19 This is arguably the subject matter of the
later Thus Spoke Zarathustra, which opens with a virtually unchanged version
of section 342.
The setting of section 342 also appears to be Zarathustra's Persia. Lake
Urmi, where the historical Zarathustra allegedly lived, is explicitly men-


154
COMIC RELIEF
tioned.20 The original Zarathustra also reportedly had a mountain cave, like
Nietzsche's Zarathustra.21
Parallels between the plots of Zarathustra's story in the Avestf and that
told in Thus Spoke Zarathustra abound. Among other themes, Zarathustra
expresses his sense of a mission.22 The historical Zarathustra also experienced
despondency over his difficulty from the outset in getting anyone to under-
stand his teachings, as does Nietzsche's hero.23 The two Zarathustras are both
moved to self-examination in response.24 The historical Zarathustra's story,
like the plot of Thus Spoke Zarathustra, involves a return home after consid-
erable wandering.25 Temptation also figures in both stories.26 These conver-
gences are further evidence that Nietzsche is concerned with the actual Zar-
athustra, not inventing a completely new character.
Let us return to section 342, which consists primarily of Zarathustra's
hymn to the sun. This might be seen as a reference to the Indie version of
the religion that ancient India shared with Persia, for the Hindu Rig-Veda
opens with an invocation of Agni, the fire god. One passage from this in-
vocation, in particular, resonates with the prayer of Nietzsche's Zarathustra,
who is moved by the brilliance of the sun to contemplate his own happiness:
To you, Agni, who shine upon darkness, we come day after day, bringing
our thoughts and homage to you, the king over sacrifices, the shining
guardian of the Order, growing in your own house. Be easy for us to reach,
like a father to his son. Abide with us, Agni, for our happiness.27
Nevertheless, a possible reference to the Rig-Veda would not be incom-
patible with gestures toward the historical Zarathustra. Hinduism in India
stemmed from the same religious root as Zoroastrianism in Persia. Agni was
a god in this early religion; Zoroastrians named this god Atar and continued
to worship him by means of fire sacrifices.28 Although the Indian religion
eventually represented its gods as devas, the spiritual beings that the Persians
should cease worshiping, according to Zarathustra, they were at one time
represented as asuras (ahuras), the beings that Zarathustra elevates, declaring
one of them, Ahuramazda, the supreme God.29 Martin Haug concludes that
even on the basis of the later parts of the Rig-veda, which describe the wars
between the devas and the asuras, "that the Asuras of the Brahmanical lit-
erature are the supreme beings of the Parsis. [Ahuramazda with his arch-
angels] is, according to these statements, hardly to be doubted."30
At any rate, Nietzsche's Zarathustra's opening prayer conforms in format
with the Avesta as well as the Rig-Veda. The Avesta begins with the hymns
that constitute the Gathas. 31 This book of Gathas is the only book scholars
now believe to have come directly from Zarathustra.32 The second Gatha,
moreover, opens with Zarathustra's prayer to Ahuramazda as the source of
fire, life, and happiness. It is enough like the prayer of Nietzsche's Zarathustra
that it may well have served as a model:
Blessed is he, blessed is every one, to whom Ahuramazda, ruling by his
own will, shall grant the two everlasting powers [health and immortality].


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
155
For this very good I beseech Thee [Ahuramazda]. Mayest Thou through
Thy angel of piety, Armaiti, give me happiness, the good true things, and
the possession of the good mind.
I believe Thee to be the best being of all, the source of light for the
world. Every one shall choose Thee [believe in Thee] as the source of light,
Thee, O Mazda, most beneficent spirit! Thou createdst all good true things
by means of the power of Thy good mind at any time, and promisedst us
[who believe in Thee] a long life....
I will believe Thee to be the powerful benefactor, O Mazda! For Thou
givest with Thy hand, filled with helps, good to the righteous man, as well
as to the wicked, by means of the warmth of the fire strengthening the
good things. For this reason the vigour of the good mind has fallen to my
lot.. .."
Nietzsche's character's invocation, addressed to the sun, also bears simi-
larities to Zarathustra's first speech in the Gathas. This speech is presented
as revelation, which Zarathustra received in a trance attained by contem-
plating sacred flames,34 and he admonishes his followers that they should
similarly contemplate the fire.
I will now tell you who are assembled here the wise sayings of Mazda, the
praises of Ahura, and the hymns of the good spirit, the sublime truth which
I see arising out of these sacred flames.
You shall, therefore, hearken to the soul of nature [i.e., to plough and
cultivate the earth]; contemplate the beams of fire with a most pious
mind!35
Why Zarathustra?
Certain elements of Zoroastrian thought appear to run directly counter to
Nietzsche's views. In the first place, Nietzsche would be unlikely to applaud
the monotheistic tendencies of Zoroastrianism. We have already observed
that Nietzsche praises polytheism for its validation of diverse types of indi-
viduals. By contrast,
[m]onotheism ... this rigid consequence of the doctrine of one normal
human type—the faith in one normal god beside whom there are only
pseudo-gods—was perhaps the greatest danger that has yet confronted hu-
manity. It threatened us with the premature stagnation that, as far as we
can see, most other species have long reached; for all of them believe in
one normal type and ideal for their species, and they have translated the
morality of mores definitely into their own flesh and blood.... [M]an
alone among all the animals has no eternal horizons and perspectives.36
The notion that there are eternal horizons and perspectives, however,
would seem to be exactly what the original Zarathustra proposed. According
to Duchesne-Guillemin:


156
COMIC RELIEF
The Veda ... shows that the antagonism between good and evil had already
been clearly formulated if not personified in Indo-Iranian times. Zoroaster
lifted it to the dignity of a major cosmic cleavage.37
Duchesne-Guillemin describes Zarathustra as being "in the fullest sense of
the word, the first theologian." 38
More straightforwardly, Zarathustra's analysis of good and evil would
seem a case of precisely the type of moral dualism that Nietzsche rejects.39
The Zoroastrian suggestion that a beatific afterlife awaits those who ally
themselves with good as opposed to evil seems even further removed from
Nietzsche's apparent position, since Nietzsche frequently argues that the af-
terlife is completely "imaginary" and an "anti-life" postulation. Furthermore,
Zoroastrianism is a predecessor of Christianity—and insofar as the two faiths
share common ground, Zoroastrianism would seem to be a target for the
same attacks that Nietzsche aims against the latter.
Despite all these evident grounds for opposing Zoroastrianism, however,
Nietzsche had good grounds for the favorable assessment that seems implicit
in his choice of a protagonist. In the first place, Zarathustra's accommodation
of lesser deities makes his monotheism a qualified matter. Zarathustra seems
to have considered the angel Sraosha, an intermediary between God and
man, as a separate, quasi-divine personality.40 Furthermore, in recommend-
ing the worship of the ahuras and not the devas, Zarathustra acknowledges
that all of them exist.
To the extent that Zarathustra is a monotheist, moreover, his monotheism
might be seen as the construction of an image in which contrary powers are
brought together and deified as all aspects of a single divine reality. If so,
Zarathustra's brand of monotheism would not be vulnerable to Nietzsche's
attack that one type of person is elevated and all others denigrated by the
viewpoint.
This becomes even more evident when one considers a second basis for
Nietzsche's high opinion of Zarathustra—the latter's conviction that good
and evil are interconnected. The popular image of Zarathustra—one en-
couraged by the reports of Diogenes Laertius and Plutarch cited earlier—is
that Zarathustra characterized good and evil as separate, opposed principles.
But this is not an accurate picture. Martin Haug (1827-1876), of whom Nietz-
sche was aware and whose scholarly work on Zarathustra was already in
circulation by the time Nietzsche wrote The Gay Science,41 contends that "a
separate evil spirit of equal power with Ahuramazda, and always opposed to
him, is entirely foreign to Zarathustra's theology."42 According to Zarathu-
stra's doctrine:
The two primeval causes ..., though different, were united, and produced
the world of material things, as well as that of the spirit. ... The one who
procured the "reality" ... is called ... "the good mind," the other, through
whom the "non-reality" ... originated, bears the name ... "the evil mind."
All good, true, perfect things, which fall under the category of "reality,"
are the productions of the "good mind"; while all that is bad and delusive,
belongs to the sphere of "non-reality," and is traced to the "evil mind."


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
157
They are the two moving causes in the universe, united from the beginning,
and therefore, called "twins." ... They are present everywhere; in Ahura-
mazda as well as in men.43
The two principles, when considered united within Ahuramazda himself, are
called spento mainyush, "the beneficent spirit," and angro mainyush, "the
hurtful spirit." The holy and destructive spirits, considered as twins, emerge
from a common divine unity. Haug emphasizes the importance of the in-
terconnection between good and evil, according to the original Zarathustra:
Both are as inseparable as day and night, and though opposed to each
other, are indispensable for the preservation of creation.... Spento-
mainyush has created the light of day, and Angro-mainyush the darkness
of night; the former awakens men to their duties; the latter lulls them to
sleep. Life is produced by Spento-mainyush, but extinguished by Angro-
mainyush, whose hands, by releasing the soul from the fetters of the body,
enables her to rise into immortality and everlasting life.44
As aspects of Ahuramazda, the two principles are considered as co-relative
and both essential to creation. They are complements evident throughout
nature, much like the Chinese yin and yang.
In the original Zarathustra's doctrine, the hurtful spirit is not Ahura-
mazda's opponent:
Angro is no separate being, opposed to Ahuramazda.. . . And, indeed, we
never find Angro mainyush mentioned as a constant opponent of Ahura-
mazda in the Gathas, as is the case in later writings.45
Only in later Zoroastrianism was the beneficent spirit interpreted as identical
with Ahuramazda and the hurtful spirit reinterpreted as separate from Ahur-
amazda and his constant antagonist. According to Haug, the consequence
was the "dualism of God and the Devil," a corruption of Zarathustra's doc-
trine that arose "in consequence of misunderstandings and false interpreta-
tions."46
The actual Zarathustra's doctrine presents a subtle view of the interde-
pendence of good and evil, which resembles Nietzsche's own perspective that
"the evil instincts are expedient, species-preserving, and indispensable to as
high a degree as the good ones; their function is merely different."47 In all
probability Nietzsche would also have had some sympathy for the Zoroas-
trian doctrine that God is the source of evil as well as good. In On the
Genealogy of Morals, Nietzsche describes his own youthful resolution of the
problem of the origin of evil:
In fact, the problem of the origin of evil pursued me even as a boy of
thirteen: at an age in which you have "Half childish trifles, half God in
your heart," I devoted to it my first childish literary trifle, my first philo-
sophical effort—and as for the "solution" of the problem I posed at that
time, well, I gave the honor to God, as was only fair, and made him the
father of evil.48


158
COMIC RELIEF
Strikingly, this is precisely the solution proposed by Zarathustra. Nietzsche
reports his childhood speculation similarly in one of his 1884 journal entries:
When I was twelve years old I conjured up for myself a marvelous trinity:
God the Father, God the Son, and God the Devil. My deduction was that
God, thinking himself, created the second person of the godhead, but that
to be able to think himself he had to think his opposite, and thus had to
create it.—That is how I began to philosophize.49
A third reason for thinking that Nietzsche's view of Zarathustra would
have been more favorable than his view of Christianity is that the ancient
prophet's views do not converge in all respects with the later religion.
Duchesne-Guillemin notes that the discrepancies between Zarathustra's vi-
sion and Christianity are numerous.
[H]is doctrine and his spirit remain different from those of Christianity.
Far from enjoining forgiveness of trespasses, Zoroaster preaches that it is
as important to ill-treat the wicked as it is to be good to the good.50
Altogether, in Zoroaster's system, suffering does not hold the essential place
it occupies in the Christian theology which centers on a crucifixion, or in
the Gnostic pessimism for which the whole universe, including the god
who made it, is evil. At the origin of Zoroaster's universe there is not a
fall, there is a choice.51
Man is born pure, not, as is taught in Christianity, defiled with original
sin. To keep his soul from sin, discernment has been given him, a faculty
which enables him to distinguish between good and evil and to choose,
from free will, either the one or the other.52
Some of the most pointed complaints that Nietzsche registers against
Christianity do not apply to Zoroastrianism. Nature is not vilified; life on
earth is treated as cosmically significant; suffering is neither emphasized nor
valued in its own right; sin is considered not a natural disposition but a
failure in discernment; the needs inherent in human psychology are not
denied but are acknowledged and respected. Nietzsche apparently noticed
differences between Zarathustra and his own moralistic contemporaries:
In the great economy of the whole, the terrible aspects of reality (in affects,
in desires, in the will to power) are to an incalculable degree more necessary
than that form of petty happiness which people call "goodness"; one ac-
tually has to be quite lenient to accord the latter any place at all, considering
that it presupposes an instinctive mendaciousness. I shall have a major
occasion to demonstrate how the historical consequences of optimism, this
abortion homines optimi, have been uncanny beyond measure. Zarathustra,
who was the first to grasp that the optimist is just as decadent as the
pessimist, and perhaps more harmful, says: "Good men never speak the
truth. "53
Zarathustra, the first psychologist of the good, is—consequently—a friend
of the evil. . .. When mendaciousness at any price monopolizes the word


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
159
"truth" for its perspective, the really truthful man is bound to be branded
with the worst names.54
In general, Zarathustra was not nearly as absolutistic as the Christians
eventually became. Duchesne-Guillemin observes:
Though Zoroastianism was never, even in the thinking of the founder, as
aggressively monotheistic as, for instance, Judaism or Islam, it does rep-
resent an original attempt at unifying under the worship of one supreme
god a polytheistic religion comparable to those of the ancient Greeks, Lat-
ins, Indians, and other early peoples.
Its other salient feature, namely dualism, was never understood in an
absolute, rigorous fashion. Good and Evil fight in an unequal battle in
which the former is assured of triumph. God's omnipotence is thus only
temporarily limited. In this struggle man must enlist because of his capacity
of free choice. He does so with his soul and body, not against his
body . .. 5 5
As this point about the role of the body suggests, Zarathustra's relative
emphasis on the natural world is another difference with Christianity that
Nietzsche would only have applauded. Zarathustra's advice to his disciples is
earthly and naturalistic. He stresses the importance of cattle raising and ag-
riculture in his opening prayer, "In Thee was Armaiti [spirit of the earth],
in Thee the very wise fertiliser of the soil, O Ahuramazda, Thou spirit!"56
Duchesne-Guillemin also reports that Zarathustra was not particularly oth-
erworldly:
Zoroaster may have ignored eschatology in the ordinary sense of the term,
because to him the end was near: the advent of the rule of God, with the
chastisement of the daeva worshippers, was to take place on this earth. To
what extent he may have felt obliged to postpone the event, or to translate
it into terms of after-life, we shall never know.57
Zarathustra's focus was concrete and earthly even in his religious inno-
vations. Haug describes them as a response to his particular political situa-
tion. Before the religious schism of which Zarathustra was a part, the religion
that was common to Persia and India involved the worship of two sorts of
deities, the devas and the ahuras. The regions gradually diverged in their
lifestyles, however. The Persians departed from the relative nomadism that
predominated in the region and developed permanent agricultural settle-
ments. Their towns became targets for attack from the east, where their
previous religious compatriots had remained largely nomadic herdsmen. The
Persians construed these attacks, waged largely in the hope of taking booty,
as evidence of the effectiveness of the Indians' sacrifices to their gods, who
by now were mostly characterized as devas.
In this context, Zarathustra's opposition to deva worship had a political
focus, not solely a religious one.58 Zarathustra used the distinction between
good, and evil to emphasize the difference between deities who were benign
and those who wrought destruction: "The evil against which Ahuramazda


160
COMIC RELIEF
and all good men are fighting is called drukhsh, 'destruction, or lie,' which
is nothing but a personification of the Devas."59
Zarathustra recognized a contradiction in his society's religious practices.
The Persians' mistake in worshipping the devas was that they were aiding
their opponents, not themselves. He asked his contemporaries, "Why are we
worshipping the enemy's gods? Aren't we making them and the enemy
stronger? And at our own expense?" In effect, Zarathustra was the original
Feuerbachian,
Zarathustra's Revaluation of Values
Probably the most important reason for Nietzsche's interest in Zarathustra
was his pioneering revaluation of values. Although Zarathustra proposed a
fundamental dichotomy of good and evil, Nietzsche suggests, the basis for
moving "beyond good and evil" was already inherent in the Zoroastrian
abhorence of lying. According to Zarathustra's doctrine, angro mainyush, the
destructive spirit, was explicitly associated with lying, while the triumph of
the good over destructiveness depended on one's allying oneself with truth.
Zarathustra's commitment to truthfulness, long before Christianity developed
its intellectual conscience, was the first step toward modernity's religious self-
overcoming.
In his autobiography Ecce Homo, Nietzsche claims that his indentification
with Zarathustra is based on their common commitment to truth:
I have not been asked, as I should have been asked, what the name of
Zarathustra means in my mouth, the mouth of the first immoralist: for
what constitutes the tremendous historical uniqueness of that Persian is
just the opposite of this. Zarathustra was the first to consider the fight of
good and evil the very wheel in the machinery of things: the transposition
of morality into the metaphysical realm, as a force, cause, and end in itself,
is his work. But this question itself is at bottom its own answer. Zarathustra
created this most calamitous error, morality; consequently, he must also
be the first to recognize it. Not only has he more experience in this matter,
for a longer time, than any other thinker—after all, the whole of history
is the refutation by experiment of the principle of the so-called "moral
world order"—what is more important is that Zarathustra is more truthful
than any other thinker. His doctrine, and his alone, posits truthfulness as
the highest virtue; this means the opposite of the cowardice of the "idealist"
who flees from reality; Zarathustra has more intestinal fortitude than all
other thinkers taken together. To speak the truth and to shoot well with
arrows, that is Persian virtue.—Am I understood?—The self-overcoming
of morality, out of truthfulness; the self-overcoming of the moralist into
his opposite—into me—that is what the name of Zarathustra means in my
mouth.60
This statement is remarkable. For Nietzsche, Zarathustra is "the self-
overcoming of the moralist. . . into me." The historical Zarathustra's project


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
l6l
contained the seeds of its own destruction, claims Nietzsche, and Nietzsche
himself is bringing the original Zarathustra's work to fruition. The interpre-
tation of reality that Zarathustra began was mistaken, but Nietzsche asks,
"So what?" Nietzsche considers Zarathustra on a par with an avant-garde
scientist. His overall framework may have been superseded, but his signifi-
cance lies in the experimental results of his research.
Nietzsche also suggests in Beyond Good and Evil that Zarathustra's efforts
opened the way to the apparently opposite endeavor, his own:
Whoever has really gazed down with an Asiatic and supra-Asiatic eye61 into
the most world-denying of all possible modes of thought—beyond good
and evil, and no longer like Buddha and Schopenhauer under the spell and
illusion of morality—perhaps by this very act, without really desiring it,
may have opened his eyes to the opposite ideal: the ideal of the most high-
spirited, energetic and world-affirming man, who has not only learned to
come to terms with and to assimilate what was and is, but who wants to
have it again as it was and is to all eternity, insatiably calling out "Once
more!"62
Zarathustra, as Nietzsche sees him, takes his moral doctrine seriously and
yet is so committed to honesty that he is willing to accept the destruction of
this doctrine at his own hand. Nietzsche identifies with this Zarathustra. In
this self-portrait, Nietzsche positions himself as the madman who is dizzied
with grief at his own murderous deed but so honest that he, like Luther,
cannot do otherwise.
The comparison of Zarathustra with Nietzsche's crazed murderer of God
might sound far-fetched, but one of Zarathustra's central accomplishments
might be characterized as deicide. Duchesne-Guillemin describes the original
Zarathustra's assault on deva worship in these terms.
Zoroaster rejects the blood sacrifice and the offering of the sacred liquor.
The gods who have been taking part in these sacrifices are now starved.
He eliminates them, together with the myths concerning them. In this way
he brings to a head the process of depreciation.... It is the first Twilight
of the Gods.63
What Zarathustra accomplished, in devaluing the devas, was the first re-
corded revaluation of what had hitherto been considered holy. Even straight-
forwardly, Zarathustra invented the move that later amounted to Nietzsche's
philosophical trademark—the revaluation of values.
Tragedy and Comedy Revisited
For all of Nietzsche's reasons to be interested in Zarathustra and Zoroastri-
anism, we have yet to penetrate the meaning of section 342'$ title, "Incipit
tragoedia—The Tragedy Begins." Which tragedy does Nietzsche have in
mind? What is tragic about the story that the section relates?


162
COMIC RELIEF
One possibility is that the title urges us to see Zarathustra as a tragic hero,
like the protagonist of a Greek tragedy. The tragic hero, Nietzsche contends
in The Birth of Tragedy, was historically a late development. The original
tragedy consisted entirely of the chorus, whose function was to effect a mys-
tical transformation of the spectators' sense of themselves:
The process of the tragic chorus is the dramatic proto-phenomenon: to see
oneself transformed before one's own eyes and to begin to act as if one
had actually entered into another body, another character.... Here we have
a surrender of individuality and a way of entering into another character.
And this phenomenon is encountered epidemically: a whole throng expe-
riences the magic of this transformation.
... In this magic transformation the Dionysian reveler sees himself as
a satyr, and as a satyr, in turn, he sees the god.. . ,64
When the presentation of a drama and the appearance of a tragic hero
were later added, the chorus retained its importance. The tragic hero came
on stage only after the chorus had instigated a transformation of the audi-
ence's experiential condition:
Now the. dithyrambic chorus was assigned the task of exciting the mood
of the listeners to such a Dionysian degree that, when the tragic hero
appeared on the stage, they did not see the awkwardly masked human being
but rather a visionary figure, born as it were from their own rapture.65
When the hero Zarathustra appears at the end of The Gay Science, I think
that Nietzsche expects that the rest of the book has served the function of
the chorus. Ideally, the reader has been initiated by reading thus far. The
book has made us aware of the transformative powers of assuming different
perspectival visions, and it has engaged us in the activity of shifting perspec-
tives, pushing us continually to reconsider. By the book's end, Nietzsche
hopes, we are capable of entertaining Zarathustra's perspective and seeing
his achievement in a transfigured light.
. Section 342's title might also refer to the religious history that followed
Zarathustra. Zoroastrianism's influence on Judaism and Christianity allow
Nietzsche to address his own tradition by means of his retelling of the Zar-
athustra story. "The tragedy" that "begins" with the historical Zarathustra
includes the Judeo-Christian developments that follow him. Historically, the
tragedy begins when Zarathustra initiated an alternative perspective on the
traditional pantheon. What Zarathustra accomplished with his primal dis-
tinction, Nietzsche's comments suggests, is the initial move toward perspec-
tivism.
Western religious and moral traditions have not gone this route with
Zarathustra's insight. They have built upon his distinction between good and
evil by elaborating it in ever finer detail. But to see Zarathustra as pronounc-
ing a principle once and for all is a mistake, in Nietzsche's view. Instead,
Zarathustra was embarked on an ongoing enterprise, the enterprise of de-
veloping discernment, greater subtlety in making distinctions.


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
163
In revaluing Zarathustra, Nietzsche no longer represents him simply as
the founder of the tradition's errors (although historically, Nietzsche recog-
nizes, this is apt). Instead, Nietzsche presents Zarathustra as the first to en-
gage in the enterprise of reconsideration, of identifying errors, a process per-
fected by refining the ability to make distinctions and revaluations.
Zarathustra's project, so understood, is the precursor of Nietzsche's revalu-
ation of values. This is the fundamental irony of Nietzsche's depiction, that
his rejection of the good/evil distinction continues the original Zarathustra's
work.
Various notes that Nietzsche made while formulating his picture of Zar-
athustra support this reading. In the notes, "I do not accuse, I will not accuse
the accuser himself" is Zarathustra's line. Zarathustra identifies himself in
this comment as the antithesis of contemporary moralists, though they pres-
ent themselves as Zarathustra's rightful heirs. The mission of Nietzsche's
Zarathustra is not to pronounce and enforce moral judgments on others.
Instead, it is the ongoing attainment and revision of insights. The work of
Nietzsche's Zarathustra is the on-going attainment and revision of insights,
which he does not dishonestly force into easy consistency with one another,
as another of Nietzsche's notes makes clear.
Du widersprichst heute dem, was du gestern gelehrt hast.—Aber dafur ist
gestern nicht heute, sagte Zarathustra.66
You contradict today what you taught yesterday.—But that is why yes-
terday is not today, said Zarathustra.
Other notes also contrast Zarathustra with the later moralist, who objects
to the basic nature of human beings, particularly their animal nature. Zar-
athustra instead sees more and more to admire as he becomes better ac-
quainted with the human animal:
Wie vielen edlen and feinen Ziegen bin ich auf Reisen begegnet! sagte
Z[arathustra].
"How many noble and fine goats have I met while traveling!" said
Zarathustra.67
Another reason for seeing Zarathustra as tragic is indicated by section i
of The Gay Science. Zarathustra is a "tragic" preacher of a meaning for hu-
man existence. This raises questions as to how the end of the book jibes with
the opening. In section i, as we have observed, Nietzsche describes human
history as periodically requiring such tragic doctrines as Zarathustra's, but
inevitably overwhelming them by the resurgence of comedy. Section 342,
however, does not indicate a comic resurgence. Nietzsche confuses the matter
further with an impish remark in his preface to the second edition of The
Gay Science, 'Incipit tragoedia, we read at the end of this awesomely aweless
book. Beware! Something downright wicked and malicious is announced
here: incipit parodia, no doubt."68
The book's end reminds us of Zarathustra's opening tragic era, initiating
philosophy in the West, but section i insists that our tragic era will end. By


164
COMIC RELIEF
ending the book with a gesture back to the beginning, Nietzsche suggests
something more: that we, as readers of The Gay Science, have already started
to end the tragic age. We have begun, experientially, to the extent that we
are already practicing "the gay science."
Nietzsche's notebook characterizations of Zarathustra offer further insight
as to how the tragedy begun with Zarathustra is linked to parody and com-
edy. The notes make it obvious that Nietzsche considered casting a more
comic light on Zarathustra. In more than one passage Nietzsche makes ref-
erence to the German proverb "Miissiggang 1st aller Laster Anfang," "Idle-
ness is the root of all evil." Nietzsche's notes include the following:
Zarathustra's Miissiggang ist aller Laster Anfang.
Zarathustra's idleness is the beginning of all evil.69
Nietzsche even considered referring to this adage in his title:
Zarathustra's Miissigang.
Von F. N.
fliissig feurig gliihend—aber hell:
das letzte Buch—
es soil majestatisch und selig einherrollen.—So sprach Z[arathustra] "ich
klage nicht an, ich will selbst die Anklager merit anklagen."
Zarathustra's idleness
by F[riedrich] N[ietzsche]
flowing, fiery, glowing—but bright:
the last book
it should roll along majestically and blissfully.—So spoke Zarathustra: I do
not accuse, I will not accuse the accuser himself.70
Nietzsche's joke is clear. Zarathustra, by seizing the leisure that Aristotle
describes as essential to philosophy, had time to speculate—and the result
was his discovery of evil. Playfully, Nietzsche reinterprets the German prov-
erb to describe the origin of "evil" as a term in Western thought. This
significant moment, arguably the dawn of Western metaphysics, stems not
from sublime virtue but from vice. (After all, isn't the leisure Aristotle ad-
vocates itself vice from the standpoint of such commonsense adages?)
Yet we have not exhausted Nietzsche's joke. The proverb he incorporates
is a manifestation of the petty brand of moralism that he rejects in his own
upbringing. It functions like a nursery rhyme he would like to replace. "Idle-
ness is the beginning of all evil" is a judgmental adage that one recites to
children to instill the proper concern for productive use of time. And what
is the result? In the modern era, Nietzsche contends, morality has come to
fuel a culture of frenetic industriousness, manifest even among thinkers.71
This industriousness leaves little time for the experience of insight that awak-
ened Zarathustra when he first distinguished good and evil.
The proverb, in Nietzsche's employ, exhibits the self-parody that Zara-
thustra's achievement, in initiating moral history, has now become. Zarathu-


ZARATHUSTRA LIVES
165
stra's cosmic distinction may not have been intended as a basis for making
judgmental accusations. But the morality that is its most recent descendant
has become so mechanical and petty, so pedantically adamant in its appli-
cation, that it would point accusingly at Zarathustra himself. The space that
Zarathustra required to discover his insight is a space for which contempo-
rary moralism has no time, quite literally.
Nietzsche's own tale of Zarathustra encourages the reader, however, to
judge differently. Zarathustra is eccentric and perverse from the standpoint
of moral commonplaces, yet Nietzsche presents him as a virtual superhero—
powerful, blissful, and majestic. We see again the figure that Emerson de-
scribes, whose form and gait cannot lie.
The comic ending of the tragedy is more decisively presented in Nietz-
sche's further work on Zarathustra. In Thus Spoke Zarathustra, Zarathustra
becomes the preacher of laughter and even learns to laugh himself. Roger
Hollingrake observes another sense in which this tale is comic. Zarathustra's
learning laughter, itself a comic story, is also parodic with respect to Wagner's
musicdramas.72
In particular, the debacle of "Das Eselsfest," construed as a parody of
Wagner's Grail scene, has a stunning concentration and audacity; while
Zarathustra's injunction "Learn to laugh," answering Siegfried's learning
of fear and Parsifal's learning of pity, is a master-stroke of literary and
psychological calculation.73
To learn laughter is in itself comic; but for Zarathustra to do so is parodic,
in that laughter supplants the serious alternatives that Wagner's music dra-
mas glorified—the learning of pity and fear, the two emotions Aristotle
classed as tragic.
Depicting Zarathustra as the learner and prophet of laughter, Nietzsche
suggests that his comic alternative to the tradition's "tragic" teachings rep-
resents the culmination of Zarathustra's own project. The quotation attrib-
