uted to "Man the Poet" in section 153 of The Gay Science can, in this light,
be seen as appropriate to Zarathustra.
Homo poeta.—"I myself, having made this tragedy of tragedies all by my-
self, insofar as it is finished—I, having first tied the knot of morality into
existence before I drew it so tight that only a god could untie it (which is
what Horace demands)—I myself have now slain all gods in the fourth
act, for the sake of morality. Now, what is to become of the fifth act? From
where am I to take the tragic solution?—Should I begin to think about a
comic solution?"74
Nietzsche's Zarathustra, representing the contemporary manifestation of the
impulse initiated by the historical Zarathustra, points toward the philosophy
of the future—the comic approach to the world that Nietzsche christens "the
gay science."
Recalling the first section of The Gay Science, we might even say that
Nietzsche considers comedy the ultimate aim of tragedy. The comic conclu-


166
COMIC RELIEF
sion to the tragic unfolding of Western philosophy resembles the reversal he
describes in his first book. There he contends that "the sublime metaphysical
illusion" that "accompanies science"—that it can penetrate and even correct
being—"leads science again and again to its limits at which it must turn into
art—which is really the aim of this mechanism." Socrates, according to Nietz-
sche, is the progenitor of this faith, and thus, ironically, the founder of a
tendency that aims at its own eventual undoing.75 Essentially, this is the same
reasoning that Nietzsche uses with respect to Zarathustra. Like Socrates,
Nietzsche sees Zarathustra as the founder of an impulse that moves inexo-
rably toward its own extinction.
In his artistic interpretation of Zarathustra, Nietzsche fulfills the hint at
the end of Plato's Symposium that the same person might be a master of
both tragedy and comedy. The narrator describes the event secondhand as
it was relayed to him by Aristodemus, of whom he reports:
He woke up just as the dawn was about to break; the roosters were crowing
already. He saw that the others had either left or were asleep on their
couches and that only Agathon, Aristophanes, and Socrates were still
awake, drinking out of a large cup which they were passing around from
left to right. Socrates was talking to them. Aristodemus couldn't remember
exactly what they were saying ... but the main point was that Socrates was
trying to prove to them that authors should be able to write both comedy
and tragedy: the skillful tragic dramatist should also be a comic poet.76
In revaluing Zarathustra, Nietzsche casts him as achieving that toward
which Plato only gestures. Zarathustra is the perspectival thinker, capable of
tragedy and comedy as well. The tradition has shown its capacity for tragedy.
Platonism and Christianity have both manifested "reason and passions of
the human spirit," offering doctrines to satisfy our acquired need for reasons
to love life. But these tragic visions do not satisfy our full human potential
to give value to life. Alas, they are content to value some of parts of life by
shunning others.
Well, then, as Nietzsche's Zarathustra says in the chronicle that is named
for him, "that has had its time!"77 This is Nietzsche's ultimate point in The
Gay Science. That project is over. It is time to set aside our reasons for loving
life and to reassess life as lovable, just as it is. It is time to vanquish the
tragic heroes of our history with a new wave of comedy, with the sponta-
neous delight in life, which was the ultimate aim of their seductive stories.
This includes the life of the mind. For our inquiries, like our existence gen-
erally, have no extrinsic purpose—they are simply the joy that they are.
The gay science—what a relief!


Nietzsche's Dog
I have been told that certain American Indian tribes who practice rock art
envision painted rocks as sources of insight. One meditates on such a rock
for a long time and eventually has a dream. The dream is a revelation: it
tells you what was in the rock.
Some of Nietzsche's passages have long struck me as being a bit like rocks.
They seem impervious. One passage of this nature is The Gay Science, section
312, entitled "My Dog":
I have given a name to my pain and call it "dog." It is just as faithful, just
as obtrusive and shameless, just as entertaining, just as clever as any other
dog—and I can scold it and vent my bad mood on it, as others do with
their dogs, servants, and wives.1
Nietzsche does not cast himself flatteringly. He speaks as a real grump, who
thinks it would be fun to have a dog whom he could abuse. Moreover, the
conjured image is rather ludicrous. Are we to picture Nietzsche, fishing pole
in hand, ambling to a favorite fishing hole with his "best friend," his pain,
by his side? Many rereadings after I first encountered this passage, I had no
idea what he could possibly mean about calling one's pain "dog."
Then one day, during a rather low moment, as self-pity descended, I
dreamed about the inside of this "rock." It struck me that what Nietzsche
calls his "pain" is the whole category of thoughts and feelings that strike one
as a basis for objecting to the way things are.2 Nietzsche seems to be partic-
ularly concerned to address psychological pain.3 He leaves the term pain
(Schmerz) open-ended, I think, because he wants to allow for the whole
network of wounds that he has imaginatively harbored and elaborated.4 In
Nietzsche's own case, for example, this network would have included his
sense of isolation, his distress over being misunderstood, and his feelings of
resentment.5 This pain, Nietzsche suggests, is like an animal one has nur-
tured. As one harbors it, one becomes intimately familiar with it, and it has
repaid with its loyalty. When personal pain intrudes on consciousness, one
imagines that it has always been there. One cannot imagine oneself without
167
CODA


l68
COMIC RELIEF
it;6 it has become part of one's personality. At least, it seems a part of one's
psychic entourage, like a faithful dog that accompanies one everywhere.
And don't we all have such a pet? Our experiences have formed us psy-
chically, much as our acquired skills have imposed a leaden ceiling, pressing
down on our metaphorical spines and undercutting our flexibility. Some-
where along the way, some pain has been internalized and become structural.
For each of us, something has gotten under the skin. We cannot but take it
seriously. When we reflect on it, we feel victimized, and we pity ourselves.
The harms that Nietzsche attributes to pity are salient in our condition. We
are distracted from where we are going because we so thoroughly resent what
we have already experienced. Because we pity ourselves, we seek someone to
blame.
But suppose your pain were a dog. Imagining this, you might distance
yourself from self-pity. Consider it a sheepdog that has barged inside when
you were particularly open. When an actual dog bursts into your living room,
you can scream and say "Get out!" or even curse your life as filled with such
intrusions. But to see yourself doing this is to see yourself as rather foolish.
The intruder is a dog, after all, and you would do better to try to tame it.
Perhaps, as with many animals that are so forthright in seeking shelter, you
will even be charmed by this dog. Perhaps you feed it and let it stay. Before
long you name it and buy it a collar. Even more than your pet peeves,
whatever they are, your personal pain becomes a trusty pet, available to you
whenever you call.
By calling his pain "dog," Nietzsche is illustrating, again, a change in
perspectives. He is demonstrating the value of perspectivism even in the case
of personal obstacles to affirming one's life. One can take different perspec-
tives even toward something as oppressive and immediate as pain. To call it
"my dog" is to domesticate it. A faithful dog is just as present as a part of
one's psychological structure, so the steady presence of one's pain is ac-
knowledged either way. But by seeing one's pain as a dog, one can take
attitudes toward it, and not simply be it.7 Without undermining one's affec-
tion for oneself or one's dog, one can feel exasperated by the dog's recalci-
trance. A dog, whether trained or not, has learned its standard behaviors,
and this learning is difficult to undo. Nietzsche suggests that one's pain has
its set of habits, too. We have each learned our pain, as a scholar has learned
a specialty. Given the set ways of an old dog, an old wound, or an old set
of skills, one realizes that one does best to work with its quirks. A wound
that will not heal is just a dog that will not heel; and this is not the worst
of gaps in a dog's training.8
Nietzsche's aphorism might provoke yet another set of realizations. Most
of us are attached to our pain, not only because we identify with it but
because we are actually fond of it. We enjoy being malcontents, and we enjoy
having articulate irritations to bemoan. Not just Nietzsche but most of us
take a strange comfort from being able to vent our anger on recollected
miseries. We have practiced these problems long and regularly. We know
them well, and we would miss them were they to vanish suddenly. Fortu-


NIETZSCHE'S DOG
169
nately, they are not likely to do so. They, too, have their habits, just like
dogs.
At least, this is what I discovered inside the rock that is section 312. The
comic portrait of Nietzsche treating his morose state as a dog opened for me
at a moment when I could use it, and its comic edge helped it reverberate.
The section being so opaque, I was more aware than usual when it finally
opened up. I think this was the kind of experience Nietzsche indicates in his
preface to On the Genealogy of Morals, when he discusses the fact that his
writings are "not easy to penetrate":
[T]he aphoristic form ... is not taken seriously enough. An aphorism, prop-
erly stamped and molded, has not been "deciphered" when it has simply
been read; rather, one has then to begin its exegesis, for which is required
an art of exegesis.... To be sure, one thing is necessary above all if one is
to practice reading as an art in this way, something that has been unleared
most thoroughly nowadays—and therefore it will be some time before my
writings are "readable"—something for which one has almost to be a cow
and in any case not a "modern man": rumination.9
Nietzsche wrote this passage in the year when the second edition of The
Gay Science appeared, and it raises questions about why he bothered to attach
Book V. Why not leave the original work, replete with aphorisms, for his
readers' rumination.10 Why not simply write another book if he had new
things to say? Yet presumably, this was not his reason for writing. Kaufmann
observes that Book V, "added only in the second edition, picks up themes
introduced earlier."" Perhaps Nietzsche sought to compensate for his read-
ers' exegetical clumsiness with strong restatements of earlier points. A remark
from Ecce Homo seems to confirm this impression. Nietzsche says of his
books of this period: "All my writings are fish hooks: perhaps I knew how
to fish as well as anyone?—If nothing was caught, I am not to blame. There
were no fish."12 No fish, but Nietzsche had his dog.
His dog, in the era of 1887, was certainly chained to his impression that
his readers had failed to understand him. One of the earliest sections in Book
V, titled "Our question mark," asks: "But you do not understand this? In-
deed, people will have trouble understanding us. We are looking for words;
perhaps we are also looking for ears."13 The relative clarity of Book V is
surely one of the reasons why many contemporary philosophers admire it.14
Structurally, Book V often sustains lines of discussion over a number of
sections,15 and it conveys ideas "in quick and vivid strokes."16 Perhaps the
clarity of prose in Book V is designed with the aim of greater communication
in mind.
Yet we also discover evidence of tension, and perhaps Nietzsche's bad
conscience, about his own attitudes toward being understood. In section 371,
"We Incomprehensible Ones," for example, Nietzsche comments:
Have we ever complained because we are misunderstood, misjudged, mis-
identified, slandered, misheard, and not heard? Precisely this is our fate . . .
it is also our distinction; we should not honor ourselves sufficiently if we


iyo
COMIC RELIEF
wished that it were otherwise. We are misidentified—because we ourselves
keep growing, keep changing, we shed our old bark, we shed our skins
every spring, we keep becoming younger, fuller of future.... Like trees we
grow—this is hard to understand, as is all life...."
One might see this passage as self-justification; but that interpretation would
hardly exhaust it. Section 371 is one of several in Book V where Nietzsche
speaks through a persona. We are addressed, in different sections, by the
hermit, the cynic, "we cheerful ones," "we unintelligible ones," "we artists,"
"we who are homeless," the fool, and "the wanderer." The soliloquys by the
masks present various views on how and how much one might want to
communicate to other human beings. By dramatizing his reveries on com-
munication in various states of mind, Nietzsche renders his problem comic.
He also communicates his concerns, sotto voce, or at least makes them avail-
able for a reader's rumination.
The hermit in section 364 presents the outlook of one who really does
not want to interact with others, describing the activity of "associating with
people" as something that "depends essentially on an aptitude (requiring
long practice) for accepting and eating a meal in whose cuisine one has no
confidence."18 He itemizes to a number of defensive strategies, and then
returns with another in the section that follows. One does best, says the
hermit, to "become" one's uniform (perhaps even a mustache):
[W]e, too, modestly don the dress in which (as which) others know us,
respect us, look for us—and then we appear in company, meaning among
people who are disguised without wanting to admit it. We, too, do what
all prudent masks do, and in response to every curiosity that does not
concern our "dress" we politely place a chair against the door.19
Even better, the hermit suggests, one can relate to others as a ghost, "which
is altogether advisable if one wants to get rid of them quickly and make them
afraid."20
Yet a few sections later, "the cynic" rejects the strategy of "dressing up"
for others: "My objections to the music of Wagner are physiological objec-
tions; why should I trouble to dress them up in aesthetic formulas?"21 The
cynic insists that he is thoroughly "anti-theatrical," fed up with the "actor's"
strategy, discussed several sections back, of putting on a role:
Such an instinct will have developed most easily in families of the lower
classes, who had to survive under changing pressures and coercions, in
deep dependency, who had to cut their coat against the cloth ..., until
they learned gradually to turn their coat with every wind and thus virtually
to become a coat... .
Reflect on the whole history of women: do they not have to be first of
all and above all else actresses? ... [T]hey "put on something" even when
the take off everything.22
The cynic lets another voice get the last word of the section, however. The
section ends with a rhetorical whisper: "(1 forgot to mention how my en-


NIETZSCHE'S DOG
171
lightened Wagnerian replied to these physiological objections: 'Then you re-
ally are merely not healthy enough for our music')."23 Miscommunication,
back for a curtain call!
Nietzsche's masks go on to explain why he fails to communicate. In sec-
tion 369 Nietzsche speaks for "we artists," suggesting that they should all
admit that "our taste arid our creative power" are quite different things. An
artist "might perhaps eventually produce works that far excel his own judg-
ment, so that he utters stupidities about them and himself... ."24 As an artist,
Nietzsche wonders, can one ever explain oneself?
As "homeless" in the modern age, Nietzsche suggests another reason for
communicative failure: one might instinctively have values that conflict with
those admired by one's contemporaries.25 Yet even that remark itself might
be misunderstood. As an admitted "homeless" adherent to the warlike virtues
of an earlier age, the author might have alienated his audience. And so "the
fool" insists two sections later, "The writer of this book is no misanthrope."
The fool goes on to elaborate on the difference between contemporary virtues
and those of eras in which people hated wholeheartedly. In our modern
world we reject hatred, opting for contempt instead. The fool recalls Nietz-
sche's account of his dog when he remarks, "And how much fine joy, how
much patience, how much graciousness even do we owe precisely to our
contempt!"26 And yet, the wanderer of section 379 insists that if we really
want to assess the prejudices of our time, we have to overcome this time in
ourselves.
In these passages we are presented with some of Nietzsche's own rever-
berating meditations about his painful failures to make himself understood.
These reflections present a counterthrust to what might seem straightforward
statements of his views.27 For all of Nietzsche's efforts to make himself un-
derstood, he resists the guise of pseudo-transparency. And so, Book V pres-
ents the opaque, comic nattering of multiple voices, with their disparate
advice about how the author should position himself. Book V also includes
Nietzsche's reflections on this matter in his own voice, especially in the sec-
tion titled "On the question of being understandable":
All the nobler spirits and tastes select their audience when they wish to
communicate; and choosing that, one at the same time erects barriers
against "the others." All the more subtle laws of any style have their origin
at this point: they at the same time keep away, create a distance, forbid
"entrance," understanding . .. —while they open the ears of those whose
ears are related to ours.28
Nietzsche approaches the end of the book by reasserting the structural
gesture that concludes the first edition. In section 382, the penultimate sec-
tion, Nietzsche describes himself as among the new Argonauts whose goal is
a new health. The description of his ideal, cited at the end of chapter 5,
explicitly recalls the characterization of Zarathustra in section 342 and con-
cludes by restating the claim of that section's title, "The Tragedy Begins."29


172
COMIC RELIEF
Yet this is not the final section. "The Tragedy Begins" is followed, this
time, with a further section, titled "Epilogue":
But as I slowly, slowly paint this gloomy question mark at the end and am
still willing to remind my readers of the virtues of the right reader . . . [I]t
happens that I hear all around me the most malicious, cheerful, and ko-
boldish laughter: the spirits of my own book are attacking me, pull my
ears, and call me back to order. "We can no longer stand it," they shout
at me; "away, away with this raven-black music! Are we not surrounded
by bright morning? . . . No! Not such tones! Let us strike up more agree-
able, more joyous tones!"30
Nietzsche responds by preparing to sing, even though he is not in voice.
Significantly, he comments:
At least what you are about to hear is new; and if you do not understand
it, if you misunderstand the singer, what does it matter? That happens to
be "the singer's curse." His music and manner you will be able to hear
that much better, and to his pipes — dance that much better. Is that your
So Nietzsche ends the body of the second edition, throwing the ball back to
the reader. "What is your will?" he asks.32 In keeping with his strategy
throughout The Gay Science, Nietzsche reminds the reader of his or her own
interpretive power. Nietzsche's gesture of following the wiser voices he hears
beyond his murky meditations on seriousness can at most be exemplary.
Yet Nietzsche has his hopes. It may be his curse, the singer's curse, for
his words to be misjudged. But if the reader has not yet seen what is inside
Nietzsche's more opaque formulations, at least the tone communicates. What
it communicates, Nietzsche hopes, is the urge to dance, to explore the world
while frolicking:
What? Do you really want to permit existence to be degraded for us like
this — reduced to a mere exercise for a calculator and an indoor diversion
for mathematicians? . . . Assuming that one estimated the value of a piece
of music according to how much of it could be counted, calculated, and
expressed in formulas: how absurd would such a "scientific" estimation of
music be! What would one have comprehended, understood, grasped of
it? Nothing, really nothing of what is "music" in it.33
I would not know what the spirit of a philosopher might wish more to be
than a good dancer. For the dance is his ideal, also his art, and finally also
his only piety, his "service of God."34
To take our lives and our capacities seriously, we must put aside what has
passed for seriousness so far. Instead of crouching within specialties with low
ceilings, why not carry what we have learned from them into the fuller world,
where "all things themselves are dancing"?35 However hunched our backs or
hoarse our voices, we can join the musical company, led by Nietzsche with
his pipes and dog. The great seriousness begins with our dancing, not our
crouching, and our dancing is gay science "insofar as life is music."36

Honestly, I admire the beautiful instinct of your friendship—hopefully
the expression does not sound too bestial to you—that right now you

with Goethe's comic paradigm. Nietzsche criticizes Wagner, but he understates this

toward Wagner, which seems all the more shrill because we read it retrospectively
through awareness of Nietzsche Contra Wagner, in which Nietzsche reedited his com-
plaints to enhance their vitriolic tenor. It is only in Book V, for example, that Nietz-
sche emphasizes the actor as the exemplar of inauthentic existence, presumably with
Wagner in mind.

that we no longer need to feel that everything depends on frail and inadequate

[T]he Dionysian man resembles Hamlet: both have once looked truly into the
essence of things, they have gained knowledge, and nausea inhibits action; for
their action could not change anything in the eternal nature of things; they
feel it to be ridiculous or humiliating that they should be asked to set right a
world that is out of joint. Knowledge kills action; action requires the veils of
illusion: that is the doctrine of Hamlet, not that cheap wisdom of Jack the
Dreamer who reflects too much and, as it were, from an excess of possibilities

in reference to Jews and Gentiles in Book V (see GS 348, p. 291; KSA 3, p. 584).
Importantly, however, the passage is respectful in its postulation of a reason why
Jewish scholars often hold logic in high esteem. The rhyme just as quickly answers
the question of anti-Semitism. "Straight pride" is not among Nietzsche's grounds for
criticism. If the person described appears rather ridiculous in this rhyme, I think this
is because Nietzsche is an equal-opportunity caricaturist, not because the object of
caricature may be Jewish.

others. The praise of radical individualism is emphatic in GS 98, 120, and 318. Nietz-
sche's own encouragement of a lighthearted sensibility can be seen in GS i and 327.

logical Types, trans. H. G. Baynes, rev. R. F. C. Hull, Bollingen Series 20 (Princeton:
Princeton University Press, 1971), pp. 136-146. It is not obvious that thinking is able

ply that changed thinking can change what we take to be habitual—but only after

NOTES TO PAGES 39—42
101 I thank Paula J. Fulks for this story.
102 TSZ, pp. 127-128; KSA 4, pp. 17-18.
103 GS, poem 36, p. 54 (my translation); KSA 3, p. 361. Cf. Koelb, "Reading as a
Philosophical Strategy," p. 152:
The commonplace German expression "das A and O" ("the alpha and the
omega") is regularly used to mean the sum total of something, even the "be-
all and end-all." The clear implication of the poem is that the author did at
one time think his wisdom was something grand, all-inclusive, and definitive.
That interpretation comes under scrutiny when a now somewhat older Nietz-
sche looks back at his early writing and finds it "nicht mehr so," no longer
what he once thought it was. The text is the same, but its meaning has radically
changed. That change is cleverly exemplified in the rereading of the poem's
initial text, the phrase "A and O" into "Ah! und Oh!" involves a dramatic
change of signification with no change at all in the (oral) signifier (the addition
of -hi) is the mark of an alteration in perspective that both does and does not
change the nature of the material interpreted.
Another dimension of this verse is evident when one reads it in light of Wagner's
discussion in Oper und Drama of the vowel sounds "a" and "o," which he takes to
be the origin of all language. See Richard Wagner, Oper und Drama, in Samtliche
Schriften und Dichtungen, 12 vols. (Leipzig: Breitkopf et Hartel, 1900-1925), 3:276.
Given Nietzsche's youthful admiration for Wagner and his belief in Wagner's Zu-
kunftkunstwerk, which would combine artistic forms into a unity, Nietzsche appears
to be hinting at the particular form taken by his own youthful wisdom. My thanks
to Joel E. Mann for directing me to Wagner's own remarks on "a" and "o."

seems to have been pleased when his name appeared in the context of erudite graffiti.

sche. "Nietzsche is dead." God.' Rarely someone adds a third line; e.g., ' "Nietsche is
spelled wrong." Kaufmann.' " I assume, therefore, that Kaufmann would have been
pleased to see his book in Birdcage.)

1 GS i, p. 73: KSA 3, p. 369.
2 GS i, p. 73; KSA 3, p. 369. Indeed, this is precisely Schopenhauer's viewpoint:
"[T]he concept good... is essentially relative, and denotes the fitness or suitableness
of an object to any definite effort of the will." Arthur Schopenhauer, The World as Will
and Representation, trans. E. F. J. Payne, 2 vols. (New York: Dover, 1969), 1:360.
3 GS i, p. 73; KSA 3, pp. 369-370. This is a reference to medieval logical conun-
drums, in which scholastic thinkers perplexed themselves about whether anything
could be considered impossible for God. A logical impossibility is impossible for God,
not because of any lack in God's abilities, but because the project proposed is inco-
herent. Nietzsche suggests here that the very notion of human behavior that could
harm the species may be a logical impossibility—or, in other words, that this is a
senseless way to put the problem. Given the range of nuclear and chemical weapons
and the current ecological crisis, readers today are unlikely to agree with Nietzsche
on this point, if indeed he intends this as an assertion. The whole problem, however,
appears to be proposed as a kind of thought experiment.

37 One might wonder why, if this is so, anyone, including Nietzsche, would choose
to be a comedian. The solution to this problem is that the comedian identifies not
with the butt of humor but with the self that is already beyond it. Even though a
comedian may effect a deadpan stage persona, the person as comedian initiates mock-
ery of a represented "self with whom he or she is not currently identifying. This
does not mean, however, that the comedian is psychologically beyond the common
fear of being mocked in personal life. In fact, being a comedian can be a defense in
the face of this fear; for by making the persona the butt of humor, the comedian
deflects mockery from his or her sense of identity.

i Kuenzli, "The Signifying Process in Nietzsche's The Gay Science," p. 48; see also

NOTES TO PAGES 69—72
2 Preface, GS, p. 35; KSA 3, p. 349.
3 BGE 6, p. 13; KSA 5, pp. 19-20.
4 Although not directly discussing this issue, Bernd Magnus suggests another
reason for being wary of describing Nietzsche as a straightforward deconstructionist.
In "Self-Consuming Concepts," International Studies in Philosophy 21, 2 (1989): 63-
71, Magnus argues that Nietzsche's basic moves depend on self-consuming concepts
(such as eternal recurrence, the Ubermensch, etc.), and he defines a self-consuming
concept in the following terms: "[t]he concept in question requires as a condition
of its intelligibility (or even its possibility) the very contrast it wishes to set aside
or would have us set aside" (p. 64). Thus, on Magnus's view, Nietzsche may want
to set aside certain binary distinctions, but his own analysis fundamentally depends
upon them. The upshot of such a move could perhaps be called "deconstructive"
in the sense that it does displace the system in which such distinctions originally
occur (although Magnus does not employ the term in his article). Nevertheless,
Magnus's charge that self-consuming concepts employ the very distinctions they
attack might be seen as a challenge to those who make similar moves with the intent
of actually disposing of particular binary analyses. Magnus himself emphasizes Der-
rida's own distance from the term deconstruction (see Magnus, Stewart, and Mileur,
Nietzsche's Case, 12-13). He also implies that the conundrums involved in decon-
structive efforts that employ self-consuming concepts are more Derrida's focus than
the prestidigitation involved in applying the "deconstructive" method. See, for ex-
ample, p. 13:
Derrida insists that if an action is to consist in saying something, then there
must be a rule which transforms the saying into doing; and the rule must cite
the expression: therefore, no citation, no performance. This elegant example
of "deconstruction" can be understood as a demonstration that a thesis actually
requires as one of its conditions the very thing it means to reject; and focus
on this conundrum is a recurrent feature of Derrida's work.
5 This appears to be Magnus's understanding of Derrida's purpose as well.

mented, "It's only money because we think it's money."

Komischen: es soil, nach Vinet, die Naivetat der Siinde sein [There is a comic def-
inition of the comic: it should, after Vinet, be the naivete of sin]." See KSA 9, 3
[67], p. 65.
12 Indeed, I think many concerned with intertextuality are engaged, similarly, in

when they take more joy in intertextuality itself than in the jokes or insights that a
recognition of intertextuality might illuminate.

15 I refer here to the case in 1993 of Eden Jacobowitz, an Israeli student at the
University of Pennsylvania, who was censured for saying to some black students who
were making noise outside his dorm room, "Be quiet, you water buffaloes." He
claimed that "water buffalo" loosely translated a common Israeli expression for re-
ferring to noisy and rude individuals. The black students claimed that he was making
a racist slur. I do not know which interpretation describes the situation more accu-
rately. The case draws attention to the sorts of arguments that arise when particular
expressions are categorized as politically correct or incorrect, and attention is directed
at how expressions used are categorized. This focus all too often results in a lack of
concern with actual politeness, which is a considerably more subtle achievement than
the mere avoidance of proscribed vocabulary.

GS, pp. 350-351; KSA 3, p. 638. Nietzsche may find "the Eternal Feminine" a somewhat
absurd expression on grounds similar to those that he uses to criticize Kant in Beyond
Good and Evil:
"How are synthetic judgments a priori possible?" Kant asked himself—and what
really is his answer? "By virtue of a faculty." ... "By virtue of a faculty"—he
had said, or at least meant. But is that—an answer! An explanation! Or is it
not rather merely a repetition of the question? How does opium induce sleep?
"By virtue of a faculty," namely the virtus dormitiva, replies the doctor in
Moliere..., But such replies belong in comedy. ... (BGE 11, pp. 18-19; KSA 5,
pp. 24-25).
23 Cf. Kofinan, "Baubo," p. 192. Kofman compares the published passage with
Nietzsche's first draft, which includes some later omitted lines: "Is not the desire to
'explain' woman a denial of the feminine instinct, a degeneration? Is it not the will
to disillusion men?" See also Alison Ainley, " 'Ideal Selfishness,' " in Exceedingly Nietz-
sche: Aspects of Contemporary Nietzsche-Interpretation, ed. David Farrell Krell and Da-
vid Wood (New York: Routledge, 1988), pp. 116-130. Ainley suggests that Nietzsche's
association of truth and "the true world" with women stems from his playing on the
fact that the German terms for such abstract concepts as Truth (die Wahrheit), Beauty
(die Schonheit), Eternity (die Ewigkeit), Wisdom (die Weisheit), Happiness (die Gluck-
seligkeit), and Idea (die Idee) are all feminine nouns. She suggests that Nietzsche is in
certain ways exposing "the metaphysical hypostatisation" of some of these ideas and
also exposing "the hypostatisation of 'Woman, 'das Ewig-Weibliche,' which is so far
beyond mortality, so far beyond women in the plural and in the world, as to become
neuter-neutral" (p. 120).
24 In his notes Nietzsche does occasionally complain about women who devote

welche im Wesentlichen ernahrt werden und nicht arbeiten, konnte sofort in eine

existence! But one sees them before a shop window full of adornments and linens!]"
(KSA 9, 11 [6], p. 442). This, however, seems to be a complaint against imbalanced
concern for adornment at the expense of other concerns, not an attack on adornment
itself.

48 Schacht observes that Nietzsche's rejection of universal metaphysical essences
does not require that he avoid general category terms in his analysis. "He does reject
the notion of 'man' as a being possessed of some sort of immutable metaphysical
essence; but he is very differently disposed toward the notion of 'man' reconceived
along the lines of the ' "naturalized" humanity' to which he seeks to direct our at-
tention in The Gay Science." Schacht, "Nietzsche's Gay Science," p. 73; see also p. 84:
"Nietzsche did indeed repudiate the notion of 'man' as a kind of 'eternal truth,' very
early on. But it is of no little significance that he did so very early—and that, having
made this point, he then went on to recast this notion, devoting a great deal of effort
to the investigation of our nature thus reconceived."

See GS 363, pp. 318-320; KSA 3, pp. 610-612. For an interpretation of section 68 that

52 Kaufmann suggests a similar point when he makes his one complimentary
comment about Nietzsche's discussion of women: "One theme that Nietzsche intro-
duces in his discussion of women is of considerable interest but has so far received
little or no attention: roles" ("Translator's Introduction," GS, p. 24). Kaufmann him-
self does not pursue this topic, although he mentions some evidence that the topic
is a matter of growing scholarly concern. He also compares Nietzsche's comments
about women's roles to his suggestion that roles are a matter of faith, and that in
some societies role faith includes the belief "that one can manage almost any role"
(p. 28). Kaufmann seems to imply here that while role faiths are, in Nietzsche's view,
a matter of faith rather than nature, Nietzsche is especially dubious of the notion that
anyone can perform any role. (Indeed, Plato was tremendously suspicious of this view
as well.) Thus, Kaufmann's reading allows room for the claim that the cultural con-
ditioning of roles implies that they could be altered; it also suggests that Nietzsche
viewed the range of options open to any individual as limited.
53 That Nietzsche intended the passages about women to serve as a paradigm for
rethinking other matters is suggested by a note from the fall of 1881: "Paradoxie im
Weibe und seiner Erziehung—sehr geheimnifivoll und interessant.—Diesen Sinn hat
alle moral. [Paradox in women and their education—very secret-filled and interest-
ing.—Every morality has this meaning]." KSA 9, 12 [no], p. 596.
54 See The Doors, "Break on Through."
55 I suspect that the notorious passages on women from Beyond Good and Evil
(sections 232-239) presuppose the former as a more desirable end than the latter. In
these passages Nietzsche rejects the strategies of the feminist movement of his era.
Some of his reasons are that: (i) it is another herd phenomenon that aims to present
a unified front, thereby suppressing individual diversity within the movement; (2) the


NOTES TO PAGES 86—87
195
objectives that feminists seek are the opportunity to engage in herd preoccupations,
such as unindividuated careerism and the right to votk in other words, to join the
clumsy ranks of men; (3) feminists' efforts to demystify themselves for men are un-
likely to improve human relationships and are very likely to uglify them; and (4) the
effort to assert conscious control over matters, such ai sexual interaction, that have
been guided by instinct will undercut one of the few manifestations of unconscious
grace that modern humanity still displays. Many of these complaints reflect the fact
that Nietzsche values individual self-expression more hibhly than crowd behavior. His
efforts in The Gay Science to induce empathetic entertainment of others' perspectives
similarly reflect the high value that he places on the individual; for he is suggesting
that however well someone may think he (or she) knows "women," any particular
woman might have surprising motivations. If one considers the passages in The Gay
Science alongside those in Beyond Good and Evil, one might be led to reflect on the
question of how compatible political activism is with empathetic entertainment of
other individuals' perspectives. Does feminist activism! require the distortion or car-
icature of men's perspectives? or even of women's?
56 "Preface for the Second Edition," GS, p. 35; KSA 3, p. 349.
57 Surely he has himself in mind when he asserts, f'Everyone bears within him a
picture of woman derived from his mother: it is this [which determines whether, in
his dealings with women, he respects them or despises them or is in general indifferent
to them." HAH I, 380, p. 150; KSA 2, p. 265. A more striking admission prefaces some
of Nietzsche's most infamous statements about wome|n, sections 231-239 of Beyond
Good and Evil:
Learning changes us; it does what all nourishment does which also does not
merely "preserve"—as physiologists know. But at the bottom of us, really
"deep down," there is, of course, something unteachable, some granite of spir-
itual fatum, of predetermined decision and answeij to predetermined selected
questions. Whenever a cardinal problem is at stakej there speaks an unchange-
able "this is I"; about man and woman, for example, a thinker cannot relearn
but only finish learning—only discover ultimately rjow this is "settled in him."
At: times we find certain solutions of problems tha|t inspire strong faith in us;
some call them henceforth their "convictions." Later—we see them only as
steps to self-knowledge, signposts to the problem jve are—rather to the great
stupidity we are, to our spiritual fatum, to what! is unteachable very "deep
down."
After this abundant civility that I have just evidenced in relation to myself
I shall perhaps be permitted more readily to state ijt few truths about "woman
as such"—assuming that it is now known from the outset how very much
these are after all only—my truths. (BGE 231, p. 162; KSA 5, p. 170)
Maudemarie Clark points out that "woman as such" is a translation of Weib-an-
sich, and she sees this as a play on the Kantian notion of Ding-an-skh, the thing-in-
itself ("Nietzsche's Misogyny," p. 7). Nietzsche denies the existence of the latter. Might
he mean to underscore that his "truths" are his own projections?

63 Arthur Danto suggests how far Aristotle went in postulating different essences

York: Prentice Hall, 1987), pp. 202-218. There Danto notes (p. 20):
Aristotle insisted, for reasons I can only guess at, that women have fewer teeth
than men. In medieval representations of him, Aristotle is often depicted on
all fours, being ridden by a woman with a whip in her hand. This was Phyllis,
the mistress of Aristotle's pupil, Alexander. One might suppose from his pos-
ture of erotic domination by a woman he was mad about, that Aristotle would
have supposed she had more teeth than men, showing that even masochists
can be sexist. In any case, one need only look in the nearest female mouth to
refute that mighty thinker.

and refined build, with a rather feminine way about him," Krell and Bates remark,

Nitschke told me, he must have been around five feet four or five inches. Krell,
however, reports that Nietzsche was five feet seven. My thanks to Earl Nitschke for
keeping me posted on recent estimates of Nietzsche's height.
68 On the one hand, I assume here that "political correctness" implies rigid and

cluded that it accorded with one's vision of social harmony—a synonym for "polite,"
in effect. That is no longer what is understood by the expression.

Marx," in The Routledge History of Philosophy, vol. 6, The Age of German Idealism,
ed. Robert C. Solomon and Kathleen M. Higgins (London: Routledge, 1993), pp. 307-
310. Nola emphasizes that "Feuerbach views himself as 'a natural philosopher in the
domain of the mind,' " a self-characterization with which Nietzsche would surely be
sympathetic. Nietzsche does not often mention Feuerbach directly; and in a couple
of his notes he lists Feuerbach on a blacklist of major philosophers of the nineteenth
century who were "theologians and churchfathers." (See KSA 11, 26 [412], p. 262; also
KSA 11 26 [8], p. 152.) Nietzsche's imagery, however, consistently supports what Feu-
erbach himself considered his "reduction" of God to the human essence. One of
Nietzsche's rare mentions of Feuerbach occurs in a critique of Wagner, who at one
time considered himself a disciple of Feuerbach:
One should recall how enthusiastically Wagner at one time followed in the
footsteps of the philosopher Feuerbach: Feuerbach's cry of "healthy sensual-
ity"—that sounded in the thirties and forties, to Wagner as to many other
Germans (they called themselves the "young Germans"), like a cry of redemp-
tion. Did he at last come to learn otherwise? For at least it seems that he finally
had the will to teach otherwise. (GM III, 3, p. 100)

23 Nietzsche's Zarathustra makes the charge that those who seek security through
a return to Christianity are childish. See TSZ, pp. 292-293; KSA 4, p. 228. The depic-
tion of "Zarathustra's Ape" in the section "On Passing By" makes a similar contrast
between the imitator without discernment and Zarathustra. See TSZ, pp. 287-290;
KSA 4, pp. 222-225.
24 Carey, A Commentary on Five Odes of Pindar, p. 59.
25 The Orphics associated Ixion with eternal recurrence; and given that Erwin
Rohde, one of Nietzsche's close associates, studied such cults, Nietzsche was probably
well aware of this connection. See Karl Lowith, Samtliche Schriften, 9 vols. (Stuttgart:
J. B. Metzlersche Verlagsbuchhandlung, 1987), 6: 415-426; citing Erwin Rohde, Psyche:
The Cult of Souls and Belief in Immortality among the Greeks, trans. W. B. Hillis
(London: Routledge, 1950), p. 342. For an excellent discussion of the history of the
idea of eternal recurrence, see Bernd Magnus, Nietzsche's Existential Imperative (Bloo-
mington: Indiana University Press, 1978), pp. 47-68.
26 Schopenhauer explicitly associates the experience of temporality with the wheel
of Ixion and penal servitude:
[T]he subject of willing is constantly lying on the revolving wheel of Ixion, is
always drawing water in the sieve of the Danaids, and is the eternally thirsting
Tantalus.
When, however, an external cause or inward disposition suddenly raises us
out of the endless stream of willing, and snatches knowledge from the thraldom
of the will, the attention is now no longer directed to the motives of willing,
but comprehends things free from their relation to the will
Then all at
once the peace, always sought but always escaping us on that first path of
willing, comes to us of its own accord, and all is well with us. It is the painless
state, prized by Epicurus as the highest good and as the state of the gods; for
that moment we are delivered from the miserable pressure of the will. We
celebrate the Sabbath of the penal servitude of willing; the wheel of Ixion stands
still. (Arthur Schopenhauer, The World as Will and Representation, trans. E. F. J.
Payne, 2 vols. [New York: Dover, 1969] I, 38, p. 196)
Nietzsche was well aware of this passage, as his gloss on the final sentence in On the
Genealogy of Morals indicates:
What vehemence of diction! What images of torment and long despair! Whan
an almost pathological antithesis between "a moment" and the usual "wheel
of Ixion," "penal servitude of volition," and "vile urgency of the will!"—But
even if Schopenhauer was a hundred times right in his own case, what insight
does that give us into the nature of the beautiful? (GM III, 6, p. 105)

7 The demons I discuss here are associated with representatives of both the reli-
gious and the philosophical tradition that Nietzsche challenges. He most likely had
other demonic precedents in mind, however. Empedocles' pantheon included both
gods and daimones, whom Jonathan Barnes characterizes as "long lived but not im-
mortal." Barnes goes on to point out that the theory of eternal recurrence, which
was endorsed by Empedocles, "seems capable of investing Empedocles' daimones with
a sort of eternity." As so often, in the case of eternal recurrence, Nietzsche takes sides
against the later philosophical tradition by endorsing resources from the pre-Socratics.
See Jonathan Barnes, The Presocratic Philosophers, vol. 2, Empedocles to Democritus
(London: Routledge, 1979), pp. 200-201. One of the arguments that Empedocles uses
in support of eternal recurrence is essentially the same as that used by Nietzsche in
his cosmological sketches. See ibid., pp. 201-202; see also Magnus, Nietzsche's Existen-
tial Imperative, pp. 90—98.

York: Cambridge University Press, 1992), pp. 200-235, f°r discussion of this problem

56 On this point, Nietzsche does not entirely converge with Spinoza, who at times
seems to consider the mind to have more endurance than the body. See, for example,
Spinoza, Ethics, Part V, Proposition 23, in The Ethics and Selected Letters-, trans. Samuel
Shirley, ed. Seymour Feldman (Indianapolis: Hackett, 1982). Spinoza, however, con-
tends that the mind and body are not separate. Spinoza, when he speaks of the eternity
of the mind, may be using a collective sense of "the mind" in keeping with Mai-
monides' belief that the individual mind makes discoveries which continue in the
collective human experience even after the individual's death; it would not, on this
reading, necessarily instantiate the kind of mind/body dualism that Nietzsche finds
so pernicious, nor would it be a defense of an "afterlife" in its usual sense.

58 Magnus indicates some other reasons why the church historically frowned upon
recurrence. He reports that Origen considered it incompatible with human freedom
(presumably because one's conduct would already be fixed in eternity once the first
recurrence began), and that Nemesius and Augustine considered it incompatible with
the Resurrection, which was to divide history in two. See Magnus, Nietzsche's Exis-
tential Imperative, p. 62. For Magnus's discussion of the history of the idea of eternal
recurrence, see pp. 47-68.
59 As a historically trained philologist, Nietzsche is well aware that linear time is
the aberration among temporal myths. Many other traditions, including many schools
of ancient Greek thought, have believed that time is cyclical. The Western monothe-
istic religions are the primary source of support for this theory; and even those
traditions have seen a fair number of dissenters. Among these, in the Islamic tradition,
Ibn Rushd, or Averroes, believed that the universe is eternal. While this was not the
prevailing view among the Peripatetics, the reliance of several of these thinkers on
emanation as the structure of creation could be seen as driving a wedge between the
process of creation and "time" in its terrestrial sense. Creation has the logical status
of dependence on God as a manifestation of his own being; but it does not obviously
presuppose temporally distinct stages, as does the model of efficient causation.
60 The cyclical account of time might seem to contradict one of the premises of
Nietzsche's occasional cosmological reflections to the effect that time is infinite but
configurations of energy finite, and that therefore the configurations of energy must
recur. If time is really infinite, then how can it recur? This problem arises because of
an equivocation on the meaning of "time." On the one hand, we often conflate the
contents of time with "time" in our speech, as when we say that "history repeats
itself" or refer to "our time." In this sense, Nietzsche does not contend that time is
infinite; in other words, he contends that the energy configurations are finite. On the
other hand, we also consider "time" to be the sequential structure within which these
contents are ordered. It is this latter sense of "time" that Nietzsche takes to be infinite.
On this view, there is no "outside" to the system.

63 The notion of the Ubermensch is presented by Nietzsche's character Zarathustra

Spoke Zarathustra. The significance of the notion is itself a subject of considerable
controversy. One argument for defining eternal recurrence and the Ubermensch in
terms of each other might be to assist our understanding of the latter concept. My


2o8
NOTES TO PAGES 145—149
contention here is that this move does not help our understanding of eternal recur-
rence, and may even hinder it.

ideal relationship to reality." Young, Nietzsche's Philosophy of Art, p. 114.

70 Although I focus here on Magnus's critique of Nietzsche's conception of eternal
recurrence, my discussion applies as well to Julian Young's interpretation. While I
agree with Young that Nietzsche is seeking, through this notion, to redeem the past
in his own view, I do not agree that Nietzsche insists on the "redemption of each
and every hitherto unredeemed event." Instead, I think that Nietzsche's view is com-
patible with the more healthy outlook that Young opposes to Nietzsche's. Young
suggests, "There is no reason to suppose that the general confidence in the future
which, in normal circumstances, plausibly is implied by a high level of psychic energy
must include faith in the future justification of every 'questionable' aspect of the past."
Young, Nietzsche's Philosophy of An, p. 116. I agree with this assessment, but I think
that Nietzsche's thought experiment is aimed only at motivating this orientation, not
at nonstop ecstasy.

81 Spinoza's vision is vastly more appealing to Nietzsche than was Descartes's.
God is not a power in opposition to the natural world or its inhabitants; instead,

all-embracing immanent cause," as Donagan puts it (ibid., p. 354). The perfection of
Nature and God amount to the single substance serving as its own standard. Although
arguably still working within the Judeo-Christian tradition in certain respects, Don-
agan concludes, Spinoza is an atheist by traditional standards (see p. 357). Compa-
rably, as Julian Young remarks, Nietzsche's notion of the affirming of eternal recur-

17 C. G. Jung, Nietzsche's Zarathustra: Notes of the Seminar Given in 1934-1939, ed.
James L. Jarret, 2 vols., Bollingen Series 99 (Princeton: Princeton University Press,

Spoke Zarathustra may be a direct reference to the Gathas. Haug reminds us that "the
name Gatha, which is also well known in Sanskrit and Pali literature, means 'song.' "
Martin Haug "The Zend-Avesta; or the Scripture of the Parsis," in Essays on the
Sacred Language, Writings, and Religion of the Parsis, 3rd ed., p. 143). This is partic-
ularly significant in that the Gathas are apparently the only part of the Avesta that
is, to a large extent, composed by Zarathustra himself. It represents, according to
Haug, "the sayings and songs of Zarathushtra himself, mixed with those of his dis-
ciples .. ." (p. 143).
The similarity between the sequence of materials in Thus Spoke Zarathustra and
that found the Gathas also suggests Nietzsche's familiarity with the latter. The opening
Gatha, Gatha Ahunavaiti, includes Zarathustra's opening speech to the multitudes, a
speech in which he frequently refers to Armaiti, the spirit of the earth, who is said
to be one with Ahuramazda. This is followed by Zarathustra's "sayings." Zarathustra
is concerned to protect the good creation from the attacks of his enemies, much as
Nietzsche's Zarathustra is concerned to protect his teachings at the beginning of Part
II of Thus Spoke Zarathustra. Haug notes that "(w]hilst the first Gatha appears to be
a mere collection of fragments of hymns and scattered verses, made without any
other plan than to transmit to posterity what was believed to be the true and genuine
sayings of the prophet," the second Gatha exhibits "a certain scheme." He adds,
"Although its contents, with the exception of a few verses only. . ., are all sayings of
Zarathushtra himself, yet they have not been put together.. . irrespective of their


NOTES TO PAGES 153—154
211
contents, but in a certain order, with the view of presenting the followers of the
prophet with a true image of the mission, activity, and teaching of their great master."
Haug, "The Zend-Avesta; or The Scripture of the Parsis," p. 154. Nietzsche's Zara-
thustra, too, moves from more haphazard to more constructed order, both in the
context of Zarathustra's opening speech and in the move from Book I to Book II.
Haug indicates that the last three of the five Gathas seem largely to include the verses
of Zarathustra's disciples and perhaps of their students. These Gathas do not appear
to be as significant a basis for the structure of Thus Spoke Zarathustm as the first
two.

26 See Ibid., p. 51. Even some of the odd details of Nietzsche's plot may refer to
historical precedents. According to the Zartusht Namah, Zarathustra is miraculously
able to pass through a sea. Jackson reports that on the way to Iran, Zarathustra's
party "passes through a sea whose waters are lowered by a miracle so as to allow a
free crossing" (ibid., p. 39). Nietzsche's character has a similarly miraculous reap-
pearance after trouble on waters. See TSZ, pp. 241—242; KSA 4, pp. 167—168.
Nietzsche's conclusion of Book III with "The Seven Seals: The Yes and Amen-
Song" involves seven verses, which might well sound like answers to "seven ques-


212
NOTES TO PAGE 154
tions," an important theme in the historical Zarathustra's story. See Jackson, Zoro-
aster: The Prophet of Ancient Iran, p. 50. "The Seven Seals" also corresponds, in its
structural position at the end of one version of Nietzsche's book, to the position of
the last of the Gathas, Gatha 53, which is associated with a wedding. Duchesne-
Guillemin remarks, "The Gatha might be called the Marriage Gatha: it seems indeed
to have been pronounced in the course of a nuptial ceremony" (The Hymns of Zar-
athustra, p. 151). This Gatha describes the ultimate bliss of the righteous, and contrasts
them with "followers of Evil" who cry "Woe!" (ibid., pp. 154-155). Nietzsche's "Yes
and Amen Song" concludes with his famous round that contrasts the perspective of
eternal recurrence with that of "Woe!"
The "Honey Sacrifice" that begins Thus Spoke Zarathustra, with its many refer-
ences to Zarathustra's honey, may also have a historical precedent. Geiger and Win-
dishmann report, "Honey, too, is mentioned in the Avesta, if according to my sup-
position in the discourse on 'Mithra,' p. 72, the Madhu employed in offerings does
not mean 'wine,' but 'honey.' " Geiger and Windishmann, Zarathushtra in the Gathas
and in the Greek and Roman Classics, p. 117.
27 Rig-Veda 2.1.1.1; cited in Wendy Doniger O'Flaherty, with Daniel Gold, Da-
vid Haberman, and David Shulman, Textual Sources for the Study of Hinduism (To-
towa, N.Y.: Barnes and Noble Books, 1988), p. 6. Nietzsche was certainly aware of
the Rig-Veda, for Daybreak (the work that immediately precedes The Gay Science)
includes a line from the Rig-Veda on the title page. The line, as Nietzsche cites it,
is "Es giebt so viele Morgenrothen, die noch nicht geleuchtet haben [There are so
many dawns that have not yet dawned]." Sprung notes that the line from the Rig-
Veda is variably interpreted, even in one form as "There are so many dawns that
have already dawned." See Sprung, "Nietzsche's Trans-European Eye," pp. 78-79.
Nietzsche's general awareness of the basic texts of Hinduism seems to have begun
early. Not only are these texts regularly cited by Schopenhauer, whom Nietzsche
first read while still a student. Nietzsche also listed the Mahabharata and Ramayana
among great world epics that present profound accounts of fate in one of his high
school essays. Johann Figl notes that the library of Nietzsche's secondary school,
Schulpforta, included contemporary scholarly works on Asia, and that certain of
Nietzsche's instructors made reference to Indian philosophy in their publications.
Johann Figl, "Nietzsche's Early Encounters with Asian Thought," trans. Graham
Parkes, in Nietzsche and Asian Thought, ed. Graham Parkes (Chicago: University of
Chicago Press, 1991), pp. 52-57. Moreover, his friendship with Paul Deussen, who
specialized in Indian philology and philosophy, may have been another source of
Nietzsche's awareness of the Indian tradition, although the extent of this influence
is debated. At any rate, Nietzsche cited Deussen's book Das System des Vedanta.
Sprung, "Nietzsche's Trans-European Eye," pp. 76-79. Sprung is convinced that
Nietzsche's interest in India was quite limited, a conclusion he draws largely from
the scarcity of Nietzsche's references to and citations of Indian texts and his failure,
according to Deussen, to discuss Indian thought with him. When Nietzsche does
make use of Indian philosophy, it is more often because of Indian references to
problems that interest him than because of evident interest in Indian thought as
such, according to Sprung.

ment," pp. 267-271.

56 Gatha Ahunavaiti, Yas. xxxi, 9; in Haug, "The Zend-Avesta; or The Scripture
of the Parsis," pp. 151-152. Nietzsche's interest in the original Zarathustra's concern
for cattle raising may be indicated by his reference, in Thus Spoke Zarathustra, to
Zarathustra's residing in a city called "the Motley Cow." Admittedly, the town's name
alludes to another religious tradition as well. Freny Mistry notes that "Motley Cow"
is "a literal translation of the name of the town Kalmasadalmya (Pali: Kammasud-
damam) visited by the Buddha on his wanderings" (Nietzsche and Buddhism, p. 17).
But the reference to cattle is also appropriately Zoroastrian. Haug observes that the
practice of agriculture, a religious duty for Zoroastrians, is expressed in terms of "the
universal soul of the earth, the cause of all life and growth." The term for this "soul
of the earth" (Geush urvd), however, literally means "soul of the cow." This expression
is a simile, according to Haug, "for the earth is compared to a cow." The cow is thus
an image for the art of agriculture, and more broadly, for humanity's activity on
earth. Haug, "The Zend-Avesta or the Scripture of the Parsis," p. 148. Aptly, Nietz-
sche's Zarathustra similarly proclaims to his multitudes, "My brothers, remain faithful
to the earth...." TSZ, p. 125; KSA 4, p. 15. The original Zarathustra also mentions
among duties of the faithful "the care and the defense of the cattle." See Duchesne-
Guillemin, Introduction, in The Hymns of Zarathustra, pp. 5-6. Nietzsche's Zarathu-
stra descends to tend his own herd, the people of "The Motley Cow." While this is
probably in part a parody of Christ as the Good Shepherd tending his flock, the focus
on cows rather than sheep suggests a reference to Zarathustra's earthly doctrine as
well.

tion, the Holy Spirit, Right, and Good Mind (son of Right) form a trinity akin to
the later Christian trinity, although he notes that "Zoroastrianism did not grow in
this direction" (pp. 15-16).

67 KSA 9, Fall 1881, 12 [136], p. 599. This reference to goats recalls again the satyrs,
the beings who are half-goat, half-man in Greek mythology, of whom the Greek tragic
chorus was fictitiously composed. According to Nietzsche:
[T]he satyr was the archetype of man, the embodiment of his highest and most
intense emotions, the ecstatic reveler enraptured by the proximity of his god,
the sympathetic companion in whom the suffering of the god is repeated, one
who proclaims wisdom from the very heart of nature, a symbol of the sexual
omnipotence of nature which the Greeks used to contemplate with reverent
wonder. (BT, p. 61; KSA i, p. 58)


NOTES TO PAGES 163—166
215
The association of Zarathustra with goats and satyrs again underscores his position
as tragic hero. (Who else, after all, does the tragic hero encounter?) In Ecce Homo,
Nietzsche describes Zarathustra as representing the type of the Dionysian. See EH,
pp. 304-306; KSA 6, pp. 343-345-
68 GS, p. 33; KSA 3, p. 346.
69 KSA 9, Fall 1881, I2[ii2], p. 596.
70 KSA 9, Fall 1881, 12 [225), p. 616.
71 See GS 329, pp. 259-260; KSA 3, pp. 556-557.
72 Hollingrake's demonstration of Nietzsche's parodies of Wagner is thoroughly
convincing. Although my own concern here is with a different pattern in Nietzsche's
account of Zarathustra, I do not see my contentions as countering Hollingrake's case.
Nietzsche is a master of multilayered allusion, as the simultaneous parody of Scripture
and Plato (as well as Wagner's Ring) in Thus Spoke Zarathustra demonstrates. In a
sense, Nietzsche's references to the actual Zarathustra in his work fortify Hollingrake's
point that he took Wagner as his model in constructing Thus Spoke Zarathustra.
Hollingrake notes, "Reverting by way of the Icelandic classics to the mythological
sun-hero of immemorial antiquity, working backwards 'in the teeth of the historical
material'. . . , Wagner endeavoured to create a prototype of the future humanity to
which ... he ... aspired" (Nietzsche: The Man and His Philosophy, p. 32). Nietzsche
counters Wagner by employing an alternative Aryan myth, the historically based saga
of Zarathustra, toward his own conception of future humanity.
73 Hollingrake, Nietzsche: The Man and His Philosophy, pp. 167-168. Nietzsche's
employment of the historical Zarathustra is especially apt for satirizing Wagner's
work. In effect, Nietzsche opposes a more indigenous Aryan story for the Icelandic
legends that became German mythology, a matter of no small significance for some-
one who was so determined to make a mark on German culture as Wagner. Zara-
thustra is also an apt parodic vehicle, given that Nietzsche took particular exception
to Wagner's Parsifal as a travesty of mythic wisdom and a gesture of pure cynicism
on Wagner's part. Parsifal glorifies the myth of the Holy Grail; it treats sexual desire
as a temptation to be avoided; its hero is ignorant and totally passive, moved to do
anything only as a form of reaction; and pity is the means by which Parsifal's trans-
formation is accomplished (see ibid., p. 117). In this light, we can see Zarathustra's
drama as antithetical to Parsifal. Parsifal represents the young fool; Zarathustra is the
old man who has absorbed the lessons of his youthful folly and developed wisdom
in the process. Parsifal succumbs to tradition, abandoning the self-reliance that was
essential to the integrity of his character. Zarathustra initiated a break with tradition
and, in Nietzsche's view, maintained truthfulness against any tradition or habit, in-
cluding his own.
74 GS 153, p. 197; KSA 3, p. 496.
75 BT, pp. 96-97: KSA i, p. 99.
76 Plato, The Symposium, trans. Alexander Nehamas and Paul Woodruff (Indi-
anapolis: Hackett, 1989), 1. 223c-d, p. 77. Although the text does not report Socrates'
rationale, his reasoning may well resemble that which he uses in the Ion. In that
dialogue Socrates judged Ion wanting because he could not apply his art to all poetry
but only to that of Homer. The problem, according to Socrates, was that Ion did not
operate by means of knowledge but by instinct alone. Full knowledge should provide
the artist with the ability to apply his or her talents to the whole spectrum of his art.
My thanks to Paul Woodruff for the suggestion that the Ion might elucidate the
meaning of this cryptic remark at the end of the Symposium.
77 TSZ, p. 439; KSA 4, p. 408. Zarathustra is speaking in this passage about pity,
his own typical "tragic" response.

Coda
1 GS 312, pp. 249-250; KSA 3, pp. 547-548. The passage rather humorously liter-
alizes the German association of dogs with misery, indicated in the expression "auf
dem Hunde sein" (literally, "to be on the dog"), which means "to be miserable,"
and the expletive "beim Hund," "by the dog!"
2 Milan Kundera describes something similar in The Unbearable Lightness of Being
when he describes "the categorical agreement with being," the belief "that the world
was created properly, that human existence is good, and that we are therefore entitled
to multiply," as being undercut by the existence of "shit, in both the literal and the
figurative senses of the word" (p. 248).
3 In section 48 of The Gay Science, for example, he emphasizes the intensity of
psychological misery when physical misery is lacking. GS 48, pp. 112-113; KSA 3,
pp. 413-414. See also his reiterated discussions of ressentiment (the French term Nietz-
sche prefers over any German term connoting resentment) and will to revenge, for
example, in Thus Spoke Zarathustra, pp. 249-253; KSA 4, pp. 177-181; and in On the
Genealogy of Morals generally. For a defense of the claim that Nietzsche is largely
concerned with psychological pain when he discusses suffering, see Danto, "Some
Remarks on The Genealogy of Morals" pp. 13-28.
4 This is the very stuff that motivates ressentiment, as Nietzsche desribes it. For a
discussion of resentment and habitual revenge in Book V of The Gay Science, see GS
359, PP. 3H-315; KSA 3, pp. 605-607.
5 Nietzsche's letters give ample testimony to such feelings. He complains about
his isolation to Franz Overbeck in a letter of August 5, 1886: "Among the living, as
among the dead, I have nobody with whom I have any affinity. It gives me the
shudders—indescribably. .. ." Selected Letters, p. 254; KSA-SB 7, p. 223. On February
3,1888, again writing to Overbeck, he laments, "The perpetual lack of a really refresh-
ing and healing human love, the absurd isolation which it entails, making almost any
residue of a connection with people merely something that wounds one—that is all
very bad indeed. . .." Selected Letters, February 3, 1888, p. 282; KSA-SB, 8, p. 242.
Nietzsche frequently insists in his letters that he is not understood. To his mother
he wrote on October 18, 1887, "Never since childhood to have heard anything deep
and understanding said about me—that is all part of my fate...." Selected Letters,
p. 271; KSA-SB 8, p. 170. To Peter Gast he wrote on July 18,1887, "[t]he approximately
one hundred copies [of Beyond Good and Evil] which have been sold have made it
quite obvious to me that I am not understood." Selected Letters, p. 269; KSA-SB 8,
p. 112. He wrote to Karl Knortz on June 21, 1888: "Of my Zarathustra, I tend to think
that it is the profoundest work in the German tongue, also the most perfect in lan-
guage. But for others to feel this will require whole generations to catch up with the
inner experiences from which that work could arise." Selected Letters, p. 299; KSA-SB
8, p. 340.
Resentment is also evident in Nietzsche's letters. In May 21, 1884, he wrote to
Overbeck, "I must put an end to the business with my relatives—for two years I have
been wearing myself out with the most good-natured efforts to put things right and
to put their minds at rest, but in vain." Selected Letters, p. 225; KSA-SB 6, p. 505.
Nietzsche wrote to Reinhart von Sedlitz on February 24, 1887: "Germany seems to
me to have become, during the past fifteen years, a real school of stultification. Water,
mess, and filth everywhere...;—that is how it looks from the distance." Selected
Letters, p. 262; KSA-SB 8, p. 31. He wrote to Seydlitz on February 12, 1888: "There are
plenty of bad and slanderous gestures in my direction; an unrestrainedly hostile tone
is paramount in the periodicals—learned and unlearned—but how is it that nobody
protests against this? that nobody ever feels hurt when I am censured? And in all the
years no solace, not a drop of humanity, not a breath of love—" Selected Letters,


NOTES TO PAGES 167—169
217
p. 284; KSA-SB 8, pp. 248-249. On November 11, 1887, he wrote to Erwin Rohde,
"Who has ever been concerned for me with even the slightest degree of passion and
pain!" Selected Letters, p. 276; KSA-SB 8, p. 195. Middleton notes that he used the
same phrase in a letter to Overbeck the next day, writing also, "This terrible decade
that I have behind me has given me a plentiful taste of what it means to be alone—
isolation such as I have known it, the isolation and exposure of a sufferer who has
no means even of protecting, even of defending himself Selected Letters, p. 276; KSA-
SB 8, p. 196.
That Nietzsche so frequently exhibits a morose side does not, I think, undercut
my case to the effect that he had comic intentions in The Gay Science. Cf. Dotson
Rader, "What Really Makes Life Fun," Parade Magazine Austin American Statesman,
September 20, 1998, p. 5., in which Robin Williams is interviewed as saying: "For me
.. . comedy starts as a spew, a kind of explosion, and then you sculpt it from there,
if at all. It comes out of a deeper, darker side. Maybe it comes from anger.... I use
comedy to find a way through."
6 In this respect, it resembles love as Nietzsche describes it in GS 334, p. 262; KSA
3, PP. 559-560.
7 My thanks to Lorin Hollander, who suggested the importance of this distinction
in his talk "Multi-culturalism in Western Art Music" presented at the "Music and
Cross-Cultural Understanding" conference at the University of Dayton, September
25> 1997- Robert John Ackermann offers an account of Nietzsche's aims with regard
to personal suffering along similar lines in Nietzsche: A Frenzied Look, pp. 40-41. See
also Liam O'Sullivan, "Nietzsche and Pain," Journal of Nietzsche Studies 11 (Spring
1996): 13-22. O'Sullivan describes pain as "a shape of consciousness" as Nietzsche sees
it:
Real suffering is personal suffering; it begins as internal suffering. This is the
suffering (not that of others) that needs to be overcome. Affirmative happiness
represents the overcoming of negative (personal) suffering. The actual trans-
valuation of suffering is from an external matter to an internal matter (happily)
provoking self-transcendence, (p. 40)
8 That pain was a central topic of concern when he wrote The Gay Science is
suggested by one of Nietzsche's remarks in Ecce Homo about the period that produced
the book. He remarks that along with The Gay Science, his "Hymn to Life" for mixed
choir and orchestra was written during that period. Of the text, by Lou Salome, he
comments: "Whoever can find any meaning at all in the last words of this poem will
guess why I preferred and admired it: they attain greatness. Pain is not considered an
objection to life: 'If you have no more happiness to give me, well then! you still have
suffering EH, pp. 292-297; KSA 6, p. 336.
My comments on section 312 may suggest that I think that Julian Young is right
in considering The Gay Science one of Nietzsche's various attempts to come to grips
with our powerlessness to change the past. Indeed, I think that this description does
get to the heart of Nietzsche's motivation in much of his writing, and I think that
eternal recurrence, for example, is articulated with this problem in mind. I disagree
with Young's assessment of The Gay Science because I do not think that Nietzsche's
formulations do require what Young and Magnus commonly see as insane: the re-
demption of every feature of the past. Young, Nietzsche's Philosophy of Art, p. 116.
